[scan 24] The Psychology of Aristotle / in Particular His Doctrine / of the / ΝΟΥΣ ΠΟΙΗΤΙΚΟΣ.

By / Dr. Franz Brentano, / Privatdozent in Philosophy at the University of Würzburg.

Though my word’s first taste / Be irksome, yet, once it has been digested, / It will leave the hearer nourishment for life.

Dante.

(With an Appendix on the Activity of the Aristotelian God.)

Mainz, / Published by Franz Kirchheim. / 1867.

[scan 26] To the memory / of my / dear fatherly friend / Dr. Joseph Merkel, / Court Librarian and Professor at the Lyceum in Aschaffenburg, / who / even in the last days of his life gave the work then taking shape / his loving interest, / dedicated / in / faithful gratitude.

[scan 28]

### Vorwort. — Preface.

The word of Dante that we have written over this treatise holds true not only of the poet himself; it is just as characteristic of the great thinker whom he honors as the master of those who know. Senseless and confused at first hearing, his pronouncements often become intelligible only to long and earnest reflection; but the light of the thought, when at last it shines forth from the dark word, is then a rich reward for all the labor.

If this holds in general and of almost every work of Aristotle, it holds in a quite special way of that passage in the third book On the Soul which contains the doctrine of the νοῦς ποιητικός, and whose explanation is the foremost endeavor of our treatise. Great connoisseurs of Aristotle, men who elsewhere joyfully admire the clarity of his mind, believed they saw nothing here but a web of contradictions; and if we ourselves have arrived at a different and opposite result, [p. VI] it was nevertheless only after much exertion and many an unsuccessful attempt.

What formerly appeared to us as murky confusion now shows itself to us as a simple and luminous thought, fully worthy of the Aristotelian mind, and perhaps the most significant thing that research into the origin of thoughts has found to this day.

Of the means we employ in the investigation, and of the plan that our whole treatise follows, the Introduction and especially the beginning of the second Section give a detailed account. From them one can see why it seemed necessary to let a comprehensive presentation of Aristotle’s psychology precede the development of the doctrine of the νοῦς ποιητικός.

Franz Brentano.

Würzburg, July 14, 1866.

[scan 30]

### Einleitung. — Introduction.

In the consideration of any philosophical system, the doctrine of the cognitive powers deserves quite particular attention; not only because its object belongs to the most sublime things with which the human mind can occupy itself, and because many of the most important questions, in particular the question of the immortality of our soul, stand in intimate connection with these inquiries, but also because in this part more than in others every philosophy offers a sure standard for judging the whole, and in the differences between theories of knowledge the characteristic differences of the systems as a whole themselves come most clearly to light. The skeptic speaks one way here, in his destructive discussions, which are at odds even with themselves, the dogmatist another; the materialist speaks one way here, the spiritualist another; the theory of knowledge of the pantheist takes one shape, that of the theist another; and even philosophies that prove to be related in kind and in their fundamental views show their specific differences here above all—as, in particular, do the two great systems of antiquity, the doctrines of Plato and of his pupil Aristotle, who in many things remained faithful to him.

But the theory of knowledge of this thinker claims our interest not merely for such general reasons. Aristotle was that inquirer who, before all others, cultivated the field of logic with great success; in its domain more than in any other his propositions have remained unshaken, and posterity gratefully honors him as the creator and father of this science. Yet what disciplines could stand closer to each other than logic and that part of psychology of which we are speaking? Every logic that goes deeper must descend into its domain, and there is no other reason why logic has at certain times become unfruitful and stunted than that it did not sink its roots into the soil of psychology and draw from it the nourishment of life.

And as logic takes its principles from psychology, so psychology ends in logic. The relation of the two [p. 2] sciences<sup>1)</sup> is quite similar to that which, as Aristotle remarks, exists between the art of healing<sup>2)</sup> and that part of natural science which more recent times have named biology or physiology (in the wider sense). It belongs to the task of the natural scientist to know the first principles of the doctrine of health and disease, and therefore, says Aristotle, we see that most natural scientists, and those physicians who pursue their art in a more scientific spirit, the former end their inquiries in the domain of medicine, while the latter, starting out from that of natural science, develop the doctrines of the art of healing.

So in the case of him who was the teacher of the centuries in logic, one can approach the consideration of his (psychological) theory of knowledge only with the most favorable prejudice, and every contribution to its understanding, and in particular an attempt to elucidate that point which belongs among the most important in it, but at the same time is probably also the most obscure of all, should be of interest to every friend of philosophy. We mean the Aristotelian doctrine of the νοῦς ποιητικός, the discussion of which we set ourselves as our chief task in this treatise.

The undertaking is a difficult one, and the difficulty has its ground partly, no doubt, in the nature of the subject, but partly [p. 3] also in the fact that Aristotle, who is everywhere brief and sparing of words, seems, at the only place where he treats directly and immediately of the νοῦς ποιητικός<sup>3)</sup>, to want to abbreviate even his brevity; partly, finally, in the evident ambiguity of many words, on whose correct understanding the understanding of the doctrine essentially depends. Thus Aristotle speaks of something separate (χωριστόν), of which he nevertheless at the same time says that it can be separated (χωρίζεσθαι), evidently in the sense of a separation that does not yet exist<sup>4)</sup>; he speaks of something incapable of being affected (ἀπαθές) and at the same time ascribes to it a being affected (πάσχειν), evidently in another sense of being affected<sup>5)</sup>; he calls something immaterial (ἄνευ ὕλης) and yet gives it a matter (ὕλη)<sup>6)</sup>; finally, he uses precisely the word on whose understanding everything chiefly depends, the word νοῦς, to designate very different things. Now he gives this name to a disposition which we only acquire, now he gives this name to a cognitive power given by nature<sup>7)</sup>; now he gives this name to something substantial, now he gives this name to a faculty of the substance<sup>8)</sup>; now he gives this name to an acting principle, now he gives this name to that which receives its action<sup>9)</sup>; now he gives this name to something free from being affected and spiritual, now he gives this name to something subject to being affected and to corruption, to something sensory and to sensory sensation [p. 4] itself<sup>10)</sup>. This readily explains the hopeless confusion of concepts that has arisen among some commentators, as well as the fact that many who likewise did not everywhere recognize the difference of meaning believed they saw irreconcilable contradictions in the doctrine of our philosopher. Nowhere, accordingly, do the interpreters of Aristotle diverge in different and opposite directions more than on this point. Let us review their opinions in brief outline.

1) More precisely, the relation between the psychological theory of knowledge and logic. With other parts of psychology ethics stands in a similarly intimate connection (cf. Nic. I, 13. p. 1102, a, 18—23.), and from this too one can recognize Aristotle’s high gift for psychological inquiries, since his Ethics too is an acknowledged and unsurpassed masterpiece.

2) De Sens. et Sens. I. p. 436, a, 17—b, 1. Likewise De Respirat. 21. p. 480, b, 22. Here we find the following remark, which may have been the beginning of a lost treatise De Sanitate et Morbo (cf. De Sens. et Sens. I. p. 436, a, 17.): περὶ δὲ ὑγιείας καὶ νόσου οὐ μόνον ἐστὶν ἰατροῦ ἀλλὰ καὶ τοῦ φυσικοῦ μέχρι του τὰς αἰτίας εἰπεῖν. ᾗ δὲ διαφέρουσι καὶ ᾗ διαφέροντα θεωροῦσιν, οὐ δεῖ λανθάνειν, ἐπεὶ ὅτι γε σύνορος ἡ πραγματεία μέχρι τινός ἐστι, μαρτυρεῖ τὸ γινόμενον· τῶν τε γὰρ ἰατρῶν ὅσοι κομψοὶ ἢ περίεργοι, λέγουσί τι περὶ φύσεως καὶ τὰς ἀρχὰς ἐκεῖθεν ἀξιοῦσι λαμβάνειν, καὶ τῶν περὶ φύσεως πραγματευθέντων οἱ χαριέστατοι σχεδὸν τελευτῶσιν εἰς τὰς ἀρχὰς τὰς ἰατρικάς. Cf. also Nic. I, 13. p. 1102, a, 21. That it is not inappropriate to compare logic, given the position Aristotle has assigned to it, with this poietic science, may be shown here by a single passage from the Topics (Top. I, 3. p. 101, b, 5.): ἕξομεν δὲ τελέως τὴν μέθοδον, ὅταν ὁμοίως ἔχωμεν ὥσπερ ἐπὶ ῥητορικῆς καὶ ἰατρικῆς καὶ τῶν τοιούτων δυνάμεων (poietic sciences; cf. Met. Θ, 2. p. 1046. b, 3. and the Comment. by Bonitz). τοῦτο δ᾽ ἐστὶ τὸ ἐκ τῶν ἐνδεχομένων ποιεῖν ἃ προαιρούμεθα. Zeller rightly remarks (Phil. der Griech. 2, 2. p. 130.) that the place here assigned to the Topics belongs, according to Aristotle, to all the remaining parts of logic as well. We never find logic enumerated as a fourth theoretical science alongside metaphysics, mathematics, and physics. It is the art of producing knowledge.

### Erster Abschnitt. — First Section.

### Ueberblick der früheren Erklärungsversuche. — Survey of Earlier Attempts at Explanation.

#### a. Auslegungen der ältesten Zeit. — a. Interpretations of the Earliest Period.

1. Even immediate pupils of Aristotle seem not to be at one in their conception of the νοῦς ποιητικός; for *Theophrastus*<sup>1)</sup> speaks of it in a way that leaves no doubt that he regarded the νοῦς ποιητικός as something belonging to the essence of man<sup>2)</sup>; but against this, appeal is made to the Ethics of *Eudemus*, in which this philosopher, of whom it is reported that he followed most faithfully in the paths of his teacher<sup>3)</sup>, seems to designate God as the active intellect<sup>4)</sup>. Were this correct, then the greatest pupils of Aristotle, who had received the doctrine immediately from the master himself, would stand in precisely that opposition to each other which forms the widest gulf that still separates the interpretations even today. But then it would surely be certain that all future time too would have to renounce the hope of a sure explanation. But no such contradiction exists. We shall see later how the passages of Eudemus and Theophrastus can be brought into perfect agreement.

2. The fragment of the fifth book of the Physics of *Theophrastus*, the second of his doctrine of the soul, that Themistius has preserved for us allows three points to be recognized even on superficial consideration:

1) In Theophrastus’ conception, Aristotle held not only the active but also the receptive intellect, which becomes everything intelligible, to be immaterial<sup>5)</sup>.

1) In Themistius, Paraphr. d. anim. f. 91.

2) Cf. *Brandis*, Entwickel. d. griech. Philos. p. 572.

3) Simpl. Phys. 93, b, m. μαρτυρεῖ δὲ τῷ λόγῳ καὶ Εὔδημος, ὁ γνησιώτατος τῶν Ἀριστοτέλους ἑταίρων.

4) Eth. Eudem. VII, 14. p. 1248, a, 25.

5) For with regard to the δυνάμει νοῦς he raises, loc. cit., among others also the following difficulty: ἀσωμάτῳ δὲ ὑπὸ σώματος τί τὸ πάθος; ἢ ποία μεταβολή; and further below he says of it: τὰς μὲν αἰσθήσεις οὐκ ἄνευ σώματος, τὸν δὲ νοῦν χωριστόν.

[p. 6] 2) He regarded both as faculties of one and the same subject<sup>6)</sup>.

3) He regarded this subject as an essential constituent of man<sup>7)</sup>.

Whether Theophrastus himself agreed with this doctrine of his master or not cannot be gathered from the passage, and this is also of no importance to us here. His agreement, however, is the more probable<sup>8)</sup>; the difficulties he raises are no proof of real doubt; they are only difficulties which he brings forward, following the method customary with him and already with his teacher, in order thereby to make the question clear and to gain points of support for the scientific inquiry that is to begin<sup>9)</sup>.

3. The reports that history has preserved for us of other Peripatetics of that earliest period are too meager for us to form a thoroughly clear and sure view even of their own philosophical opinions, let alone to see from them how they may have interpreted the doctrines of Aristotle, from which, as is well known, they had little hesitation in departing. *Strato*, the head of the school after Theophrastus, already denied all spiritual cognition, as indeed the whole domain of the spiritual<sup>10)</sup>, and something similar is reported of some fellow pupils of Theophrastus, of *Aristoxenus* and *Dicaearchus*<sup>11)</sup>.

6) That the νοῦς ποιητικός too is something accidental (for with regard to the νοῦς δυνάμει there can be no doubt in this respect, since otherwise, as potentiality, it would have to be identical with substantial matter) is shown by the question: τί τὸ ὑποκείμενον . . τῷ ποιητικῷ; but that its subject is the same as that of the νοῦς δυνάμει is proved (to leave aside another statement of which it is doubtful whether it belongs to Theophrastus and not rather to Themistius) by the difficulty raised soon afterward, why the active intellect does not move the receptive intellect always and from the beginning: εἰ μὲν οὖν σύμφυτος ὁ κινῶν, καὶ εὐθὺς ἐχρῆν καὶ ἀεί [sc. κινεῖν]. Cf. below Section II, Part IV, n. 338.

7) He raises the question: ὁ δὲ νοῦς πῶς ποτε ἔξωθεν ὢν καὶ ὥσπερ ἐπίθετος, ὅμως συμφυής; and further below he remarks, preparing the way for a solution: ἀλλὰ τὸ ἔξωθεν ἄρα οὐχ ὡς ἐπίθετον ἀλλ᾽ ὡς ἐν τῇ πρώτῃ γενέσει συμπεριλάμβανον (read συμπεριλαμβανόμενον) θετέον.

8) Torstrik, Arist. d. Anim. Comment. critic. p. 189, holds the opposite.

9) For some of these difficulties the solution is already found in the fragment preserved to us itself. That it was not lacking for the others either seems to me to follow from the words of Themistius: καὶ τὰ ἐφεξῆς μακρὸν ἂν εἴη παρατίθεσθαι καίτοι μὴ μακρῶς εἰρημένα ἀλλὰ λίαν συντόμως καὶ βραχέως τῇ γε λέξει· τοῖς γὰρ πράγμασι μεστά ἐστι πολλῶν μὲν ἀποριῶν, πολλῶν δὲ ἐπιστάσεων, *πολλῶν δὲ λύσεων*. So too he remarks at the end: ὅτι δὲ μάλιστα ἄν τις ἐξ ὧν συνηγάγομεν ῥήσεων λάβοι τὴν περὶ τούτου γνῶσιν Ἀριστοτέλους καὶ Θεοφράστου . . ., τοῦτο οὖν πρόχειρον ἴσως διισχυρίζεσθαι. Had Theophrastus contradicted the Aristotelian doctrine, Themistius would surely not have expressed himself in this way.

10) Cic. Acad. IV, 38. De Nat. Deor. I, 13. Sext. Math. VII, 350.

11) Cic. Tusc. I, 10. and 22.

[p. 7] 4. The Peripatetic school in the first and second centuries after Christ, on the other hand, again had greater reverence for the pronouncements of its founder and made it almost its exclusive task to explain and defend the works of Aristotle. From it we possess some writings, all of which belong to *Alexander of Aphrodisias*, and among them also some that treat of the doctrine of the soul. Now Alexander does in fact interpret the doctrine of Aristotle to mean that the νοῦς ποιητικός is a purely spiritual substance separate from the essence of man and acting upon him, the first ground of things, the divine intelligence itself<sup>12)</sup>. Through its influence, on his view, man becomes actually cognizant, while the capacity to receive this influence<sup>13)</sup> is the result of a certain mode of mixture of the elements in the human body; the soul of man is therefore, in its thinking and being, wholly dependent on the body, and mortal<sup>14)</sup>.

Given the high esteem that Alexander enjoyed, and that caused the name of the Exegete κατ᾽ ἐξοχήν to be bestowed upon him, many others certainly adopted this interpretation of his; and to them seems to refer what Themistius relates<sup>15)</sup>, that there are those who held the νοῦς ποιητικός to be the Deity.

5. Others, who departed as much from the Aphrodisian as from Theophrastus, whose testimony Themistius holds up against both<sup>16)</sup>, believed that by the νοῦς ποιητικός they had to understand the immediately known propositions and the truths that follow from them, probably referring to the νοῦς ποιητικός some passages in the last chapter of the Posterior Analytics and in the Nicomachean Ethics<sup>17)</sup>.

12) De Anim. I. f. 139, b, m.: ἀπαθὴς δὲ ὢν (sc. ὁ ποιητικὸς νοῦς) καὶ μὴ μεμιγμένος ὕλῃ τινὶ καὶ ἄφθαρτός ἐστιν. . . . τοιοῦτον δὲ ὂν δέδεικται ὑπ᾽ Ἀριστοτέλους τὸ πρῶτον αἴτιον ὃ καὶ κυρίως ἐστὶ νοῦς. ibid. f. 144, a, o. τοῦτο δὴ τὸ νοητόν τε τῇ αὐτοῦ φύσει καὶ κατ᾽ ἐνέργειαν νοῦς, αἴτιον γινόμενον τῷ ὑλικῷ νῷ τοῦ κατὰ τὴν πρὸς τὸ τοιοῦτον εἶδος ἀναφορὰν χωρίζειν τε καὶ μιμεῖσθαι καὶ νοεῖν καὶ τῶν ἐνύλων εἰδῶν ἕκαστον καὶ ποιεῖν νοητὸν αὐτό, θύραθέν ἐστι λεγόμενος νοῦς ὁ ποιητικός, οὐκ ὢν μόριον καὶ δύναμίς τις τῆς ἡμετέρας ψυχῆς, ἀλλ᾽ ἔξωθεν γινόμενος ἐν ἡμῖν, ὅταν αὐτὸ νοῶμεν . . . χωριστὸς δέ ἐστιν ἡμῶν τοιοῦτος ὢν εἰκότως.

13) He calls this capacity νοῦς ὑλικός, a designation which has its ground in the words of Aristotle (De Anim. III, 5. p. 430, a, 10. 13. 19.) and was retained by the Arabs.

14) De Anim. I. f. 126. 127.

15) Paraphr. d. Anim. f. 89. ἐκ δὲ τῶν αὐτῶν ῥήσεων (sc. Θεοφράστου) θαυμάσαι κἀκείνων ἄξιον ὅσοι τὸν ποιητικὸν τοῦτον νοῦν ἢ τὸν πρῶτον θεὸν ᾠήθησαν εἶναι κατὰ Ἀριστοτέλην ἢ τὰς προτάσεις καὶ τὰς ἐξ αὐτῶν ἐπιστήμας, αἳ ὕστερον ἡμῖν παραγίνονται.

16) See the preceding n.

17) See above Introd. p. 3. n. 7.

[p. 8]

#### b. Auffassungen im Mittelalter. — b. Conceptions in the Middle Ages.

6. Thus men disputed in antiquity; just as little could they reach agreement in the Middle Ages. The *Arabian philosophers*, who through the Syrians stood in a traditional connection with the Peripatetic school in Alexandria<sup>18)</sup>, are evidently influenced by Alexander of Aphrodisias, though by no means in complete agreement with him<sup>19)</sup>. In particular, the conceptions of the two most famous teachers among the Arabs, Avicenna (Ibn Sina) and Averroes (Ibn Rushd), differed from that of Alexander in that, as Themistius and Theophrastus had done, they regarded as something immaterial, besides the νοῦς ποιητικός, also the νοῦς that receives thoughts, which is at first all things in potentiality. We shall set out their views too in brief, especially since the accounts given hitherto do not seem suited to give a thoroughly clear and correct picture of them. From the consequences that show themselves here we shall best be able to see how foreign to the spirit of the Aristotelian philosophy is the element which the Aphrodisian brought into psychology by separating the νοῦς ποιητικός from the individuality of man.

7. *Avicenna* teaches, like Alexander of Aphrodisias, that of that twofold intellect which Aristotle distinguishes in the fifth chapter of the third book On the Soul, the intellect that becomes all things and the intellect that makes all things, the former, but not the latter, is found in man as its subject.

His doctrine is, in its basic features, as follows:

1) The material intellect (intellectus<sup>20)</sup> materialis), for so Avicenna, following Alexander<sup>21)</sup>, calls the Aristotelian νοῦς [p. 9] δυνάμει, not in order to designate it as corporeal, but only in order to designate it as the passive substrate of ideas, as the possibility of thoughts, is a cognitive faculty peculiar to the nature of man, with which he grasps the intelligible form.

18) See *Renan*, De philos. peripat. apud Syros p. 73.

19) There were, however, also many who followed him blindly, for Averroes complains that in his time no one was considered a genuine Aristotelian who did not pay homage to the opinions of Alexander. De Anim. III, 1. t. c. 5.

20) The Latin translation before us always designates the νοῦς δυνάμει by the name *intellectus*, but the νοῦς ποιητικός mostly by the expression *intelligentia*. This is evidently meant to indicate the different nature that belongs to the one and to the other νοῦς according to Avicenna’s doctrine; for in the writings translated from the Arabic (at least in the translations of earlier times) intelligentia is the designation for the incorporeal spirits. Thomas Aquinas remarks on this in his Summa theologica, 1a, 79, 10.: Hoc nomen intelligentia proprie significat ipsum actum intellectus, qui est intelligere. In quibusdam tamen libris de arabico translatis substantiae separatae, quas nos angelos dicimus, intelligentiae vocantur, forte propter hoc, quod hujusmodi substantiae semper actu intelligunt. We conjectured that the difference in the use of intellectus and intelligentia might have its ground in an analogous difference in the Arabic original. This, however, as we have been informed by a learned friend, is not the case.

21) See above p. 7. n. 13.

2) Its subject is not a bodily organ, but the soul alone. For in man the soul is, with respect to its highest part, a spiritual substance, not mixed with the body<sup>22)</sup>, and therefore (and indeed this holds of the individual human soul)<sup>23)</sup> it does not perish in the death of the body either; with respect to one part it is, in its existence, independent of the body and immortal<sup>24)</sup>.

3) At first the material intellect is only potentially cognizant. In order that it may actually cognize, the ideas must be communicated to it by another, purely spiritual substance separate from the essence of man<sup>25)</sup>.

22) Lib. Natur. VI. p. 5. c. 2. princ. Id de quo nulla est dubitatio hoc est, quod in homine est aliqua substantia, quae apprehendit intelligibilia recipiendo. Dicemus ergo, quod substantia, quae est in subjectum intelligibilium, non est corpus neque habens esse propter corpus ullo modo, eo quod est virtus in eo aut forma ejus. There follows a multitude of proofs, some taken from Aristotle, some peculiar to Avicenna.

23) Avicenna does indeed believe that a plurality of human souls is not conceivable without relation to the bodies animated by them, and therefore disputes the possibility of a preexistence of the soul before the body. (Lib. Natur. VI. p. 5. c. 3. med. Dicemus autem, quod anima humana non fuit prius existens per se et inde venerit in corpus. Animae enim humanae unum sunt in specie et definitione. Si autem posuerimus, quod habuerunt esse per se et non inceperunt esse cum corporibus, impossibile est, ut animae in ipso esse habeant multitudinem. Hence there would be only a single soul in all human bodies, which, as Avicenna rightly says, needs no refutation. Ibid. Anima una numero erit in duobus corporibus. Hoc per se patet falsum esse. Ibid. Nos scimus etiam quod haec anima non est una in omnibus corporibus.) But he does not let this keep him from assuming that the souls will continue to exist in their plurality after the death of the body, that is, after the dissolution of the union that once obtained. He seeks to show in the following way that the two assertions do not contradict each other: Although the souls are without doubt bodiless after death, he says, the consequences of their earlier union with the body are nevertheless not canceled. If at that time they were of different being and essence because of the difference of their matters, of their times of creation, and of their affections corresponding to the different bodies, then they are so now as well. Add to this that the various theoretical and moral activities they exercised during life perhaps left lasting traces in them, and further the possibility of individualizing qualities unknown to us. (Ibid.)

24) Lib. Natur. VI. p. 5. c. 4.

25) Ibid. c. 5. princ. Dicemus, quod anima humana prius est intelligens in potentia, deinde fit intelligens in effectu. Omne autem, quod exit de potentia ad

[p. 10] 4) For all intelligible forms preexist immaterially in the pure spirits, the intelligences, of which the highest<sup>26)</sup> moves the outermost sphere, and the others the remaining celestial spheres. From the highest intelligence they flow into the second, from the second into the third, and so on down to the last, which is the so-called active intelligence (intelligentia agens)<sup>27)</sup>.

5) From this active intelligence, then, the intelligible forms finally flow into our soul, just as, on the other hand, the sublunary substantial forms also flow into corporeal matter; for it is the active intelligence alone that gives natural things their [p. 11] forms, the activity of the lower causes everywhere remaining merely preparatory and confining itself to making matter fit for the reception of the form. Now the case is quite analogous with the reception of the intelligible forms in the material intellect. Here too it is the active intelligence alone that communicates the forms, and all phantasms can do nothing more than prepare the material intellect for the reception of the emanation<sup>28)</sup>.

effectum, non exit nisi per causam, quae habet illud in effectu et extrahit ad illum. Ergo est haec causa, per quam animae nostrae in rebus intelligibilibus exeunt de potentia ad effectum. Sed causa dandi formam intelligibilem non est nisi intelligentia in effectu, penes quam sunt principia formarum intelligibilium abstractarum. Cujus comparatio ad animas nostras est sicut comparatio solis ad visus nostros, quia, sicut sol videtur per se in effectu, et videtur luce ipsius in effectu, quod non videbatur in effectu, sic est dispositio hujus intelligentiae quantum ad nostras animas. — Ibid. p. 1. c. 5. he distinguished a potentia in a threefold sense: potentia prima vocatur absoluta *materialis*, secunda potentia *potentialis*, tertia est *perfectio*; and correspondingly, then, a threefold intellectus: 1) intellectus *materialis*, 2) int. *in habitu* (et potest hic vocari intellectus in effectu comparatione primi . . ., quamvis etiam possit vocari intellectus in potentia comparatione ejus, qui sequitur post ipsum (cf. Aristot. De Anim. III, 4. §. 6. p. 429, b, 5.), 3) int. *accommodatus* ab alio, qui vocatur int. accommodatus per hoc, quod declarabitur nobis, quia int. in potentia non erit ad effectum nisi per intellectum, qui semper est in actu (this is the active intelligence, which always actually cognizes because it is pure spirit; see p. 8. n. 20.), et quia, cum conjunctus fuerit intellectus, qui est in potentia, cum illo intellectu, qui est in actu, aliquo modo conjunctionis, imprimetur in eo secundum aliquem modum formandi ille, qui est accommodatus ab extrinsecis (this is the νοῦς ἐνεργείᾳ, actual thinking).

26) The highest, i.e. the highest of the created intelligences, not the Deity, of which Avicenna holds that, because of the perfect unity and simplicity of its essence, it is not possible for it to be the immediate cause of more than one single thing. This one thing, he says, is the first intelligence, from which, since, receiving its being from another, it is mixed of potentiality and actuality and thus not without all plurality, a plurality of effects can for that very reason also proceed. For insofar as it knows itself as participating in potentiality, it produces the substance of the sphere moved by it; insofar, further, as it knows itself as having come to partake of actuality, it produces the soul of this sphere; finally, insofar as it knows its principle, it produces a second intelligence, which moves the next, lower sphere; and so it goes on down to the sphere of the moon. (Metaph. tract. IX. c. 4.) Alfarabi had already taught something similar, Font. Quaest. c. 8.

27) Avicenna calls it the giver of forms, a designation sufficiently explained by the efficacy which, as we shall hear presently, he ascribed to it. Cf. also *Shahrastani* p. 383 and 426. transl. by Haarbrücker II, p. 265 and 328.

6) This preparation, however, is certainly an essential precondition of its cognition<sup>29)</sup>. Only by looking upon the individual presentations that are in the power of imagination is the material intellect illuminated by the light of the active intelligence to the knowledge of the universal; it is the activities of the imagination and of the sensory thinking power (virtus cogitativa) that first put it in a position to join itself to the active intelligence and to receive the intelligible forms that stream forth from it<sup>30)</sup>.

7) But how does the material intellect behave after this reception? Will it perhaps hold fast the ideas once grasped and preserve them in itself for all the future? — By no means! rather, the ideas remain in it only so long as it actually cognizes them; and in this there is at first nothing that would distinguish it from other cognitive faculties; for the sensible forms too, if they continue to exist in us after the actual perception, are preserved not in the apprehensive powers themselves but in other faculties, which are to be called the treasuries of the apprehensive powers [p. 12] Thus the imagination is the treasury for the common sense or phantasy<sup>31)</sup> (treasury of forms), and memory the treasury for the estimative and for the sensory thinking power (treasury of intentions). To these, then, the apprehensive powers must turn whenever a presentation received earlier is to be renewed in them<sup>32)</sup>.

28) Lib. Natur. VI. p. 5. c. 5. Sic anima rationalis, cum conjungitur formis aliquo modo conjunctionis, aptatur, ut contingant in ea ex luce intelligentiae agentis ipsae formae nudae ab omni permixtione.

29) That is, so long as the human soul is united with its body. Once it has become free of the body, it no longer needs the preparatory sensory powers either. Lib. Natur. VI. p. 5. c. 6. fin. Cum autem anima liberabitur a corpore et ab accidentibus corporis, tunc poterit conjungi intelligentiae agenti et tunc inveniet in ea pulchritudinem intelligibilem et delectabilem perennem.

30) Ibid. c. 5. Cum enim virtus rationalis considerat singula, quae sunt in imaginatione, et illuminatur luce intelligentiae agentis in nos, quam praediximus, fiunt nuda a materia et ab ejus appenditiis et imprimuntur in anima rationali; non quasi ipsa de imaginatione mutentur ad intellectum nostrum, neque quia intentio pendens ex multis, cum ipsa in se sit considerata nuda, per se faciat similem sibi, sed quia ex consideratione coaptatur anima, ut emanet in eam ab intelligentia agente abstractio. Cogitationes enim et considerationes motus sunt aptantes animam ad recipiendum emanationem, sicut termini medii praeparant ad recipiendum conclusionem necessario, quamvis illud fiat uno modo et hoc alio, sicut postea scies. Cum autem accidit animae rationali comparari ad hanc formam nudam mediante luce intelligentiae agentis, contingit in anima ex forma quiddam, quod secundum aliquid est sui generis et secundum aliud non est sui generis, sicut cum lux cadit super colorata, et fit in visu ex illa luce operatio, quae non est similis ei ex omni parte etc.

8) But for the ideas of the material intellect there cannot also be a treasury of this kind; for if this were the case, then, since every spiritual subject actually cognizes all the forms that it has in itself, so that with it having-forms-in-itself and cognizing-forms are synonymous expressions, the treasury would have to be the faculty of a bodily organ. But this is impossible, because no form that exists in a power which makes use of a bodily organ in its operation is intelligible otherwise than potentially.

9) Hence nothing remains for us<sup>33)</sup> but to assume that whenever [p. 13] we cognize something spiritually, the intelligible form flows anew from the active intelligence into our material intellect, so that [p. 14] learning is nothing other than the acquiring of a perfect aptitude for uniting oneself with the active intelligence in order to receive the intelligible form.

31) According to Avicenna the two seem to be identical. In Lib. Natur. VI. p. 1. c. 5. he enumerates the powers of the soul in the following way: a) sensory: 1. sensus communis seu phantasia, 2. imaginatio, 3. vis existimationis (which elsewhere he also calls aestimativa, but in man cogitativa), 4. memorialis et reminiscibilis; b) spiritual: 1. virtus agendi, quae est principium movens corpus hominis ad actiones, 2. virtus sciendi.

32) Ibid. p. 4. c. 1. Quod recipit, non est id, quod retinet. Thesaurus *ejus, quod apprehendit sensus*, est virtus *imaginativa*, thesaurus vero apprehendentis *intentionem* est virtus *custoditiva*. Ibid. p. 5. c. 6. princ. Imaginata, et quaecunque adhaerent eis, cum anima avertitur ab eis, sunt reposita in virtutibus conservativis eorum, quae vere non sunt apprehendentes (si enim hoc esset, essent apprehendentes et conservantes simul), sed sunt thesaurus, ad quem cum convertit se virtus apprehendens judicans seu aestimatio aut anima aut intellectus, inveniet ea jam haberi. . . . Animalis autem animae discretae sunt virtutes, et unicuique virtuti per se separatim attributa sunt instrumenta, et *formis* assignatus est thesaurus, quas aliquando non contemplatur aestimatio, et assignatus est *intentionibus* thesaurus, quas aliquando non considerat aestimatio. Non enim aestimatio est locus in quo stabiliantur ista, sed est judicans. Et propter hoc dicimus, quod existimatio aliquando respicit formas et intentiones repositas in his *duobus* virtutibus et aliquando avertitur ab eis.

33) Avicenna conducts his proof taking into account the possibility of a fourfold assumption. According to the first, the treasury of the intelligible forms would be in a bodily organ, according to the second in the spiritual part of the soul; according to the third there would be no such treasury at all, but the ideas, existing independently outside the mind, would be mirrored, as it were, anew in the soul whenever it turned to them; according to the fourth, finally, it would be not ideas existing as things by themselves but the active intelligence with which the soul united itself ever anew, so that the latter might pour the intelligible forms into it. The first three assumptions are found by him to be objectionable, and so the truth of the last is inferred indirectly. Lib. Natur. VI. p. 5. c. 6. Dicemus nunc de humanis animabus, quae

in ipsa intelligentia (sc. agente) intelligibilia apprehendunt et deinde convertuntur ab illis ad alia, ita ut non sint in illis perfecte in effectu, et ideo ipsae non intelligant ea perfecte in effectu, an habeant thesaurum, in quo reponant? — Sed hic thesaurus aut est *cum essentia earum* (2nd assumption), aut *corpus* earum (1st assumption), aut *aliquid corporale* earum. — Jam autem diximus (against the 1st assumption), quod corpus earum, et quod pendet ex corpore earum, non est dignum ad hoc neque est dignum, ut sit subjectum intelligibilium; quia non est dignum ut formae intellectae sint situm habentes, sed conjunctio earum cum corpore faciet eas habere situm; si autem essent in corpore habentes situm, non essent intelligibiles. — Aut dicimus (3rd assumption), quod ipsae *formae intelligibiles* sunt res *per se existentes*, quarum unaquaeque est species et res per se existens, sed intellectus aliquando aspicit illas et aliquando avertitur ab illis et postea convertitur ad illas, et est anima quasi speculum, ipsae vero quasi res extrinsecae, quae aliquando apparent in ea et aliquando non apparent, et hoc fit secundum comparationes, quae sunt inter eas et animam. — Aut (4th assumption) *ex principio agente* emanat in animam forma post formam secundum petitionem animae, a quo principio postea, cum avertitur, cessat emanatio. Quod si ita esset, esset necesse omnibus horis addiscere sicut primitus (this last is an objection against the 4th assumption, which is resolved later). — Dicemus ergo, ultimam partem hujus divisionis esse veram. Impossibile est enim dici, hanc formam esse in anima in effectu perfecte et non intelligi ab ea in effectu perfecte. Sensus enim de hoc, quod (i.e., the meaning of the statement that etc.) eam intelligit, non est, nisi quia forma existit in ea (likewise it says further below: formam enim intellectam esse in anima hoc idem est, quod apprehendi eam), unde impossibile est, esse thesaurum ejus, et impossibile est etiam essentiam animae esse ejus thesaurum; hoc enim, quod est thesaurus ejus, nihil aliud est, nisi quia forma intellecta existit in ea. etc. (This is the refutation of the 2nd assumption) . . . Item postea declarabitur in sapientia prima (in the Metaphysics), quod haec forma non est per se existens (refutation of the 3rd assumption). Restat ergo, ut ultima pars sit vera, et ut *discere* non sit nisi *acquirere perfectam aptitudinem conjungendi se intelligentiae agenti*, quousque fiat ex ea intellectus, qui est simplex, a quo emanent formae ordinatae mediante anima in cogitatione (the truths derived from those simple cognitions). — Aptitudo autem, quae praecedit *discere*, est imperfecta, postquam autem discitur, est integra. . . . Ergo primum discere est sicut oculi curatio, qui, factus sanus, cum vult, aspicit aliquid unum et sumit aliquam formam, cum vero avertitur ab illo, fit illud sibi in potentia proxima effectui. . . . Cum enim dicitur: Plato est sciens intelligibilia, hic sensus est, ut, cum voluerit, revocet formam ad mentem suam; cujus etiam sensus est, ut, *cum voluerit possit conjungi intelligentiae agenti*, ita ut ab ea in ipso formetur ipsum intellectum; non quod intellectum sit praesens suae menti et formatum in suo intellectu in effectu semper, neque (as the objection made above would have had it) sicut erat prius quam disceret, hic enim modus intelligendi in potentia est virtus, quae acquirit animae, quod, intelligere cum voluerit, conjungetur intelligentiae, a qua emanat in eam forma intellecta, quae forma est intellectus adeptus verissime (what he here calls int. adeptus he called int. accommodatus above (see p. 9. n. 25.); one would be mistaken in identifying it with what he there called int. in habitu, as one might at first be inclined to do, because the expression int. adeptus appears to be modeled on the νοῦς ἐπικτητός, and the int. in

So much for Avicenna. Anyone who has made himself even somewhat familiar with the doctrines of Aristotle sees clearly what a strange transformation they have undergone here; the sensory ceases to be the source of spiritual cognition; in a manner that evidently approaches Plato, the sensory presentation is to count merely as an occasion of our spiritual knowledge.

8. Very different from the doctrine of Avicenna is that of Averroes, who, an excessive enthusiast for Aristotle<sup>34)</sup>, endeavors to develop his pure doctrine. We shall see with what success.

*Averroes* conceives the two principles which Aristotle distinguishes in the fifth chapter of the third book On the Soul, the intellect that becomes all things and the intellect that produces all things, as two purely spiritual substances separate in essence from the sensory human being, and teaches the following about their nature and about the manner of their union with him:

1) The child, when it is born, lacks not only all actual spiritual cognition; it also has as yet no spiritual cognitive faculty, and in general there is nothing in it that is not corporeal and corruptible. Only insofar as the child is by its nature so constituted that a spiritual cognitive power can later unite with it, and insofar as it has within it sensory images (phantasms) which are potentially intelligible, can one say that it is potentially spiritually cognizant<sup>35)</sup>.

habitu on the νοῦς καθ᾽ ἕξιν, which names mean one and the same thing in Alexander of Aphrodisias), et haec virtus est intellectus in effectu, secundum quod est perfectio (see ibid.).

34) Numerous passages bear witness to this boundless veneration of his for Aristotle. Thus he says De Anim. III, 2. t. c. 14. Omnes enim hoc opinantes non credunt, nisi propter hoc, quod dixit Aristoteles, quoniam ita est difficile hoc: adeo, quod si sermo Aristotelis non inveniretur in eo, tunc valde esset difficile cadere super ipsum, aut forte impossibile, nisi inveniretur aliquis talis ut Aristoteles. Credo enim, quod iste homo fuerit regula in natura et exemplar, quod natura invenit ad demonstrandum ultimam perfectionem humanam in materiis. He sees all the salvation of science in the imitation of Aristotle, and therefore reproaches Avicenna, a far clearer head than he himself is, for his freer movement, ibid. c. 5. t. c. 30.: Avicenna non imitatus est Aristotelem nisi in Dialectica, sed in aliis erravit et maxime in Metaphysica, et hoc quia incoepit quasi a se.

35) De Anim. III, 1. t. c. 5. Venet. 1550. f. 164, b. Et ideo cum dicimus puerum esse intelligentem potentia, potest utique dupliciter intelligi. Uno quidem modo, quod formae imaginatae, quae in eo existunt, sint intelligibiles potentia.

[p. 15] 2) Nonetheless it is already man, and differs in species from the irrational animals; for what gives man his specific difference is not a spiritual but a sensory power, which Aristotle<sup>36)</sup> calls the passible intellect (intellectus passibilis)<sup>37)</sup>, and which has its seat in the middle cell of the head<sup>38)</sup>. Through this faculty we distinguish the individual presentations and compare them with one another, which none of the animals can do, since in its place they have only a certain judgment through natural instinct (virtus aestimativa naturalis), by virtue of which, for example, the lamb regards the wolf as its enemy. We have already heard both of these named by Avicenna as virtus cogitativa and virtus aestimativa or existimativa<sup>39)</sup>.

3) According to the disposition of the passive intellect, men differ with respect to their aptitudes for spiritual knowledge<sup>40)</sup>, and through its activity, in connection with the activities of imagination and memory, they acquire habitual knowledge, whose subject is not something spiritual, but precisely the intellectus passibilis<sup>41)</sup>.

Alio vero modo potest intelligi, videlicet quod intellectus materialis, qui est aptus recipere intelligibile ipsum ipsius formae imaginatae, est utique recipiens potentia et copulatus nobis potentia.

36) De Anim. III, 5. §. 2. p. 430, a, 24. νοῦς παθητικός.

37) De Anim. III, 4. t. c. 20. f. 171, b. Et per istum intellectum [quem vocat Aristoteles passibilem] differt homo ab aliis animalibus.

38) De Anim. III, 5. t. c. 33. f. 174, b.

39) See above p. 12. n. 31. Averroes does indeed say once or twice that the intellectus passibilis is the imagination, as for example De Anim. III, 1. t. c. 20. f. 171, a., where he distinguishes a fourfold meaning in which Aristotle uses the word intellectus in this book: Aristoteles intendebat hic per intellectum passibilem virtutem imaginativam humanam . . .; hoc nomen igitur *intellectus* secundum hoc dicitur in hoc libro quatuor modis. Dicitur enim de intellectu materiali et de intellectu, qui est in habitu, et de intellectu agente et virtute imaginativa. But this is loosely spoken, and he seems here to use the name of imagination in the vaguer sense of a sensory faculty. In other places he says that the intell. passibilis is one with the virtus cogitativa (thus for example ibid. t. c. 5. f. 165, b. Intelligit Aristoteles per intell. passibilem ipsam virtutem cogitativam), which he distinguishes as a higher faculty from the imagination, ibid. t. c. 6. f. 167, a.: Virtus cogitativa est de genere virtutum existentium in corpore. Et hoc aperte dixit Aristoteles in illo libro [de Sens. et Sens.], cum posuit virtutes individuales distinctas in quatuor ordinibus. In primo posuit sensum communem, deinde virtutem imaginativam, deinde cogitativam et postea rememorativam. Et posuit rememorativam magis spiritualem, deinde cogitativam, deinde imaginativam et postea sensibilem. Licet igitur homo proprie habeat virtutem cogitativam, tamen hoc non facit hanc virtutem esse rationabilem distinctivam; illa enim distinguit intentiones universales non individuales.

40) Ibid. 4. t. c. 20. f. 171, b.

41) Ibid. Et debes scire, quod usus et exercitium sunt causae ejus, quod

[p. 16] 4) It is otherwise with actual spiritual knowledge. This can be found only in a spiritual faculty, and man attains to it through union with two spiritual substances that are separate in essence both from the body of man and from each other: the material intellect (intellectus materialis)<sup>42)</sup>, so called because by its nature it is merely in potentiality to the intelligible forms<sup>43)</sup>, and the active intellect (intellectus agens), which is called active because it makes the sensory images in man, which are potentially intelligible (the phantasms), actually intelligible, and so moves the material intellect<sup>44)</sup>. [p. 17] The material intellect receives the concepts that have become intelligible, which are in the phantasms; the active intellect does not receive them, indeed it has no knowledge of them at all<sup>45)</sup>, but it makes them knowable for the material intellect; not, however, as though it too were not a knowing being; only, the objects of its thinking all belong to another, higher domain: it knows the world of spirits.

apparet de potentia intellectus agentis, qui est in nobis ad abstrahendum, et intellectus materialis ad recipiendum. Sunt, dico, causae propter *habitum existentem* per usum et exercitium *in intellectu passibili* et corruptibili, quem vocavit Aristoteles passibilem et dicit aperte ipsum corrumpi.

42) That the intellectus materialis too is spiritual, Averroes states most clearly, for example De Anim. I, 1. t. c. 12. Et haec est sententia ejus (Aristotelis) in intellectu materiali, scilicet quod est abstractus a corpore. Ibid. III, 1. t. c. 4. f. 160, a. Aristoteles declaravit haec duo de intellectu [materiali], scilicet ipsum esse in genere virtutum passivarum et ipsum esse non transmutabile, quia neque est corpus, neque virtus in corpore; nam haec duo sunt principium quae dicuntur de intellectu. Ibid. 4. t. c. 20. f. 171, b. Nullus potest ratiocinari per hoc (namely, that Aristotle speaks of a corruptible intellect), quod intellectus materialis admiscetur corpori etc. Cf. also p. 15. n. 39., where Averroes distinguishes between the intellectus passibilis and the int. materialis, and the following notes.

43) De Anim. III, 1. t. c. 5. f. 160, b. Definitio ipsius intellectus materialis haec utique est, nempe quod est id, quod est in potentia ad omnes conceptus formarum materialium universalium et non est actu aliquid entium, antequam intelligat ipsas. By this, however, Averroes does not mean to say that it does not also know what is spiritual; for ibid. f. 166, a. he says: Praeterea intellectus recipiens necesse est ut intelligat illum intellectum, qui actu existit. Nam si intelligit formas materiales, longe magis debet intelligere formas immateriales, et id quod intelligit de ipsis formis separatis, hoc est de ipso intellectu agente, non impedit ipsum intelligere formas materiales. Likewise t. c. 20. f. 171, a.: Intellectus materialis perficitur per agentem et intelligit ipsum. And 5. t. c. 36. f. 179, b. Intellectus materialis intelligit utrumque, videlicet formas materiales et formas abstractas.

44) De Anim. III. 1. t. c. 5. f. 165, a. Consentaneum est credere reperiri in anima duas partes intellectus, quarum una est, quae *recipit*, quae quid sit, hic fuit probatum, alia vero, quae *agit*, et est illa, quae facit, ut illae intentiones et conceptus existentes in virtute imaginativa moveant intellectum materialem actu, postquam erant moventes ipsum in potentia . . ., et has duas partes esse ingenitas et incorruptibiles, et quod ratio ipsius agentis ad recipiens est veluti ratio ipsius formae ad ipsam materiam. (From some of the expressions used here one might think that Averroes counted the int. materialis and agens as belonging to the essence of man, which, however, is not the case; this is explained by the fact that Averroes retains Aristotle’s mode of expression even where he connects quite different ideas with it.) Ibid. 5. t. c. 36. f. 178, b. Dicamus ergo, quod, cum intellectus, qui in nobis existit, duas habeat actiones (ea scilicet ratione, qua

5) Each of these two spiritual beings, to which we owe our knowledge, is a single substance, which is not multiplied with the number of knowing men<sup>46)</sup>; all who were, are and will be know what they know spiritually in the same cognitive faculty and through the activity of the same active power. These two alone are what is eternal in man, while everything that belongs particularly to the individual perishes in the death of the body, just as it came into being with the coming into being of the body<sup>47)</sup>.

6) The union with them, however, takes place in the following way: First the sensory thinking power (the passible intellect), in connection with imagination and memory, makes the phantasms, in which the intelligible forms are according to their real essence, fit to receive the influence of the active intellect, through which they become actually [p. 18] intelligible<sup>48)</sup>; we might therefore compare them to certain subordinate arts which prepare the instruments for the work of the higher art, as, for example, the art of grinding for the art of sculpture, and the art of drill for the art of the general.

est relatus ad nos), quarum una est de genere *passionis* et illa quidem est ipsum intelligere, altera vero est de genere *actionis*, nempe quae est abstrahere formas easque denudare a materiis, quod nihil aliud est, quam facere eas intelligibiles actu, postquam erant intelligibiles in potentia, manifestum est, quod etc. etc.

45) De Anim. III, 1. t. c. 19. f. 170, a. intelligentia agens nihil intelligit ex eis, quae sunt hic.

46) This is admittedly, at least in the case of the int. materialis, with which we are supposed to receive our thoughts, striking in the highest degree and a quite ridiculous assertion; nevertheless Averroes teaches it in clear words, for example De Anim. III, 1. t. c. 5. f. 163, b. Contra id, quod dicit Aristoteles, non pauca insurgunt dubia, quorum . . . secundum, quod est caeteris difficilius, est, quod ultima perfectio (the intell. speculativus f. 161, b.) in homine numeraretur ad numerationem individuorum hominis, et prima perfectio (the intell. materialis ibid.) esset *una numero in omnibus*. Ibid. f. 164, a. Illud vero secundum dubium, quod dicebat, quo pacto possit *intellectus materialis* esse *unus numero in cunctis individuis hominum* et ingenitus atque incorruptibilis, et ipsa intelligibilia, quae in eo existant actu, quae quidem sunt ipse intell. speculativus numerentur ad numerationem individuorum hominum generenturque atque corrumpantur ad generationem et corruptionem ipsorum hominum, hoc inquam dubium est satis difficile et arduum. Ibid. f. 165, a. Ex hoc dicto nos possumus opinari *intellectum materialem* esse *unicum in cunctis individuis*. Destr. Destr. f. 349, b. Necesse est, ut sit anima non divisibilis ad divisionem individuorum.

47) De Anim. II, 2. t. c. 21. f. 130, b. Hoc [quod intellectus abstrahitur a corpore, quemadmodum sempiternum a corruptibili] erit, cum quandoque copulatur cum illo et quandoque non copulatur cum illo.

7) When they have done this, and the activity of the active intellect has made the phantasms intelligible<sup>49)</sup>, the material intellect, which stands to all intelligible forms in the relation of potentiality, receives from the phantasms the concepts of sensible things<sup>50)</sup>, and the intelligible forms that are known accordingly have a twofold subject: 1) the phantasms and 2) the material intellect<sup>51)</sup>, just as the sensible forms too, for example colors, have a twofold subject: 1) one outside the one perceiving and 2) the faculty of sight.

But if in this way our phantasm on the one hand and the material intellect on the other are united with the same intelligible form, then evidently a form of the material intellect is joined with us by means of the phantasm; and if a form of the material intellect, then, since every form forms a unity with its subject, the material intellect itself must also be united with us, and we now know through the material intellect as through an innate cognitive power<sup>52)</sup>.

48) De Anim. III, 1. t. c. 7. f. 167, b. Virtus cogitativa est de genere virtutum sensibilium. Imaginativa autem et cogitativa et rememorativa . . . . omnes juvant se ad repraesentandum imaginem rei sensibilis, ut aspiciat eam virtus rationalis abstracta et extrahat intentionem universalem et postea recipiat eam, i. e. comprehendat eam. If the animals had an intellectus passibilis, they too would be joined with the active and material intellect. De Anim. III, 4. t. c. 20. f. 171, b. Et per istum intellectum differt homo ab aliis animalibus et, si non, tunc necesse esset, ut continuatio intellectus agentis et recipientis cum animalibus esset eodem modo.

49) The activity of the intell. agens must precede that of the intell. materialis. De Anim. III, 5. t. c. 36. f. 178, b. Haec autem actio, quae est generare intelligibilia eaque facere, prior existit in nobis, quam actio intellectionis. See also the preceding note.

50) De Anim. III, 3. t. c. 18. f. 169, b. Necesse est cum hoc, quod posuimus, quod proportio intentionum imaginatarum ad intellectum materialem est sicut proportio sensibilium ad sensus, ut Aristoteles dicet, ponere alium motorem esse (i.e., the intell. agens), qui facit eas movere in actu intellectum materialem, et hoc nihil est aliud, quam facere eas intellectas in actu abstrahendo eas a materia.

51) De Anim. III, 1. t. c. 5. f. 163, b. Oportet dari duo subjecta ipsis intelligibilibus actu existentibus, quorum *unum* est illud subjectum, propter quod ipsa intelligibilia sunt vera, nempe formae, quae sunt imagines verae, *alterum* vero est illud subjectum, propter quod ipsa intelligibilia sunt unum ex entibus mundi, et illud quidem est ipse intellectus materialis. Cf. p. 20. n. 55.

52) De Anim. III, 1. t. c. 5. f. 164, b. Dicamus ergo nos, manifestum esse,

[p. 19] 8) We all, it was said, know through one and the same material intellect; does it not follow from this that we all know the same thing? — By no means! Let us only consider in what respect we know through the material intellect. We do so only insofar as it is brought into connection with us through the phantasms, which must be disposed in a certain way for this. Now, however, we do not all have the same phantasms, and even in those who have the same phantasms, they are not disposed in the same way; hence the one is united with the material intellect in one way, the other in another, and for that reason the one does not know what the other knows<sup>53)</sup>.

9) This difficulty, then, would be removed; but at once another inconvenience seems to result from our assertions. We said that the material intellect receives the intelligible forms from the phantasms in us; must there not, for that reason, be change in it, since one and the same intelligible form now becomes actual in it, now ceases to be actual? — By no means! For the material intellect receives the intelligible forms not merely from a single man, but from all men living on the whole face of the earth, and among these there were, and are, and will also in all future time always be, such as have the disposition of the phantasms required for each intelligible form. It is a necessity of nature that a philosopher be found in the human race<sup>54)</sup>. And so the intelligible forms are eternal and at the same time [p. 20] ever new. Their eternity they have from the one subject in which they are, namely from the material intellect; their newness from the other, namely from the phantasm. The sciences can neither come into being nor pass away, except per accidens, that is, insofar as they are connected with Socrates or Plato<sup>55)</sup>.

ipsum hominem non esse actu intelligentem nisi propterea, quod copulatur cum eo intellectus in actu. . . . Et cum jam probatum fuerit, quod intellectus non potest copulari cum omnibus individuis, ut numeretur ad eorum numerationem per eam partem, quae se habet ad eum ut forma, videlicet per intellectum materialem, relinquitur, ut copuletur ipse intellectus nobis omnibus hominibus per copulationem conceptuum seu intentionum intelligibilium nobiscum, quae quidem sunt ipsi conceptus imaginati seu intentiones imaginatae; hoc est per illam partem ipsarum, quae in nobis existit, quae quodam pactu se habet ut forma. (It is evident that the material intellect, which is joined to us in this way, cannot be called a form and entelechy of ours in the same sense as other cognitive faculties. Hence Averroes says ibid.: Ex dictis igitur jam constat, primam perfectionem (i.e., πρώτην ἐντελέχειαν; cf. De Anim. II, 1. §. 5. p. 412, a, 22.) ipsius intellectus differre a primis perfectionibus reliquarum virtutum, et quod hoc nomen perfectio dicitur de eis modo aequivoco.) Ibid. III, 5. t. c. 36. f. 179, b. Homo . . . intelligit omnia entia per intellectum adeptum, quando est copulatus cum formis imaginariis, propria intellectione.

53) Averroes therefore traces the difference between poietic and theoretical knowledge back to differences of preparation in the passible intellect. De Anim. III, 4. t. c. 20. f. 171, b. Intellectus quidem operativus differt a speculativo per diversitatem praeparationis existentis in hoc intellectu.

54) De anim. beatitud. f. 354, a. Ex necessitate est, ut sit aliquis philosophus in specie humana.

55) De Anim. III, 1. t. c. 5. f. 164, a. Et cum omnia ista sint, sicut narravimus, non contingit, ut ista intelligibilia, quae sunt in actu, videlicet ipsa speculativa, sint generabilia et corruptibilia nisi ratione ipsius subjecti, per quod sunt vera (of which Averroes had remarked shortly before that it is that quod aliquo pacto movet intellectum) non ratione subjecti, per quod sunt unum entium, scilicet intellectus materialis. Ibid. f. 165, a. Existimandum est in anima reperiri tres partes intellectus. Prima est ipse intell. *recipiens*, secunda vero ipse *agens*, tertia autem est intell. *adeptus seu factus* (actual thinking); et horum trium duo quidem sunt aeterni, nempe agens et recipiens, tertius vero est partim generabilis et corruptibilis, partim vero aeternus. Sed cum ex hoc dicto nos possumus opinari, intellectum materialem esse unicum in cunctis individuis, possumusque adhuc ex hoc existimare, humanam speciem esse aeternam, ut in aliis locis declaratum fuit: ideo oportebit, intellectum materialem non posse denudari a principiis universalibus natura notis universae humanae speciei (dico autem primas illas propositiones illosque conceptus proprios particulares, qui cunctis communicant rebus), quoniam hujusmodi intelligibilia sunt utique unum ratione recipientis plura vero ratione ipsius conceptus recepti. Ea igitur ratione, qua sunt unica in ipso, sunt utique aeterna, cum ipsum esse non sequeretur ab ipso subjecto recepto, hoc est ab ipso movente, quod quidem est ipsa intentio seu conceptus ipsarum formarum imaginatarum, nullumque reperitur ibi prohibens ratione ipsius recipientis. Idcirco nullam habebit generationem et corruptionem nisi ratione pluralitatis, quae eis accidit, et non ea ratione, qua sunt unum in ipso. Et idcirco si corrumpatur aliquod primorum intelligibilium seu primarum notionum propter corruptionem subjecti ipsius, quo conjungitur nobis et copulatur et est verum, tunc oportebit illud intelligibile non esse corruptibile simpliciter sed corruptibile in respectu unius individuorum . . . . . f. 165, b. At si hujusmodi intelligibilia considerentur, quatenus habent esse simpliciter et non in respectu alicujus individui, tunc vere dicentur habere aeternum esse, et non esse quandoque intelligibilia, quandoque non, sed eodem modo semper existere . . . . . Existimatur quod universum habitatum non potest esse expers alicujus habitus ipsius philosophiae, sicut opinandum est, quod universum habitatum non potest esse expers artium naturalium. Quoniam licet in aliqua parte defuerint ipsae artes, exempli gratia in quadra septentrionali ipsius terrae, non propterea reliquae quadrae privabuntur eis. Nam jam fuit probatum, quod in parte meridionali potest esse habitatio, quemadmodum in parte septentrionali. Ergo fortasse reperietur philosophia in majori parte subjecti omni tempore, quemadmodum homo ex homine et equus ex equo gignitur. Intellectus ergo speculativus est ingenitus et incorruptibilis hac ratione. Ibid. 3. t. c. 20. f. 171, a. Intellectus, qui dicitur materialis, non accidit ei, ut quandoque intelligat, quandoque non nisi in respectu formarum imaginationis existentium in unoquoque individuo, non in respectu speciei; exempli gratia non accidit ei, ut quandoque intelligat intellectum equi et quandoque non, nisi in respectu Socratis et Platonis, simpliciter autem et respectu speciei semper intelligit hoc universale, nisi humana species deficiat omnino, quod est impossibile. . . . Intellectus qui est in potentia, cum non fuerit acceptus in respectu

[p. 21] 10) We have seen in what way our union with the material intellect takes place; let us now consider in what way we are united with the active intellect, a union through which, when it becomes perfect, we attain to the knowledge of the pure spirits and thereby to the highest beatitude of man. Not in another life may we hope for this<sup>56)</sup>, since, as we have already said, our individual existence ceases with death; but in this life it can fall to our share, even if only in the evening of life.

11) For the union with the active intellect takes place gradually, in an ever more and more perfect way, and indeed in the measure in which our knowledge of the corporeal world, mediated by the phantasms, becomes more complete. This may be shown as follows: It is evident that we know the conclusions through the known principles; but since the active intellect too is the cause of all our knowing, it is clear that here one and the same thing must be regarded as the effect of two. But one and the same effect can be ascribed to two different things in only two cases: first, if the one is the instrument of the other, as one can, for example, ascribe healing to the physician and to the medicine<sup>57)</sup>; or second, if the one stands to the other as the form to its subject, as, for example, heat to fire, for which reason one can say both that the fire and that the heat of the fire is what makes hot. Hence the active intellect too must stand to those propositions which become for us the principles of new knowledge either as form to matter, or as the principal cause to its instrument, a relation which is quite similar to the first; for in this case too the active intellect appears in a certain way as the perfection, and those immediate cognitions as that which is perfected by it<sup>58)</sup>. Now where that which [p. 22] is perfected is received, there the perfection itself is always received at the same time as well, as, for example, in the pupil the actually visible color and the light that makes it visible are received at the same time<sup>59)</sup>. Hence in our case too the active intellect is received in the material intellect together with the known truths, and the more thoughts someone receives in the material intellect, the more he unites himself with the active intellect, [p. 23] until at last, when he has attained the whole of the knowledge concerning the corporeal world, he is completely united with the active intellect. In the totality of what is perfectible, the perfection has completely joined itself with him<sup>60)</sup>.

alicujus individui, sed fuerit acceptus simpliciter et in respectu cujuslibet individui, tunc non invenitur aliquando intelligens et aliquando non, sed semper invenitur intelligens. Destr. Destr. f. 349, b. Scientiae sunt aeternae et non generabiles nec corruptibiles nisi per accidens, scilicet ex copulatione earum Socrati et Platoni . . ., quoniam intellectui nihil est individuitatis. (Renan wrongly concludes from this passage that Averroes held the intell. materialis to be a universal, as our discussions so far and the earlier citations will have sufficiently shown. What Averroes denies is nothing other than that the intellect belongs to that which constitutes the individuality of this or that man. See above p. 17. n. 46.)

56) As Avicenna had believed. See above p. 11. n. 29.

57) Arist. De generat. et corr. I, 7. p. 324, a, 29.

58) De Anim. III, 5. t. c. 36. f. 179, a. Intellectus, qui in nobis existit, duas obtinet actiones, quae scilicet sunt cognoscere intelligibilia et facere ea (see above p. 16.

n. 44.). Fiunt autem in nobis ipsa intelligibilia bifariam, nempe aut *naturaliter*, et sunt illae primae propositiones, quae quidem sunt nobis ignotae, quando scilicet et unde vel qua ratione nobis evenerint, aut *voluntarie*, et sunt illa intelligibilia, quae ex illis primis propositionibus seu principiis acquiruntur. Jam autem fuit probatum, ipsa intelligibilia, quae natura adipiscimur, esse necessarium, ut proveniant a re, quae est de se ipsa intellectus denudatus et abstractus a materia, qui quidem est ipse intellectus agens. Cum ergo hoc sit probatum, necesse est, ut intelligibilia, quae sunt in nobis adepta ex primis propositionibus seu primis principiis, sint quid factum ex congregato ex *propositionibus notis* et *intellectu agente*. Non enim possumus dicere, ipsas primas propositiones nihil facere ad inventionem intelligibilium acquisitorum et adeptorum, neque etiam possumus dicere illas propositiones solas efficere illa intelligibilia, jam enim probatum est, ipsum agens esse unum et aeternum. . . . Oportet itaque intellectum speculativum esse quid genitum ex intellectu agente et primis propositionibus, oportetque hoc genus intelligibilium esse voluntarium, intelligibilibus primis naturalibus contrario modo se habentibus. Quaelibet autem actio, quae ex aggregato duarum rerum diversarum resultat, oportet utique, ut una illarum duarum rerum se habeat veluti *materia et instrumentum*, altera vero veluti *forma aut agens*. Intellectus ergo, qui in nobis est, componitur utique ex intellectu adepto et ex intellectu agente, aut ita, quod propositiones sint veluti materia, et intell. agens sit veluti forma, aut ita, quod propositiones sint veluti instrumentum, et intellectus agens sit veluti efficiens; dispositio enim in hoc est satis similis (cf. Arist. De Anim. II, 1. §. 13. p. 413, a, 8., where he also calls the moving principle ἐντελέχεια). Averroes now raises a difficulty against the doctrine just developed and, in removing it, arrives at the more precise determination that between the active intellect and the immediately known truths there exists not a relation as between form and matter, or principal cause and instrument in the proper sense, but only an analogue of it: Dico ergo, quod, cum dicitur, si conclusiones a nobis acquirantur per intellectum agentem et per propositiones, tunc oportere, ut ipsae propositiones se habeant ad intellectum agentem veluti vera materia et verum instrumentum, hujusmodi dictum non est necessarium, sed hoc tantum est necessarium, nempe ut detur *aliqua proportio et ratio* qua intellectus adeptus similetur materiae et intellectus agens similetur formae. Further below (f. 179, b.) he explains this as follows: Nam quaecunque duae res, quarum subjectum est unum et una earum est perfectior altera, oportet, ut ita se habeat perfectior ad imperfectiorem sicut se habet forma ad materiam. But the active intellect and the speculative principles, he says, have, insofar as the material intellect knows both, one and the same subject, namely precisely the intellectus materialis.

59) De Anim. III, 5. t. c. 36. f. 179, b. In hoc enim ita se habet res sicut in ipso transparente (such as air, water, glass, the pupil, and the like), quod quidem recipit colores et lucem simul; lux autem est efficiens colores.

12) Through this there now opens up to him also the knowledge of the whole realm of spirits; for the active intellect possesses by nature the knowledge of all spiritual substances, and whoever has perfectly received the active intellect in the material intellect now knows through the active intellect what the latter knows<sup>61)</sup>; and in this sublime contemplation he finds the highest happiness and consummate beatitude; he has arrived at the last and uttermost goal that was attainable for a man<sup>62)</sup>.

Such is the doctrine of the Arab, of which the sober-minded philosopher of Stagira certainly never dreamed, but which, in spite of its wondrous mysticism and its sophistical turns, not only found great approval among the Arabs, but also [p. 24] managed to win for itself so many adherents in the *Christian* West that the great Scholastics, such as, in particular, *Thomas Aquinas*, believed they had to inveigh against it with all their might. In view of such far-reaching misinterpretations, which no longer let the shape of the Aristotelian doctrine be recognized at all, the Angelic Doctor cries out, full of indignation, that Averroes is to be called not so much a Peripatetic as a corrupter of the Peripatetic philosophy. “Non tam peripateticus quam peripateticae philosophiae depravator<sup>63)</sup>!”

60) De Anim. III, 5. t. c. 36. f. 179, b. Jam ergo invenimus modum, quo possit iste intellectus (agens) copulari nobis in fine. Causa vero, ob quam non copulatur nobis in initio est quidem propterea, quoniam . . . . . oportebit, ut intell. agens copuletur nobis per copulationem intelligibilium speculativorum. Palam autem est, quod quotiescunque omnia intelligibilia speculativa exstant in nobis in potentia, quod ipse quoque est conjunctus nobis potentia, et quotiescunque omnia intelligibilia inerunt nobis actu, ipse quoque copulabitur tunc nobis actu, et quod si aliqua eorum fuerint potentia, aliqua vero actu, copulabitur ipse quoque tunc nobis secundum unam partem ejus, secundum vero aliam partem non, et tunc nos dicemur moveri ad copulationem. Manifestum autem est, quod cum hujusmodi motus fuerit completus, quod statim copulabitur nobis intellectus iste omni ex parte. In the fragment of the Epistola Averroys de intellectu which Renan (Averr. et l’Averr. p. 348.) has published, the process is presented thus: Et iste intellectus, qui est in actu, est, quem homo in se, licet in fine, apprehendit, et iste est intellectus, qui vocatur acquisitus, et est complementum et actus, et quod yles primum potens fuit ad illum. Et propter hoc hora, qua renovata fuit forma, renovata fuit in eo potentia separatarum formarum, quousque descendit vel ascendit de complemento ad complementum et de forma ad formam nobiliorem et propinquiorem ad actum, adeo quod in fine perveniat ad hoc complementum et ad hunc actum in quo nullatenus misceatur potentia aliqua. Just as the fragment in general contains many obscurities, which are for the most part to be ascribed to the poor translation and the corruption of the text, so this passage too is, to me at least, not entirely intelligible.

61) De Anim. III, 5. t. c. 36. f. 180, a. Et hinc quoque patebit cur non copulemur cum hoc intellectu ab initio sed in fine, propterea quia, dum est forma existens in nobis potentia, est conjunctus nobis potentia, et dum est conjunctus nobis potentia, non poterimus intelligere quicquam per ipsum, nisi efficiatur forma in actu, quod quidem fit cum actu conjungitur; et tunc intelligemus per ipsum omnia illa, quae intelligimus (intelligit?) et agemus per illum actionem sibi propriam.

62) De anim. beatitud. c. 4. and 5.

9. But what interpretation has he himself, the greatest thinker of the Middle Ages, who with his congenial mind often divined rather than read the most difficult doctrines of Aristotle out of the much corrupted text, given to the words of the Philosopher? — He gives an explanation which, in a noteworthy way, coincides in all the points stated above with that fragment of Theophrastus which has been preserved for us in the paraphrase of Themistius.

For to him too, on the one hand, not only the intellectus agens but also the intellectus possibilis (for so he calls, departing from the mode of expression of the Arabs, the intellect that is potentially all things)<sup>64)</sup> is something immaterial; and to him too, on the other hand, not only the intellectus possibilis but also the intellectus agens is something belonging to the essence of man and not a purely spiritual substance alien to it; both are faculties of the human soul. When Aristotle says that they are separate from the body<sup>65)</sup>, he means to signify by this nothing other than that they have no organ, as the potencies of the vegetative and sensitive part do, but are found in the soul alone as their subject. For the human soul, standing on the boundary of the corporeal and the spiritual world, surpasses on account of the sublimity of its nature the capacity of matter and cannot be wholly enclosed in it; and so it possesses powers that are not potencies of the ensouled body but belong exclusively to it; there remain to it activities in which corporeal matter has no share. In this way, then, the intellectus possibilis and the intellectus agens are incorporeal in their acting and their being, unmixed with matter<sup>66)</sup>.

63) Opuscul. XV. De unitate intellectus contra Averroistas.

64) At the beginning of the opusculum just named he says: [Averroes] asserere nititur intellectum, quem *Aristoteles possibilem vocat*, ipse autem inconvenienti nomine materialem etc. This doubtless refers to De Anima III, 4. §. 3. p. 429, a, 21. ὥστε μηδ᾽ αὐτοῦ εἶναι φύσιν μηδεμίαν ἀλλ᾽ ἢ ταύτην, ὅτι δυνατόν.

65) De Anim. III, 4. §. 5. p. 429, b, 5. ibid. 5. §. 1. p. 430, a, 17.

66) Comment. De Anim. III. lect. 7. Dicitur enim separatus intellectus, quia non habet organum sicut sensus. Et hoc contingit propter hoc, quia anima humana propter suam nobilitatem supergreditur facultatem materiae corporalis et non potest

[p. 25] The intellectus possibilis is the cognitive faculty proper of the spiritual part; all our ideas are found in it. But they are not actually in it from the beginning; rather, it is at first the mere potentiality of thoughts, and like an empty tablet on which nothing is written. It receives the intelligible forms through a kind of being affected, and for that reason Aristotle says<sup>67)</sup> that thinking is a being affected<sup>68)</sup>.

But every being affected presupposes, besides the passive principle in which it is, an active one. What, then, is the active principle that produces the intelligible forms in our mind? Aristotle says that the origin of our knowledge is from the senses<sup>69)</sup>, and in harmony with this is what he teaches in another place, that the soul knows nothing without phantasms<sup>70)</sup>. But nothing corporeal can produce an impression in something incorporeal, and therefore, according to Aristotle, the mere power of sensible bodies does not suffice for the production of our thoughts; rather, something higher is required: “the active surpasses the passive in dignity,” he says in the third book On the Soul<sup>71)</sup>.

This higher agens is another spiritual faculty of the soul, the so-called intellectus agens. It makes the phantasms received from the senses, which, because individual matter still adheres to them, are only potentially intelligible, actually intelligible through abstraction, and is therefore the proper and principal (efficient) cause of spiritual knowledge, while the phantasms are only the co-cause and, as it were, the matter of the cause<sup>72)</sup>.

The intellectus agens illuminates the phantasms and abstracts the intelligible species from the phantasms. It *illuminates* them, that is, the phantasms, which stand to the intellect as colors to the sense of sight, are made fit, through the influence of the intellectus agens, to let the concepts of the intellect be abstracted from them, just as the sensitive part is raised to a higher power through its union with the intellective. It *abstracts* the intelligible species from the phantasms, that is, through the power of the intellectus agens we can grasp in our consideration, without the individual determinations, the universal essence of things, whose likenesses are received as forms in the intellectus possibilis<sup>73)</sup>.

totaliter includi ab ea. Unde remanet ei aliqua actio, in qua materia corporalis non communicat. Et propter hoc potentia ejus ad hanc actionem non habet organum corporale et sic est intellectus separatus.

67) De Anim. III, 4. §. 2. p. 429, a, 13.

68) Summ. theol. 1a, 79, 2. corp.

69) E.g., Anal. Post. II, 19. p. 100, a, 10.

70) De Anim. III, 7. §. 3. p. 431, a, 16.

71) De Anim. III, 5. §. 2. p. 430, a, 18.

72) Summ. theol. 1a 84, 6. corp.

73) Ibid. 85, 1. ad 4um.

[p. 26] 10. This explanation, so entirely different from the conceptions of Alexander and the Arabs, recommends itself, as was said, by the fact that it is found to be in harmony with what we took from the statements of Theophrastus.

But against it too difficulties arise which seem hard to remove, and which in part *Durandus* had already urged<sup>74)</sup>, who then decides, for his part, to give up the intellectus agens altogether. The intellectus agens, so it was said in Thomas, acts upon the phantasms; otherwise the phantasms could not produce the thoughts in the intellect, since nothing corporeal is able to act upon something spiritual. If this were so, the intellectus agens would render the required help only if by its operation it produced something spiritual in the imagination, transformed the phantasms into something spiritual. But in a sensory faculty, bound to an organ, a spiritual accident cannot possibly be found; therefore the effect ascribed to the intellectus agens is something manifestly impossible.

But even supposing that the intellectus agens could transform the phantasms into something spiritual, they would in any case, after the transformation, no longer be what they had been before it; they would no longer be phantasms. But now Aristotle says that we can never think anything without at the same time having the corresponding phantasm in us<sup>75)</sup>; therefore, according to him, the phantasm has evidently not been transformed into something higher, intelligible, at the moment of knowing.

11. Thus we see ourselves entangled in perplexities here too, and from them is explained the transformation which the Thomistic doctrine underwent at the hands of *Suárez*<sup>76)</sup>, who, to be sure, himself believed that he was not departing from the views of St. Thomas<sup>77)</sup>.

Suárez, namely, maintains “that the abstracting activity is to be conceived not as an action of the spiritual cognitive power (the intellectus agens) upon the sensory presentation, but as an activity immanent in reason itself.
