Since it is the same soul that knows through sense and through reason, [p. 27] the presence of the sensory presentation suffices for reason to be stimulated to the exercise of its activity, and for this activity to be directed to the object of sensibility; but no sensory presentation can have any further influence on the origination of the intellectual one; for it must be held most strictly that nothing material acts so as to change something immaterial.” One must therefore not believe that “reason (the intellectus agens) purifies the sensory presentation, as it were stripping off what is material, in order to transfer it, thus transformed and spiritualized, from the imagination into itself (the intellectus possibilis). The abstracting activity, in general, produces no change at all in the sense presentation, and consists solely in this: that reason produces in itself the intelligible image of the object of which the imagination possesses the sensory one<sup>78)</sup>.”

74) Sent. I. dist. 3. q. 5.

75) De Anim. III, 8. §. 3. p. 432, a, 8.

76) De Anim. l. IV.

77) How very much this is nevertheless the case will escape no one who compares the following faithful account of the view of Suárez, which we take word for word from the Philos. d. Vorzeit of Jos. Kleutgen (Münster 1860), with the pronouncements of St. Thomas. See, for example, Summ. theol. 1a 79, 3. ad 2um and Cont. Gent. II, 77. cc. med. In the latter place it says: Est igitur in anima intellectiva virtus *activa in phantasmata*, faciens ea intelligibilia actu, et haec potentia animae vocatur intellectus agens.

12. But a mere stimulation by the sensory presentation might perhaps have sufficed for Plato; it cannot, however, suffice for Aristotle. For whereas Plato believed that all higher knowledge, as acquired in an earlier life, is present in us from birth, so that according to him the soul needed only an occasion for recollection, with Aristotle it is a question of an original acquisition of thoughts. According to his doctrine the spiritual part is completely bare of ideas, and hence the intellectus agens too, if it belongs to the soul, has no thought in itself; how, then, is it to be able to communicate the concepts to the intellectus possibilis? It is indeed true that in the sensory presentations of the imagination the intelligible is in a certain way found, since the universal is concretely in the individual; but since the phantasms, as material, are supposed in no way to act upon the mind, there is now evidently a total lack of a sufficient active principle that would bring the potential thoughts to actuality.

Further, let us suppose that the stimulation by the sensory presentation suffices, and that the intellectus agens could produce the ideas in the mind at once out of the fullness of its sovereign power; then, since the phantasms change nothing in it, all ideas would evidently have to be contained in it virtually from the beginning. But then it would remain inexplicable, just as with Plato, whom Aristotle reproaches on this score<sup>79)</sup>, why the lack of a sensation is always bound up with the lack of a knowledge too, let alone that one could understand why, according to Aristotle, even after knowledge has been acquired, actual knowing should be possible only so long as one holds fast the corresponding individual presentations in the imagination<sup>80)</sup>. [p. 28] The Aristotelian view of the relation of sensory cognition to spiritual cognition is here evidently abandoned.

78) Philos. d. Vorzeit p. 125.

79) Metaph. A. 9. p. 993, a, 7.

80) See p. 25. n. 70. and De Anim. III, 8. §. 3. p. 432, a, 8.

Further: no activity without a striving toward activity, Aristotle teaches<sup>81)</sup>. Hence, if the intellectus agens is, by acting, to produce spiritual cognition, a striving after knowledge must be presupposed in man. Now this striving, from which the operation of the intellectus agens follows, is either to be thought of as an unconscious drive, like the one from which the vegetative activity of plants and the acting of lifeless nature proceed; but then sense cognition can evidently in no way come into consideration for it, since the sole precondition of such activation is, after all, the presence and right disposition of that which is capable of receiving the effect<sup>82)</sup>; but on this conception the intellectus possibilis is by nature disposed to receive the influence of the intellectus agens and is most intimately united with it, so the spiritual part would have to generate thoughts in itself even without any sensation and from the very beginning. Or else the striving from which the operation of the intellectus agens follows is a conscious one; then it must be a sensory or a spiritual appetite. But a sensory appetite it is not, for how should the sensory part desire truth? and how should an appetite of the sensory part guide the motion of the intellectus agens, especially on a theory that does not let the sensory act upon the spiritual at all? But neither can it be a spiritual appetite, for every spiritual willing presupposes a spiritual thinking, as Aristotle teaches us in the twelfth book of the Metaphysics<sup>83)</sup>; the intellective soul, however, is supposed only now to attain to thinking, and when it does attain to it, not even then is the first thing it cognizes the truth of thinking, toward which the appetite from which the activity of the intellectus agens is supposed to proceed would surely have to be directed, but rather the nature of external things<sup>84)</sup>. Thus such a view of the νοῦς ποιητικός is entirely incompatible with the remaining Aristotelian doctrines.

And so we must, then, take leave of the medieval commentators too, without having found anything that seemed completely satisfying, in order to turn to the more recent interpreters. The opinions of every single one, so as not to become all too prolix, [p. 29] we cannot go through here either<sup>85)</sup>; rather, in a manner similar to what we did with regard to the earlier times, we restrict ourselves to considering the conceptions of some of the most eminent German and French experts on the Aristotelian philosophy.

81) See below Section II. Part I, n. 15.

82) Metaph. Θ, 5. p. 1048, a, 5. — Phys. VIII, 1. p. 251, b, 5. Aristotle therefore says: εἰ τοίνυν μὴ ἀεὶ ἐκινεῖτο, δῆλον ὡς οὐχ οὕτως εἶχον ὡς δυνάμενα τὸ μὲν κινεῖσθαι τὸ δὲ κινεῖν, ἀλλ᾽ ἔδει μεταβάλλειν θάτερον αὐτῶν.

83) Metaph. Λ, 7. p. 1072, a, 29.

84) De Anim. III, 4. §. 6. p. 429, b, 5. cf. also Metaph. Λ, 9. p. 1074, b, 35.

#### C. Neuere Erklärungen. — C. More Recent Interpretations.

13. Before all else we encounter here *Trendelenburg*, who in his meritorious commentary on the three books On the Soul (Jena 1833) has treated the doctrine of the νοῦς ποιητικός in detail. We summarize his remarks in the following points:

1) According to the Aristotelian doctrine—and herein precisely lies its chief difficulty—the νοῦς is at one time most intimately bound up with the other powers of the soul, so that it seems unable to subsist without them (νοῦς παθητικός), at another time, when it is conceived as the highest νοῦς, as νοῦς ποιητικός, separated from the rest of man’s being as something higher and set over against it as its master<sup>86)</sup>.

2) What, now, are we to understand by the one and the other of them? — We believe that with the expression νοῦς παθητικός Aristotle designated all the lower powers, intertwined as it were into one knot, insofar as they are required for the thinking of things. He calls these powers Παθητικός νοῦς partly because they are brought to completion by the νοῦς ποιητικός, partly because they are affected by their objects<sup>87)</sup>. The acquisition of the universal concept from the comparison of the individual sensations is, if one looks to the help that the senses afford, the business of the νοῦς παθητικός<sup>88)</sup>.

3) Distinct from it and higher than it is the νοῦς ποιητικός, even though it is not, as some whom Themistius already refuted wished to maintain, to be taken for the divine intellect itself. It is something belonging to the soul of man<sup>89)</sup> and therefore also not a single one for all<sup>90)</sup>.

4) What it actually is, what the boundaries of its domain are, how it makes use of its power for the production of knowledge—on all this Aristotle nowhere gives us information<sup>91)</sup>. Only this much is certain, that it grasps the first and last principles of knowledge, and that in the end it is its testimony, trusting in which we assent to the truth<sup>92)</sup>. Without it we would lack every guarantee [p. 30] ; for there is no knowledge of the principles, since they are indemonstrable; but neither can the νοῦς παθητικός be guarantor, since it depends on the comparison of sensations, and so, by appealing to it, we would likewise fall into the fault of a circular argument; there remains, then, only the νοῦς ποιητικός, which grasps the first principles by its own power<sup>93)</sup>. To these suprasensible principles refer the words of the eleventh book of the Metaphysics: λαμβάνουσι δὲ τὸ τί ἐστιν [αἱ ἐπιστῆμαι] αἱ μὲν διὰ τῆς αἰσθήσεως αἱ δ᾽ ὑποτιθέμεναι<sup>94)</sup>. But whence do they lay them down as a foundation, if not from their own mind<sup>95)</sup>?

85) Cf., besides the special treatises that *Ueberweg*, Gesch. d. Philos. I. 2nd ed. p. 144., lists, *Prantl*, Gesch. d. Logik im Abendl. I. p. 108 ff.

86) Comment. in Arist. d. Anim. p. 168.

87) Ibid. p. 493.

88) Ibid. p. 173.

89) Ibid. p. 492.

90) Ibid. p. 493.

91) Ibid. p. 496.

92) Ibid. p. 494. 495. 173.

5) Our νοῦς ποιητικός, we said, is not the divine spirit; but it is indeed something akin to God. The divine spirit too is a νοῦς ποιητικός; for to anyone who does not deny the existence of God, it can be nothing other than that νοῦς from which the truth of things flows. Aristotle intimated this kinship of the divine and the human spirit in the twelfth book of the Metaphysics<sup>96)</sup>, without, to be sure, determining anything, here or in any other place, about the manner in which the human spirit partakes of the divine<sup>97)</sup>.

6) But since he held it to be something so divine, he was consistently led not to let it develop out of matter, but to let it come to the other powers from outside. He lets it stem from the Godhead<sup>98)</sup> and from it enter into the fetus, as this is in perfect harmony with his whole doctrine of the spirit<sup>99)</sup>.

14. This explanation too, although it wishes cautiously to guard against going beyond the clear determinations of Aristotle, nonetheless seems to us to contain several points which, compared with the words of the Philosopher, prove that here too the doctrine of the νοῦς is not presented in a manner that would fully correspond to Aristotle’s meaning.

Before all else we must assert this with regard to the νοῦς to which, as to the νοῦς that becomes all things, the fifth chapter of the third book On the Soul opposes the νοῦς ποιητικός. According to Aristotle it is not something sensory but something spiritual, as is shown in particular by the fourth chapter, which from beginning to end treats of it alone, and in which it is designated as belonging to the ψυχὴ νοητική (§. 4.), as unmixed with the body (§. 3.), as separated from the body (§. 4.), as simple (§. 9.), as without matter (§. 12.). Nowhere [p. 31] is even the slightest intimation to be found that the discussion turned to another faculty<sup>100)</sup>, and the νοῦς ποιητικός is introduced only with the beginning of the fifth chapter.

93) Ibid. p. 173.

94) Met. Κ, 7. p. 1064, a, 7. (cf. ibid. Ε, 1. p. 1025, b, 10.)

95) Comment. in Arist. d. Anim. p. 495.

96) Met. Λ, 7. p. 1072, b, 18—30.

97) Comment. in Arist. d. Anim. p. 492 f.

98) Ibid. p. 175.

99) Ibid. p. 496.

15. On this point, then, *Brandis* too, if we understand him rightly, departs from Trendelenburg<sup>101)</sup>, whose conception otherwise corresponds completely to his own. For he too decides, in his Geschichte der griechisch-römischen Philosophie (1857), in favor of the view that the νοῦς ποιητικός belongs to the individuality of man, and more recently (1862), in his Entwickelungen der griechischen Philosophie, he upholds the same view with still greater definiteness against other interpretations. We take the liberty of quoting verbatim a passage from it which will best make clear his conception—a conception that also does not depart from Trendelenburg in that it assigns to the νοῦς ποιητικός the cognition of the principles that are true and certain in themselves<sup>102)</sup>, and to the passive νοῦς mediating thinking<sup>103)</sup>.

After remarking that the spirit of man is untouched by matter, he continues: “In its belonging together with presentation, insofar as it borrows from it and from sense perception the material for mediating thinking and needs thought-images (schemata), or let us say, insofar as it acts as mediating thinking, it is to be designated as passive spirit, and simplicity and eternity do not belong to it. Only spirit in the narrower sense of the word, the theoretical or the energetic spirit, is said, when detached from the body, to be what it (truly) is, immortal and eternal, and the real I or self of man is said to rest on it. It comes to us from outside, it is itself divine or the most divine thing in us—so it is said, in order to designate its independence from the organic body, not in order to designate it as a manifestation of the universal world-spirit passing temporarily into us<sup>104)</sup>.”

100) Apart from the fact that no such transition is to be perceived anywhere, some expressions even in the later parts unambiguously mark out the νοῦς that becomes all things. In the last paragraphs of the chapter, as at the beginning, the νοεῖν is designated as πάσχειν (cf. with §. 2. p. 429, a, 13. §§. 9. and 11., p. 429, b, 24. and b, 29.), and precisely at the end is found the comparison with the so-called tabula rasa, which nobody will refer to the νοῦς ποιητικός (§. 11. p. 429, b, 31.). Finally, in the determinations given in the middle of the chapter too, one clearly recognizes what is characteristic of the νοῦς that becomes all things, as indeed in §. 4. and §. 6. (p. 429, a, 29. and b, 8.) it is designated as something that is in potentiality.

101) Zeller too has attacked Trendelenburg’s account for this reason. Phil. d. Griech. 2nd ed. II, 2. p. 442. n. 1.

102) Cf. Griech.-röm. Philos. II, 2. 2. p. 1177.

103) Ibid. p. 1178.

104) Entw. d. griech. Philos. p. 518.

[p. 32] 16. One difficulty would be removed by this altered conception of the νοῦς that becomes all things, except that it now seems not quite comprehensible how it is supposed to perish with the body if it belongs to the spiritual part of man. In its operations it may perhaps be hindered, but how could it be impaired in its existence?

But apart from that, there remains another difficulty, which directly concerns the conception of the νοῦς ποιητικός itself. For if the νοῦς ποιητικός is not a higher, divine spirit but a power proper to the individual soul, it appears impossible to conceive it as a thinking faculty. For one could say neither that it thinks from the beginning and always, nor that it newly receives thoughts. Not the former, for such an assertion would offend as much against experience as against the doctrine of Aristotle in the books On the Soul and in the logical writings, which appeal precisely to this experience<sup>105)</sup>; not the latter, for the receiving of thoughts is, after all, precisely that becoming of the νοητά, that being affected, which, precisely in contrast to the νοῦς ποιητικός, is ascribed to the νοῦς that is in potentiality. Accordingly, it is said of the latter, not, say, that it becomes the “mediated thoughts,” no, but that it becomes *all things*<sup>106)</sup>, namely everything intelligible, as Aristotle explains in the eighth chapter<sup>107)</sup>.

So even our high esteem for the judgment of two such distinguished experts on the Aristotelian philosophy cannot move us to assent to a view with which these clear and important determinations of Aristotle cannot possibly be reconciled.

17. While these explanations at least approach Theophrastus in that they assign the νοῦς ποιητικός to the individuality of man, there are also, in more recent times, others that show themselves more akin to the conceptions of Alexander and of the Arabic commentators. Among them we mention first that of *Ravaisson*.

In the first volume of his Essai sur la métaphysique d’Aristote he declares<sup>108)</sup> that according to Aristotle man has only a passive intellect, which can grasp all forms, receive all ideas, and, analogously to prime matter, become all things. “It is,” he says, “the universal possibility in the world of ideas, as prime matter is in the world of the real.” The νοῦς ποιητικός, on the other hand, is, on his account, “the absolute [p. 33] intelligence, the creative activity, which brings all possible forms to actuality and produces all thoughts.”

105) De Anim. III, 4. §. 12. p. 430, a, 5. τοῦ δὲ μὴ ἀεὶ νοεῖν τὸ αἴτιον ἐπισκεπτέον. Anal. Post. II, 19. p. 99, b, 26.

106) De Anim. III, 5. §. 1. p. 430, a, 14. καὶ ἔστιν ὁ μὲν τοιοῦτος νοῦς, τῷ πάντα γίνεσθαι, κ. τ. λ.

107) Ibid. 8, §. 1. p. 431, b, 22. cf. also 4, §. 3. p. 429, a, 17. and §. 11. p. 429, b, 30.

108) Ess. sur la métaph. d’Aristit. I. p. 586 f. cf. II. p. 17. 19.

This recalls the doctrine of Avicenna, except that the latter lets every form and every thought flow immediately from the intelligentia agens, whereas according to Ravaisson Aristotle—just as he does not deny that bodily beings too, as secondary causes, generate others—assumes in a corresponding way that, as a secondary principle, one thought awakens another in us, so that in the one case as in the other a higher substance need be conceived only as first mover. This higher substance, the Godhead itself, immediately gives the principles from whose power all knowledge and all discursive thinking proceed; and what holds in the theoretical domain holds in the practical as well: divine wisdom gives the primitive light for distinguishing good and evil and gives the will its first impulse, so that virtue appears merely as an instrument of the absolute thought<sup>109)</sup>.

In the more precise determination of the νοῦς that is all things in potentiality, Ravaisson then very nearly coincides with Alexander. The sensitive principle is at bottom the same as the intellective and rational one; hence the intellect also compares and distinguishes the abstract form, which is its own object, and the sensible form, which it could not do if it did not embrace both in *one* consciousness. And so the whole difference between sense and intellect reduces to that between two modes of being of one and the same thing. Naturally, therefore, the intellect too is bound to the body in its existence; nothing that is individual to man is immortal.

18. By this conception alone, Ravaisson thinks, can the doctrine of the intellect be brought into harmony not merely with itself but also with Aristotle’s metaphysics; it alone, he holds, corresponds to the spirit of the Aristotelian system; and in this he lays, as it seems, the greatest weight on the analogy of the possible intellect and prime matter, both of which are supposed to require God as first mover.

Nevertheless, the conception of the intellect that becomes all things as an organic faculty is, as was shown earlier, incompatible with Aristotle’s statements. But even supposing that his account did not contradict the clear pronouncements of our Philosopher on this point, then precisely the last thing that could recommend it to us would vanish as well. For if the intellect that becomes all things were found in an organ as its subject, the power of the first mover, insofar as that power governs the corporeal world, would evidently reach it too, and [p. 34] indeed just as well as it reaches the senses. Something spiritual it could not in any case produce in it, as in a faculty mixed with matter.

109) Eth. Eudem. VII, 14. p. 1248, a, 24.

Further, if the intellect that becomes all things were one with sense and different from it only in state, then, since sense perception comes first, it would, when it grasps the first universal presentation, really no longer be a faculty stripped of all actuality that receives its first form; and Aristotle would have had even more reason to assume a new, immediate intervention of the Godhead for the production of the first sense perception than for the awakening of the first universal thought. Avicenna, however far he departs from Aristotle, nonetheless stands closer to him insofar as he does not fail to recognize the spirituality of the receptive intellect, and he also knows better how to preserve his analogy between it and prime matter.

19. Others, who likewise conceive the νοῦς ποιητικός as a spirit separate from the essence of man, nevertheless depart from Ravaisson in important points, without, to be sure, being more fortunate in their attempts, or even being satisfied with them themselves.

*Renan* claims to see in the doctrine of the νοῦς ποιητικός a theory fairly similar to the view of Malebranche<sup>110)</sup>, and since he cannot deny that this doctrine is little in harmony with the general spirit of the Peripatetic philosophy, he appeals to the fact that Aristotle very often took fragments of older schools into his system without troubling to reconcile them with his own views. Thus the whole theory of the νοῦς is supposed to be borrowed from Anaxagoras<sup>111)</sup>, and if the doctrine of the origin of our intellectual cognitions in the Analytics, indeed if a multitude of statements in the books On the Soul themselves—all of which Renan himself admits—stand in the starkest contradiction with his conception of the doctrine of the νοῦς, this, he thinks, ought not to mislead us in the least. It is childish, he says, to want to bring Aristotle into harmony with himself, since he himself cared little about such a thing<sup>112)</sup>.

20. These words of his own relieve us of any criticism. One may perhaps grant that some thinkers of the less mature period of Greek philosophy, as, for example, that the older Pythagoreans, [p. 35] or that Empedocles, often neglected to give an account of the compatibility of certain doctrines adopted merely externally with other views and even with the principles of their system; but in the case of Aristotle, who always keeps an eye awake on every side, and to whose method it even belongs to point everywhere to the apparent contradictions and to make these very contradictions motives of progress in his inquiry, such an assumption is unjustified in the highest degree, and more than in the case of anyone else. We therefore turn to another of the more recent interpreters.

110) Averr. et l’Averroisme p. 96. Ce qui résulte de tout cela, c’est une théorie assez analogue à celle de Malebranche.

111) Whom Aristotle expressly reproaches for having given no account whatever of the manner and the ground of the cognition of the νοῦς. De Anim. I, 2. §. 22. p. 405, b, 21.

112) Averr. et l’Averr. p. 97.

21. *Zeller*, who likewise conceives the νοῦς ποιητικός as a universal spirit, as the absolute thinking of the Godhead, but neither agrees with Ravaisson nor makes Aristotle into a Greek Malebranche, arrives at a view according to which we would have to credit our Philosopher with a still far stranger theory, one that would have contained the same absurdities that made Averroes famous in the Middle Ages.

The highest thinking, which rests perfected in its object<sup>113)</sup>, man thinks, according to Aristotle as Zeller understands him, in the intellect of the universal spirit, so that the thinking activity of all men, insofar as it does not develop out of experience, is a single one, and indeed one and the same with the thinking activity of God. That Averroes lets not merely a certain spiritual thinking of man, but all of it, take place in a separate substance that is united with him precisely through his thinking, would on this account be the only point in which he deviated from Aristotle, and so he could be charged only with a multiplication of the absurdity, by no means with an aggravation of it.

22. But if the strangeness and absurdity of this theory are already enough to make us doubt the correctness of such results, then nothing whatever remains that could recommend this conception when one sees how not a few statements of Aristotle stand in open contradiction with it. Even Zeller admits this and points to several passages where [p. 36] we find the νοῦς ποιητικός presented as something belonging to the individual soul<sup>114)</sup>. With the νοῦς that becomes all things, too, he does not know what to do; he cannot bring it into closer connection with the ποιητικός, since the latter is supposed to be no part of the human being but the absolute world-spirit, and so he sees himself driven to count it, like the senses, among what is bodily in man<sup>115)</sup>; on the other hand, however, he admits that it can in no way be counted among the material, and he therefore reproaches Trendelenburg’s account with evidently altering the Aristotelian doctrine on this point<sup>116)</sup>.

113) See Philos. d. Griech. II, 2. p. 441., where the domain of the passive νοῦς is delimited from that of the νοῦς ποιητικός. On p. 438. it is ascribed to the νοῦς that it grasps in immediate cognition the highest principles, which cannot be the object of mediated thinking, and the following remarks show that this is to be referred to the νοῦς ποιητικός. — These highest principles, then, we men would, in Aristotle’s opinion, think in the intellect of God. Strange already in itself, and stranger still if, as Zeller interprets Aristotle’s doctrine, this divine intellect itself by no means thinks the principles of our knowledge, nor indeed several principles at all, but solely and only itself!

23. Still other variations of the conception that separates the νοῦς ποιητικός from the individuality of man could be adduced, but those already mentioned, which have been attempted by such distinguished inquirers, suffice to demonstrate the contradictions and the confusion that is necessarily brought into the Aristotelian doctrine by every attempt of this kind, and that grows the greater, the more one takes all the individual statements into account. It must be said to Zeller’s credit that he has done this the most, but precisely for that reason it is also precisely in his account that this part of the Aristotelian psychology appears most of all as a tangle of confused notions and as a heap of mutually contradictory statements.

If this were Aristotle’s theory, then indeed, in decrying him as a sensualist, one would not have injured his honor as a philosopher; one would still have judged him all too favorably; sensualism is, after all, still a view, whatever one may rightly find deficient in it, but such talk would be devoid of all sense and reason<sup>117)</sup>.

114) Ibid. p. 441. n. 3.

115) Ibid. p. 443. n. 4.

116) Ibid. p. 442. n. 1.

117) On the attempted explanation of Denis in his Rationalisme d’Aristote see below Section II, Part IV. n. 320.

[scan 68]

### Zweiter Abschnitt. — Second Section.

### Entwickelung der Aristotelischen Lehre vom — Development of the Aristotelian Doctrine of the

### νοῦς ποιητικός.

#### Rückblick. — Anhaltspuncte der Untersuchung. — Retrospect. — Points of Reference for the Inquiry.

1. We have surveyed many and various explanations that the Aristotelian doctrine of the νοῦς ποιητικός has received in earlier and more recent times, partly in order to stimulate our own interest as well by pointing to the interest it has found among others, but partly, and chiefly, in order to make clear, from the disagreement of the greatest commentators, the difficulty of our task. Now, however, I might almost fear that I have achieved this aim all too well; for if anyone looks at the diversity of the directions in which the most acute interpreters here part ways, and at the same time sees hardly a single one arrive at a satisfying result, he might well, far from following a new inquiry in this field with keener attention, feel rather moved to withhold his interest from every attempt of this kind, as one that strives after the impossible. Only under *one* condition, it seems, would we perhaps still have a right to entertain hope of a happier result of our research: namely, if, besides the obscure and scanty statements of Aristotle that have hitherto formed the points of reference, we were able to point to new sources for the knowledge of his doctrine. But this is by no means the case; our sources are none other than the writings of Aristotle, all of which lay before the earlier interpreters as well.

2. Nevertheless, there are several things that keep our hope alive. First, the consideration of the earlier attempts at explanation has without doubt already substantially advanced our task. One has brought this true moment to bear, another that, and even where an inquirer evidently strayed from the right path, his efforts have put us in his debt, in that we, warned by the consequences, are now no longer tempted [p. 38] to turn off into the same byway. Our choice is narrowed, and a mistake therefore less likely.

We have seen from many attempts, and in particular from the attempt of *Zeller*, that it is impossible to take the νοῦς ποιητικός for the Godhead or for some other higher substance foreign to us in essence. We have further gathered from the attempts of *Trendelenburg* and *Brandis* that it is impossible to conceive it as something proper to the soul and at the same time as something thinking. If it is an active principle of thoughts, it does not follow, after all, that it has the thoughts themselves in itself, since thinking, if it is a kind of being affected, must, like motion, be found not in that which acts, as such, but in that which is affected. Finally, we have recognized from the attempt of *Suarez* that it will not do either to declare the νοῦς ποιητικός an active power of the soul which with its efficacy immediately touches the soul’s passive spiritual faculty, and which thus does not, indeed, think, but yet produces the thoughts in the thinking faculty by immediate action upon it. What, then, remains for us but to assume with *Thomas Aquinas* that the νοῦς ποιητικός is indeed an active principle of the intellective soul, but with its activity immediately turned toward a lower part? But here too we are warned by the critique of *Durandus* against defining its acting in such a way as if something spiritual were thereby produced in the sensory. And so almost all paths are barred to us. But there must, unless my confidence in Aristotle deceives me, remain a way out, and it is this that will then convey to us the correct understanding of the doctrine.

3. Under *one* condition, we said, hope of a happy solution of our task remains to us: namely, if we saw new sources for the knowledge of the doctrine of the νοῦς ποιητικός opening up to research. Now this, as we have already remarked, is not the case, insofar as our sole source too, if we disregard the remarks of Theophrastus that Themistius has handed down to us, remains the writings of Aristotle, which were known already before and were no doubt also used for this purpose. For since in the third book On the Soul Aristotle speaks only briefly and obscurely, while in other writings, e.g., in his logical treatises, he does not disdain to express himself more fully and in clearer words, nothing was more natural than to call these too to aid in investigating so difficult a point.

But if we can use nothing new, we can still perhaps use some things as a source in a new way; for when hitherto other writings were consulted for the explanation of the obscure words in the fifth chapter of the third book On the Soul, this was done [p. 39] chiefly with the intention of finding parallel passages, or at least such statements as might be able, by indicating new peculiarities of the νοῦς ποιητικός, to supplement our concept of it. Now, if anywhere there was talk of a νοῦς to which a high significance was ascribed, people believed they could recognize the νοῦς ποιητικός in it and hastened to combine this passage with the statements in the third book On the Soul, an attempt that could only produce confusion, since in truth nowhere in the logical writings (and the same holds of the Metaphysics) is the νοῦς ποιητικός immediately discussed.

We, then, intend likewise to make use of the books of logic, in particular the doctrine of the Analytics on the origin of our first spiritual cognitions, but in an entirely different way, one that does not, indeed, lead so immediately to a result, but in which a mistake is also not so much to be feared. For we intend to attend to that moment in the origin of our spiritual cognitions which, since Aristotle observed it and was by no means able to explain it solely from his νοῦς that becomes all things or from the activities of the senses, compelled him to assume the νοῦς ποιητικός. The traces of the efficacy ascribed to it cannot escape us; and through them we may surely expect to gain information about what Aristotle intended with his νοῦς ποιητικός and understood by it. For the method that Aristotle recommended as necessary and himself followed everywhere was, after all, to penetrate from the knowledge of the effects and activities into the nature of the powers<sup>1)</sup>; and if we therefore take this path, we follow, so to speak, in his footsteps, which will surely lead us best to his true view of the intellective powers.

4. Finally, we intend to employ yet one more means, from which I promise myself the best success: it is the consideration of this part of the Aristotelian doctrine of the soul in the light of the whole. Aristotle, who, just as he demands unity of poetry, sees a unity in the real world—it is not to be torn apart like a bad tragedy<sup>2)</sup>—necessarily strives for unity and harmony in his philosophy too, and it is not to be thought that his doctrine of the soul is no whole, but only a heap of statements of which no later one takes account of the earlier. But if this is not the case, if rather the Aristotelian doctrine of the soul is a whole, harmoniously articulated and pervaded by *one* spirit [p. 40] , then, by considering this whole, we shall be prepared for the understanding of a difficult particular; we shall comprehend it, I might say, from its end, since here too the proposition cannot have lost all significance: the whole is the end of its parts.

1) Cf. De Anim. II, 4. §. 1. p. 415, a, 14. also ibid. I, 1. §. 6 and 7. p. 402, b, 9. For the reasons for this see Metaph. Θ, 8. p. 1049, b, 10. ff. ibid. 9. p. 1050, b, 29.

2) Metaph. Ν. 3. p. 1090, b, 19. cf. Λ, fin.

There will be a harmony between the relation in which Aristotle unites the spiritual and the sensory in the essence of man and the relation in which he thinks of them as touching each other in intellectual cognition and sense perception; and whoever has understood him on the one point will thereby also have been brought nearer to understanding the other. Further, analogies will be found in his views of the individual parts of the soul which, if anywhere an obscure passage has put a difficulty in the way of understanding, will let us recognize its correct meaning, since, even before we have explained it, we can already say in advance what must, by analogy, be conjectured here.

5. So let us, then, survey the whole Aristotelian psychology with a quick glance. Only here and there will a point that is interpreted differently by different people compel us to linger somewhat longer, while on the other hand we may also pass over entirely many things, in particular what is more physiological and, as it has lost its significance in general, cannot in particular become decisive for our inquiry.

[scan 72]

### Erster Theil. — First Part.

### Von der Seele und den Seelenkräften im Allgemeinen. — On the Soul and the Powers of the Soul in General.

#### a. Von dem Wesen der Seele und ihrer Vereinigung mit dem Leibe. — a. On the Essence of the Soul and Its Union with the Body.

1. Before all else it will be our task to establish, with Aristotle, to which of the genera the human soul belongs, and what it is; whether, namely, it is a substance, or a quality, or a quantity, or is found in some other category<sup>1)</sup>. The starting point of this inquiry will then have to be the question of the difference between the living and the lifeless; for since by the living and the ensouled, and likewise by the lifeless and the soulless, we understand one and the same thing<sup>2)</sup>, it is clear that if the living as living differs substantially from the lifeless, the ensouled as such is substance, and the soul, as the ground of a substantial difference, belongs to the category of substance<sup>3)</sup>.

The difference of substances reveals itself in the difference of their motions and activities<sup>4)</sup>; yet one cannot infer back from every motion to the nature of the substance. If a stone is hurled upward, one would be mistaken in wishing to count it among the light bodies because it takes an upward direction, and something similar holds of all violent [p. 42] motions; only the motions and operations that are natural to the substance can come into consideration, because they alone have their principle in the essence of the thing itself<sup>5)</sup>. Thus we see our problem reduced to the question whether that which lives differs from that which does not live in its natural operations and motions. Only if this is the case will the difference between the living and the lifeless have been proved to be a difference of substance, and the soul, the principle of life, to be something substantial.

1) De Anim. I, 1. §. 3. p. 402, a, 22. πρῶτον δ᾽ ἴσως ἀναγκαῖον διελεῖν ἐν τίνι τῶν γενῶν καὶ τί ἐστι (ἡ ψυχή), λέγω δὲ πότερον τόδε τι καὶ οὐσία ἢ ποιὸν ἢ ποσὸν ἢ καὶ τις ἄλλη τῶν διαιρεθεισῶν κατηγοριῶν. Cf. ibid. §. 1. p. 402, a, 7. and Anal. Post. II, 13. p. 96, b, 19.

2) De Anim. II, 2. §. 2. p. 413, a, 20. λέγομεν οὖν ἀρχὴν λαβόντες τῆς σκέψεως, διωρίσθαι τὸ ἔμψυχον τοῦ ἀψύχου τῷ ζῆν.

3) For the principles of a substance must themselves belong to the category of substance. Cf. Metaph. Λ, 4. f.

4) This thought underlies numerous statements of Aristotle. We refer here only to some passages of the books On the Soul. De Anim. I, 1. §. 9. f. p. 403, a, 8. II, 1. §. 3. p. 412, a, 13. and 4. §. 1. p. 415, a, 16. III, 4. §. 4. f. p. 429, a, 24. etc.

Now, that particular operations belong to what lives is evident; for we say that something lives if it thinks, perceives by sense, moves itself locally, nourishes itself and grows, whether all these operations are found in it or only one or another of them<sup>6)</sup>.

But that these motions are not violent but natural seems just as little subject to doubt; indeed, in the case of no motion that we perceive in things does this seem as certain as precisely in the case of these<sup>7)</sup>. Hence the ensouled differs in its nature from the soulless, life is the substantial being of the living, and the soul, the principle of life<sup>8)</sup>, will, as the principle of substances, itself have to belong to the domain of the substantial<sup>9)</sup>.

2. The living differs substantially from the lifeless; death and animation are substantial changes<sup>10)</sup>; the soul is a principle of something substantial, it is substance.

But not everything that is the ground of a substance is one with the substance of which it is the ground. One animal generates another, and the generating animal is not *one* substance with the one generated. The question [p. 43] therefore arises whether the soul is an inner principle of the ensouled, or a substance distinct from it, and so a principle external to its essence and in it only in the way, say, that the boatman is in the boat moved by him<sup>11)</sup>.

5) Cf. Phys. II, 1. p. 192, b, 13. 20. and VIII, 4. p. 254, b, 12. De Coel. III, 2. p. 301, b, 17. Metaph. Δ, 4.

6) De Anim. II, 2. §. 2. p. 413, a, 22. πλεοναχῶς δὲ τοῦ ζῆν λεγομένου, κἂν ἕν τι τούτων ἐνυπάρχῃ μόνον, ζῆν αὐτό φαμεν, οἷον νοῦς, αἴσθησις κίνησις καὶ στάσις ἡ κατὰ τόπον, ἔτι κίνησις ἡ κατὰ τροφὴν καὶ φθίσις τε καὶ αὔξησις.

7) Hence Aristotle says in De Anim. I, 3., although he does not deny that it is natural to ascribe certain motions to the soul itself, namely those which are vital functions (4. §. 10. p. 408, b, 1. φαμὲν γὰρ τὴν ψυχὴν λυπεῖσθαι, χαίρειν, θαῤῥεῖν, φοβεῖσθαι, ἔτι δὲ ὀργίζεσθαί τε καὶ αἰσθάνεσθαι καὶ διανοεῖσθαι· ταῦτα δὲ πάντα κινήσεις εἶναι δοκοῦσιν. ὅθεν οἰηθείη τις ἂν αὐτὴν κινεῖσθαι.), that even someone who wished to invent them would scarcely be able to specify violent motions of the soul (§. 4. p. 406, a, 26.): ποῖαι δὲ βίαιοι τῆς ψυχῆς κινήσεις ἔσονται καὶ ἠρεμίαι, οὐδὲ πλάττειν βουλομένοις ῥᾴδιον ἀποδοῦναι.

8) De Anim. II, 4. §. 4. p. 415, b, 13. τὸ δὲ ζῆν τοῖς ζῶσι τὸ εἶναί ἐστιν, αἰτία δὲ καὶ ἀρχὴ τούτων ἡ ψυχή.

9) De Anim. II, 1. §. 4. p. 412, a, 19. ἀναγκαῖον ἄρα τὴν ψυχὴν οὐσίαν εἶναι.

10) De Longit. et Brevit. Vitae 2. p. 465, a, 26.

But this too, after what has been said, is plainly no longer possible. The soul is not merely no accident; it is also no substance accidentally joined with the ensouled, for in this case too the vital functions would not be, as was just said, natural operations of the ensouled, but would be violent to it, just as the movement of the boat by the boatman is violent. The vital functions have their ground in the nature of the living; if, therefore, their principle is the soul, the latter cannot be separated from its nature; it must be an inner principle of the living, which forms *one* substance with it<sup>12)</sup>.

3. All earthly living beings, as experience teaches, are mortal, so far as they fall under the senses; the lifeless comes to be from the living, just as, conversely, the living comes to be from the lifeless, and precisely in the transitions of dying and of coming to life it shows itself quite clearly that (earthly<sup>13)</sup>) bodily beings are subject not merely to accidental changes, but also to a change of substance. For the living and the lifeless, we said, are different substances; if, then, the lifeless comes to be from the living and the living from the lifeless, one substance comes to be from the other substance.

But from this it follows that these substances, living as well as lifeless, are inwardly constituted by two principles, so that, since the soul has turned out for us to be an inner principle of the living, we now see a new doubt spring up again, namely, for which of the two principles one is to take the soul<sup>14)</sup>.

For in everything that is subject to a change, something twofold can be distinguished: something that remains during the transformation and underlies it, and something else that disappears during it and through it<sup>15)</sup>. Something that remains, we say; [p. 44] for if nothing remained that was first the one and then the other, one could not say that the one had become the other, or that this had formerly been that. The one would indeed have perished entirely, and the other, as something wholly new, have taken its place; it would be not out of the other, but only after the other.

11) De Anim. II, 1. §. 13. p. 413, a, 8. ἔτι δὲ ἄδηλον εἰ οὕτως ἐντελέχεια τοῦ σώματος ἡ ψυχὴ ὥσπερ πλωτὴρ πλοίου. Cf. Phys. VIII, 4. p. 254, b, 27.

12) De Anim. II, 2. §. 14. p. 414, a, 20. σῶμα μὲν γὰρ οὐκ ἔστι (ἡ ψυχή), σώματος δέ τι.

13) Aristotle believed of the heavenly bodies that they are incorruptible. Hence he says in Metaph. Λ, 1. p. 1069, a, 30. οὐσίαι δὲ τρεῖς, μία μὲν αἰσθητή, ἧς ἡ μὲν ἀΐδιος ἡ δὲ φθαρτή . . . ἄλλη δὲ ἀκίνητος, κ. τ. λ.

14) De Anim. I, 1. §. 3. p. 402, a, 25. ἔτι δὲ (ἀναγκαῖον διελεῖν) πότερον τῶν ἐν δυνάμει ὄντων (ἡ ψυχή) ἢ μᾶλλον ἐντελέχειά τις· διαφέρει γὰρ οὔ τι σμικρόν. Cf. ibid. II, 1. §. 2. ff. p. 412, a, 7. and 2, §. 12. f. p. 414, a, 4.

15) Cf. on this and on what follows Phys. I, 7. p. 190, a, 14. b, 10. ff. Metaph. Η, 1. p. 1042, a, 32. ibid. Λ, 2. p. 1069, b, 3.

But it is just as clear that in every transformation something must disappear; for if everything remained, there would be no becoming, no transformation, but perfect rest.

Thus, for example, the change of place is underlain by the body, which, as something that is potentially here and there, remains in the motion, while its local determination becomes now one, now another. Likewise in heating, the body that is potentially cold and warm remains, while the cold yields to the warmth. It is similar with extension and with every accidental change; *one* accidental form is lost and another acquired in its stead, while the substance, which has in itself the potentiality for the one and the other, remains as the subject of the accidental becoming.

But now what will underlie the substantial change such as is found, for example, between living and lifeless bodies? Will it be another actual substance, or will it perhaps be the accidents? — Both are impossible! for in the one case the substance would have to be an accident of another substance, which would be contradictory; in the other case, however, the relation between substance and accident would be downright reversed: the accidents would become the subject of the substance, whereas the substance is in fact the subject of the accidents<sup>16)</sup>. It therefore only remains to assume that that which, common to what comes to be and what passes away, underlies the substantial change is indeed something substantial, but by no means an actual substance; rather, it is mere substantial potentiality, in which, since it does not inhere in another subject as the accidental potentialities do, we must therefore see the ultimate subject of what is<sup>17)</sup>.

It is this that through different substantial actualities is first the one, then the other; that with the one actuality inwardly constituted what passes away, out of which the arising substance then developed; and that remains in the latter too as the one of the two elements<sup>18)</sup> constituting it. Aristotle calls it matter [p. 45] <sup>19)</sup>, substance in the sense of matter<sup>20)</sup>, or in the sense of the underlying<sup>21)</sup>, the principle from which something comes to be, or is<sup>22)</sup>, the receptive<sup>23)</sup>, potentiality or substance in potentiality<sup>24)</sup>, non-being<sup>25)</sup>, and the like; and in contrast to it he calls the other principle form<sup>26)</sup>, actuality<sup>27)</sup>, perfection<sup>28)</sup>, that into which something changes (i.e., into which it comes in changing) and in which matter is when it is in actuality<sup>29)</sup>, the ground of existence<sup>30)</sup>, that in virtue of which something is what it is<sup>31)</sup>, species<sup>32)</sup>, concept<sup>33)</sup>, essence or first essence<sup>34)</sup>, the what-it-was-to-be<sup>35)</sup>, and the like—names which sufficiently indicate how through [p. 46] this principle the substance first receives being and determination of essence, while matter as such is the indeterminate<sup>36)</sup>.

16) Cf. Metaph. Ζ, 13. p. 1038, b, 27. and 29.

17) Metaph. Ζ, 3. p. 1029, a, 23. τὰ μὲν γὰρ ἄλλα τῆς οὐσίας κατηγορεῖται, αὕτη δὲ τῆς ὕλης. ὥστε τὸ ἔσχατον καθ᾽ αὐτὸ οὔτε τὶ οὔτε ποσὸν οὔτε ἄλλο οὐθέν ἐστιν.

18) Elements (στοιχεῖα) in the sense in which this word is used, e.g., in Metaph. Λ, 4. p. 1070, a, 34. and often in this and the following chapter, not in the more usual sense of the simple bodies.

19) ὕλη e.g. De Anim. II, 1. p. 412, a, 9. 19. Metaph. Η, 1. p. 1042, a, 27. and in countless other places.

20) ὡς ὕλη οὐσία. De Anim. l. c. a, 7. Metaph. Η, 2. p. 1042, b, 9. and elsewhere.

21) ὑποκείμενον De Anim. II, 1. p. 412, a, 19. ibid. 2, p. 414, a, 14. Phys. I, 9. p. 192, a, 31. Metaph. Α, 3. p. 983, a, 30. ibid. Ζ, 8. p. 1035, a, 28. 31. and 13. p. 1038, b, 2. and elsewhere. ἡ ὡς ὑποκειμένη οὐσία ibid. Η, 2. p. 1042, b, 9.

22) ἐξ οὗ γίγνεται e.g. Metaph. Α, 3. p. 983, b, 24. ibid. Β, 4. p. 999, b, 7. Δ, 2. princ. Ζ, 7. p. 1032, a, 17. Phys. I, 9. p. 192, a, 31. — ἐξ οὗ Metaph. Ζ, 7. p. 1032, a, 21. ibid. Δ, 2. p. 1013, b, 20. cf. Δ, 4. princ. etc.

23) δεκτικόν e.g. De Generat. et Corr. I, 10. p. 328, b, 10. De Anim. II, 2. p. 414, a, 10. Metaph. Δ, 4. p. 1015, a, 16. etc.

24) δύναμις e.g. De Anim. II, 1. p. 412, a, 9. ibid. 414, a, 16. ἡ δυνάμει οὐσία e.g. Metaph. Η, 2. p. 1042, b, 10. δυνάμει τόδε τι ibid. b, 27.

25) μὴ ὄν e.g. Phys. I, 8. p. 191, b, 27. De Gen. et Corr. I, 3. p. 317, b, 15. Metaph. Γ, 4. p. 1007, b, 28. ibid. Ν, 2. p. 1089, a, 28. cf. also Metaph. Ζ, 3. p. 1029, a, 24.

26) μορφή e.g. Phys. II, 1. p. 193, a, 30. b, 4. 18. 19. De Anim. II, 1. p. 412, a, 8. ibid. 2, p. 414, a, 9. Metaph. Η, 6. p. 1045, b, 18.

27) ἐνέργεια e.g. De Anim. II, 2. p. 414, a, 9. Metaph. Η, 2. p. 1043, a, 12. 20. ibid. 3. p. 1043, b, 1.

28) ἐντελέχεια e.g. De Anim. II, 1. p. 412, a, 10. ibid. 2. p. 414, a, 16. 25. Metaph. Ζ, 13. p. 1038, b, 6. cf. ibid. Δ, 24. p. 1023, a, 34.

29) εἰς ὃ μεταβάλλει e.g. Metaph. Λ, 3. p. 1070, a, 2. ibid. a, 11. cf. Θ, 8. p. 1050, a, 15.

30) τὸ αἴτιον τοῦ εἶναι De Anim. II, 4. p. 415, b, 12.

31) τὸ καθ᾽ ὅ e.g. Metaph. Δ, 18. princ. ibid. Ζ, 7. p. 1032, a, 21. De Anim. II, 1. p. 412, a, 8.

32) εἶδος e.g. De Anim. II, 1. p. 412, a, 8. 10. Metaph. Ζ, 7. p. 1032, b, 1.

33) λόγος e.g. De Anim. I, 2. p. 403, b, 2. 8. ibid. II, 2. p. 414, a, 9. 13. 27. De Gener. Animal. I, 1. p. 715, a, 5. 8. Metaph. Η, 1. p. 1042, a, 28. ibid. 2. p. 1043, a, 12. Λ, 2. p. 1069, b, 34. and elsewhere. οὐσία, ἡ κατὰ τὸν λόγον, De Anim. II, 1. p. 412, b, 10. Metaph. Ζ, 10. p. 1035, b, 15. εἶδος τὸ κατὰ τὸν λόγον Phys. II, 1. p. 192, a, 31.

34) οὐσία e.g. De Anim. II, 4. p. 415, b, 11. πρώτη οὐσία Metaph. Ζ, 7. p. 1032, b, 2. ibid. 13. p. 1038, b, 10., on which see *Trendelenburg*, Gesch. d. Kat. p. 40.

35) τὸ τί ἦν εἶναι e.g. De Anim. II, 1. p. 412, b, 11. Metaph. Α, 3. p. 983, a, 27. ibid. Ζ, 7. p. 1032, b, 1. 14.; 10. p. 1035, b, 32. and 13. p. 1038, b, 3.

Let us now look back on what has been established regarding the soul. We had recognized it as something substantial and as an inner principle of the ensouled substance; the ensouled substances, however, so far as they are subject to a substantial change, i.e., so far as they are mortal, have now turned out for us to be inwardly constituted by two principles, of which the one is substance in the sense of matter, the other in the sense of form. One of the two is the soul; but which of the two?

The question is easily resolved; for since the soul does not remain in what dies, and since, further, it is *it* that gives the living thing its determination of essence and makes it something actually living, it plainly cannot be the matter; rather it must be that which completes the potentiality of the substance into actual being, that which animates the matter; it must be the substantial form, the actuality, the entelechy of the living being. This, then, will be the concept that belongs in common to the souls of mortal beings: “*The soul is the first entelechy of a natural body which has life in potentiality*<sup>37)</sup>.”

But such a body is organic, for the parts of plants too are organs, although simpler organs than the organs of animals. Thus the leaf is a protection of the pericarp, and the latter a protection of the fruit, while the roots appear analogous to the mouth, inasmuch as through them the plant sucks in its nourishment; and so we shall be able to designate the soul of a mortal being also as “*the first entelechy of a physical, organic body*”<sup>38)</sup>.

On this expression see *Trendelenburg*, Rhein. Mus. 1828. no. 4. De Anim. p. 192 ff. 471 ff. and Gesch. d. Kateg. p. 34 ff. *Schwegler*, Arist. Metaph. IV. p. 369 ff. *Bonitz* in Metaph. p. 311 ff. *Zeller*, Philos. der Griech. 2nd ed. II, 2. p. 146. n. 1.

36) ἀόριστον Phys. III, 6. p. 207, a, 30. Metaph. Γ, 4. p. 1007, b, 26. and 5. p. 1010, a, 3. ibid. Ζ, 11. p. 1037, a, 27. cf. 3, p. 1029, a, 20. Hence it is also not knowable in and for itself, Phys. l. c. Metaph. Ζ, 10. p. 1036, a, 8. ἡ δ᾽ ὕλη ἄγνωστος καθ᾽ αὐτήν. Cf. also Phys. I, 7. p. 191, a, 7.

37) De Anim. II, 1. §. 5. p. 412, a, 27. διὸ ψυχή ἐστιν ἐντελέχεια ἡ πρώτη σώματος φυσικοῦ δυνάμει ζωὴν ἔχοντος. cf. ibid. 2. §. 12 ff. p. 414, a, 4. Metaph. Ζ, 10. p. 1035, b, 14. ibid. Η, 6. p. 1045, b, 11., with which cf. ibid. b, 16. Λ, 3. p. 1070, a, 21—27. and elsewhere.

38) De Anim. II, 1. §. 6. p. 412, a, 28. τοιοῦτο δὲ (σῶμα) ὃ ἂν ᾖ ὀργανικόν. ὄργανα δὲ καὶ τὰ τῶν φυτῶν μέρη, ἀλλὰ παντελῶς ἁπλᾶ, οἷον τὸ φύλλον περικαρπίου σκέπασμα, τὸ δὲ περικάρπιον καρποῦ. αἱ δὲ ῥίζαι τῷ στόματι ἀνάλογον. ἄμφω γὰρ ἕλκει τὴν τροφὴν (cf. Phys. II, 7. p. 199, a, 23.). εἰ δή τι κοινὸν ἐπὶ πάσης ψυχῆς δεῖ λέγειν, εἴη ἂν ἐντελέχεια ἡ πρώτη σώματος φυσικοῦ ὀργανικοῦ.

[p. 47] 4. From this result of our inquiry a series of facts of experience is now explained, for which those who regard the soul as a substance by itself, which merely dwells in the body and moves it as the boatman moves the boat, are scarcely able to give a reason.

Thus, for example, if this view were the correct one, any matter whatever could well be ensouled, and indeed each could be ensouled by any soul one pleases — for why should not an animal soul too be able to enter into a human body, and a human soul find room in an animal or plant body, or even in a stone? — But experience teaches that it is always only bodies of a certain constitution that have a soul, and indeed this or that soul<sup>39)</sup>, and this in such a way that if the body undergoes a change destructive of its essence, the soul is at once no longer found in it. Whence, now, this phenomenon, which in its universality sufficiently makes its necessity known? — For us the question is easily answered; the soul itself, after all, determines the essence of its body, since it is nothing other than the actuality of the living body. Indeed, it would be as impossible for the soul of one living being to take up its dwelling in the body of another as for the nature of the flute to pass into a violin, so that one could now play the flute on the violin<sup>40)</sup>.

39) One might object here that experience does not teach that certain kinds of souls are only in certain kinds of bodies, but merely that they never manifest themselves in them through their activities. — But let it be considered that if this latter is, according to experience, *never* the case, it plainly cannot be the case. For why should the soul of a lion, if it were in the body of a hare, not be able to show in it too the courage peculiar to it, and the soul of a dog in the body of a cat not be able to show in it too the fidelity and attachment that distinguish it? — But even supposing that some reason could perhaps be found why the souls of the lion and the dog could not reveal their own proper strength and excellence in alien bodies (and for the soul of the animal at least, if it were found in a plant, and for that of the plant, if it were in an inorganic body, the lack of corresponding organs would indeed serve as a sufficient explanation), the union of such souls and bodies would at any rate plainly be purposeless, and this alone would suffice to deny it with certainty. We can therefore say with definiteness that a certain kind of soul always dwells only in a certain kind of body; and since this is universally the case, it is necessarily the case, and the ground of this necessity must be sought out (for the recognition of that purposelessness does not relieve us of this question); it must follow from the correctly grasped concept of the soul.

40) De Anim. II, 2. §. 14. p. 414, a, 20. σῶμα μὲν γὰρ οὐκ ἔστι (ἡ ψυχή), σώματος δέ τι, καὶ διὰ τοῦτο ἐν σώματι ὑπάρχει, καὶ ἐν σώματι τοιούτῳ, καὶ οὐχ ὥσπερ οἱ πρότερον

[p. 48] 5. In the same way another phenomenon is explained, which must serve in no small measure to confirm our view of the soul. Experience teaches us, namely, that in plants, if one cuts them up, one perceives the vegetative vital functions in each of the separated parts, and something similar becomes noticeable in certain animals, inasmuch as, when they are cut up, both parts perceive by sense and move themselves locally, and hence plainly also have imagination and sensory appetite, since these are the inseparable companions of sensation. Out of *one* ensouled being two have come to be here<sup>41)</sup>, and so not merely the bodily substances, [p. 49] but also the souls have multiplied, and indeed through precisely that division which multiplied the bodily substance<sup>42)</sup>; for surely no one will want to maintain that the one part could have life and sensation through the soul that is in the other part. But if this is impossible, if rather a particular soul must belong to each of the fragments, the question arises whence, then, so suddenly, on so arbitrary an occasion, the second soul has come, if, that is, it does not belong to the nature of the body itself but is separate from it in essence and forms a substance by itself alone? It might indeed be difficult even to invent anything by which a solution of the riddle would be given in any way that is at all probable.

εἰς σῶμα ἐνήρμοζον αὐτήν, οὐθὲν προσδιορίζοντες ἐν τίνι καὶ ποίῳ, καίπερ οὐδὲ φαινομένου τοῦ τυχόντος δέχεσθαι τὸ τυχόν. οὕτω δὲ γίνεται καὶ κατὰ λόγον· ἑκάστου γὰρ ἡ ἐντελέχεια ἐν τῷ δυνάμει ὑπάρχοντι καὶ τῇ οἰκείᾳ ὕλῃ πέφυκεν ἐγγίνεσθαι. Ibid. I, 3. §. 22. p. 407, b, 13. ἐκεῖνο δὲ ἄτοπον συμβαίνει καὶ τούτῳ τῷ λόγῳ καὶ τοῖς πλείστοις τῶν περὶ ψυχῆς· συνάπτουσι γὰρ καὶ τιθέασιν εἰς σῶμα τὴν ψυχήν, οὐθὲν προσδιορίσαντες διὰ τίν᾽ αἰτίαν καὶ πῶς ἔχοντος τοῦ σώματος. καίτοι δόξειεν ἂν τοῦτ᾽ ἀναγκαῖον εἶναι· διὰ γὰρ τὴν κοινωνίαν τὸ μὲν ποιεῖ τὸ δὲ πάσχει καὶ τὸ μὲν κινεῖται τὸ δὲ κινεῖ, τούτων δ᾽ οὐθὲν ὑπάρχει πρὸς ἄλληλα τοῖς τυχοῦσιν. οἱ δὲ μόνον ἐπιχειροῦσι λέγειν ποῖόν τι ἡ ψυχή, περὶ δὲ τοῦ δεξομένου σώματος οὐθὲν ἔτι προσδιορίζουσιν, ὥσπερ ἐνδεχόμενον κατὰ τοὺς Πυθαγορικοὺς μύθους τὴν τυχοῦσαν ψυχὴν εἰς τὸ τυχὸν ἐνδύεσθαι σῶμα· δοκεῖ γὰρ ἕκαστον ἴδιον ἔχειν εἶδος καὶ μορφήν. παραπλήσιον δὲ λέγουσιν ὥσπερ εἴ τις φαίη τὴν τεκτονικὴν εἰς αὐλοὺς ἐνδύεσθαι· δεῖ γὰρ τὴν μὲν τέχνην χρῆσθαι τοῖς ὀργάνοις, τὴν δὲ ψυχὴν τῷ σώματι. Cf. ibid. 4. §. 8. p. 408, a, 25.

41) How wholly insufficient is the explanation of some recent physiologists, who think that no actual sensing, and consequently no soul either, is to be ascribed to those fragments, but that one must rather take their apparent manifestations of life, despite the most deceptive resemblance to those that stem from consciousness, for merely mechanical reflex movements: this shows itself especially in those kinds of animals in which the parts, growing out again, complete themselves into whole animal organisms that lack not a single one of the vital powers that belonged to the original animal. I know well that, in order to come to terms with this fact, people have, breaking away from the attempt at explanation applied in the other cases, taken refuge in a further hypothesis (the existence of several animal souls in *one* body), which stands in no kind of kinship with that first one. But apart from everything strange and awkward that attaches to it, the very inconstancy and arbitrariness that prevail in this procedure would furnish the fully valid proof that one cannot have been successful either in this or in the other attempt at explanation. In the one case and the other the ground of explanation must be one and the same, because in the one case and the other the phenomenon to be explained is also one and the same. Aristotle himself, to whom none of those cases in which the fragments live on is yet known, rightly remarks concerning the rest that, if the parts of the dissected animal do not live on, this is of no significance, since it comes from their not having the organs necessary for their preservation (De Anim. I, 5. §. 26. p. 411, b, 22. De Longit. et Brevit. Vit. 6. p. 467, a, 20. De Juvent. et Senect. 2. p. 468, b, 5.). Close off the paths of nourishment to a perfect living being,

For us, on the other hand, the explanation lies close at hand. If a triangle is divided into two others, these are no longer triangular through *one* triangular shape; in place of the one, two new accidental forms have appeared. Whence have they come? Both were potentially in what was actually a single triangle. It is no different in similar cases with other accidental forms, and also with the substantial forms of corruptible things. If a bodily substance falls apart into several bodies, there was in it actually only one, but potentially many substantial actualities, through which that plurality of bodies that have arisen is now actual. Whether the bodies arising are of the same kind as the destroyed one depends on the particular circumstances, but frequently it is the case, as, for example, when a stone is broken into several stones of the same kind; and so there is nothing at all remarkable if in certain cases two living beings of the same kind also come forth by division from one living body. Where in actuality there is *one* soul, there have been in [p. 50] potentiality two souls<sup>43)</sup>; for such a soul is, after all, nothing other than the substantial form of an ensouled matter; it is no substance by itself, it belongs essentially to the body and is posited as something distinct only by the abstracting intellect; and so a phenomenon is explained and easily falls under the general laws which, on other views of the soul, was strange in so high a degree.

open the veins of another, and neither will be capable of living on permanently; but who would wish to maintain that therefore all the signs of life which they give after that operation are also merely apparent? So with those explanations one has in fact only lent support to the opinion which believes it must deny soul and life to the animals—something that is surely said in defiance of all sound sense and can be called a partial skepticism.

42) De Anim. II, 2. §. 8. p. 413, b, 16. ὥσπερ γὰρ ἐπὶ τῶν φυτῶν ἔνια διαιρούμενα φαίνεται ζῶντα καὶ χωριζόμενα ἀπ᾽ ἀλλήλων, ὡς οὔσης τῆς ἐν τούτοις ψυχῆς ἐντελεχείᾳ μὲν μιᾶς ἐν ἑκάστῳ φυτῷ, δυνάμει δὲ πλειόνων, οὕτως ὁρῶμεν καὶ περὶ ἑτέρας διαφορὰς τῆς ψυχῆς συμβαῖνον ἐπὶ τῶν ἐντόμων ἐν τοῖς διατεμνομένοις· καὶ γὰρ αἴσθησιν ἑκάτερον τῶν μερῶν ἔχει καὶ κίνησιν τὴν κατὰ τόπον, εἰ δ᾽ αἴσθησιν καὶ φαντασίαν καὶ ὄρεξιν· ὅπου μὲν γὰρ αἴσθησις, καὶ λύπη τε καὶ ἡδονή, ὅπου δὲ ταῦτα, ἐξ ἀνάγκης καὶ ἐπιθυμία. Cf. ibid. I, 4. §. 18. p. 409, a, 9. and the passages cited in the preceding note.

6. With equal simplicity, however, we also see yet a further question resolved which has occupied minds very seriously and frequently; we mean the question of the manner in which the soul is joined in its body, and with it, into a unity.

If the soul is a form of the substantial matter, it is in it and one with it, just as accidental forms too are in accidental matters and one with them. As little, then, as any inquiry is still needed to show how the seal is one with the shaped wax, so little have we still to investigate the unity of the soul with its body and the manner of its existence in it; for there can, after all, be no more intimate union than that which exists between the potency and its actuality<sup>44)</sup>.

7. The soul is the actuality of the living. The soul of mortal beings is therefore not itself the living thing, but, since the latter is here composed of matter and form, only a constituent of that which lives<sup>45)</sup>.

43) See the preceding note.

44) De Anim. II, 1. §. 7. p. 412, b, 6. διὸ καὶ οὐ δεῖ ζητεῖν εἰ ἓν ἡ ψυχὴ καὶ τὸ σῶμα, ὥσπερ οὐδὲ τὸν κηρὸν καὶ τὸ σχῆμα, οὐδ᾽ ὅλως τὴν ἑκάστου ὕλην καὶ τὸ οὗ ὕλη· τὸ γὰρ ἓν καὶ τὸ εἶναι ἐπεὶ πλεοναχῶς λέγεται, τὸ κυρίως ἡ ἐντελέχειά ἐστιν. Cf. Metaph. Η, 6. p. 1045, b, 7. — fin.

45) In things consisting of matter and form, the form is not that which is, but only that in virtue of which that which is, is; the matter is here that which is, through the form (De Anim. II, 1. §. 2. p. 412, a, 6. λέγομεν δὴ γένος ἕν τι τῶν ὄντων τὴν οὐσίαν, ταύτης δὲ τὸ μὲν ὡς ὕλην, ὃ καθ᾽ αὐτὸ μὲν οὐκ ἔστι τόδε τι, ἕτερον δὲ μορφὴν καὶ εἶδος, καθ᾽ ἣν ἤδη λέγεται τόδε τι, καὶ τρίτον τὸ ἐκ τούτων). This is also expressed in the proposition that the form does not come to be (Metaph. Ζ, 8. p. 1033, b, 5—19., ibid. Η, 1. p. 1042, a, 30. Λ, 3. princ. and elsewhere); for since the form is not before the becoming (Metaph. Λ, 3. p. 1070, a, 21.), then, if in the becoming it is not that which comes to be, after the becoming too it is not that which has come to be, and hence not that which is, but only a constituent of that which is, and being with it and in it. Now since the being of ensouled substances is life (De Anim. II, 4. §. 4. p. 415, b, 12.), in them it is not the soul that lives, but the body through the soul. And as it is the body and not the soul that lives, so it is also the body and not the soul that exercises the vital activities; only it exercises them through the soul. Hence Aristotle says (De Anim. I, 4. §. 12.

[p. 51] But there are also incorruptible substances, that is, such as are free from matter, pure actualities, and life too can belong to them, since, as we shall yet see, the body has no share in some of those operations which we designated as vital functions, in particular in thinking. In such spiritual beings, then, since they are pure forms, soul and ensouled would coincide; the soul would not merely be that whereby the living thing lives, it would itself be that which lives<sup>46)</sup>.

Finally, a third case is still conceivable, namely, that a living being should be partly mortal and partly immortal, that is, that in the one part it should be pure form, but in the other, mortal part should consist of matter and form. For that it would be contradictory if a single substance united in itself such wholly dissimilar parts—this, I say, cannot be urged against the assumption of such a possibility, since what stands before our eyes as fact in numerous examples, belonging in particular to the plant and animal world, is more than sufficient to invalidate this objection. Plants and animals are indeed, as we have seen, living substances, and in all of them, but especially in the higher kinds, the great multiplicity and inequality of the organs comes clearly to light. The parts here, then, have the most opposed properties and the most diverse dispositions; different parts are mixed from different elements<sup>47)</sup>; but all these differences and oppositions do not cancel the unity of the living being, [p. 52] the unity of the substance. And so one cannot conclude from the outset, from the difference between the corruptible and the incorruptible, that their union into a single living substance is impossible.

III, 4. §. 4. p. 429, a, 27.), or that it has desire or aversion, fear, pleasure or pity, and the like (De Anim. I, 1. §. 9. f. p. 403, a, 5.)

46) See Metaph. Λ, 7 ff. — If Aristotle (Metaph. Λ, 7. p. 1072, b, 29.) calls God ζῷον ἀΐδιον ἄριστον (cf. ibid. Δ, 26. p. 1023, b, 32.), and correspondingly (De Anim. I, 1. §. 5. p. 402, b, 7.) also speaks of a ψυχὴ θεοῦ, then, since God is pure actuality (Metaph. Λ, 7. p. 1072, a, 25.), this ψυχὴ is itself at the same time the ἔμψυχον. Hence he calls him not merely ζῷον but also ζωή (ibid. b, 28.), and, after saying that life and eternity belong to the god, adds the words: “for this is the god” (where, to be sure, by life he seems to understand the vital activity rather than the being of the living, which in God, but not in other living substances, coincides with the latter).

47) Hence Aristotle argues against Empedocles, who, in declaring the soul to be the ratio of the mixture of the elements, is able to preserve neither the unity of the soul nor that of the living being (De Anim. I, 4. §. 6. p. 408, a, 14. ibid. 5. §. 12. p. 410, b, 10. cf. also the last §§. of the chap.). — In the animals that move themselves locally Aristotle recognizes self-moving substances, but the possibility of this self-motion is explained for him by the plurality and diversity of their parts. (Phys. VIII. 4. p. 254, b, 13. 27.)

In such a case, however, the soul will be neither by itself alone the whole living being, nor will it be able to be the mere principle animating the matter. Only in one part is it mixed with matter; in part, then, it animates the matter, while in part it is itself living and the subject of the vital functions. And therefore, if the bodily part of such a substance is corrupted, the soul will perish with it only in part, inasmuch as other forms become actual in the matter in its stead; that part of it which is free from matter will not be touched by this death, but will carry on, as a substance by itself, a life that will never end at all.

Now such a soul the human soul must be if, as is said, it does not meet its destruction in the death of the body; and it really is such a soul, since we shall show from the operations of man that he is only partly mortal. For the present it suffices us to have envisaged the possibility of such a partly mortal, partly immortal substance<sup>48)</sup>.

8. If we briefly sum up the result of the investigations so far, it is the following: The soul is the substantial entelechy of a living being; and since the living substances, as they are found on earth, are all corruptible at least in one part, and hence composed of matter and form, the soul of every earthly being is a substantial form which is, either wholly or in part, the actuality of an organic body that is potentially alive<sup>49)</sup>.

48) To this Aristotle too at first confines himself. Thus in the first book, e.g., De Anim. I, 1. §. 9 f. p. 403, a, 3., and in the second, e.g., 2. §. 9. p. 413, b, 24. The reason why he does so lies in the general rule, already mentioned, which he gives in De Anim. II, 4. §. 1. (p. 415, a, 16.). Although, therefore, in the first two books On the Soul he often touches on the question of the spirituality of the intellective part, and in some passages already lets his opinion be clearly recognized (cf. the following note), indeed in a few even states it with definiteness and prepares the way for its proof, yet only in the third book, where the investigation of the activity of the intellect has given him the necessary points of support, can he treat the subject exhaustively.

49) De Anim. II, 1. §. 12. p. 413, a, 4. ὅτι μὲν οὖν οὐκ ἔστιν ἡ ψυχὴ χωριστὴ τοῦ σώματος, ἢ μέρη τινὰ αὐτῆς, εἰ μεριστὴ πέφυκεν, οὐκ ἄδηλον· ἐνίων γὰρ ἡ ἐντελέχεια τῶν μερῶν ἐστὶν αὐτῶν. οὐ μὴν ἀλλ᾽ ἔνιά γε οὐθὲν κωλύει, διὰ τὸ μηθενὸς εἶναι σώματος ἐντελεχείας.

[p. 53]

#### b. Von den Theilen der menschlichen Seele. — b. On the Parts of the Human Soul.

9. We now turn to some questions which, standing in the closest connection with what has just been discussed, will mediate for us the transition from the consideration of the soul in general to the special psychological investigations of Aristotle.

First, we ask: Are there among the beings living on earth substances of different kinds, and hence also souls of different kinds, or do all have the same essential constitution<sup>50)</sup>?

Second: Can one and the same living being be ensouled by several souls, or is this case unthinkable<sup>51)</sup>? Finally,

Third: Can a plurality of parts be distinguished in the soul, and in what sense<sup>52)</sup>?

10. In order to arrive at the solution of the first question, we must return to the principle already stated above, that the differences of natures reveal themselves in the different kinds of natural operations. If, then, not everything that lives on earth shares in the same vital functions, this is for us sufficient proof that the living substances, and consequently also the souls, as their inner essence-determining principles, must be of unlike kinds<sup>53)</sup>.

Thus the souls of plants and of animals are plainly of different kinds; for if the animals have the vegetative functions of nutrition, growth and generation in common with the plants, the plants nevertheless have no share in the sensation that is found in animals. Further, some animals have only the sensation of touch and taste, while others unite with touch and taste the remaining activities of sense as well. Again, these animals too do not all share in local movement; and even among those living beings in which we find the vegetative powers and all the faculties of sensation joined with the capacity for local movement, a difference still shows itself, inasmuch as most of them lack the rational power of thinking, which appears to have been bestowed on man as that which distinguishes him before everything else that lives on earth [p. 54] <sup>54)</sup>. In all these gradations of life a plurality of species clearly makes itself known.

50) De Anim. I, 1. §. 4 f. p. 402, b, 1. (σκεπτέον) πότερον ὁμοειδὴς ἅπασα ψυχὴ ἢ οὔ· εἰ δὲ μὴ ὁμοειδής, πότερον εἴδει διαφέρουσιν ἢ γένει. κ. τ. λ.

51) De Anim. I, 1. §. 6. p. 402, b, 9. εἰ μὴ πολλαὶ ψυχαὶ ἀλλὰ μόρια. Ibid. II, 2. §. 7. p. 413, b, 13. πότερον δὲ τούτων (namely of the ἀρχὴ θρεπτική, αἰσθητική, διανοητική, κινητική) ἕκαστόν ἐστι ψυχὴ ἢ μόριον ψυχῆς.

52) De Anim. I, 1. §. 4. p. 402, b, 1. σκεπτέον δὲ καὶ εἰ μεριστὴ (ἡ ψυχὴ) ἢ ἀμερής.

53) Cf. De Anim. II, 2. §. 11. p. 413, b, 32. and ibid. §. 6. p. 413, b, 11.

11. Now since, however, every higher stage unites the powers of the lower with other, newly added faculties, the thought is not far off that the higher living being, just as it has within itself the vital functions of the lower and, besides these, others which the latter lacks, may well also have a similar principle of life, a soul like the soul of the lower living thing, and besides this another, which would be found only in it and would thus explain its difference from the first.

Nevertheless such an assumption is inadmissible and in conflict with the results our inquiry has already obtained. *One* matter can never be determined by several forms at the same time<sup>55)</sup>, *one* actual thing can never be actual through several actualities<sup>56)</sup>; if, then, as we have seen, the soul is the substantial actuality of the living thing, it is unthinkable that any ensouled thing should ever be ensouled and animated by more than *one* soul<sup>57)</sup>.

From the form the substance receives being and unity<sup>58)</sup>; so far, [p. 55] then, from its being permissible to assume, e.g., in man a plurality of souls which would be bound into a certain unity only by their common dwelling in the same body, we must rather say that it is the human soul which gives the parts of the body their unity; which is why we also see the body fall apart when in death it has been deprived of the soul<sup>59)</sup>.

54) De Anim. II, 2. §. 2 ff. p. 413, a, 22. ibid. 3. §. 1 f. p. 414, a, 29. ibid. §. 7. p. 415, a, 1. ibid. III, 12. p. 434, a, 22.

55) Phys. I, 7. p. 190, b, 28. ἓν δὲ τὸ εἶδος. Metaph. Λ, 4. p. 1070, b, 19. ἕκαστον τούτων (namely εἶδος, στέρησις, ὕλη) ἕτερον περὶ ἕκαστον γένος ἐστίν, οἷον ἐν χρώματι λευκόν, μέλαν, ἐπιφάνεια, φῶς, σκότος, ἀήρ· ἐκ δὲ τούτων ἡμέρα καὶ νύξ. Cf. ibid. Δ, 6. p. 1016, a, 24. and 28. p. 1024, b, 9. If, then, several forms were in the same matter at the same time, they would have to be of the same genus, as are, e.g., the form of the cube and the spherical shape, or the red and the green color. But if these could be united, then cold too could be united with heat, i.e. the cold could at the same time be warm, and in general the opposite could be the opposite; for one and the same law stands against all of this, the law on which the definition Metaph. Δ, 10. (p. 1018, a, 22.) is founded: ὅσα μὴ ἐνδέχεται ἅμα παρεῖναι τῷ ἀμφοῖν δεκτικῷ, ταῦτα ἀντικεῖσθαι λέγεται, ἢ αὐτὰ ἢ ἐξ ὧν ἐστίν. φαιὸν γὰρ καὶ λευκὸν ἅμα τῷ αὐτῷ οὐχ ὑπάρχει· διὸ ἐξ ὧν ἐστὶν ἀντίκειται. But by this all our thinking would lose its footing; for then nothing would any longer stand in the way of the true also being at the same time false, and the affirmed being at the same time denied.
