57) Since this stands in such evident contradiction with the fundamental doctrines of our philosopher, Aristotle, in De Anim. I, 1. §. 6. (p. 402, b, 9.) and likewise ibid. II, 2. §. 7. (p. 413, b, 13.), passes over it, only lightly touching on the question. He presupposes that no one who has been to some extent introduced into his doctrine will come to any serious doubt in this respect, although he himself reports that earlier philosophers, e.g. Empedocles, whose assertions he, in his usual manner, develops further into their consequences, had assumed a plurality of souls. De Anim. I, 2. §. 6. p. 404, b, 12.

58) De Anim. II, 4. §. 4. p. 415, b, 12. he says: τὸ γὰρ αἴτιον τοῦ εἶναι πᾶσιν ἡ

12. We come to the third question: Is there a plurality of parts of the soul, and in what sense, perhaps, can such a plurality be asserted?

Where there are parts, there is separation, or separability<sup>60)</sup>; this holds just as much of the logical as of the really distinct parts of a thing; for the generic concept is found without the specific difference, even if not the reverse<sup>61)</sup>, and if one divides a square by the diagonal, one actually separates the two triangles of which it had consisted. Now since in earthly living beings, which, as we said, all consist of matter and form, soul and ensouled do not mean the same thing, since here rather the soul itself is not a living thing but only the principle of a living thing (a proposition which even in man admits of an exception only to a limited extent), it is clear above all that, just as it is not the soul that lives, so too it is not the soul that comes to life and dies and dissolves into parts, but the ensouled<sup>62)</sup>.

But it is equally clear that, because the soul here is not a being by itself, but only an inner principle of that being, [p. 56] to which matter also belongs, only the ensouled comes to stand directly in the category of substance, while the soul can be ranked only reductively both under this highest generic concept and under the specific concepts<sup>63)</sup>. In consequence of this the soul has no independent definition, but can be defined only with regard to the other constituent, matter, as was already shown by the general definition of the soul given above<sup>64)</sup>. It follows, then, in a similar way as before, that one cannot speak of parts of the soul which would belong to it properly and in the first instance.

οὐσία. But the one is convertible with being. Metaph. Γ, 2. p. 1003, b, 22. τὸ ὂν καὶ τὸ ἓν ταὐτὸν καὶ μία φύσις, τῷ ἀκολουθεῖν ἀλλήλοις ὥσπερ ἀρχὴ καὶ αἴτιον. Cf. also Metaph. Β, 4. p. 999, b, 21.

59) Cf. De Anim. I, 5. §. 23 ff. — In §. 24. p. 411, b, 5. it says: λέγουσι δή τινες μεριστὴν αὐτήν, καὶ ἄλλο μὲν νοεῖν ἄλλο δὲ ἐπιθυμεῖν. τί οὖν δή ποτε συνέχει τὴν ψυχήν, εἰ μεριστὴ πέφυκεν; οὐ γὰρ δὴ τό γε σῶμα· δοκεῖ γὰρ τοὐναντίον μᾶλλον ἡ ψυχὴ τὸ σῶμα συνέχειν· ἐξελθούσης γοῦν διαπνεῖται καὶ σήπεται. εἰ οὖν ἕτερόν τι μίαν αὐτὴν ποιεῖ, ἐκεῖνο μάλιστ᾽ ἂν εἴη ψυχή. κ. τ. λ. At first sight the passage seems not to belong here, since Aristotle is speaking not of a plurality of souls but of parts of the soul. But the difference between the two questions is not great, for, as the course of the inquiry shows, he is speaking here of such parts as, if assumed, would let the soul subsist only as an ordered sum of different principles of life. Hence the question: πότερον ἓν (not ἁπλοῦν or ἀμερές) ἢ πολυμερές; — In De Anim. I, 5. §. 12. p. 410, b, 10., where he combats the opinion of Empedocles, he says: ἀπορήσειε δ᾽ ἄν τις καὶ τί ποτ᾽ ἐστὶ τὸ ἑνοποιοῦν αὐτά. ὕλῃ γὰρ ἔοικε τά γε στοιχεῖα, κυριώτατον δ᾽ ἐκεῖνο, τὸ συνέχον, ὅ τί ποτ᾽ ἐστίν· τῆς δὲ ψυχῆς εἶναί τι κρεῖττον καὶ ἄρχον ἀδύνατον.

60) Hence Aristotle asks, De Anim. II, 2. §. 7. p. 413, b, 14. εἰ μόριον, πότερον οὕτως ὥστ᾽ εἶναι χωριστὸν λόγῳ μόνον ἢ καὶ τόπῳ; cf. Metaph. Δ, 25. p. 1023, b, 12.

61) Categ. 3. p. 1, b, 16.

62) See above p. 50. n. 44.

Yet, to be sure, both these parts and the parts of the ensouled distinguished in the earlier way can in a certain manner be transferred to the soul, inasmuch as the soul is the form of the whole in all its parts. It determines the concept and all its differences, it animates the living thing and all the organs of the living thing; the eye dies with the animal and after its death can be called an eye only homonymously<sup>65)</sup>.

13. Thus we have already come to know two ways in which a plurality of parts can be distinguished in the soul. Both belong to it in relation to the parts of the substance whose form it is, and distinctions of a similar kind are possible in the case of all forms of material substances.

But the soul, as the form of a living substance, is at the same time the first principle of the vital functions; all the vital powers are rooted in it<sup>66)</sup>; and since the domain of the vital functions and vital powers also has parts, inasmuch as some are in a certain way separated or separable from others, we must, when we find several such parts united in one living being, also trace back the whole of the vital powers and all its parts to the soul; and with regard to these parts of its domain of powers we can then speak of parts of the soul, just as we can with regard to the parts of the ensouled being.

But this distinction of parts is a twofold one; the [p. 57] one rests on the physical, the other on the logical division of the substance—for of that plurality of parts which equals the number of powers, inasmuch as each power differs from the others in its being and concept<sup>67)</sup>, even though in reality it never appears separated from them, we do not wish to speak. Accordingly there remain for our consideration only the separation of powers inasmuch as some are found in this, others in that separable part of the substance (separation in different parts of the subject)<sup>68)</sup>, and the separation of powers inasmuch as not all living beings that participate in the one participate also in the others (separation in different species).

63) Cf. my treatise: Von der mannigfachen Bedeutung des Seienden nach Aristoteles, p. 138 ff.

64) See above p. 46. n. 37. and 38.

65) De Anim. I, 4. §. 8. p. 408, a, 25. ibid. II, 1. §. 9. p. 412, b, 17. Meteor. IV, 12. p. 390, a, 10. De Generat. Anim. II, 1. p. 734, b, 24. and p. 735, a, 6. — Only if one distinguishes substantial parts in the ensouled in the sense that one sets it as a whole over against its two inner principles, matter and form, can one of course in no way attribute this plurality of parts to the soul, since indeed the whole soul is conceived in abstraction from matter.

66) Cf. De Anim. II, 4. §. 6. p. 415, b, 21.

In the first respect, the separation of the powers of the immortal part of man from the rest will become important for us<sup>69)</sup>.

In the second respect we have already seen that the vegetative powers are separable from the sense of touch, this from the higher faculties of sensation, these in turn from locomotion, and finally this too from the rational powers. When Aristotle distinguishes parts of the soul, he does so for the most part with reference to these parts of its domain of powers that are separable in the species<sup>70)</sup>.

But the parts of the soul distinguished in this way coincide outright neither with the number of the powers of the soul nor with the parts of its definition, although, as we said, they rest on the latter. If every part of the whole of powers is grounded in a new difference of the essence, still, conversely, not every difference is bound up with a distinction in the number of powers<sup>71)</sup>. In many living substances the diversity of nature reveals itself only in the modifications of the same vital activities. Thus, e.g., the horse and the lion have the same vital functions, but they are [p. 58] modified; if both have generative power, still the one generates a horse and the other a lion.

67) De Anim. III, 10. §. 5. p. 433, a, 31. cf. ibid. II, 2. §. 10. p. 413, b, 29.

68) Aristotle uses for this the expression: separated as to place, χωριστὸν τόπῳ (De Anim. II, 2. §. 7. p. 413, b, 14. ibid. III, 2. §. 13. p. 427, a, 5., to which add ibid. III, 4. §. 4. p. 429, a, 27. καὶ εὖ δὴ οἱ λέγοντες τὴν ψυχὴν εἶναι *τόπον εἰδῶν*, πλὴν κ. τ. λ. Cf. also De Memor. et Remin. 2. p. 453, a, 24. De Somn. et Vigil. 2. p. 456, a, 23.) Related is another designation: μεγέθει χωριστόν. (De Anim. III, 9. §. 1. p. 432, a, 20. ibid. 10. §. 8. p. 433, b, 25. cf. ibid. II, 12. §. 2. p. 424, a, 26.), which, however, is applicable only to such powers as are mixed with the body.

69) A local separation of the sensitive powers from one another was assumed by Aristotle just as little as a separation of the vegetative from the sensitive. Cf. provisionally De Part. Animal. II, 1. p. 647, a, 24.

70) Because of their relation to the powers of the soul Aristotle sometimes even calls them δυνάμεις, e.g. De Anim. II, 4. §. 2. p. 415, a, 25. ibid. §. 9. p. 416, a, 19, 21.

71) Cf. Histor. Animal. I, 1. p. 486, a, 22.

Just as little does the soul, as the principle of each individual power, form a part of the soul of its own in this sense; for certain powers are absolutely inseparable, as, e.g., sensation and sensory appetite: whoever has the one necessarily has the other as well<sup>72)</sup>.

On the other hand, Aristotle had to maintain as particular parts of the soul of man a nutritive part and, since he believed that many plants and animals do not propagate themselves by generation, a generative part, further a part that feels through the sense of touch and taste, further one that perceives through the higher senses (more precisely, both a seeing and a hearing one)<sup>73)</sup>, further a part that moves locally, and finally a rationally thinking part; for these powers are found separately in different species of living beings.

14. Yet in no passage where Aristotle speaks of the parts of the soul has he enumerated them with such completeness: in some, evidently, so as not to become too prolix, since indeed anyone who knows the principle and sees it illustrated in the examples that Aristotle gives can easily supply the missing members of the series as well; in others, on the contrary, with the clearly apparent intention of a reduction to three principal parts, the vegetative, the sensitive and the intellective, of which the first is common to man with the animals and plants, the second with the animals, while the third, among all earthly beings, belongs exclusively to him.

Indeed it is evident at first glance that these parts are the principal parts of the soul, the proper stages of life, while the others, compared with them, can count only as subdivisions and complementary elevations of a stage already begun to its full height<sup>74)</sup>.

The plant, which acts unconsciously and only according to blind impulses, like lifeless substance, rises above the world of inorganic bodies only by the fact that in so doing it moves itself, which becomes possible for it through the plurality of its organs<sup>75)</sup>. It therefore belongs—a point which [p. 59] many have disputed—to the living beings, to be sure<sup>76)</sup>; but evidently there is a vast distance between its life and the life of the animals, which do not merely act according to blind impulses but strive with consciousness, inasmuch as they already have appetite in the proper sense, and can therefore also move themselves and other things with consciousness.

72) De Anim. II, 3. §. 2. p. 414, b, 1. εἰ δὲ τὸ αἰσθητικόν (ὑπάρχει), καὶ τὸ ὀρεκτικόν· ὄρεξις μὲν γὰρ ἐπιθυμία καὶ θυμὸς καὶ βούλησις, τὰ δὲ ζῷα πάντ᾽ ἔχουσι μίαν γε τῶν αἰσθήσεων, τὴν ἁφήν· ᾧ δ᾽ αἴσθησις ὑπάρχει, τούτῳ ἡδονή τε καὶ λύπη καὶ τὸ ἡδύ τε καὶ λυπηρόν, οἷς δὲ ταῦτα, καὶ ἡ ἐπιθυμία· τοῦ γὰρ ἡδέος ὄρεξις αὕτη. κ. τ. λ. Likewise loc. cit.

73) As is shown, e.g., by Metaph. Α, 1. (p. 980, b, 23.), where he denies hearing to bees.

74) Cf. De Anim. II, 4. §. 15. p. 416, b, 23. ibid. III, 1. §. 4. p. 425, a, 9. ibid. 9. §. 6. p. 432, b, 23. and 11. §. 1. p. 433, b, 31. De Part. Animal. II, 10. p. 655, b, 29. De Somn. et Vigil. 2. p. 455, a, 7.

75) Phys. VIII, 4. p. 255, a, 12. ἔτι πῶς ἐνδέχεται συνεχές τι καὶ συμφυὲς αὐτὸ

But far greater still is the distance that shows itself between animal life and the life of man, if indeed man, as Aristotle teaches, has within him spiritual powers whose subject is the soul alone<sup>77)</sup>. None of the sensory powers are yet powers of the soul in the full sense of the word; their principle, to be sure, is the soul, but their subject is the ensouled body<sup>78)</sup>. Hence the being of the animals is only the highest formation of the material, and their life ends as the body falls apart; not so man, who, along with his lower part, akin to the animals, unites at the same time something akin to God<sup>79)</sup>, something immortal<sup>80)</sup>, in the unity of his being.

Let us now consider, over against such differences of the stages of life, each of which rises so high above the other<sup>81)</sup> that it makes what lives appear lifeless beside it, the remaining distinctions among the parts of the soul named above; evidently they are of no significance, or at any rate of an incomparably lesser significance. For the intimate kinship of the higher and lower senses is evident, and locomotion stands just as close to certain activities of lower parts as higher and lower sensations stand to each other<sup>82)</sup>. For all sentient beings, even those which, like the plants, are fettered to *one* place, have certain voluntary movements [p. 60] <sup>83)</sup> of the limbs, which they use, e.g., for taking in food<sup>84)</sup>; and locomotion is related to these movements quite similarly to the way hearing or sight is related to touch and taste.

ἑαυτὸ κινεῖν; ᾗ γὰρ ἓν καὶ συνεχὲς μὴ ἁφῇ, ταύτῃ ἀπαθές· ἀλλ᾽ ᾗ κεχώρισται, ταύτῃ τὸ μὲν πέφυκε ποιεῖν τὸ δὲ πάσχειν. οὔτ᾽ ἄρα τούτων οὐθὲν αὐτὸ ἑαυτὸ κινεῖ (συμφυῆ γάρ), οὔτ᾽ ἄλλο συνεχὲς οὐδέν, ἀλλ᾽ ἀνάγκη διῃρῆσθαι τὸ κινοῦν ἐν ἑκάστῳ πρὸς τὸ κινούμενον, οἷον ἐπὶ τῶν ἀψύχων ὁρῶμεν, ὅταν κινῇ τι τῶν ἐμψύχων αὐτά. Cf. ibid. p. 254, b, 30. and VII, 1. p. 242, a, 14.

80) De Anim. I, 4. §. 13. p. 408, b, 18. ibid. II, 2. §. 9. p. 413, b, 24.

I, 23. p. 731, a, 33. αἴσθησιν γὰρ ἔχουσιν (τὰ ζῷα), ἡ δ᾽ αἴσθησις γνῶσίς τις. ταύτης δὲ τὸ τίμιον καὶ ἄτιμον πολὺ διαφέρει σκοποῦσι πρὸς φρόνησιν καὶ πρὸς τὸ τῶν ἀψύχων γένος. πρὸς μὲν γὰρ τὸ φρονεῖν ὥσπερ οὐδὲν εἶναι δοκεῖ τὸ κοινωνεῖν ἁφῆς καὶ γεύσεως μόνον, πρὸς δὲ ἀναισθησίαν βέλτιστον· ἀγαπητὸν γὰρ ἂν δόξειε καὶ ταύτης τυχεῖν τῆς γνώσεως, ἀλλὰ μὴ κεῖσθαι τεθνεὸς καὶ μὴ ὄν.

82) Cf. De Mot. Animal. 4. p. 700, a, 23.

Further, it becomes clear from yet another circumstance that those members inserted between the division into plant, animal and human soul do not open up a new domain of life, but only complete an earlier one. Every properly new stage of life must have powers that stand higher than all those already present at the earlier stage. But this is by no means the case with those intermediate members. One may acknowledge the sense of sight as something higher in comparison with touch<sup>85)</sup>; yet it in no case stands as high as the inner faculty of sensation<sup>86)</sup>, which, as we shall see in more detail later, perceives and distinguishes the sensations of the external senses; and this is found in all animals, even in those whose domain of sense is most restricted. Likewise locomotion certainly does not equal hearing and seeing in dignity, which is presumably why no one will pity a lame man more than a blind one<sup>87)</sup>; and all the more does it, too, rank below the aforementioned inner sense, the sensation of sensations, above which it would nevertheless have to come to stand high according to the order of the parts of the soul.

Finally, we see that, among the principal stages of life in all earthly living beings, every lower stage is a preliminary stage of the higher one which is never skipped; but with those subdivisions the same is not uniformly the case: there are animals that participate not in the higher senses, but yet in locomotion<sup>88)</sup>, and according to Aristotle there are not only plants but also animals without generative power.

Thus, then, the reduction which Aristotle carries out, of that larger number of parts of the human soul to three parts in the narrower sense, to the vegetative, sensitive and intellective, [p. 61] appears justified in every respect. Of them alone does he speak in the second book On the Generation of Animals, chap. 3., in the second book On the Soul, chap. 4. §. 1., and in the same work in the third book, chap. 9. §. 3.; further in the first book of the Nicomachean Ethics, chap. 6., and in the same work chap. 13.<sup>89)</sup>; and to them alone, if one wishes to be precise, must one also restrict the comparison of souls with the geometrical figures in the third chapter of the second book On the Soul, §. 6.<sup>90)</sup>, inasmuch as, just as in every succeeding figure the preceding one is contained, in the quadrilateral the triangle and in the pentagon the quadrilateral, so too in every succeeding stage of life the earlier one, in sensitive life the vegetative and in intellective life the sensitive, is necessarily found included in earthly living beings<sup>91)</sup><sup>92)</sup>; with those intermediate parts, which in a wider sense could *also* be called parts of the soul, this is indeed, as was said, not universally the case. Only insofar as these differences of the domain of powers, too, point to specific differences of the souls, to which the concept of soul is common as a generic concept, does the comparison with the figures hold fully for them as well, since all participate in common in the concept of soul<sup>93)</sup>; from which it then follows that, like the concept of figure, the concept of soul is the complete definition of no single soul, and that the task of investigating the more precise determinations still remains to be solved<sup>94)</sup>.

83) It is precisely these that serve us as the criterion of the sensitive capacity and animal nature of the living being.

84) De Anim. III, 13. §. 4. p. 435, b, 22. γεῦσιν δὲ (ἔχει τὸ ζῷον) διὰ τὸ ἡδὺ ἢ λυπηρόν, ἵνα αἰσθάνηται τὸ ἐν τροφῇ καὶ ἐπιθυμῇ καὶ κινῆται.

85) De Insomn. 2. p. 460, b, 21.

87) Metaph. Α, 1. p. 980, a, 24. οὐ γὰρ μόνον ἵνα πράττωμεν, ἀλλὰ καὶ μηθὲν μέλλοντες πράττειν τὸ ὁρᾶν αἱρούμεθα ἀντὶ πάντων ὡς εἰπεῖν τῶν ἄλλων. If, with Aristotle, we regard thinking as the highest among the spiritual activities, then analogy alone requires that among the vital functions of the ensouled body, too, we hold the cognitive activity to be the most excellent.

88) De Anim. III, 11. §. 1. p. 433, b, 31.

#### c. Von der mehrfachen Gattung der Kräfte in den höheren Seelentheilen. — c. On the Several Genera of Powers in the Higher Parts of the Soul.

15. In the quadrilateral the triangle is contained as a part and is joined with another part which is likewise a triangle, as [p. 62] one finds when one actually divides the quadrilateral into its parts by the diagonal. With these the pentagon, in which a diagonal can be drawn from the vertex of one angle to each of the two opposite ones, joins in the same way yet another triangle as the third part; and so the quadrilateral appears in a certain way as two figures, the pentagon as three. Now we find something similar in the souls of plants, of animals and of man. The soul of the animal is in a certain way two souls, while the soul of man is to be called three souls, namely (since what is at issue here is a division of the soul according to its domain of powers) three souls in power. The *one* human soul appears as the principle of a threefold domain of powers, each of which by itself alone comprises within itself everything that belongs to the acting of a substance.

III, 3. §. 6. p. 414, b, 28. παραπλησίως δ᾽ ἔχει τῷ περὶ τῶν σχημάτων καὶ τὰ κατὰ ψυχήν· ἀεὶ γὰρ ἐν τῷ ἐφεξῆς ὑπάρχει δυνάμει τὸ πρότερον ἐπί τε τῶν σχημάτων καὶ ἐπὶ τῶν ἐμψύχων, οἷον ἐν τετραγώνῳ μὲν τρίγωνον, ἐν αἰσθητικῷ δὲ τὸ θρεπτικόν. ὥστε καὶ καθ᾽ ἕκαστον ζητητέον, τίς ἑκάστου ψυχή, οἷον τίς φυτοῦ καὶ τίς ἀνθρώπου ἢ θηρίου.

93) De Anim. II, 3. §. 5. p. 414, b, 19. δῆλον οὖν ὅτι τὸν αὐτὸν τρόπον εἷς ἂν εἴη λόγος ψυχῆς τε καὶ σχήματος· οὔτε γὰρ ἐκεῖ σχῆμα παρὰ τὸ τρίγωνόν ἐστι καὶ τὰ ἐφεξῆς, οὔτ᾽ ἐνταῦθα ψυχὴ παρὰ τὰς εἰρημένας. γένοιτο δ᾽ ἂν καὶ ἐπὶ τῶν σχημάτων λόγος κοινός, ὃς ἐφαρμόσει μὲν πᾶσιν, ἴδιος δ᾽ οὐδενὸς ἔσται σχήματος. ὁμοίως δὲ καὶ ἐπὶ ταῖς εἰρημέναις ψυχαῖς.

94) De Anim. II, 3. §. 5. p. 414, b, 25. and §. 6. b, 32.

This requires a more detailed explanation.

It is a principle frequently stated by Aristotle that everything that comes to be comes to be from something synonymous<sup>95)</sup>. This law holds for art and nature alike; for one man, he says, generates another man, and the house, which as to its idea is in the intellect of the builder, becomes the principle of the building to be erected in the external world<sup>96)</sup>. Even where something happens by fortune or arises by chance, although the one of them is a privation of art, the other a privation of the natural cause<sup>97)</sup>, we still find the same law in force, at least to a certain extent<sup>98)</sup>. Aristotle also expresses it in another formulation by saying that like brings forth like<sup>99)</sup>, or again that potentiality precedes in the individual, but actuality precedes absolutely<sup>100)</sup>.

A second law of acting is that all acting proceeds from a striving<sup>101)</sup>, whether this striving be necessarily connected with the form on which it follows—as, e.g., a warm body by virtue of its warmth necessarily has the inclination to warm a colder one, which is why we have only to bring the two into contact with each other [p. 63] in order to be sure of success—or whether the striving be an appetite that turns freely in opposite directions<sup>102)</sup>, as it is possible, e.g., that of two physicians who both have the same concept of health, the one wishes to bring about recovery, the other sickness<sup>103)</sup>.

95) Metaph. Λ, 3. p. 1070, a, 4. ἑκάστη ἐκ συνωνύμου γίγνεται οὐσία.

96) Metaph. Λ, 4. p. 1070, b, 30.

97) Metaph. Λ, 3. p. 1070, a, 7. ἡ μὲν οὖν τέχνη ἀρχὴ ἐν ἄλλῳ, ἡ δὲ φύσις ἀρχὴ ἐν αὐτῷ· ἄνθρωπος γὰρ ἄνθρωπον γεννᾷ· αἱ δὲ λοιπαὶ αἰτίαι (the τύχη and the αὐτόματον) στερήσεις τούτων.

98) Metaph. Z, 7. and 9. cf. below Part IV, No. 32. toward the middle.

99) De Anim. II, 5. p. 417, a, 20. and §. 7. p. 418, a, 4.

100) Metaph. Θ, 8. p. 1049, b, 17. and p. 1050, b, 1. De Anim. III, 7. §. 1. p. 431, a, 2.

101) Unconscious nature, too, has a striving; see, e.g., De Anim. II, 4. §. 2. p. 415, b, 1. De Generat. et Corrupt. II, 10. p. 336, b, 27. and in many other places.

This striving, in which we must recognize the proximate principle of the effect<sup>104)</sup>, stems from that likeness by virtue of which, as was remarked, what is to be effected preexists in the agent: like has an inclination toward like; and therefore we must likewise recognize in that likeness a principle, and indeed an earlier principle, of becoming. The efficient cause moves only by being moved by the effect insofar as this preexists, according to likeness, in the agent, i.e. by the end. Intellect and nature, says Aristotle, act for the sake of an end<sup>105)</sup>; and when, in the books of the Physics and in the first book of the Metaphysics and in other places, he distinguishes four principles of becoming—matter, form, efficient and final cause—he grants the first place to the final cause, or the end.

From these two causes, now, the effect itself proceeds, which consists in this, that what undergoes actually receives the form which it had within it potentially; for the effect is in what undergoes, not in the agent, at least not in the agent as such<sup>106)</sup>. But by the fact that what had the faculty of undergoing actually becomes undergoing, that which had the faculty of acting also becomes actually acting; the one and selfsame actuality that is received in the passive faculty also actualizes the active one that stands over against it, and, while it is the undergoing of the one, belongs at the same time to the other as its acting<sup>107)</sup>.

Let us now look back to the point from which we started. We said that through the division of the human powers of the soul [p. 64] into vegetative, sensitive and intellective, the domain of powers is divided in such a way that in each part by itself alone everything is found that belongs to the acting of a substance, so that the human soul is virtually three souls. By this we meant nothing other than that to each of the three parts, the vegetative, the sensitive and the intellective, there belongs not merely a particular acting, but also a particular inclination to act and a particular manner of participation in that likeness which the agent must have with what is to be effected. To be sure, the sensitive soul depends in its activity on the vegetative, and the intellective on the sensitive<sup>108)</sup>, inasmuch as the vegetative builds the organs for the sensitive, and, as we shall yet see, the intellective too, so long as it is united with the body, cannot operate without simultaneous activity of the sensitive; to be sure, conversely, the higher soul also intervenes in many ways determiningly in the work of the lower, and stands in connection with the external world only by means of it—the intellective through its influence on the work of the sensitive, and the latter through the movement of the organs of the body, which are the work of the vegetative: but if the unity of human life reveals itself in this, it nevertheless does not exclude that at each new stage of life a wholly new realm of living efficacy opens up, insofar as to each higher part of the soul there belongs not merely a new manner of acting but also of striving<sup>109)</sup>, which striving at the same time proceeds from a form by virtue of which what is to be effected preexists in the agent in a new manner.

107) Phys. III, 3. p. 202, a, 13. φανερόν, ὅτι ἐστὶν ἡ κίνησις ἐν τῷ κινητῷ· ἐντελέχεια γάρ ἐστι τούτου, καὶ ὑπὸ τοῦ κινητικοῦ. καὶ ἡ τοῦ κινητικοῦ δὲ ἐνέργεια οὐκ ἄλλη ἐστίν· δεῖ μὲν γὰρ εἶναι ἐντελέχειαν ἀμφοῖν· κινητικὸν μὲν γάρ ἐστι τῷ δύνασθαι, κινοῦν δὲ τῷ ἐνεργεῖν· ἀλλ᾽ ἔστιν ἐνεργητικὸν τοῦ κινητοῦ, ὥστε ὁμοίως μία ἡ ἀμφοῖν ἐνέργεια ὥσπερ τὸ αὐτὸ διάστημα ἓν πρὸς δύο καὶ δύο πρὸς ἕν, καὶ τὸ ἄναντες καὶ τὸ κάταντες· ταῦτα γὰρ ἓν μέν ἐστιν, ὁ μέντοι λόγος οὐχ εἷς. ὁμοίως δὲ καὶ ἐπὶ τοῦ κινοῦντος καὶ κινουμένου. κ. τ. λ. De Anim. III, 2. p. 425, b, 25. see also the preceding note.

Let us consider the vegetative part. Like the acting of lifeless [p. 65] bodies, so the whole vegetative vital activity, too, still follows a blind impulse which stems from the nature and constitution of the human body, just as it is also directed to nothing other than the body’s preservation and perfection in the individual and the species<sup>110)</sup>. The form by which what acts is like what is effected is here in the agent in the same manner in which it is, after the undergoing, in that which experiences the effect; and the striving is an unconscious natural inclination. Quite differently, by contrast, does the sensitive part move, strive and participate in the form of what is to be effected. An animal, e.g., sees the food to be devoured and has the presentation of devouring; it desires to devour it, and actually devours it. Here not only is the moving something different; the inclination too from which it proceeds is, as conscious striving, wholly different from the inclination from which the efficacy of plants and of lifeless bodies stems; and the form of that toward which the movement is directed is in the agent as a presentation. Finally, the intellective part in turn differs from the sensitive and rises above it in its activity in a threefold respect, as the sensitive rises above the vegetative. The universal concept of the house, which exists in the intellect of the builder, awakens the inclination of his will, which is unlike not only the vegetative impulses but also the sensory affections, and stands in the same relation to them as thoughts to sensory presentations; and action follows on willing: the intellective part moves the sensitive and, through its mediation<sup>111)</sup>, the limbs of the body, so that the building is erected according to the plan.

108) The very fact that universally the intellective soul is not found without the sensitive, nor the sensitive without the vegetative, indicates that a relation of dependence of the higher on the lower must exist. The lower part must be the precondition and preparatory disposition for the higher. Indeed, for this reason, even in one and the same being the lower soul precedes the higher in *time*; it is the earlier in *coming to be*. But precisely for this reason the higher is the earlier by *nature* (cf. Metaph. Μ, 2. p. 1077, a, 26.), and hence, conversely, the lower is also dependent on the higher as on its end (De Generat. Animal. II, 3. p. 736, b, 4.). The same holds of the operations. Sensation first becomes possible when the vegetative soul has formed the organs; irregular formations of the organs make sensation impossible. Thinking first becomes possible when there are sensory presentations in the imagination; disturbances of the images of the imagination can make thinking impossible. Conversely, the sensitive soul rules over the work of the vegetative, the body, and moves it according to its desire, while the intellective rules, at least according to the natural order, over the sensory presentations and through them over the desires and movements. Cf. provisionally Polit. I, 3. p. 1254, a, 34.

109) De Anim. III, 9. §. 3. p. 432, b, 6. εἰ δὲ τρία ἡ ψυχή, ἐν ἑκάστῳ ἔσται ὄρεξις.

16. Now since in the vegetative part the likeness of that toward which its acting is directed is already found by nature, and inheres in it in the same manner in which the forms from which their efficacy proceeds are also in lifeless things; and since, further, the striving of the vegetative part is likewise nothing other than that blind natural impulse which also results in the motions of lifeless substances: it is clear that all the vegetative faculties of the soul must be moving powers, whose activity consists in producing something like the agent.

It will be otherwise, by contrast, with the sensitive and intellective part. Neither the sensory presentations nor the ideas<sup>112)</sup> adhere by nature in the cognizing being; and since the inclination has the form already received, and thus appetite has cognition, [p. 66] as its presupposition, it is evident that the sensitive and intellective soul are not only in and for themselves bare of all forms, but that they also cannot originally have any actual appetite within them. Hence it will be necessary to assume for both higher parts of the soul, besides moving powers, also passive faculties, and indeed of two genera: the one in order to apprehend the likeness of what is to be effected, the other in order to strive toward it; so that for each of them we shall have to distinguish three genera of powers of the soul: first, form-apprehending (apprehensive) faculties, which are the potentiality of presentations; second, appetitive ones, which are the potentiality of strivings; and third, moving faculties<sup>113)</sup>. Thus we see the life of the soul here much richer in its powers and much more manifold in the unfolding of its activity.

110) Cf. De Anim. II, 4. §. 2. p. 415, a, 23.

111) On which see below Part IV, no. 28.

112) On which see below Part IV, no. 2.

It is true that, despite all these faculties, the sensitive and intellective soul seem in a certain way less well equipped for their acting than the vegetative, to which the principles of acting are already actually given by nature. But precisely this lack becomes an advantage<sup>114)</sup>. Just as nature has given the animals, ready-made, all the weapons and tools they need, while to man it granted nothing but the hands, and in them only the possibility of the tools necessary for meeting his needs; but just as it did not therefore treat him in stepmotherly fashion, since precisely from this there grows for him that multiplicity and variety of instruments which enables him to accomplish things in a far more extended sphere: so too the sensitive and intellective parts, because they at first lack all sensible and intelligible images and have only the potentiality of sense perception and of thinking, are only apparently at a disadvantage compared with the vegetative soul; for in these faculties they possess potentially not merely one form or another but *all* sensory and spiritual forms<sup>115)</sup>, so that, just as the hand can be called the tool of tools, the sensitive and intellective soul can be called the form of forms<sup>116)</sup>.

113) We shall see later how the disposition to move is one and the same with the faculty of appetite, since actual appetite, when it is directed to something practical, itself becomes the principle of motion. But the faculty of moving and the faculty of desiring nevertheless remain distinct, because their acts are of different genera.

114) Cf. below Part IV, no. 2. and n. 14. and 15.

115) De Anim. III, 8. §. 1. p. 431, b, 21. ἡ ψυχὴ τὰ ὄντα πώς ἐστι πάντα. ἢ γὰρ αἰσθητὰ τὰ ὄντα ἢ νοητά, ἔστι δ᾽ ἡ ἐπιστήμη μὲν τὰ ἐπιστητά πως, ἡ δ᾽ αἴσθησις τὰ αἰσθητά.

116) De Anim. III, 8. §. 2. p. 432, a, 1. ὥστε ἡ ψυχὴ ὥσπερ ἡ χείρ ἐστιν· καὶ γὰρ ἡ χεὶρ ὄργανόν ἐστιν ὀργάνων, καὶ ὁ νοῦς εἶδος εἰδῶν καὶ ἡ αἴσθησις εἶδος αἰσθητῶν. Cf.

[p. 67] Likewise, when in the sensitive and intellective parts the appetitive faculty, as a mere potentiality of strivings, takes the place of the inclination of the vegetative part, which is determined by the natural form and always actual, a superabundant compensation is nevertheless offered. There the striving is uniform; here, on the other hand, we have a great manifoldness of appetite, which in the rational part, as we shall yet see<sup>117)</sup>, has so completely broken all bonds of natural determination that in one and the same case it can turn with freedom to the opposite side.

17. We have distinguished three genera of powers of the soul not only in the sensitive but also in the intellective part. But it might seem as though in doing so we had departed from the doctrine of Aristotle, since in the third chapter of the second book On the Soul he enumerates only five genera of faculties of the soul, namely that of the nutritive, that of the appetitive, that of the sensing, that of the locally moving, and finally that of the rationally thinking faculties<sup>118)</sup>. Now if the first genus belongs to the vegetative part and the three following to the sensitive, then evidently only a single genus remains for the intellective part, and this seems to belong to the form-apprehending powers, since the thinking power of the human mind, the intellect, is according to Aristotle a form-receiving faculty, which is related to the intelligible forms, the ideas, as sense is to the sensible forms<sup>119)</sup>.

But this passage, in which Aristotle, as he expressly remarks, has regard only to what has already been discussed [p. 68] <sup>120)</sup> (he has, however, not yet spoken at all of the spiritual part, or has touched on it only here and there with a few anticipatory words), cannot be authoritative for us in such a way. Indeed, if the intellect were not a spiritual faculty and not distinct in subject from the senses, it would not be necessary to assume a special genus of appetitive faculties for it, and likewise we would in this case be spared the assumption of a new genus of moving powers; for neither do we need such assumptions for each of the senses individually, and the intellect would indeed in this case be only one sense among other senses. It is otherwise, on the other hand, if, as Aristotle shows in the later discussions<sup>121)</sup>, the intellect is something supersensible; for from this there follows most clearly the necessity of a supersensible striving power<sup>122)</sup>, to which, similarly as to sensitive appetite, a corresponding movement will then also be attached<sup>123)</sup>. Had Aristotle denied these faculties to the spiritual part, he would at the same time have had to deny every influence of the intellect on the bodily part of man; far removed from this, he rather granted it so significant an efficacy with respect to the body that he even made this efficacy the principle of a division, distinguishing a domain of the bodily capacities that is subject to the intellect and one that withdraws from its rule<sup>124)</sup>. Later we shall become acquainted with the numerous passages<sup>125)</sup> in which his view makes itself known in the most indubitable way, so that in the face of them no one, presumably, will wish to appeal to our passage, in which we may think of the intellective appetite<sup>126)</sup> as also included, still undistinguished, in the ὀρεκτικόν, and the spiritually moving power—at least in a certain measure, and insofar as it indirectly becomes decisive for local movement<sup>127)</sup>—as also included in the κινητικὸν κατὰ τόπον.

De Part. Animal. IV, 10. p. 687, a, 16. οὐ διὰ τὰς χεῖράς ἐστιν ὁ ἄνθρωπος φρονιμώτατος, ἀλλὰ διὰ τὸ φρονιμώτατον εἶναι τῶν ζῴων ἔχει χεῖρας. ὁ γὰρ φρονιμώτατος πλείστοις ἂν ὀργάνοις ἐχρήσατο καλῶς, ἡ δὲ χεὶρ ἔοικεν εἶναι οὐχ ἓν ὄργανον ἀλλὰ πολλά· ἔστι ὡσπερεὶ ὄργανον πρὸ ὀργάνων. τῷ οὖν πλείστας δυναμένῳ δέξασθαι τέχνας τὸ ἐπὶ πλεῖστον τῶν ὀργάνων χρήσιμον τὴν χεῖρα ἀποδέδωκεν ἡ φύσις. ἀλλ᾽ οἱ λέγοντες ὡς συνέστηκεν οὐ καλῶς ὁ ἄνθρωπος ἀλλὰ χείριστα τῶν ζῴων (ἀνυπόδητόν τε γὰρ αὐτὸν εἶναί φασι καὶ γυμνὸν καὶ οὐκ ἔχοντα ὅπλον πρὸς τὴν ἀλκήν) οὐκ ὀρθῶς λέγουσιν. τὰ μὲν γὰρ ἄλλα μίαν ἔχει βοήθειαν, καὶ μεταβάλλεσθαι ἀντὶ ταύτης ἑτέραν οὐκ ἔστιν, ἀλλ᾽ ἀναγκαῖον ὥσπερ ὑποδεδεμένον ἀεὶ καθεύδειν καὶ πάντα πράττειν, καὶ τὴν περὶ τὸ σῶμα ἀλεώραν μηδέποτε καταθέσθαι, μηδὲ μεταβάλλεσθαι ὃ δὴ ἐτύγχανεν ὅπλον ἔχων. τῷ δὲ ἀνθρώπῳ τάς τε βοηθείας πολλὰς ἔχειν, καὶ ταύτας ἀεὶ ἔξεστι μεταβάλλειν, ἔτι δ᾽ ὅπλον οἷον ἂν βούληται καὶ ὅπου ἂν βούληται ἔχειν. ἡ γὰρ χεὶρ καὶ ὄνυξ καὶ χηλὴ καὶ κέρας γίνεται καὶ δόρυ καὶ ξίφος καὶ ἄλλο ὁποιονοῦν ὅπλον καὶ ὄργανον· πάντα γὰρ ἔσται ταῦτα διὰ τὸ πάντα δύνασθαι λαμβάνειν καὶ ἔχειν.

117) Part III, no. 22.

118) De Anim. II, 3. §. 1. p. 414, a, 31. δυνάμεις δ᾽ εἴπομεν θρεπτικόν, ὀρεκτικόν, αἰσθητικόν, κινητικόν κατὰ τόπον, διανοητικόν.

119) De Anim. III, 4. §. 3. p. 429, a, 15. see below Part IV, no. 2 ff.

18. Indeed, far from its being possible that the spiritual part should lack an analogue of the three genera of the sensitive, we must rather suppose that a fourth genus of powers was ascribed to it by Aristotle besides. For according to his doctrine the relation which exists between the vegetative and the sensitive part, and that which exists between the sensitive and the intellective part, is not the same; rather, between the two latter the [p. 69] distance is greater, inasmuch as, as we have already remarked<sup>128)</sup>, they are separate not merely in species but also in subject, whereas the vegetative soul forms a unity with the sensitive in this respect; both are mixed with the body, and their seat in the body is one and the same. If, therefore, after an animal has been cut apart both pieces go on living, then *both* are animals, i.e. both have the vegetative and the sensitive capacities; it is not the case that the one has the vegetative and the other the sensitive; and in general no one can really separate the vegetative and the sensitive part of an animal, whereas the intellective part of man is indeed separable from the others and in death is really detached from them<sup>129)</sup>.

120) He says: δυνάμεις δ᾽ *εἴπομεν* κ. τ. λ. see above p. 67. n. 118.

121) See below Part IV, no. 4 ff.

122) See ibid. no. 24.

123) See ibid. no. 25.

124) Eth. Nicom. I, 13.

125) Part IV, no. 23.

126) De Anim. II, 3. §. 2. p. 414, b, 2. he designates, besides ἐπιθυμία and θυμός, βούλησις too as included under ὄρεξις.

127) Cf. De Anim. III, 10.

Hence it is surely evident that, if an analogy exists between the parts of the soul, it will exist more perfectly between the intellective part and the vegetative-sensitive taken together as one than between the intellective and the sensitive, or between the sensitive and the vegetative part; and since the vegetative-sensitive part comprises four genera of powers, of which one is form-apprehending, another appetitive, to which are added two acting ones, namely one that moves consciously and one that moves unconsciously, we have to expect in the intellective part likewise four genera of powers as analogues of them. Three of these have already been mentioned; it therefore remains to add something about the spiritual power that acts unconsciously.

19. But may we really, merely on the ground of the analogy with the bodily part of the ensouled being, infer such a fourth power of the spiritual part? — A closer consideration of the circumstances will show that this assumption is no vague and arbitrary hypothesis.

If we look at the whole world of things that immediately surround the human mind, we see that here every substance exerts an unconscious influence on others. One body displaces the other body, or presses upon it, or draws it upward, or warms it, or exerts some other and perhaps manifold unconscious influence upon it. On this constant unconscious efficacy rests the whole unified coordination of the corporeal world; and all the more, naturally, when *one* substance, as is the case with organic bodies, comprises a plurality of distinct parts, there exists between these part-substances such an [p. 70] unconscious causal relation. Now would it not be strange if, in the case of the spiritual part of the soul, which is indeed also substance, and belongs, namely as a part-substance, to the essence of man, this general law were to suffer an exception? — Or is there perhaps something contained in the concept of spirit that contradicts such unconscious acting? This at least we would deny with certainty. For that the spirit can exert an influence on the bodily with *consciousness* evidently does not exclude the possibility of an *unconscious* influence; only the assertion that one and the same effect proceeds from it at once consciously and unconsciously would be contradictory. And it serves to confirm this that that organ, too, which is the seat of sensation—even if it may not also be, as Aristotle taught, the center of sensitive and vegetative life—at any rate, since it is indeed corporeal, possesses besides its conscious action also an unconscious action upon the remaining parts of the body.

128) See above no. 13. and n. 69. as well as no. 14. and n. 77 ff.

129) De Anim. II, 2. §. 9. p. 413, b, 25. (ὁ νοῦς) ἔοικε ψυχῆς γένος ἕτερον εἶναι, καὶ τοῦτο μόνον ἐνδέχεται χωρίζεσθαι, καθάπερ τὸ ἀΐδιον τοῦ φθαρτοῦ. τὰ δὲ λοιπὰ μόρια τῆς ψυχῆς φανερὸν ἐκ τούτων ὅτι οὐκ ἔστι χωριστά, καθάπερ τινές φασιν· τῷ δὲ λόγῳ ὅτι ἕτερα, φανερόν.

Yet perhaps what has been said does not yet suffice to make the existence of an unconscious influence of the spiritual upon the bodily man probable to everyone. That in the earthly world every body acts unconsciously on other bodies surrounding it is surely undeniable, as it in turn also undergoes such an influence from them; but that something spiritual, however closely it may be connected with something corporeal, should act unconsciously upon it might nevertheless seem doubtful to someone, because, he could say, conversely, too, the corporeal with its unconscious drives is unable to produce any effect in the spirit. For in fact one can conceive neither that the body warms the spirit, nor that it cools it, nor that it illuminates it, or alters it in any other way through its qualities, as it would a corporeal substance.

But however that may be, certainly no one, even if he is not at first willing to grant that an *unconscious* action upon the bodily part belongs to the spiritual part, will deny that it acts upon it in *some way or other*<sup>130)</sup>; and indeed we have also [p. 71] already pointed, at least in general, to the phenomena of outward life in which the governing influence of a spiritual power acting with consciousness cannot be mistaken. What, now, if precisely from this the necessity of an unconscious influence of the spiritual upon the bodily should result? The conscious acting of the spiritual presupposes willing and thinking. But these two, like sensing and sensitive appetite, are at first present in the soul only potentially, and therefore require, in order to become actual, that, as upon the sensing organ, so upon *their* subject too, something else act so as to change it. But what should this other be, if not the bodily part of man? And if, therefore, the latter, as a body, does not of itself and in consequence of its natural qualities have a drive to act upon the spiritual, it is evident that it must have received the impulse to do so from something else, and indeed from something spiritual; and this will have been the spiritual part of man itself, for surely no one will wish, without an entirely compelling reason, to call upon a foreign spiritual substance for help like a deus ex machina.

130) Otherwise the union of the two would appear purposeless. Cf. De Anim. III, 12. §. 4. p. 434, b, 3. — It may be remarked in passing that we cannot agree with Torstrik when he declares the words ‘ἀλλὰ μὴν οὐδὲ ἀγέννητον’ to be interpolated (they are probably directed against Plato, cf. De Anim. I, 3. §. 11 ff. p. 406, b, 25. esp. §. 19.); for evidently Aristotle’s argument is conclusive not merely for earthly beings but for all corporeal beings, and he could not at all restrict it arbitrarily to the one or the other. But how his doctrine of the celestial spheres and of the spirits that move them is to be reconciled with this, and what in general, according to him, the relation of the one and the other to each other is, cannot be investigated in this place.

Only *one* evasion would perhaps still remain open, namely if one were to assume that the bodily part of man strives for an effect in the spiritual part not, indeed, with an unconscious drive, but with its sensitive appetite. — But this assumption would evidently be the most absurd of all; for since whoever desires something with consciousness must also have grasped it with his presentation, we would, according to this hypothesis, have a sensory presentation of something spiritual, which is certainly unthinkable. The sensitive part, then, has no *conscious* striving to act upon the spiritual; and if, therefore, the body, as was quite rightly remarked, also has no innate drive to act upon it *unconsciously*, then, far from its following that the lack of such a drive would thereby become probable for the spiritual part as well, precisely from this there follows most clearly the necessity of a spiritual power that unconsciously asserts its influence in the bodily part<sup>131)</sup>.

131) In the same proportion in which the acting of an earthly living being is more perfect, it appears less perfectly equipped for it by nature. We have already called attention earlier to the fact that in the vegetative operations the likeness of what is to be effected is actually given from the very beginning, whereas in the conscious acting of the sensitive and intellective soul the forms at which the acting is directed are at first found in the agent only potentially. But now a similar difference shows itself again between the power acting with sensitive consciousness and that acting with intellective consciousness; the latter, which is evidently far exalted above the former, nevertheless appears originally less well equipped for acting

[p. 72] On closer consideration one will find nothing incongruous in the one-sidedness of the relation; for the spirit is in its substance pure actuality, whereas the body is mixed with substantial potentiality, and it is entirely in keeping with this if, where the spiritual and the bodily touch each other in a causal connection, the spirit appears more active, the body more passive, since the principle of acting is an actuality, the principle of being affected a potentiality.

The acting principle, says Aristotle<sup>132)</sup>, surpasses the passive one in dignity; if, therefore, in any case a *reciprocal intercourse* between spirit and body takes place, it is nevertheless always probable from the outset, not to say necessary, that the priority of acting and its proper principle will be found in the spiritual part.

If the principle were in the bodily part of man, we would be compelled to assume in it, besides the four genera of vital powers already mentioned, a fifth and highest genus as well, namely that by virtue of which it moved the spiritual, while on the side of the spiritual part only three genera would be found; but as it is, we have in each part a fourfold kind of powers, [p. 73] which correspond to each other in the most perfect analogy. Both parts have form-apprehending, both have appetitive, both have consciously and unconsciously moving faculties.

than the other. If the power acting with sensation still lacks the likeness of what is to be effected, nature has nevertheless given the living being, in the objects, the corresponding principles through which the sensible forms that are potentially in it become actual. But in the case of the faculty acting with spiritual cognition, not even this is so; for since the spirit becomes cognizing through the influence of the bodily part, and, as we shall yet see, the first spiritual cognizing relates to something corporeal, the inequality between subject and object is greater here; the object can no longer exert an influence upon the intellective soul by its own power, and so the spiritual part’s own efficacy must first make it capable of becoming the principle of its thoughts. We can compare this relation of a higher perfection which nevertheless at the same time stands originally farther from its completion with a phenomenon in the vegetative life of plants, animals and men. To the plant nourishment is given by nature; the animal, and in particular the higher species of animals, must seek it, but they find it ready to hand according to need; man, finally, must first obtain and prepare it by his own labor; and yet precisely the human body, which is renewed and produced from the nutritive matter, is indisputably the most perfect of all organisms, and thus the vegetative power of man the noblest and highest. In a similar way, then, as we said, what acts unconsciously also has by nature the likeness by virtue of which what is effected must preexist in the agent; what is sensitively conscious, on the other hand, must first receive it, and the intellective must even first form it through its own activity, by making what is potentially intelligible actually intelligible.

132) De Anim. III, 5. §. 2. p. 430, a, 18. ἀεὶ γὰρ τιμιώτερον τὸ ποιοῦν τοῦ πάσχοντος καὶ ἡ ἀρχὴ τῆς ὕλης.

Thus it has become more and more apparent how the assumption of a spiritual power acting unconsciously, analogous to the unconscious powers of the bodily part, was indeed a necessity for Aristotle. It is this power of which he speaks in the fifth chapter of the third book On the Soul, for it is none other than that νοῦς ποιητικός which is active before all thinking, since it is the acting principle of spiritual cognition. The proof of this must be reserved for later discussions<sup>133)</sup>; for the present we only wished to call attention to the fact that the harmonious construction of the Aristotelian doctrine of the soul required such a fourth genus of spiritual faculties.

133) Part IV, n. 32.

[scan 105]

### Zweiter Theil. — Second Part.

### Von den Seelentheilen im Einzelnen und zuerst von der vegetativen Seele. — On the Parts of the Soul in Particular, and First on the Vegetative Soul.

1. We have treated of the soul and of the parts of the soul in general; we now pass on to the particular consideration of the individual parts. It will confirm our conjectures, justify anticipatory statements, and in particular will first supply the foundation for everything that related to the as yet unproven spirituality of the intellective part.

First we speak of the vegetative, then of the sensitive, and lastly of the intellective part—an order which is the most appropriate both for the particular task of our treatise and also, through a happy coincidence, for the presentation of the Aristotelian doctrine of the soul in and for itself. For the vegetative soul is the first and most universal and the precondition of the sensitive, and a like relation obtains in mortal living beings between the sensitive and the intellective soul. For this reason Aristotle, too, in the books On the Soul themselves, recommended this order<sup>1)</sup> and, one may say, kept to it to the end; for if, after having already treated of the spiritual cognitive powers, he only in later chapters of the third book<sup>2)</sup> speaks of local movement and thus returns to the sensitive part, he does so without turning away from the intellective, since at the same time, and indeed quite especially, he is intent on showing how intellective cognition participates in local movement. Likewise, in the discussions that follow, he develops not merely the reasons why the vegetative part is the precondition of the sensitive, and why the higher senses and local movement are never found without the sense of touch, [p. 75] but he also explains why no body that does not share in sensation can be united with an intellective soul<sup>3)</sup>.

1) De Anim. II, 4. §. 2. p. 415, a, 23.

2) De Anim. III, 9—11.

2. In concept the acts are prior to the potencies, and the objects prior to the acts; therefore, says Aristotle<sup>4)</sup>, in investigating the vegetative part, and likewise in investigating the sensitive or the intellective, we must start from the consideration of the object. First, therefore, we have to speak of nourishment<sup>5)</sup>, upon which the activities of all the vegetative powers are directed, though in different ways; for the one relates to it insofar as nourishment can be transformed into the substance of the living being itself<sup>6)</sup>, the other insofar as it can contribute through its size to the perfect measure of that being<sup>7)</sup>, a third, finally, insofar as it can be reshaped into the substance of a new, similar living being<sup>8)</sup>.

With regard to nourishment, however, the opinions of the earlier philosophers diverge in opposite directions<sup>9)</sup>. Some say that nourishment must be similar to the body into which it is transformed, that like is nourished and grows by like. The others, on the contrary, maintain that opposite is always nourished by opposite; for, they say, like cannot be affected by like, but nourishment is changed and concocted, and thus, like everything that is changed, is changed into something opposite. Further, they ground their opinion on the consideration that, if what nourishes itself and that with which it nourishes itself were similar to each other, the living being would have to be worked upon by the nourishment just as the nourishment is by it, which, however, is by no means the case, since it rather stands toward its nourishment as the artist toward his material.

Aristotle settles the dispute by showing that both views are in a certain way true and in a certain way false. [p. 76] By the name of nourishment, namely, he says<sup>10)</sup>, one can designate two things:

3) De Anim. III, 12. §. 4. p. 434, b, 3. — The plan of the three books On the Soul is clearly thought out and carried through in a fitting manner from beginning to end. Treating local movement after spiritual cognition spares many a repetition and is already foreshadowed by the remark De Anim. I, 2. §. 2. p. 403, b, 25. and what follows, with which De Anim. II, 2. §. 5. p. 413, b, 9.)

1) the last thing, which after complete preparation grows together with the body<sup>11)</sup>,

2) but also the original, still unworked material. If nourishment is taken in the first sense, then the proposition holds that like is nourished by like; in the second sense, on the other hand, one must say that the unlike is the nourishment of the unlike.

3. Now upon this nutritive material the vegetative soul, as remarked, acts in a threefold way: first, by making use of it as nourishment proper, i.e. for the preservation of individual life, for the ensouled being dies when it is deprived of nourishment<sup>12)</sup>; then, by employing the material in such a way that it becomes a means of growth, for the living being, in order not merely to be but also to be perfect in its kind, requires a certain measure of size<sup>13)</sup>, which it does not attain at its first coming into being but only grows up to gradually; finally, the vegetative soul also works up the nutritive material in such a way that it forms it into seed, from which another but homogeneous being comes forth<sup>14)</sup>, and this too is a kind of self-preservation, indeed even the more excellent manner of it<sup>15)</sup>; for through nutrition the ensouled body can prolong its life only for a short time, but by propagating itself through generation it lives and preserves itself, at least according to its kind, for all time, participating, so far as a mortal being is able, in the eternal existence of the Godhead. But to share in the divine as far as possible—that is what the whole [p. 77] nature strives for; and for this reason Aristotle also calls generation the most natural among the functions of life<sup>16)</sup>.

10) De Anim. II, 4. §. 11. p. 416, b, 3. πότερον δ᾽ ἐστὶν ἡ τροφὴ τὸ τελευταῖον προσγινόμενον ἢ τὸ πρῶτον, ἔχει διαφοράν. εἰ δ᾽ ἄμφω, ἀλλ᾽ ἡ μὲν ἄπεπτος ἡ δὲ πεπεμμένη, ἀμφοτέρως ἂν ἐνδέχοιτο τὴν τροφὴν λέγειν· ἡ μὲν γὰρ ἄπεπτος τὸ ἐναντίον τῷ ἐναντίῳ τρέφεται, ἡ δὲ πεπεμμένη, τὸ ὅμοιον τῷ ὁμοίῳ. ὥστε φανερὸν ὅτι λέγουσί τινα τρόπον ἀμφότεροι καὶ ὀρθῶς καὶ οὐκ ὀρθῶς.

The sense of both expressions is the same here, and one could explain the one by the other. Cf. the προςγενόμενον De Sens. et Sens. 6. p. 446, a, 14. — Cf. Trendelenburg on our passage.

14) De Anim. II, 4. §. 13. p. 416, b, 15.

15) The generative power counts for Aristotle as the highest of the three vegetative powers, and he therefore also wants this whole part of the soul to be named after it. De Anim. II, 4. §. 15. p. 416, b, 23. ἐπεὶ δὲ ἀπὸ τοῦ τέλους ἅπαντα προσαγορεύειν δίκαιον, τέλος δὲ τὸ γεννῆσαι οἷον αὐτό, εἴη ἂν ἡ πρώτη ψυχὴ γεννητικὴ οἷον αὐτό.

4. These remarks confirm what we have said about the reason why in the vegetative part, besides the acting faculties of nutrition, growth and generation, no faculty of the soul of another genus is to be found. What the living substance accomplishes as vegetative is only the introduction of its own substantial form into a foreign matter<sup>17)</sup>; its object, if we designate as such the terminus of its acting<sup>18)</sup>, is identical in species with itself. Therefore it has no need first to take up through a vital activity that likeness according to which, as we saw above, what is to be effected must be contained in the agent, and it requires no faculties such as are given to the sensitive part, e.g., in the senses. It has that likeness because it is what it is, and this likeness is natural to it in just the way in which the form of a lifeless substance is natural to the latter.

Accordingly, then, that striving too which is the second presupposition of all acting is in its case of no other kind than the unconscious natural drive from which the natural motions of inorganic substances proceed; it is no special vital function. The second class of faculties of the soul, namely that of the appetitive faculties, is therefore, just like that of the form-receiving powers, necessarily excluded from it.

Accordingly, for the vegetative domain there remains solely and alone the genus of the acting, and indeed of the unconsciously acting, powers. To it belong the three faculties of the soul of which we have spoken. That they are different emerged for us from the difference of what they effect; but that they are to be traced back to one soul as their principle, and thus are really faculties of the soul, [p. 78] we have already shown above<sup>19)</sup>, and we will here too briefly repeat the reason given there. Even if an activity of self-preservation or of growth can be ascribed to other, evidently lifeless beings as well, it nevertheless does not belong to them in the same way as to plants, which accomplish all this by moving themselves, by virtue of the plurality of their organs, the one acting upon the other; and it is self-movement that characterizes everything living.

Cf. De Generat. Animal. II, 1. p. 731, b, 24. Oecon. I, 3. p. 1343, b, 23.

17) In all vital activities the soul is the οὗ ἕνεκα ᾧ, but in the vegetative ones at the same time also the οὗ ἕνεκα οὗ. De Anim. II, 4. §. 5. p. 415, b, 20. διττῶς δὲ (ἐστὶν ἡ ψυχὴ) τὸ οὗ ἕνεκα, τό τε οὗ καὶ τὸ ᾧ. Cf. Torstrik on the passage.

18) And this is in fact the proper object of the acting activities, from which, as from their goal, they receive their specific difference. Thus, e.g., heating is the motion toward heat and healing the motion toward health.

Hence Aristotle, in the first chapter of the second book On the Soul, demonstrated from the organism of plants the necessity of assuming a plant soul<sup>20)</sup>. In the fourth chapter, where he treats specifically of the vegetative soul, he maintains against Empedocles that the growth of plants upward and downward is to be traced back not to the elements of fire and earth, which strive in opposite directions, but to the soul, while at the same time he proves to him his complete misunderstanding of the plant organism<sup>21)</sup>. Against others, who wanted to trace all growth back to the nature of fire, he urges that in that case living beings would expand without measure, whereas for every species of plants and of animals a natural limit of growth is found to be set. He grants that heat does indeed take part in the vegetative functions, but says that it is only a contributing cause<sup>22)</sup> and, as it were, an instrument of the first soul<sup>23)</sup>.

[scan 110]

### Dritter Theil. — Third Part.

### Von der sensitiven Seele. — On the Sensitive Soul.

Contenting ourselves, so as not to depart from our proper task, with this quick glance at the vegetative part of the soul, we pass on to the sensitive soul, which, being directed to a less restricted object, unites in itself, as remarked, three genera of vital functions. We consider first the sensitive functions of the first genus, namely sensation and imagination, and since the latter arises from the former, we will treat first of sensation.

#### a. Von der Empfindung im Allgemeinen und von der Zahl der äusseren Sinne. — a. On Sensation in General and on the Number of the External Senses.

2. We have sensation in being moved and affected by what is sensed<sup>1)</sup>. If, therefore, we ask whether what senses is similar to what is sensed or not, the answer to this follows from the general law that what is affected is unlike that by which it is affected before being affected, but like it after being affected. What senses is, before sensing, potentially such as its object already is actually; it is affected, therefore, while it is unlike, but after being affected it has been assimilated to the object and is such as the latter is<sup>2)</sup>.

But between being affected and being affected there is a great difference, according as one understands by it an alteration in the proper sense, which is always the corruption of something actual by something opposite<sup>3)</sup>, or a being affected which, without any corruption, without any loss of a form on the part of the affected subject, [p. 80] brings what lay potentially in the latter to actuality, and thus only brings the incomplete into the state of completion<sup>4)</sup>. A being affected in the first sense is, e.g., the becoming yellow of a red-colored body, or the becoming cold of a warm one, and the like; for the one is the corruption of the red, the other the corruption of the warm as such; the one brings about in the subject the loss of the red color, the other that of warmth. Such a being affected, then, sensing is not. It may indeed be bound up with an alteration, inasmuch as, e.g., the warm hand that touches something cold becomes colder during the sensation and through the action of what is sensed. But it is not insofar as we become cold that we sense the cold—otherwise plants and inorganic bodies would sense too<sup>5)</sup>—but insofar as the cold exists in us objectively, i.e., as something known<sup>6)</sup>, and thus insofar as we receive the coldness without ourselves being its physical subject, which can receive this or any other form only by being altered. Therefore Aristotle says in the twelfth chapter of the second book On the Soul that the sense receives the sensible forms without matter [p. 81] receives<sup>7)</sup>, and illustrates this with the simile of the wax, which takes the shape of the seal into itself without the gold or iron. This comparison, to be sure, is not perfectly apt<sup>7a)</sup>, since the shaped wax bears in itself not individually the same form as the seal, but only one of the same kind, whereas the sense, which receives the form of the sensible, bears the same one. And for this reason, too, the wax receives the shape of the seal as a physical subject and through a kind of corruption, for it loses the figure that had until then been its own; the sense, however, receives the sensible form not through alteration properly so called, even if, as already said, such an alteration may accompany its reception, and, e.g., the sensing body, when it touches the warm, at the same time becomes something that feels the warm, from something not feeling it, and something warm, from its contrary, something cold. It *feels* something warm, i.e., it has a warmth objectively in itself; it *is* warm, i.e., it has a warmth physically, materially in itself. The reception in this latter way was an alteration properly so called, a loss of coldness, a becoming of contrary from contrary; the reception in the first way was only a simple actualization of what was present potentially in the subject, and thus certainly no πάσχειν in the proper sense, although it is included under πάσχειν in general, under the category of being affected<sup>8)</sup>.

1) De Anim. II, 5. §. 1. p. 416, a, 33. ἡ δ᾽ αἴσθησις ἐν τῷ κινεῖσθαί τε καὶ πάσχειν συμβαίνει, καθάπερ εἴρηται (cf. ibid. 4. §. 6. p. 415, b, 23.)· δοκεῖ γὰρ ἀλλοίωσίς τις εἶναι.

2) De Anim. II, 5. §. 3. p. 417, a, 17. ibid. §. 7. p. 418, a, 3.

3) In a still narrower and more proper sense we restrict being affected to those alterations in which the form that is lost was the one more suitable to the subject, as, e.g., in the falling ill of the healthy body.

4) De Anim. II, 5, §. 5. p. 417, b, 2. οὐκ ἔστι δ᾽ ἁπλοῦν οὐδὲ τὸ πάσχειν, ἀλλὰ τὸ μὲν φθορά τις ὑπὸ τοῦ ἐναντίου, τὸ δὲ σωτηρία μᾶλλον τοῦ δυνάμει ὄντος ὑπὸ τοῦ ἐντελεχείᾳ ὄντος καὶ ὁμοίου οὕτως ὡς δύναμις ἔχει πρὸς ἐντελέχειαν. This latter being affected, to which, as we shall see at once, sensation belongs, is a complete actuality, just as the quantities, qualities, etc. are complete actualities; the former, on the other hand, if it is something lasting, is, as motion in the proper sense, an incomplete actuality. (De Anim. II, 5. §. 3. p. 417, a, 16. Metaph. Θ, 6. p. 1048, b, 28. 33.) Just as something that is warm is warm at every instant as long as it is warm, so too someone who has the sensation of warmth has this sensation at every instant as long as he has it. So too we see an object at every point of time as long as we see it; and the same holds of the other sense perceptions. It would be otherwise if sensation were a motion in the first and proper sense. The flying arrow does not fly in an instant from a to d, but not to c or b either. It does indeed move in every *part of time*; but in a *point of time* taken by itself we cannot say that it has moved forward even in the least, although it never rested but sped along with the greatest swiftness. Cf. Phys. VI, 3. p. 234, a, 24. ibid. 8. p. 239, b, 1. and 9. princ. and b, 30.

5) De Anim. II, 12. §. 4. p. 424, a, 32.

6) We use the expression “objective” here and in what follows not in the sense that is usual in recent times, but in that which the Aristotelians of the Middle Ages were accustomed to connect with it (with the scholastic objective), and which makes possible a very brief and precise designation of the Aristotelian doctrine. Materially, as a physical quality, the coldness is in the cold thing; as object, i.e., as something sensed, it is in the one who feels cold. Cf. De Anim. III, 2. §. 4 ff. p. 425, b, 25, where Aristotle says that the αἰσθητὸν κατ᾽ ἐνέργειαν is in the sense.

7) De Anim. II, 12. §. 1. p. 424, a, 17. καθόλου δὲ περὶ πάσης αἰσθήσεως δεῖ λαβεῖν ὅτι ἡ μὲν αἴσθησίς ἐστι τὸ δεκτικὸν τῶν αἰσθητῶν εἰδῶν ἄνευ τῆς ὕλης· οἷον ὁ κηρὸς τοῦ δακτυλίου ἄνευ τοῦ σιδήρου καὶ τοῦ χρυσοῦ δέχεται τὸ σημεῖον, λαμβάνει δὲ τὸ χρυσοῦν ἢ τὸ χαλκοῦν σημεῖον, ἀλλ᾽ οὐχ ᾗ χρυσὸς ἢ χαλκός· ὁμοίως δὲ καὶ ἡ αἴσθησις ἑκάστου ὑπὸ τοῦ ἔχοντος χρῶμα ἢ χυμὸν ἢ ψόφον πάσχει, ἀλλ᾽ οὐχ ᾗ ἕκαστον ἐκείνων λέγεται, ἀλλ᾽ ᾗ τοιονδί, καὶ κατὰ τὸν λόγον. Ibid. §. 4. p. 424, b, 2. Aristotle says, in order to explain why plants, although they are warmed, do not sense: αἴτιον γὰρ τὸ μὴ ἔχειν ... τοιαύτην ἀρχὴν οἵαν τὰ εἴδη δέχεσθαι τῶν αἰσθητῶν, ἀλλὰ πάσχειν μετὰ τῆς ὕλης. Ibid. Aristotle says (having previously remarked: ἔστι δ᾽ ἡ ἐπιστήμη μὲν τὰ ἐπιστητά πως, ἡ δ᾽ αἴσθησις τὰ αἰσθητά §. 1. b, 22.): ἀνάγκη δ᾽ ἢ αὐτὰ ἢ τὰ εἴδη εἶναι. αὐτὰ μὲν γὰρ δὴ οὔ· οὐ γὰρ ὁ λίθος ἐν τῇ ψυχῇ, ἀλλὰ τὸ εἶδος· ὥστε ... ὁ νοῦς εἶδος εἰδῶν καὶ ἡ αἴσθησις εἶδος αἰσθητῶν. Cf. further ibid. 12. §. 2. p. 434, a, 29.

7a) It holds only insofar as the wax takes on not the nature of the seal and all its properties but only its shape, and likewise the sensing faculty, e.g., the sense of warmth, does not assimilate itself to the sensed object in every respect, but only reproduces in itself one or another of its sensible qualities—the sense of warmth its warmth or coldness, but not its sweetness; the sense of taste its sweetness, but not its color, etc.

8) The question why the sensing organ, although it always has sensible qualities in it, does not always sense, and the remarks on the innate aptitude for sensing in contrast to the necessity of the spiritual

[p. 82] 3. But we have a plurality of sensing faculties. Now since the potencies are distinguished when their acts are, and these when their objects are different, we shall have to look to the diversity of their objects in order to determine the difference and the proper character of each of the faculties of sensation. But not every difference among the objects will be decisive for us here. If something is found in an object that in no way co-determines the affection of the sense, this, since it evidently stands in no relation whatever to the faculty of sensation, does not come into consideration in determining its essence. If, further, something belongs to an object that does indeed result in a diversity in the affection of the sense, but exercises this influence only by virtue of another property of the object, this too will not let us recognize the specific difference of the sensitive power. Rather, we must attend solely and exclusively to that property of the object which is the properly active principle of the sensation, and to which the sense, as the passive principle, therefore stands in natural relation.

Hence Aristotle, in the sixth chapter of the second book On the Soul, where he is about to pass on to the special consideration of the individual senses, after having remarked that with each sense one must speak first of the sensible object<sup>9)</sup>, deems it necessary at the same time to distinguish a threefold way in which something can be called sensible. “Sensible,” he says, “a thing is called in three ways. What is sensible in the first and second ways is sensed *as such*, but what is sensible in the third way, only *per accidens*. Of the first two, the one is the *proper* object of each individual sense, while the other is *common* to all the senses<sup>10)</sup>.

By the *proper* object of sense Aristotle understands that property of the perceived thing which is the active principle of the alteration of the sense, and according to which, because it is the natural correlate of the passive faculty<sup>11)</sup>, we shall, as already [p. 83] remarked, have to determine the essence of the sense. It therefore goes without saying that this object cannot be common to different senses. Aristotle gives as its distinctive marks that it cannot be perceived by any other sense, and that with regard to it the sense is subject to no deception. He illustrates it by the examples of color, sound, and flavor, of which, he says, the first is the proper object of sight, the second of hearing, the third of the sense of taste<sup>12)</sup>.

learning, which Aristotle connects with what has just been discussed, we shall discuss when treating of the intellective part.

9) De Anim. II, 6. §. 1. p. 418, a, 7. λεκτέον δὲ καθ᾽ ἑκάστην αἴσθησιν περὶ τῶν αἰσθητῶν πρῶτον.

10) Ibid. λέγεται δὲ τὸ αἰσθητὸν τριχῶς, ὧν δύο μὲν *καθ᾽ αὐτά* φαμεν αἰσθάνεσθαι, τὸ δὲ ἓν *κατὰ συμβεβηκός*. τῶν δὲ δύο τὸ μὲν *ἴδιόν* ἐστιν ἑκάστης αἰσθήσεως, τὸ δὲ *κοινὸν* πασῶν.

11) De Anim. II, 6. §. 4. p. 418, a, 24. τῶν δὲ καθ᾽ αὐτὰ αἰσθητῶν τὰ ἴδια κυρίως ἐστὶν αἰσθητά, καὶ πρὸς ἃ ἡ οὐσία πέφυκεν ἑκάστης αἰσθήσεως. Hence Aristotle begins the following chapter thus: οὗ μὲν οὖν ἐστὶν ἡ ὄψις, τοῦτ᾽ ἐστὶν ὁρατόν.

By the name of the *common* object of sense, on the other hand, he designates those peculiarities of a thing which do modify sense perception, but only through the properly active property just discussed. It is clear that with such secondary objects of sense the reason falls away on account of which the proper sensible had to be restricted to the affection of a single sense. They are therefore perceptible to many, indeed to all, senses (even if to the one more, to the other less<sup>13)</sup>), as is indicated in their very name. Aristotle enumerates, in the sixth chapter of the second book On the Soul<sup>14)</sup>, five, and in the first of the third book<sup>15)</sup>, six such common sensible objects: motion, rest, number, the one (which, though not named in the first passage, is evidently included in number), figure, and magnitude; and if, in the treatise On [p. 84] Sense and the Objects of Sense<sup>16)</sup>, he names only four, namely figure, magnitude, motion, and number, it is clear that the difference is not essential, since he could evidently reduce rest, as a privation, to motion. It is therefore all the less to be doubted that he held this number to be a closed one, as in the passage of the third book the purpose of the argument, too, presupposes the completeness of the kinds cited.

Which, as the examples immediately following show (λευκὸν καὶ μέλαν, γλυκὺ καὶ πικρόν), is to be referred to the ἴδιον αἰσθητόν. In the same way, ibid. 1. §. 8. p. 425, b, 7. καὶ αὐτὴ λευκοῦ is to be explained. λευκόν stands, as the foremost color, for color in general. Cf. also Top. I, 15. p. 106, a, 29. De Part. Animal.
