III, 1. §. 5. p. 425, a, 13. ἀλλὰ μὴν οὐδὲ τῶν κοινῶν οἷόν τ᾽ εἶναι αἰσθητήριόν τι ἴδιον, ὧν ἑκάστη αἰσθήσει αἰσθανόμεθα κατὰ συμβεβηκός (οἷον κινήσεως, στάσεως, σχήματος, μεγέθους, ἀριθμοῦ, ἑνός· ταῦτα γὰρ πάντα κινήσει αἰσθανόμεθα, οἷον μέγεθος κινήσει· ὥστε καὶ σχῆμα, μέγεθος γάρ τι τὸ σχῆμα [μέγεθός τι, similarly φωνή τις, see n. 64., a determinate mode of a magnitude, cf. my Mannigf. Bedeut. d. Seiend. p. 156 f. p. 202 ff.; a surface that has the shape of a rectangle perhaps has the same magnitude as another that is a square, but it has it in a different way: the ratio of length to breadth is a different one in it; from this close relation of figure and magnitude it follows that whoever perceives the one also perceives the other]· τὸ δ᾽ ἠρεμοῦν τῷ μὴ κινεῖσθαι· ὁ δ᾽ ἀριθμὸς τῇ ἀποφάσει τοῦ συνεχοῦς) καὶ τοῖς ἰδίοις· ἑκάστη γὰρ ἓν αἰσθάνεται αἴσθησις. On the reading and the meaning of this passage cf. Part III. n. 55.

*Per accidens* sensible, finally, Aristotle calls everything that belongs to a perceived thing without becoming in any way co-determining for the sensation<sup>17)</sup>. He illustrates it by an example. Someone sees something white, and this is the son of Diares. Now one can indeed say that he sees the son of Diares, but he does not see him as such; he sees the white, and it belongs to the white that he sees to be the son of Diares<sup>18)</sup>. In this way, too, what belongs to the proper object of the one sense is perceptible to the other senses<sup>19)</sup>: one tastes the colored and sees the sounding, and the like. If, therefore, Aristotle told us above that the proper object of sense can be perceived only by *one* sense, he took no account in this of perceiving per accidens; and in fact it is indeed also correct that what is sensed merely per accidens is, properly speaking, not sensed at all. Therefore, too, the per accidens sensible can least of all come into consideration in determining the specific differences of the sense faculties; rather, what is perceived as such, and indeed as proper object of sense, is alone decisive here. To it, says Aristotle, the essence of each sense is by nature ordered<sup>20)</sup>, and herein lies also the reason why, unless morbid conditions supervene, with regard to it the sense is subject to no [p. 85] deceptions<sup>21)</sup>, for it would be nature itself that deceived it.

16) De Sens. et Sens. 1. p. 437, a, 8.

17) De Anim. II, 6. §. 4. p. 418, a, 23. διὸ καὶ οὐδὲν πάσχει ᾗ τοιοῦτον ὑπὸ τοῦ αἰσθητοῦ.

18) De Anim. II, 6. §. 4. p. 418, a, 20. Perhaps these words refer to a fallacy of the Sophists well known in Aristotle’s time, which the context, especially in the first chapter of the third book (§. 6. f. p. 425, a, 24.), makes fairly probable (cf. below n. 55.). In essentials the sophism would then have been no other than the one that still figures today in the textbooks of logic as an example of the fallacia per accidens. Do you know who the disguised man is? — No! — Then you do not know who your father is! Cf. the 24th chap. De Soph. Elench., which deals with the fallacia per acc. (esp. p. 179, b, 29.); that it was with this above all that the Sophists made mischief, Aristotle expressly remarks. Metaph. E, 2. p. 1026, b, 15. ibid. K, 8. p. 1064, b, 28.

19) De Anim. III, 1. §. 7. p. 425, a, 30,

20) See above n. 11.

4. Thus we shall have to define the sense of sight as the sense of color<sup>22)</sup>, hearing as the sense of sound, and shall have to proceed analogously with every other sense as well; the particular nature of the proper object will let us recognize the particular nature of the sensing faculty. Aristotle therefore starts, in the investigation of the sense of sight, from the consideration of color, in that of hearing from the consideration of sound, and remains faithful to the same method with all the senses.

But in thus pursuing the same fundamental thought through the whole domain of the senses, he arrives, in the case of the sense of touch, at the result that it is only erroneously held to be a single faculty of sensation<sup>23)</sup>. The warm and cold, just like the dry and moist, bears all the marks of the ἴδιον αἰσθητόν; if, therefore, the sense for warm and cold and for dry and moist were one and the same, in this case *one* sense would have two proper objects of sense, which, since the proper object gives the sense its specific determinateness, would naturally involve a contradiction.

#### b. Von dem Sinne der Sensation. — b. On the Sense of Sensation.

5. But along this path we must be led to the assumption of yet another sense as well, and Aristotle does not hesitate to draw this consequence too. For since we sense that we see and hear, the question arises whether we perceive this with sight and hearing themselves, or with another sense<sup>24)</sup>. If we perceived with the sense of sight itself that we see, then, since the activity of this sense is seeing, we would have to see that we see<sup>25)</sup>, and that which sees, as seeing, would thus have to [p. 86] belong either to the proper object of the sense of sight or to one of the common objects of sense. But both are impossible; for in the first case, since color is the proper object of the sense of sight, while a plurality of proper objects is possible for no sense, that which sees, as such, would have to be something colored<sup>26)</sup>, which was already shown by us earlier to be erroneous. For a thing becomes colored insofar as it receives the color physically, materially, through a πάσχειν μετὰ τῆς ὕλης<sup>27)</sup>, but seeing, on the other hand, insofar as it receives the color objectively, τὸ εἶδος ἄνευ τῆς ὕλης<sup>28)</sup>. In the second case, however, seeing would have to stand to the seen color just as other common objects of sense do, i.e., like magnitude or shape or motion, it would have to be found united with it and, visible together with the color and through the color, be perceived in the same act of seeing. The white stone that someone sees would thus be that which sees, just as it is the thing of such and such a size and of such and such a shape—which would evidently be a ridiculous inversion of the relation of subject and object. To be sure, every being seen is in a certain sense a seeing: the seeing of the one is the being seen of the other<sup>29)</sup>; but the seen is not on that account that which sees, and the seen, in being seen, acquires nothing by which its appearance could be modified; for the motion is in the moved<sup>30)</sup>, and we must not trust language here, which designates being seen as an undergoing and seeing as an acting, whereas that which sees is in truth what undergoes<sup>31)</sup>. It is therefore evident that that which sees is seen at most per accidens, and that, if we nevertheless perceive that we see, this must come about through the activity of another sense<sup>32)</sup>.

21) De Anim. II, 6. §. 2. p. 418, a, 12. ibid. III, 3. §. 12. p. 428, b, 18.

22) See above n. 11.

23) De Anim. II, 11. §. 1—10. 32) That this sense cannot be the sense of touch is evident, among other reasons, especially because otherwise we would have to perceive seeing by touching that which sees. Perhaps this is the meaning of the obscure passage De Anim. III, 2. §. 11. p. 426, b, 15. ἢ καὶ δῆλον ὅτι ἡ σὰρξ οὐκ ἔστι τὸ ἔσχατον αἰσθητήριον· ἀνάγκη γὰρ ἦν ἁπτόμενον *αὐτοῦ* (i.e. τοῦ κρίνοντος) κρίνειν τὸ κρῖνον. In which case σάρξ would be used to designate the sense of touch, ἔσχατον αἰσθητήριον to designate the inner sense. Cf. the imprecise use of ἀκοή and ἁφή De Anim. III, 1. §. 3. p. 425, a, 4. 7. Another explanation, which is suggested by a certain verbal echo of II, 11. §. 9. p. 423, b, 20., would make the passage appear very unclear, and I do not understand how it is supposed to fit into the context. See on this *Torstrik*, De Anim. p. 169.

[p. 87] 6. Following other traces as well, we arrive at the assumption of such a faculty of sensation.

Each sense is able to perceive the differences of the object sensed by it; sight, e.g., can distinguish white and black, taste sweet and bitter. But since we are also able to distinguish white from sweet, the question arises which sense cognizes that they are different; for that a sense reveals it to us we cannot doubt, since the one as well as the other belongs among sensible things<sup>33)</sup>. But it is equally clear that this sense can be neither sight nor taste, since, as we already said above, the one of them senses the sweet, the other the white, only per accidens, i.e., properly speaking, not at all. We therefore see ourselves compelled to assume another sense, different from both, which makes that distinction possible for us. — Or can we perhaps perceive the difference of white and sweet through the simultaneous sensations of two different senses? Certainly not! this is as little possible as that two different men, of whom the one senses this object and the other that one at the same time, should on that account be able to cognize their difference<sup>34)</sup>.

7. Nevertheless we must not leave out of account an objection that might be raised against this argument. The sensations of sight and of taste, one could say, are not separated like the sensations of different men; the sensitive part is only one; for, as Aristotle at least assumed, and as is still probable today on psychological grounds, even if physiology has established nothing certain about it, the various sensations are conveyed from all sides to *one* organ, so that they can be found together and united in it simultaneously. The misgivings against the possibility of such an assumption are removed by Aristotle in the book On Sense and the Object of Sense<sup>35)</sup> with the correct remark that, just as in the [p. 88] body to which the sensation is directed color and warmth and qualities of another genus can be actual at the same time, so too in [p. 89] the organ that senses, color perception and the sensation of warmth, in short a plurality of sense activities, can subsist at the same time. Now if this is correct, our distinguishing of heterogeneous objects of sense seems to become explicable even without the assumption of a new sensing power. The senses are not separated; they are in one and the same subject, and this subject, in receiving two objects simultaneously through different faculties, will precisely thereby—so one should at least think—be able to distinguish the one from the other<sup>36)</sup>.

33) De Anim. III, 2. §. 10. p. 426, b, 8. ἑκάστη μὲν οὖν αἴσθησις τοῦ ὑποκειμένου αἰσθητοῦ ἐστίν, ὑπάρχουσα ἐν τῷ αἰσθητηρίῳ ᾗ αἰσθητήριον, καὶ κρίνει τὰς τοῦ ὑποκειμένου αἰσθητοῦ διαφοράς, οἷον λευκὸν μὲν καὶ μέλαν ὄψις, γλυκὺ δὲ καὶ πικρὸν γεῦσις. ὁμοίως δ᾽ ἔχει τοῦτο καὶ ἐπὶ τῶν ἄλλων. ἐπεὶ δὲ καὶ τὸ λευκὸν καὶ τὸ γλυκὺ καὶ ἕκαστον τῶν αἰσθητῶν πρὸς ἕκαστον κρίνομεν, τίνι καὶ αἰσθανόμεθα ὅτι διαφέρει; ἀνάγκη δὴ αἰσθήσει· αἰσθητὰ γάρ ἐστιν.

34) De Anim. III, 2. §. 11. p. 426, b, 17. οὔτε δὴ κεχωρισμένοις ἐνδέχεται κρίνειν ὅτι ἕτερον τὸ γλυκὺ τοῦ λευκοῦ, ἀλλὰ δεῖ ἑνί τινι ἄμφω δῆλα εἶναι. οὕτω μὲν γὰρ κἂν εἰ τοῦ μὲν ἐγὼ τοῦ δὲ σὺ αἴσθοιο, δῆλον ἂν εἴη ὅτι ἕτερα ἀλλήλων. δεῖ δὲ τὸ ἓν λέγειν ὅτι ἕτερον· ἕτερον γὰρ τὸ γλυκὺ τοῦ λευκοῦ. λέγει ἄρα τὸ αὐτό. ὥστε ὡς λέγει, οὕτω καὶ νοεῖ καὶ αἰσθάνεται. ὅτι μὲν οὖν οὐχ οἷόν τε κεχωρισμένοις κρίνειν τὰ κεχωρισμένα, δῆλον.

35) De Sens. et Sens. 7. p. 449, a, 13. ἢ ὥσπερ ἐπὶ τῶν πραγμάτων αὐτῶν ἐνδέχεται,

οὕτω καὶ ἐπὶ τῆς ψυχῆς. τὸ γὰρ αὐτὸ καὶ ἓν ἀριθμῷ λευκὸν καὶ γλυκύ ἐστι καὶ ἄλλα πολλά, εἰ μὴ χωριστὰ τὰ πάθη ἀλλήλων, ἀλλὰ τὸ εἶναι ἕτερον ἑκάστῳ. ὁμοίως τοίνυν θετέον καὶ ἐπὶ τῆς ψυχῆς τὸ αὐτὸ καὶ ἓν εἶναι ἀριθμῷ τὸ αἰσθητικὸν πάντων, τῷ μέντοι εἶναι ἕτερον καὶ ἕτερον, τῶν μὲν γένει (e.g., for sweet and white), τῶν δὲ εἴδει (e.g., for white and other colors). ὥστε καὶ αἰσθάνοιτ᾽ ἂν ἅμα τῷ αὐτῷ καὶ ἑνί, λόγῳ δ᾽ οὐ τῷ αὐτῷ. Cf. also the preceding detailed discussion of the question of the unity or plurality of the sensing subject (p. 448, b, 17. f.). The same doctrine, that the organ which properly senses is only one, we find De Anim. III, 7. §. 3. p. 431, a, 17. ὥσπερ δὲ ὁ ἀὴρ τὴν κόρην τοιανδὶ ἐποίησεν, αὐτὴ δ᾽ ἕτερον, καὶ ἡ ἀκοὴ (i.e. the proximate organ of hearing, cf. ibid. 1. §. 3. p. 425, a, 4.) ὡσαύτως. τὸ δὲ ἔσχατον ἕν, καὶ μία μεσότης· τὸ δ᾽ εἶναι αὐτῇ πλείω. Cf. further De Anim. I, 5. §. 26. p. 411, b, 26. ibid. II, 2. §. 10. p. 413, b, 27. III, 9. §. 3. p. 432, b, 1. De Memor. et Remin. I. p. 450, a, 29. ibid. 2, p. 453, a, 24. De Somn. et Vigil. 2. p. 455, a, 20. ἔστι μὲν γὰρ μία αἴσθησις, καὶ τὸ κύριον αἰσθητήριον ἕν· τὸ δ᾽ εἶναι αἰσθήσει τοῦ γένους ἑκάστου ἕτερον, οἷον ψόφου καὶ χρώματος (αἴσθησις is again an imprecise expression for αἰσθητικόν). Ibid. a, 33. b, 10. 34. De Insomn. 3. p. 461, b, 3. De Juvent. et Senect. p. 467, b, 21. 25—29. ibid. 3. p. 469, a, 10. and 4. p. 469, b, 4. De Part. Animal. II, 1. p. 647, a, 24. ibid. 10. p. 656, a, 27. b, 24. III, 3. p. 665, a. 10. ibid. 4. p. 666, a, 11. 34. ibid. 5. p. 667, b, 21. and 10. p. 672, b, 16. IV, 5. p. 681, b, 15. 32. De Mot. Animal. (a work that we hold to be genuine, since, just as it announces itself as Aristotelian, so too it is worthy of him in content and in form perfectly in keeping with his manner of writing. The words 10. p. 703, a, 10., on account of which it seemed suspect to recent critics, need not necessarily be taken as a citation of the work περὶ πνεύματος; they can also refer to De Generat. Animal. II, 2. p. 735, b, 37. and 3. p. 736, b, 37., and in them too not the least deviation from the doctrine of Aristotle is to be found.) 9. p. 702, b, 20. ibid. 11. p. 703, b, 23. (where a colon is to be placed after αἰτίαν, and γὰρ is to be inserted after τὰς with Codd. P. and S.) — To be sure, there is no lack of passages in which Aristotle seems to assume a diversity of subject for different faculties of sensation. Yet with many of them at least the apparent contradiction is resolved if one observes that Aristotle speaks of a sense organ (αἰσθητήριον) in more than one sense. Frequently he gives this name to the organs that mediate sensation, e.g., eye and ear and other sense instruments, and of these there are many; in other places, however, he understands by it the subject of sensation itself, which, to distinguish it from those, he sometimes calls πρῶτον αἰσθητήριον (De Anim. II, 12. §. 2. p. 424, a, 24. De Somn. et Vigil. 2. p. 455, b, 10. p. 456, a, 21. ibid. 3. p. 458, a, 28. De Part. Animal. III, 4. p. 666, a, 34. and elsewhere), elsewhere also ἔσχατον αἰσθητήριον (e.g. De Anim. III, 2. §. 11. p. 426, b, 16.), and this, according to his doctrine, if we understand it correctly, is a single one. To this may be added carelessness of expression, e.g., αἰσθητικόν for αἰσθητήριον, and the like, and an accommodation to the ordinary way of speaking, since we too often say that the eye sees and the ear hears. Some passages, however, always remain difficult to explain. — But if according to Aristotle the sensing subject is *one*, it naturally does not on that account count for him as an indivisible unity, as a mathematical point. It is extended (De Anim. II, 12. §. 2. p. 424, a, 26.);

Indeed, the problem seems to be solved in this way more easily and more perfectly than by the assumption of *one* distinguishing power that senses both. For one and the same sensing faculty can have only a single sensation at one and the same time; potentially, to be sure, different sensations do not exclude one another in it, since, just as the warm is at the same time potentially cold, so too the sense that senses warmth at the same time has in itself potentially the sensation of cold; but actually only the one of the two sensations is ever found in the sense, just as a thing can never be warm and cold at the same time<sup>37)</sup>. No [p. 90] matter has two forms, no potency two actualities, at the same time<sup>38)</sup>.

and therefore it happens that, as we have already mentioned above, when animals are cut apart, both pieces often sense. (Cf. also de Part. Animal. IV, 5. p. 682, a, 4. and ibid. 6. b, 29.) If we ask about the grounds on which Aristotle’s opinion of the unity of the sensing part rested, they were probably partly teleological, as the first of the two passages just cited indicates, partly facts of experience. That the proximate sense instruments, such as the eye, do not sense was proved to him by cases like the one he relates De Sens. et Sens. 2. (p. 438, b, 12.), in which a warrior lost the faculty of sight through an injury at the temples. Further, the observation that we cannot have two color presentations or two tone presentations simultaneously—for when the axes of the eyes are distorted, one image disturbs the other, and when two people say something into our ears simultaneously, the one into the one ear, the other into the other, we cannot properly understand either of them—led him to infer the unity of the sense of sight and likewise of hearing, whereby, as a matter of course, the unity of the seeing and of the hearing part was at the same time proved and the assumption of a unity of the sensing part in general was suggested. (De Sens. et Sens. 7. p. 448, b, 22. — p. 449, a, 2. and ibid. p. 447, a, 17.) Finally, he must have been confirmed in his opinion by the fact that, when someone sleeps, a part never sleeps alone. It does not happen that someone who falls asleep as regards the sense of sight should remain awake as regards hearing, or the reverse. But why is this? Because sleep is an affection of the first organ, the one common to the senses. If their subject were not common, that state of immobility and bondage which sleep is (p. 454, b, 20.) would not necessarily be common to them either. De Somn. et Vigil. 2. p. 455, a, 25.

36) De Anim. III, 2. §. 13. p. 427, a, 2.

37) De Anim. III, 2. §. 14. p. 427, a, 5.

8. To this is added another misgiving that reaches still deeper. For if we assume that we cognize the difference of the sweet and the white through *one* sense, this is possible only if we ascribe to this sense the capacity to sense both the sweet and the white as such. We must therefore assume a multiple proper object of sense for one and the same sensing faculty, and thereby overturn a principle that stood so firm for us that for its sake we believed we even had to divide the sense of touch into two senses.

9. In fact, the objection seems well founded; nevertheless we shall not let ourselves be led astray by it; and since our assumption is reproached on two counts, first, that it is superfluous, second, that it is impossible, we shall first investigate whether the unity of the sensing subject, or the local unity, as Aristotle calls it, really suffices for distinguishing different objects of sense, and then turn our attention to whether, and how, the misgivings raised against the Aristotelian theory may perhaps be removed.

As regards the first point, then, it is easy to see that the unity of the sensing organ, if it is not *one* insofar as it is organ, i.e., insofar as it is subject of the faculty of sensation<sup>38)</sup>, does not suffice for the cognition of the diversity of two objects. For distinguishing is the sensing of the difference<sup>40)</sup>, but every sensing is the actuality of a sensing faculty, and indeed of *one* sensing faculty, since no actuality can be in more than *one* potency; no form actualizes two matters. If, then, according to the assumption opposed to us, we distinguished the sweet from the white through two faculties, we would distinguish the two objects through two actualities; we would distinguish them twice. But surely not without cognizing them twice? — It is therefore rather evident that we would not distinguish them at all, since through neither of the two faculties, neither through taste nor through sight, are we capable of having this sensation.

10. But what do we reply to the two objections which would show the Aristotelian assertion to be impossible and contradictory to earlier determinations? [p. 91] Shall we deny that, just as the unity of that which senses, so too the unity of the time of sensation is required for both objects in order that their diversity be cognizable? — By no means! Aristotle expressly remarks not only that one and the same thing cognizes the difference of the one and the other, but also that precisely when it cognizes that this object is different from that one, it also cognizes that that one differs from this<sup>41)</sup>. But must not his opponents too admit that *one* faculty of sensation can nevertheless perceive the diversity of several objects? They will certainly not deny that, as was said above, taste cognizes the diversity of the bitter and the sweet, and hearing that of high and low tone. What is admittedly the case with these senses will therefore be no absurd assumption if it is asserted also of that faculty which distinguishes between the sweet and the white, and between each object of sense and every other<sup>42)</sup>.

38) De Sens. et Sens. 7. p. 447, b, 17. Cf. Top. II, 10. p. 114, b, 34. and the passages cited above, Part I, n. 55.

39) De Anim. III, 2. §. 10. p. 426, b, 9. (ἡ αἴσθησις ὑπάρχει) ἐν τῷ αἰσθητηρίῳ ᾗ αἰσθητήριον.

40) De Anim. III, 2. §. 10. p. 426, b, 14.

But how is the difficulty resolved? For it is not yet resolved hereby, even if it has been shown to be resolvable. — As it is the same for all distinguishing faculties, so it is resolved for all in the same way too, namely by attending to what is peculiar to temporal presence, to the *now*. Just as one can call a point that connects two lines in a certain sense one and in a certain sense two, so too the now, since in it what was and what will be, that which passes away and that which comes to be, touch each other; and some have therefore called it outright a point<sup>43)</sup>. The connecting point is the end point of the one and the starting point [p. 92] of the other line, and to that extent two and divided, although it is properly *one* and indivisible; and in quite a similar way the now is indeed *one* and undivided, but, as end point of past time and starting point of future time, two and divided; and if, therefore, a change takes place in it, as, e.g., when two presentations follow one another, then there is in it the limit of two things, the limit of two presentations, which, like every limit, is one and undivided and yet at the same time in a certain sense two and divided. “Insofar, then, as the point (the point of time, the now) is undivided, that which distinguishes is one and simultaneous<sup>44)</sup>; but insofar as it is divided, it makes use of one and the same point twice at the same time. Insofar, then, as it makes use of the limit as two, it distinguishes two objects and is something separated, as something that senses what is separated<sup>45)</sup>; but insofar as the limit is one, it distinguishes by one thing and simultaneously<sup>46)</sup>.”

41) De Anim. III, 2. §. 12. p. 426, b, 22. ὅτι μὲν οὖν οὐχ οἷόν τε κεχωρισμένοις κρίνειν τὰ κεχωρισμένα, δῆλον· ὅτι δ᾽ οὐδ᾽ [ἑνὶ] ἐν κεχωρισμένῳ χρόνῳ, ἐντεῦθεν. ὥσπερ γὰρ τὸ αὐτὸ λέγει ὅτι ἕτερον τὸ ἀγαθὸν καὶ τὸ κακόν, οὕτω καὶ ὅτε θάτερον λέγει ὅτι ἕτερον, καὶ θάτερον.

42) De Anim. III, 7. §. 4. p. 431, a, 20. see below n. 49. For an understanding, cf. also n. 46.

43) Phys. IV, 10. p. 218, a, 33. Aristotle reports that some declared the sphere of the heavens itself to be time. Presumably, since they conceived time as a spatial magnitude, the now counted for them as a point in it. But Aristotle himself also often designates it as a kind of point. See e.g. Phys. IV, 10. p. 218, a, 20. ibid. 11. p. 220, a, 4. and what follows, to the end of the chap. (a passage that is especially serviceable for elucidating ours, De Anim. III, 2. §. 15. (see n. 46.). In Metaph. B, 5. p. 1002, b, 5., too, he compares it to the point. Likewise Phys. IV, 13. princ.; only, he says, it is not a permanent point. Cf. in the same chap. p. 222, b, 1., further Phys. VI, 1. p. 231, b, 6. ibid. 3. p. 233, b, 35. — p. 234, a, 24. and Phys. VIII, 1. p. 251, b, 20., where the now is likewise spoken of in a way similar to that in our passage. From the fact that by the point here the now is to be understood, the expression is now explained:

Let us consider this in an example. When hearing perceives two tones immediately one after the other, the instant in which the one sense perception follows upon the other contains the limit of both; it is in this instant, then, that we cognize the change of the two tones and their diversity. It is similar with taste and with all the other senses; and with the sense, too, which, as we said, distinguishes the white from the sweet and every object of sense from every other, it can therefore not be otherwise; with it, too, we cognize, at the instant when two sensations alternate with each other, both objects, and cognize that they are different. With this the first objection would be removed, and we can pass on to the resolution of the second.

ἣν καλοῦσί τινες στιγμήν, which hitherto seemed inexplicable. Cf. Trendelenburg on the passage.

44) That is, it has *one* sensation in itself, it distinguishes by *one* act and distinguishes in *one* moment.

45) That which distinguishes is something separated, says Aristotle, i.e., it has two αἰσθήματα (or νοήματα) in itself, which are not at the same time but one after the other.

46) Cf. De Anim. III, 6. §. 1. f. p. 430, a, 26. De Anim. III, 2. §. 15. p. 427, a, 9. ἀλλ᾽ ὥσπερ ἣν καλοῦσί τινες στιγμήν (namely the νῦν), ᾗ μία καὶ ᾗ δύο, ταύτῃ καὶ διαιρετή. ᾗ μὲν οὖν ἀδιαίρετον (sc. τὸ νῦν, which has just been paraphrased with the expression ἣν καλοῦσί τινες στιγμήν and was expressly named in §. 12. (p. 426, b, 28.), to which our passage refers; otherwise σημεῖον would have to be thought of as the subject, which does not seem probable to us), ἓν τὸ κρῖνόν ἐστι καὶ ἅμα, ᾗ δὲ διαιρετὸν ὑπάρχει, δὶς τῷ αὐτῷ χρῆται σημείῳ ἅμα. ᾗ μὲν οὖν δυσὶ (read δὶς or, better, ὡς δυσί. cf. Torstrik, who refers to Phys. VIII, 8. p. 262, a, 19. p. 263, a, 23. and ibid. IV, 12. p. 220, a, 4.) χρῆται τῷ πέρατι, (the punctuation belongs here, not before τῷ; what is here called πέρας is called ὅρος in De Anim. III, 7. §. 4. p. 431, a, 22.) δύο κρίνει καὶ κεχωρισμένα ἐστὶν ὡς κεχωρισμένων (sc. αἰσθητῶν αἰσθανόμενον)· ᾗ δ᾽ ἕν, ἑνὶ καὶ ἅμα. Cf. De Anim. III, 7. §. 4. p. 431, a, 20. n. 49.

[p. 93] 11. The difficulty, however, was this: if, it was said, one were to cognize the difference between sweet and white by *one* sense, then *one* sense would have to be capable of perceiving both; this sense would thus have a manifold proper object, which is impossible. We add that a further consequence seems to follow, namely that several senses would share in the same proper object, since sight, after all, also perceives the white, and taste the sweet. All the earlier determinations thus seem to be in contradiction with such an assumption.

But these contradictions vanish on closer consideration. If we look back on the course of our discussions, we have proved the existence of the sense of which we are speaking in a twofold way. In the first proof we had started from the fact that within the range of our perceptions there is found a proper sense object which is different from the object of each of the senses already assumed. We see color, we said, but we do not see that we see it; we hear sound, but we do not hear that we hear it; nevertheless we perceive that we see and hear, and we do not perceive this in the way in which common sense objects are perceived. From this we inferred the existence of a special sense.

If we now ask ourselves what the proper object of this sense is, it is evident that we must designate our sensation as such. The external objects are not its object. But because the differences of the sensations stand in a relation analogous to the differences of the objects, the difference of the latter necessarily also makes itself known in the difference of the former, and hence it comes about that the distinguishing of heterogeneous sense objects can be traced back to the power of this sense.

And herein lies the solution of the objection raised. None of the contradictions to which our assumption was supposed to lead really follows from it; for it follows neither that the sense which distinguishes the heterogeneous objects has the same proper object as other senses, nor yet that a manifold proper object belongs to it itself. Its proper object is solely and exclusively the sensations, as colors are the proper object of sight; but in perceiving that we see the white and taste the sweet, and in distinguishing these sensations, it at the same time acquaints us with the analogous difference of the white and the sweet themselves<sup>47)</sup>.

47) Cf. here De Memor. et Remin. 1. p. 450, b, 20. ff. esp. p. 451, a, 5.,

[p. 94] When a black and a white body are perceived by the sense of sight, it is not they themselves but analogues of them<sup>48)</sup> that are in the sense of sight, and since these are now united in *one* sense and in their difference correspond to the difference of the external things, the sense distinguishes the external objects by means of them. Let A be the white and B the black, and let C be to D as A is to B, and hence also conversely. If now C and D are in *one* sense, namely in the sense of sight, then the relation not merely of C and D but also of A and B is in it<sup>49)</sup>.

Now, however, we must explain in quite a similar way the process in the distinguishing of sense objects of different genera, as, e.g., of the white and the sweet; the problem is still essentially the same as there, where we distinguished between objects which are opposed within *one* genus<sup>50)</sup>. Let A be the white and B the sweet, and let C and D be the sensations of the white and the sweet analogous to the external objects, of which the one is in the sense of sight, the other in the sense of taste; E and [p. 95] F, however, are to stand to these two sensations as they stand to their objects. If now E and F are in *one* sense, namely in that which perceives the sensations, then not merely the relation of E and F is in it, but also that of C and D, and hence also that of A and B, i.e., the relation of the white and the sweet, whose distinguishing was in question<sup>51)</sup>.

a passage which shows that our solution is surely given in Aristotle’s sense. For memory is in the κοινὴ αἴσθησις, by which precisely the sense of which we are treating is to be understood. It can be called common inasmuch as it distinguishes every sensible object from every other and becomes active in consequence of the action of the one and of the other (ibid. p. 450, a, 10.).

48) De Memor. et Remin. 2. p. 452, b, 12.

49) De Anim. III, 7. §. 4. p. 431, a, 20. τίνι δ᾽ ἐπικρίνει (the *one*, ultimate sense organ, to which the others convey the sensations) τί (perhaps better ὅτι, cf. ibid. 2. §. 10. p. 426, b, 14.) διαφέρει γλυκὺ καὶ θερμόν, εἴρηται μὲν καὶ πρότερον (namely in chap. 2.), λεκτέον δὲ καὶ ὧδε. ἔστι γὰρ ἕν τι, οὕτω δὲ καὶ ὡς ὅρος (namely δύο cf. above n. 46.)· καὶ ταῦτα (namely the two αἰσθητά that are in the sense) ἐν τῷ ἀνάλογον καὶ τῷ ἀριθμῷ ὃν ἔχει πρὸς ἑκάτερον (ἐστιν) ὡς ἐκεῖνα πρὸς ἄλληλα. (The sensations of the one and of the other object stand to each other, as sensations, as the objects themselves stand to each other. 18 and 12 stand to 3 and 2 as sixfold multiples; in this sixfold multiplicity they (18 and 12) have exactly the same relation to each other as 3 and 2. So it is also with the sensations and their objects. The perceptions of red and green stand to red and green as perceptions to their objects, and as perceptions they have among themselves exactly the same relation as the red and the green as sensible qualities.) .... ἔστω δὴ ὡς τὸ Α τὸ λευκὸν πρὸς τὸ Β τὸ μέλαν, τὸ Γ πρὸς τὸ Δ ὡς ἐκεῖνα πρὸς ἄλληλα· ὥστε καὶ ἐναλλάξ. εἰ δὴ τὰ ΓΔ ἑνὶ εἴη ὑπάρχοντα, οὕτως ἕξει ὥσπερ καὶ τὰ ΑΒ, τὸ αὐτὸ μὲν καὶ ἕν, τὸ δ᾽ εἶναι οὐ τὸ αὐτό, κἀκεῖνο (read, as Torstrik also proposes, κἀκεῖνα) ὁμοίως. The last two words correspond to the ὥστε καὶ ἐναλλάξ.

50) De Anim. III, 7. §. 4. p. 431, a, 24. τί γὰρ διαφέρει τὸ ἀπορεῖν πῶς τὰ μὴ ὁμογενῆ (for this, as Torstrik too has recognized, is the correct reading) κρίνει ἢ τἀναντία, οἷον λευκὸν καὶ μέλαν;

In some such way we can, with Aristotle, answer the objections that stood opposed to his doctrine of an inner sense different from all the external senses, i.e., of a special sense directed upon the inner motions of the sensitive part itself. It must indeed be this sense which, as it perceives that we have sensations, also lets us cognize the other sensitive operations, e.g., sensory appetite, and gives us self-consciousness, so far as this belongs to the sensory part<sup>52)</sup>. It is therefore without question the most eminent of all the senses.

12. Perhaps, however, not everyone notices what high importance it has for us also through the fact that it makes possible for us the distinguishing of every sense object from every other, and we therefore believe we must draw attention to this, at least in a few words. For above all it is to be observed that without this inner sense we should fail to perceive not merely the differences of the proper objects of different faculties of sensation, but also those of such objects as are indeed perceptible by several senses, but of which each is in fact grasped by only *one* external sense. Someone, e.g., feels an angular object and at the same time sees a round one, and cognizes the difference of the two shapes; but he distinguishes the two neither by the sense of sight nor by touch; rather, according to our discussions, it can only be the inner sense that makes the distinction possible for him. So too we can perceive the local difference of two things by touch as well as by sight<sup>53)</sup>, but in a case where someone in fact merely sees the one and merely feels the other, it is again only the inner sense that acquaints him with the local separation of the two.

But now, since every sense that notices the differences of an object is also able to cognize the absence of such differences, the inner sense, since it perceives the local difference of two things of which we grasp the one by touch and the other by the sense of sight, will also, when we feel and see one and [p. 96] the same thing simultaneously, notice that that difference does not exist here<sup>54)</sup>, and so we are able to cognize that what is felt is one with what is seen, in that it coincides with it in place.

51) Aristotle adds, only quite briefly, at the end of the passage cited in n. 49. the words: ὁ δ᾽ αὐτὸς λόγος καὶ εἰ τὸ μὲν Α τὸ γλυκὺ εἴη, τὸ δὲ Β τὸ λευκόν.

52) Cf. De Sens. et Sens. 7. p. 448, a, 29.

53) Cf. de Anim. II, 6. §. 2. p. 418, a, 16. and Anal. Post. I, 31. p. 87, b, 30.

From this it follows that when, e.g., warmth and redness, existing together in *one* subject, are perceived by us simultaneously, we do indeed perceive that the warm is red and that the red is warm, but that we neither see nor feel this, but perceive it by a sense activity different from both, namely by that of the inner sense. Without it we would perceive the unity of the warm and the red only per accidens, since the sense of sight is capable of cognizing the warm, and touch the white, only per accidens; we would thus, properly speaking, not perceive it at all<sup>55)</sup>.

54) De Anim. III, 1. §. 5. p. 425, a, 18.

55) It will be well to consider somewhat more closely the passage in which Aristotle states this doctrine, since it is subject to no slight difficulties. In the first chapter of the third book On the Soul Aristotle seeks to show that there can be no faculty of sensation besides those which we possess. His discussion is not satisfactory in all its parts, and, owing to the nature of the question, could not be so at all. For how would anyone prove with certainty that there can be no sensible quality entirely unknown to us? But as long as this is not proved, the impossibility of a sense faculty foreign to us is evidently not demonstrated either. (cf. De Anim. III, 1. §. 4. p. 425, a, 11.).

On the other hand, Aristotle could certainly prove that there can be no special sense, lacking to us, for the so-called common sense objects; indeed, this proof follows very easily and simply from what we established earlier. For since no sense can perceive more than *one* sensible quality, but *we* are able, by our senses and even by each of them, to perceive all the common sense objects together with the proper sensible quality, it is evident that the common sense objects are not sensible qualities, i.e., not such properties as can be the proper object of a sense. It is thus utterly unthinkable that there should be a sense of magnitude or number as there is, e.g., a sense of color. Aristotle conducts this proof in §. 5. p. 425, a, 13—21. in such a way that he first gives the minor premise, at the same time making clear that every sense cognizes all the common sense objects. For every sense, even the most imperfect, perceives, besides its proper sense object, local motion; but if this, then it also perceives extension, shape, rest and number (p. 425, a, 14—19.). Then follows the major premise (p. 425, a, 19.): ἑκάστη γὰρ ἓν αἰσθάνεται αἴσθησις (namely ἓν ἴδιον αἰσθητόν. cf. De Anim. II, 11. §. 2. p. 422, b, 32.). Finally comes the conclusion (p. 425, a, 20—21.), which had also already been stated at the beginning in a somewhat different way (p. 425, a, 13—14.).

[p. 97] Anyone, however, may easily draw the consequences that would result from such an isolation of the sense perceptions for [p. 98] science, for art, for every practical activity, since even the simplest movement would scarcely still be possible. So important, then, is the inner sense—apart from the self-consciousness that it confers on us—merely as the faculty that distinguishes the sense objects of different senses from one another. It is evidently the highest sense, higher even than hearing and sight, and yet, because it is at the same time indispensable for all animals, it is found, as we have already remarked above, even in the lowest species of animals, which share in no other external sense besides touch and taste<sup>56)</sup>. From this we were rightly able to derive an argument for the unity of the sensitive part.

Thereupon he removes an objection, and this part of the discussion is the most important for us (§. 5. p. 425, a, 21. — §. 7. b, 4.). Someone, namely, could reply that the major premise of the syllogism is false, for it contradicts experience, which offers us countless cases in which, through the perception of *one* sense, we cognize the proper sense object of another. Whoever sees water cognizes, together with the color, at the same time its wetness; whoever sees fire, at the same time its warmth; whoever sees honey, at the same time the sweetness of the object seen (§. 5, a, 22.). This objection, then, which he only quite briefly indicates, Aristotle resolves by saying that if we cognize the sweet through the sense of sight, this is possible only because we have, besides the sense of sight, also the sense of taste. For thereby it can happen, he says, that, having earlier already cognized the union of the sweetness with the object seen in consequence of simultaneous perceptions of the sense of sight and the sense of taste, we recall its sweetness on seeing it again (for with Bekker and Trendelenburg one must hold to the reading ἀναγνωρίζομεν §. 6, a, 22—24.). But if such a simultaneous perception has not preceded, then, he says, we cognize (read αἰσθανόμεθα) the sweet by seeing in no other way than we cognize the son of Cleon, in that we see that he is white, but not that he is the son of Cleon (cf. what we remarked in n. 18. about this example), thus merely per accidens (a, 24—27.). This, however, is not how it stands with our knowledge of the common sense objects. It is not by means of recollection and with the help of another sense that we cognize, through seeing and hearing, the local motion of an object etc., but we perceive it per se with each of our senses; and thus the groundlessness of the objection already shows itself (§. 7. a, 27—28.). But it is refuted still more strikingly by the fact that in this case—if, namely, the common sense objects were sensible qualities and the proper objects of senses that we lack—we could have no knowledge of them at all. For only because we have the sense of taste as well, we said, is it possible to cognize the sweetness of an object by the sense of sight (§. 7. a, 28—30.).

After Aristotle has resolved the objection in this way, he adds a closer explanation of the manner in which, through the perception of one sense, we arrive at knowledge of the proper object of another. If someone sees gall and cognizes that it is bitter, then, according to what we have said, a *simultaneous* perception of the sense of sight and the sense of taste must already have preceded. But even this earlier simultaneous perception would not suffice unless the *union* of the visible and the tastable quality had been cognized. Now with which of the two senses did we perceive their union? With neither of them could we perceive it, and we therefore could not have perceived it at all, had not the inner sense perceived the two simultaneous sensations. With it, then, we cognized at that time the union of this color and this taste in the gall, and through it there also now arises in us, on seeing, the knowledge of the bitter taste. §. 7. p. 425, a, 30. τὰ δ᾽ ἀλλήλων ἴδια κατὰ συμβεβηκὸς αἰσθάνονται αἱ αἰσθήσεις, οὐχ ᾗ αὐταί (so we believe we must read, with Torstrik), ἀλλ᾽ ᾗ μία (namely the κοινὴ αἴσθησις, the sense of sensation), ὅταν ἅμα γένηται ἡ αἴσθησις ἐπὶ τοῦ αὐτοῦ, οἷον χολὴν ὅτι πικρὰ καὶ ξανθή·

#### c. Von dem Subjecte der Empfindung. — c. On the Subject of Sensation.

13. All the senses, even this highest, inner sense, are not in the soul, but in the ensouled body as their subject. This already follows from a phenomenon discussed by us earlier, namely from the fact that in many animals, when they are cut up, life and sensation show themselves in both parts, and indeed external sensation as well as sensitive self-consciousness. Out of *one* sentient being two have here come to be, which [p. 99] sensitive soul has multiplied together with the body, which could not possibly have happened if it were something spiritual<sup>57)</sup>.

οὐ γὰρ δὴ ἑτέρας γε τὸ εἰπεῖν ὅτι ἄμφω ἕν· (let it be remarked here in passing that a comparison of these words with De Anim. III, 3. §. 12. p. 428, b, 19. shows that the traditional text τοῦ συμβεβηκέναι ταῦτα is not corrupt) διὸ καὶ ἀπατᾶται καὶ ἐὰν ᾖ ξανθόν, χολὴν οἴεται εἶναι. — Cf. De Memor. et Remin. 1. p. 451, a, 17. and before that p. 450, b, 28.

To this long note we have only still to add 1) a few words on the text De Anim. III, 1. §. 5. p. 425, a, 15., where Torstrik wants to read οὐ κατὰ συμβεβηκός instead of κατὰ συμβεβηκός. We believe that the change is not necessary if one assumes that Aristotle, as Trendelenburg thinks, used κατὰ συμβεβηκός here in a sense other than the usual one. This can be made probable by many other passages, e.g. Metaph. Z, 10. p. 1036, a, 11. (on which see Part IV. n. 107.) and De Anim. III, 3. §. 12, p. 428, b, 24. Cf. Metaph. Θ, 2. p. 1046, b, 13. ibid. A, 1. p. 981, a, 20. and De Anim. I, 1. §. 2. p. 402, a, 15. The κοινὰ αἰσθητά would in this case be designated as perceived κατὰ συμβεβηκός because they are merely secondary sense objects (ἀκολουθοῦντα De Anim. III, 1. §. 8. p. 425, b, 5.), not that first object to which the sense is ordered by nature. — 2) we still find it necessary to make a remark on the words καὶ τοῖς ἰδίοις (De Anim. III, 1. §. 5. p. 425, a, 19.). What Simplicius proposes seems to us best (of whom it is wrongly believed that he read κοινῇ instead of κινήσει at a, 16., since he cites only according to the sense), namely that one take οἷον κινήσεως — συνεχοῦς (a, 15—19.) as a parenthetical clause and think of it as enclosed as if in brackets. κατὰ συμβεβηκὸς καὶ τοῖς ἰδίοις then complete each other into *one* clear expression, and the sense is a perfectly fitting one. — 3) ἀλλ᾽ ἢ οὕτως ὥσπερ — ὁρᾶν (a, 29—30.) is not a useless repetition, though dispensable. — 4) On μέγεθος γάρ τι see n. 15.

56) Cf. De Somn. et Vigil. 2. p. 455, a, (15.) 22.

Further, the same can be shown from the consequences of many sensations. For when a sense has perceived a very vivid sense object—sight, e.g., a very bright light, hearing a very loud sound, the sense of smell a very intense odor—it remains for a time incapable of perceiving weaker objects; indeed it can happen that it is thereby permanently weakened, if not entirely destroyed in its being<sup>58)</sup>. This clearly indicates that the sentient subject is something corporeal and corruptible, that the faculty of sensation is a form mixed with matter, a λόγος ἔνυλος<sup>59)</sup>. A spiritual subject could not have been altered and have suffered harm thereby; rather, its faculty would only have been developed to greater proficiency by such intense acts<sup>60)</sup>.

Finally, we can derive a third proof from the necessary kinship of the sense faculties and the sense objects, and this proof, since it starts from the real ground of sensation, will be the properly apodictic one. We have distinguished a manifold sense object; the foremost among them was the proper sense object, namely that corporeal quality which is the active principle of sensation; for *it* is perceived first and through itself, everything else only with it and through it. Now since the sense, being by its whole nature ordered to this [p. 100] object<sup>61)</sup>, stands to it as the passive principle to its adequate active principle<sup>62)</sup>, it is necessarily akin to it in its essence and cannot be so far exalted above it<sup>62 a)</sup> as it would be in the case where it was immaterial and immortal while the former is corporeal and perishable. But quite especially that object whose sensation is the most agreeable for each of the senses must serve to make its nature and constitution knowable to us. For hearing, e.g., such an object is harmonious concord, and since this consists in a determinate proportion of mixed tones, hearing too will be a determinate proportion in the sentient organ and a mean between extremes. And therefore Aristotle repeatedly calls the faculty of sensation a μεσότης<sup>63)</sup>; for we must say something similar to what we say of hearing of all the other senses as well. With them too there are extremes and a mean and mixture of the extremes which is perceived as most pleasant, and the reason for this can be no other with them too than that the mixed sensation corresponds to the sense faculty more than that of the pure extremes. The nature of the sense furnishes the measure for the sensations. If this measure is exceeded too greatly in the one direction or the other, the sense is offended and injured. Therefore not only disharmonious tones but also tones that are too shrill or too dull offend the ear, seeing in too great brightness or darkness ruins the eyes, and the too sweet or bitter affects the sense of taste, the too hot or cold the sense of touch, unpleasantly<sup>64)</sup>. A sense motion which is too [p. 101] powerful for the organ dissolves its proportion, just as a harp, too, goes out of tune and the concord of its strings is dissolved when they are struck too violently<sup>65)</sup>. From all this, then, it clearly follows<sup>66)</sup> that the subject of the senses and of the sense activities must be not the soul alone but the ensouled body.

57) See above Part I. n. 42.

58) De Anim. III, 4. §. 5. p. 429, a, 29. ibid. II, 12. §. 3. p. 424, a, 28.

59) De Anim. I, 1. §. 10. p. 403, a, 25.

60) Cf. what will be discussed on this in Part IV. n. 8. — The proof we have given here is not entirely conclusive, because even if the subject of sensation were spiritual, our faculty of sensation could be impaired, or even rendered incapable of any perception, through the corruption of the mediating bodily organs. Indeed, the capacity for color images of the imagination which persists after blindness caused by too violent impressions of light proves that the sense faculty has at any rate not been entirely destroyed; for, as we shall see presently, the presentations of the imagination are in the corresponding perceiving powers. But the imagination and memory too, which forms a part of the imagination, suffer and, as is well known, regularly decline, especially in old age. Thus the proper subject of sensation is evidently also subject to alteration and something bodily (De Anim. I, 4. §. 14. p. 408, b, 25. ibid. III, 5. §. 2. p. 430, a, 23.) To this is added that after very vivid sense perceptions we are not merely, for the time immediately following, less capable of other perceptions of the same genus, but also do not have the same power over our imagination as otherwise. The color appearance remains, or rather transforms itself according to special physiological laws, and hinders or at least disturbs the voluntary presentations. Something similar holds of sounds, of the melodies which, as we say, we cannot get rid of, and the like (cf. De Mem. et Rem. 2. p. 453, a, 28.)

61) See above n. 11.

62) De Sens. et Sens. 6. p. 445, b, 7. cf. De Anim. III, 2. §. 4—8. p. 425, b, 25 — p. 426, a, 27.

62 a) It is not the passive principle that is exalted above the active, but the active above the passive. De Anim. III, 5. §. 2. p. 430, a, 18. ἀεὶ γὰρ τιμιώτερον τὸ ποιοῦν τοῦ πάσχοντος καὶ ἡ ἀρχὴ τῆς ὕλης.

63) E.g. De Anim. II, 11. §. 11. p. 424, a, 4. ibid. 12. §. 4. p. 424, b, 1. III, 7. §. 2. p. 431, a, 11. 19. and 13. §. 1. p. 435, a, 21. In the passages where he speaks more precisely, he designates not the faculty but the subject of sensation as μεσότης.

64) De Anim. III, 2. §. 9. p. 426, a, 27. εἰ δ᾽ ἡ συμφωνία (harmonious concord, which, as is well known, is the most pleasant of everything audible) φωνή τις (a determinate mode of a sound, cf. μέγεθός τι, n. 15.; our text is therefore probably not to be regarded as corrupt), ἡ δὲ φωνὴ καὶ ἡ ἀκοὴ ἔστιν ὡς ἕν ἐστι καὶ ἔστιν ὡς οὐχ ἓν τὸ αὐτό (as has been shown in the preceding), λόγος δ᾽ ἡ συμφωνία, ἀνάγκη καὶ τὴν ἀκοὴν λόγον τινὰ εἶναι. καὶ διὰ τοῦτο καὶ φθείρει ἕκαστον ὑπερβάλλον, καὶ τὸ ὀξὺ καὶ τὸ βαρύ, τὴν ἀκοήν· ὁμοίως δὲ καὶ ἐν χυμοῖς τὴν γεῦσιν, καὶ ἐν χρώμασι τὴν ὄψιν τὸ σφόδρα λαμπρὸν ἢ ζοφερόν, καὶ ἐν ὀσφρήσει ἡ ἰσχυρὰ ὀσμὴ καὶ γλυκεῖα καὶ πικρά, ὡς λόγου τινὸς ὄντος τῆς αἰσθήσεως. διὸ καὶ ἡδέα μέν, ὅταν εἰλικρινῆ καὶ ἀμιγῆ ἄγηται εἰς τὸν λόγον, οἷον τὸ ὀξὺ ἢ γλυκὺ ἢ ἁλμυρόν· ἡδέα γὰρ τότε. ὅλως δὲ μᾶλλον

14. With this, however, the further question is at once connected whether, since the body of an animal has many members, all, or only some, or perhaps only one single part of the animal organism is the bearer of sensation. That *all* perceive, hardly anyone will wish to maintain, since this evidently and, so to speak, palpably contradicts experience. On the other hand, many—and among them, naturally, all those who think that the eye sees and the ear hears, etc.—assume at least a *certain* plurality of sentient organs. Aristotle, however, rejected this view too. According to him the sensitive part is a single one as regards its subject, and as the different radii of a circle meet in *one* center, so too the heterogeneous actions of sensible qualities ultimately arrive at a single organ, which alone has that special constitution that is required for sensation. We have already made mention of this earlier and at the same time indicated some reasons which were decisive for Aristotle in this assertion<sup>67)</sup>. In part they were of a teleological kind, in part they rested on the observation of phenomena which refuted the common view that the external organs are the sentient ones and made the unity of the sentient subject at least probable. Aristotle did not appeal to the consideration that, if the sensations were not all in one organ, one being would be what sees, another what hears, etc., and he could not do so either, since according to him the whole body of the living being belongs to one and the same substance. Hence, just as in man, although sensing is something bodily but intellectual thinking something spiritual, it is nevertheless one and the same thing that thinks and senses, so too, [p. 102] if seeing were found in the one organ and hearing in the other, one and the same thing would nevertheless see and hear. This reason, then, Aristotle did not overlook; rather, given his whole fundamental psychological outlook, it was not applicable for him. Those, on the other hand, which he does adduce are still worthy of attention even today and are in no way impaired by the fact that, on the strength of further conjectures, he held the heart to be this unitary seat of sensation, a view which, as is well known, has long since proved erroneous with the further development of physiology.

τὸ μικτὸν συμφωνία ἢ τὸ ὀξὺ ἢ βαρύ, ἁφῇ δὲ τὸ θερμαντὸν ἢ ψυκτόν. ἡ δ᾽ αἴσθησις ὁ λόγος· ὑπερβάλλοντα δὲ λυπεῖ ἢ φθείρει.

II, 12. §. 2. p. 424, a, 24. αἰσθητήριον δὲ πρῶτον ἐν ᾧ ἡ τοιαύτη δύναμις. ἔστι μὲν οὖν ταὐτόν τὸ δ᾽ εἶναι ἕτερον· μέγεθος μὲν γὰρ ἄν τι εἴη τὸ αἰσθανόμενον· οὐ μὴν τό γε αἰσθητικῷ εἶναι οὐδ᾽ ἡ αἴσθησις μέγεθός ἐστιν, ἀλλὰ λόγος τις καὶ δύναμις ἐκείνου. φανερὸν δὲ ἐκ τούτων καὶ διὰ τί ποτε τῶν αἰσθητῶν αἱ ὑπερβολαὶ φθείρουσι τὰ αἰσθητήρια· ἐὰν γὰρ ᾖ ἰσχυροτέρα τοῦ αἰσθητηρίου ἡ κίνησις, λύεται ὁ λόγος, τοῦτο δ᾽ ἦν ἡ αἴσθησις, ὥσπερ καὶ ἡ συμφωνία καὶ ὁ τόνος κρουομένων σφόδρα τῶν χορδῶν.

66) Aristotle holds this to be so evident that he thinks it really needs no proof. De Sens. et Sens. 1. p. 436. b, 6.

67) See above n. 35.

#### d. Von der Phantasie. — d. On the Imagination.

15. Sensing in the proper sense is not the only operation of the sensitive part through which it participates in foreign forms, for even without sense perception we have sensible forms in us objectively (as presented)<sup>68)</sup><sup>69)</sup>. They are called phantasms, and the capacity to have phantasms in oneself is called imagination<sup>70)</sup>. The phantasms, considered in and for themselves, differ in nothing from the images which are present in us during sense perception<sup>71)</sup>, and as these divide into different genera according as they are perceived by means of the eye, or the ear, or another sense instrument, and according as color, or sound, or another proper sense object is the active principle for them, so the phantasms too divide into several genera wholly corresponding to those<sup>72)</sup>; there are phantasms in which color, others in which sound, others in which another sensible peculiarity forms the fundamental determination. There are also phantasms of the peculiar object of that inner sense directed upon the sensations themselves, and in particular we have phantasms of this kind in every recollection, for one recollects having seen or heard something earlier, and the like<sup>73)</sup>, thus recollects an earlier seeing or hearing, and without these acts now really existing and being able to be perceived, we have the presentation of them in us.

Now since the phantasms and the sensations are wholly the same, they are evidently also in the same potencies and in the same subject. The phantasms are thus in the senses and in the first sense organ as such<sup>74)</sup>.

69) De Anim. III, 3. §. 7. p. 428, a, 7. 15. cf. De Insomn. 1. p. 459, a, 15.

70) De Anim. III, 3. §. 6. p. 428, a, 1.

71) De Somn. et Vigil. 2. p. 456, a, 26.

72) De Anim. III, 3. §. 11. p. 428, b, 11. ἡ δὲ φαντασία . . . δοκεῖ εἶναι . . . ὧν αἴσθησίς ἐστιν. cf. below n. 74.

73) De Memor. et Remin. 1. p. 450, b, 20. f. especially p. 451, a, 5.

74) De Insomn. 1. p. 459, a, 1. ἆρ᾽ οὖν τὸ μὲν μηδὲν ὁρᾶν (ἐν τῷ ὕπνῳ) ἀληθές,

[p. 103] 16. But in what, then, does the difference between imagination and sensation consist, which we ourselves after all had to acknowledge? Both are motions of the same senses<sup>75)</sup>, a being affected of the same kind; but they differ in this, that (since every being affected is an acting) sensation is the action of the present sensible object, whereas imagination has its ground in earlier sensations<sup>76)</sup>.

For the motion which the sensible object produces often continues, even when the sensible object no longer acts, in further similar motions. As the air goes on sounding even when the ringing bell has fallen silent, so too the tone can go on resounding in the sense instruments (in the secondary ones and in the first)<sup>77)</sup> and be heard persistingly in the ultimate sense organ<sup>78)</sup>. Often, to be sure, a motion of the imagination does not follow immediately upon the sense perception, but even then it is a motion through the sense motion, in that the latter has made a lasting impression on the sense organ and left behind in it such a constitution, such a permanent disposition<sup>79)</sup>, [p. 104] by virtue of which, under certain circumstances, and in particular when another sense presentation acts as a stimulus, the earlier sensible form returns in the sense. Whatever appears in the imagination has earlier, though perhaps in other combinations, been received through a sense perception.

τὸ δὲ μηδὲν πάσχειν τὴν αἴσθησιν οὐκ ἀληθές, ἀλλ᾽ ἐνδέχεται καὶ τὴν ὄψιν πάσχειν τι καὶ τὰς ἄλλας αἰσθήσεις, ἕκαστον δὲ τούτων ὥσπερ ἐγρηγορότος προσβάλλει μέν πως τῇ αἰσθήσει, οὐχ οὕτω δὲ ὥσπερ ἐγρηγορότος· καὶ ὁτὲ μὲν ἡ δόξα λέγει ὅτι ψεῦδος τὸ ὁρώμενον, ὥσπερ ἐγρηγορόσιν, ὁτὲ δὲ κατέχεται καὶ ἀκολουθεῖ τῷ φαντάσματι. De Anim. I, 4. §. 12. p. 408, b, 17.

75) Although the *faculty* of imagination too is *conceptually* to be distinguished from that of sense perception (cf. de Anim. III, 3. §. 7. p. 428, a, 5. Insomn. 1. p. 459, a, 16.), because the acts of imagination are different from the sense perceptions, and the potencies are conceptually determined according to the acts (cf. De Anim. II, 2. §. 10. p. 413, b, 29.). In some passages Aristotle seems to doubt that all animals have imagination, i.e., that all animals can present their sense images after the sensation. So, e.g., in the one just named (III, 3. §. 7.). But this is not his real opinion (cf. De Anim. II, 2. §. 8. p. 413, b, 22. ibid. III, 11. §. 1. p. 434, a, 4.); even to those animals which are deprived of the higher senses he grants an imagination, albeit an imperfect one. But even if not all animals had imagination, we would not on that account have to regard sense and imagination as different faculties; for it would by no means follow that in those in which a presentation remains after the sense perception, it does not remain in the perceiving faculty, but only that some perceiving faculties are so imperfect in their kind that they cannot retain the presentation of the object any longer than the object acts on them.

76) De Anim. III, 3. §. 13. p. 429, a, 1. ἡ φαντασία ἂν εἴη κίνησις ὑπὸ τῆς αἰσθήσεως τῆς κατ᾽ ἐνέργειαν γιγνομένη. cf. §. 11. p. 428, b, 10.

78) Aristotle compares this phenomenon to the continued motion of a body after an impact. De Insomn. 2. p. 458, a, 28.

79) Aristotle calls it ἕξις De Memor. et Remin. 1. p. 450, a, 30. ibid. 2. p. 451, b, 3., though not in the ordinary sense of an acquired proficiency such as, e.g., knowledge is, of which more below.

As an after-effect of sense perception, imagination is weaker than the latter, and Aristotle therefore calls it a weak sensation<sup>80)</sup>; likewise deception is also more frequent and more manifold in imagination<sup>81)</sup>.

Because of the similarity of the phantasms to the sense perceptions, they also move appetite in the absence of the sensible object, as the latter move it in its presence, and therefore Aristotle says that beings living sensitively let themselves be guided in their conduct in many ways by their imaginings: the animals always, because they lack reason, but men when their reason is veiled by passion or illness or by sleep<sup>82)</sup>.

#### e. Von dem sinnlichen Begehren und der willkürlichen Bewegung des Leibes. — e. On Sensory Appetite and the Voluntary Movement of the Body.

17. Herein the dependence of the sensorily appetitive and the moving faculty on the sentient ones is already indicated. We shall touch on both only with a brief word, and even this will in part be a mere repetition of what we already discussed earlier when we spoke of the parts of the soul in general, since a further treatment would depart from the purpose of our treatise.

We have already remarked above that the sensitive soul shares in the genus of the striving vital powers, and that in every sentient being an appetitive faculty is found<sup>83)</sup>. We have further also recognized the reason for this phenomenon and for the difference that exists in this respect between the sensitive soul and the vegetative soul, which is restricted to a single genus of vital powers: because the object of its activity is innate to the latter as regards likeness, it has no form-receiving vital powers, and for precisely this reason that impulse which follows the natural form also takes the place of the striving vital activities<sup>84)</sup>.

80) Rhetor. 1, 11. p. 1370, a, 28. De Insomn. 3. p. 460, b, 32. and p. 461, a, 18.

81) Cf. De Anim. III, 3. §. 7. p. 428, a, 11. and esp. ibid. §. 12. p. 428. b, 18.

82) De Anim. III, 3. §. 15. p. 429, a, 4. cf. ibid. 10. §. 1. p. 433, a, 9. and this whole chapter as well as the following one.

83) De Anim. II, 2. §. 8. p. 413, b, 23. ibid. 3. §. 2. p. 414, b, 1.

84) Cf. the relevant discussions in the first part of this Section (n. 16.).

[p. 105] Because of the belonging-together of sensation and sensory appetite, of which the one is as necessarily found with the other as the natural form with the natural impulse, it is evident that the subject of sensory appetite too must be not the soul alone but the ensouled body, and indeed the same organ that is the subject of sensation<sup>85)</sup>. Besides this, the reasons adduced with respect to sensation can all be used in the same way to show that the sensorily appetitive power is mixed with the body; indeed, we can here appeal to many phenomena which show the co-affection of the body, if possible, still more plainly and strikingly than in the case of sensation<sup>86)</sup>.

18. We have so far spoken of the sensorily appetitive faculty as of a unity. This is in contradiction with the view of some older and more recent interpreters of our philosopher, who think that Aristotle, following Plato, distinguished a twofold appetitive faculty of the sensitive part, namely the faculty of desire (ἐπιθυμία) and the angrily striving power (θυμός). To us this view does not seem probable, both because of some statements in the books On the Soul and on more general grounds, because of the whole manner in which Aristotle is accustomed to judge about the unity and plurality of powers.

In the ninth chapter of the third book On the Soul<sup>87)</sup> he says that the faculty of imagination differs from that of sensation more than the faculty of desire differs from the faculty that strives with angry spirit. Now since it has resulted from our earlier discussions that, according to his doctrine, the presentations of the imagination are in the same senses which grasp the sensible forms through perception, it can, as it seems to us, be recognized clearly enough from these words that according to him we may not regard desire and anger either as activities of two different powers.

In another passage of the same book Aristotle says that pleasure and desire and the sensory excitation opposed to the one and the other are affections of the same faculty<sup>88)</sup>. [p. 106] But if pleasure or displeasure, for which nowadays one mostly assumes a special faculty, namely the so-called feeling, is according to Aristotle an affection of the same capacity in which sensory desire is found as another affection, then it is certainly highly improbable that he should have held it necessary to assume a special power for angry excitement, which seems to stand nearer to each of the two than the one to the other.

85) De Anim. III, 7. §. 2. p. 431, a, 13. οὐχ ἕτερον τὸ ὀρεκτικὸν καὶ φευκτικόν, οὔτ᾽ ἀλλήλων οὔτε τοῦ αἰσθητικοῦ· ἀλλὰ τὸ εἶναι ἄλλο. Cf. what immediately precedes. n. 88.

86) Cf. De Anim. I, 1. §. 10. p 403, a, 16—27.

87) De Anim. III, 9. §. 2. f. p. 432, a, 22. cf. ibid. 10. §. 5. p. 433, a, 31.

88) De Anim. III, 7. §. 2. p. 431, a, 8. τὸ μὲν οὖν αἰσθάνεσθαι ὅμοιον τῷ φάναι μόνον καὶ νοεῖν· ὅταν δὲ ἡδὺ ἢ λυπηρόν, οἷον καταφᾶσα ἢ ἀποφᾶσα, διώκει ἢ φεύγει· καὶ *ἔστι τὸ ἥδεσθαι καὶ λυπεῖσθαι τὸ ἐνεργεῖν τῇ αἰσθητικῇ μεσότητι πρὸς τὸ ἀγαθὸν ἢ κακόν, ᾗ τοιαῦτα. καὶ ἡ φυγὴ δὲ καὶ ἡ ὄρεξις τοῦτο* ἡ κατ᾽ ἐνέργειαν. Cf. also below n. 103.

But we are confirmed in this opinion by still other considerations as well. First, it speaks in favor of it that all sensitive striving is active in immediate dependence on one and the same form-receiving faculty, namely on the inner sense, as plainly follows from the fact that, even if the οὗ ἕνεκα οὗ (that which one desires) is, in the striving of the sensitive part, something foreign to the one striving, the οὗ ἕνεκα ᾧ (that for which one desires something)<sup>89)</sup> can nevertheless be nothing other than the one striving itself, for which reason such a striving would not be conceivable without self-consciousness<sup>90)</sup>.

Moreover, all motions of the sensory affections are called forth by presentations of what is sensibly good or bad, pleasant or unpleasant, although in manifold mixtures and gradations<sup>91)</sup>. Their diversity is not greater than the diversity of colors is, and hence, just as the latter does not suffice to abolish the unity of the sensible object and consequently that of the sense of sight, so too, despite the manifoldness of what excites the affections, the appetible object and thus the sensorily desiring faculty will remain a single one; for, as has already been remarked more than once, the unity of the faculty always depends on the unity of the proper object.

To this it should be added that, if there were several faculties of the sensory affections, a plurality of such motions could take place in us simultaneously<sup>92)</sup>. But this is never the [p. 107] case; at least such a plurality can in no case be demonstrated; only, to be sure, observation is uncertain here, and it can easily happen that what is only a mixed affection is taken for a double affection, and vice versa. Therefore the earlier reasons might carry greater power of conviction.

89) Cf. above, Part II. n. 17.

90) Cf. De Anim. III, 7. §. 6. p. 431, b, 12., where it is said, in the first instance with regard to the intelligible good, that it differs from other things that we know τῷ ἁπλῶς καὶ τινί.

91) Of ἐπιθυμία Aristotle says, De Anim. II, 3. §. 2. p. 414, b, 6., that it is the ὄρεξις τοῦ ἡδέος. cf. Eth. Nicom. III, 4. p. 1111, b, 15. Instead of θυμός he uses, Rhetor. I, 10. p. 1369, a, 2., the expression ὀργή, of which he says, De Anim. I, 1. §. 11. p. 403, a, 30., that it is the ὄρεξις ἀντιλυπήσεως ἤ τι τοιοῦτον. But revenge too is without doubt sweet in a certain way, even though the pleasure in it is not pure and unmixed. When Aristotle, in the passage from the third book On the Soul cited in n. 88., equates the sensible good and bad with the ἡδύ and λυπηρόν and designates it as the object of sensory ὄρεξις, he seems to comprehend under it both ἐπιθυμία and θυμός together.

92) It would be similar to the case of sense presentations, for which the passage from De Sens. et Sens. 7. is to be compared, above n. 35.

The reasons which the contrary view can adduce in its favor are easily refuted. They are chiefly the following two: First, Aristotle several times lists rational willing, desire, and anger side by side in a way that makes them appear as if standing on the same line<sup>93)</sup>. But willing, as we shall see later, is something spiritual, and therefore not merely another exercise of the same power as desire; thus, according to Aristotle, a special faculty seems to correspond to angry excitement as well.

This objection, however, loses all probative force if one attends to other passages where, as for example in the third chapter of the third book On the Soul<sup>94)</sup>, sense perception, opinion, knowledge, and insight (i.e., knowledge of the principles) likewise appear placed in one series, although the last three are in the intellect, while the first is in the senses. Likewise we find imagination, whose faculty according to Aristotle is not really distinct from that of sense perception, sometimes named beside sensation and at the same time beside such activities as are not in the same faculty<sup>95)</sup>.

Still less weight belongs to a second argument, which relies on the fact that in the Nicomachean Ethics<sup>96)</sup> a twofold dominion of the passions (ἀκρασία) is distinguished, one of the desires (τῶν ἐπιθυμιῶν) and one of the angry outbursts (τοῦ θυμοῦ). From this, we say, one cannot infer a duality of faculties, for the reason that in other and probably in all powers too there can be different habits for different acts. Thus, for example, the skill in the movements of the zither player is a different one from the skill in the movements of the draftsman, and the habitual knowledge of the mathematician a different one from physical science.

But however others may interpret Aristotle’s statements differently on this point, this much at any rate is certain: that Aristotle thought of all sensory appetite as united in one and the same subject, namely in the central organ of sensitive life<sup>97)</sup>, [p. 108] and it is this determination that is chiefly of importance for us.

94) De Anim. III, 3. §. 6. p. 428, a, 4.

95) E.g. De Anim. III, 9. §. 3. p. 432, a, 31.

96) Eth. Nicom. VII, 7.

97) The concupiscible power is in it according to the already

19. Most closely connected with sensory appetite is the faculty of the conscious movements of the body, of which Aristotle in the books On the Soul has treated in any detail only local movement, as the most eminent. But what holds of it must essentially hold also for the movements of the mouth and palate in the taking of food, as well as for other partial movements of the limbs that are voluntary in a similar way.

Just as, in the consideration of sensation, psychology is interested less in its secondary organs than in its primary organ, and less in its mediating faculties than in the power that properly senses, so too, with regard to the movements of the body, the question of their first and proper principle is of more importance to it than the question of their mediating instruments<sup>98)</sup>. Is this principle, it asks, something spiritual or is it something bodily? and if the latter, is it distinct in subject from the faculties of sense perception and appetite, or is it united with them and perhaps even one and the same with one or the other of them?

Now that it is something bodily is evident, since such a moving power is found not only in man but also in the animals, which have no share in spirit<sup>98 a)</sup>, and it is likewise clear that it must be in the same organ in which the faculties of sensory appetite and of sensation are, [p. 109] <sup>99)</sup>, since every conscious and voluntary movement indeed stems from a cognizing and a desiring<sup>100)</sup>. But that the moving power should be identical with one of these two faculties is not possible, if indeed the proposition is correct that the concept and essence of a potency are determined by its acts, and that activities of different genus presuppose a difference of powers; for the sensitive affections, and all the more the sensations, are without any question different in genus from the movements of the body and its limbs.

cited passage De Anim. III, 7. §. 2. (see above n. 88.), and the irascible power, which must be thought of as included at this passage too, according to De Anim. I, 1. §. 11. p. 403, a, 31.; for Aristotle held the heart to be the sensitive central organ.

98) De Anim. III, 10. §. 7. p. 433, b, 19. ᾧ δὲ κινεῖ ὀργάνῳ ἡ ὄρεξις, ἤδη τοῦτο σωματικόν ἐστιν· διὸ ἐν τοῖς κοινοῖς σώματος καὶ ψυχῆς ἔργοις θεωρητέον περὶ αὐτοῦ. He then adds the following brief remark on the propagation of movement from the first moving organ: νῦν δὲ ὡς ἐν κεφαλαίῳ εἰπεῖν, τὸ κινοῦν ὀργανικῶς ὅπου ἀρχὴ καὶ τελευτὴ τὸ αὐτό, οἷον ὁ γιγγλυμός· ἐνταῦθα γὰρ τὸ κυρτὸν καὶ κοῖλον τὸ μὲν τελευτὴ τὸ δ᾽ ἀρχή· διὸ τὸ μὲν ἠρεμεῖ τὸ δὲ κινεῖται, λόγῳ μὲν ἕτερα ὄντα, μεγέθει δ᾽ ἀχώριστα· πάντα γὰρ ὤσει καὶ ἕλξει κινεῖται. διὸ δεῖ ὥσπερ ἐν κύκλῳ μένειν τι, καὶ ἐντεῦθεν ἄρχεσθαι τὴν κίνησιν. In De Mot. Animal., where this is explained more fully in the eighth chapter, there is found, 10. p. 703, a, 29., the well-known beautiful comparison with the state: ὑποληπτέον δὲ συνεστάναι τὸ ζῷον ὥσπερ πόλιν εὐνομουμένην. ἔν τε γὰρ τῇ πόλει ὅταν ἅπαξ στῇ ἡ τάξις, οὐδὲν δεῖ κεχωρισμένου μονάρχου, ὃν δεῖ παρεῖναι παρ᾽ ἕκαστον τῶν γινομένων, ἀλλ᾽ αὐτὸς ἕκαστος ποιεῖ τὰ αὑτοῦ ὡς τέτακται, καὶ γίνεται τόδε μετὰ τόδε διὰ τὸ ἔθος· ἐν δὲ τοῖς ζῴοις τὸ αὐτὸ τοῦτο διὰ τὴν φύσιν γίνεται καὶ τῷ πεφυκέναι ἕκαστον οὕτω συστάντων ποιεῖν τὸ αὑτοῦ ἔργον, ὥστε μηδὲν δεῖν ἐν ἑκάστῳ εἶναι ψυχήν, ἀλλ᾽ ἔν τινι ἀρχῇ τοῦ σώματος οὔσης τἄλλα ζῆν μὲν τῷ προσπεφυκέναι, ποιεῖν δὲ τὸ ἔργον τὸ αὑτῶν διὰ τὴν φύσιν.

98 a) De Part. Animal. I, 1. p. 641, b, 4,

Nonetheless, in a certain sense one can call the capacity to desire one and the same with the capacity to produce such movements<sup>101)</sup>; namely, if one wishes to designate by this name not the faculty but the original disposition to the one or the other operation. For the appetitive faculty is something passive<sup>102)</sup> and therefore a potentiality, the potentiality of the sensory affections, whereas the moving faculty is something active and therefore an actuality, and this actuality is none other than an act of the appetitive faculty itself. For when desire has been added to the sensory presentation, movement follows from it, inasmuch as the form received in the sense acts as end, while desire acts as moving cause<sup>103)</sup>, from which two principles, [p. 110] according to the general doctrines of Aristotelian physics, the effect itself proceeds.

99) See the passage De Anim. III, 10. cited in the preceding note. The ὥσπερ ἐν κύκλῳ μένον is the heart. De Somn. et Vigil. 2. p. 455, b, 34. ὅτι μὲν οὖν ἡ τῆς αἰσθήσεως ἀρχὴ γίνεται ἀπὸ τοῦ αὐτοῦ μέρους τοῖς ζῴοις ἀφ᾽ οὗ περ καὶ ἡ τῆς κινήσεως διώρισται πρότερον ἐν ἑτέροις. κ. τ. λ. De Part. Animal. III, 4. p. 666, a, 11. and elsewhere. Hence he says of the heart, because all the receptive, appetitive, and moving powers are united in it: ἡ δὲ καρδία ... οἷον ζῷόν τι πέφυκεν ἐν τοῖς ἔχουσιν. In the animals that have no heart, the same then of course holds of the analogue of the heart.

100) De Anim. III, 10. §. 1. p. 433, a, 9. φαίνεται δέ γε δύο ταῦτα κινοῦντα, ἢ ὄρεξις ἢ νοῦς, εἴ τις τὴν φαντασίαν τιθείη ὡς νόησίν τινα· .... ἄμφω ἄρα ταῦτα κινητικὰ κατὰ τόπον, νοῦς καὶ ὄρεξις.

101) De Anim. III, 10. §. 6. p. 433, b, 10. εἴδει μὲν ἓν ἂν εἴη τὸ κινοῦν, τὸ ὀρεκτικόν, ᾗ ὀρεκτικόν. ibid. §. 9. a, 27. ὅλως μὲν οὖν, ὥσπερ εἴρηται, ᾗ ὀρεκτικὸν τὸ ζῷον, ταύτῃ αὑτοῦ κινητικόν.

102) For the desires are in it; cf. De Anim. III, 2. §. 5. p. 426, a, 4. ἡ γὰρ τοῦ ποιητικοῦ καὶ κινητικοῦ ἐνέργεια ἐν τῷ πάσχοντι ἐγγίνεται. ibid. 10. §. 7. p. 433, b, 17. κινεῖται γὰρ τὸ ὀρεγόμενον (for this, as Torstrik rightly says, and as Trendelenburg was already inclined to hold, is the correct reading) ᾗ ὀρέγεται, καὶ ἡ ὄρεξις κίνησίς τίς ἐστιν ἡ ἐνεργείᾳ (corr. Torstrik).

103) De Anim. III, 10. §. 2. p. 433, a, 18. τὸ ὀρεκτικὸν (for this reading, which is found in all manuscripts with the exception of a single one and is also the reading of Simplicius, is the correct one) γὰρ κινεῖ, καὶ διὰ τοῦτο ἡ διάνοια κινεῖ, ὅτι ἀρχὴ αὐτῆς ἐστι τὸ ὀρεκτόν. καὶ ἡ φαντασία δὲ ὅταν κινῇ, οὐ κινεῖ ἄνευ ὀρέξεως. ἓν δή τι τὸ κινοῦν, τὸ ὀρεκτικόν (as Torstrik rightly reads). ibid. §. 7. p. 433, b, 14. τὸ δὲ κινοῦν διττόν, τὸ μὲν ἀκίνητον, τὸ δὲ κινοῦν καὶ κινούμενον· ἔστι δὲ τὸ μὲν ἀκίνητον

To be sure, not every sensory affection will have a movement as its consequence, just as not every form received in the sense will excite an affection. For this to be the case, what is presented must be in some way pleasant or unpleasant to the one presenting it<sup>104)</sup>, and for a movement of the body then to follow further, it must at the same time appear as something attainable through that movement<sup>105)</sup>. If these two conditions are present, and the affection has set in, then the movement takes place, unless a morbid disposition<sup>106)</sup> or an external hindrance stands in its way, with the same necessity as the effect of unconscious impulse in disposed matter<sup>107)</sup>.

20. But how can this last remark be reconciled with the fact that only the weak man, enslaved by his own passion, carries into execution everything that his desire demands, whereas the virtuous man, however a passion may stir in him, does not act as long as reason forbids him to act<sup>108)</sup>? Does it not seem to follow from this that the movement of the body stands in no more intimate relation to the sensory powers than to the spiritual ones, indeed that the relation to the latter is by nature even the more intimate, since it is plainly precisely the virtuous man who acts in accordance with nature, and incontinence and licentiousness must be regarded as a corruption of nature<sup>109)</sup>. How, then, can our assertions that the moving power belongs to the sensitive part, and that its efficient principle is a sensory affection, be brought into harmony with such phenomena? — The answer to this follows very simply from the fact that the spiritual part, although it does not itself have within it the immediate principle of local movement, nevertheless possesses an influence upon it, for the reason that it can now excite, now suppress the sensory affections, or modify them in such a way that a movement no longer proceeds from them. [p. 111] Hence, just as according to the ancient view of the world the movement of the higher sphere carries along that of the lower, so the higher appetite of man by nature carries the lower along with it and thereby mediately determines the movement of the body as well, which is comparable to the movement of a third sphere<sup>110)</sup>.

τὸ πρακτὸν ἀγαθόν, τὸ δὲ κινοῦν καὶ κινούμενον τὸ ὀρεκτικόν. Because according to Aristotle there is only *one* sensorily appetitive faculty, while its desires are many, he says in the passage cited in n. 101. that in kind the moving principle is a single one, but in number they are many.

104) Cf. De Anim. III. 10. §. 6. p. 433, b, 7.

105) De Anim. III, 10. §. 4. p. 433, a, 27.

106) Eth. Nicom. I, 13. p. 1102, b, 18.

107) Cf. Metaph. Θ, 5. p. 1048, a, 5—21.

108) De Anim. III, 9. §. 9. p. 433, a, 6. ἀλλὰ μὴν οὐδ᾽ ἡ ὄρεξις ταύτης κυρία τῆς κινήσεως· οἱ γὰρ ἐγκρατεῖς ὀρεγόμενοι καὶ ἐπιθυμοῦντες οὐ πράττουσιν ὧν ἔχουσι τὴν ὄρεξιν, ἀλλ᾽ ἀκολουθοῦσι τῷ νῷ.

109) Polit. I, 5. p. 1254, a, 36. (see the following note.)

110) De Anim.
