III, 11. §. 3. p. 434, a, 12. we find the following words, which have caused the interpreters much difficulty: νικᾷ δ᾽ ἐνίοτε καὶ κινεῖ (ἡ ὄρεξις) τὴν βούλησιν, ὁτὲ δ᾽ ἐκείνη ταύτην, ὥσπερ σφαῖρα σφαῖραν (as Torstrik, not without good reason, proposes to read instead of the bare σφαῖρα or σφαῖραν), ἡ ὄρεξις τὴν ὄρεξιν, ὅταν ἀκρασία γένηται· (the ἀκρατής is, as we see from the Nicomachean Ethics, the weak man, who has indeed not made it his principle to strive for pleasure as the highest good and to sacrifice everything else to it (cf. Eth. Nicom, VII, 9. p. 1151, a, 11. and the ἀκόλαστος ibid. 8. p. 1150, a, 19.), and who for that reason also does not always and in every case give himself over to the passions at the expense of reason (he is indeed εὐμετάπειστος ibid. 9. p. 1151. a, 14. cf. the end of the chap.), but who nevertheless frequently, when the lure of temptation grows more powerful, lets himself be overcome and consents to it. In him, therefore, now sensory appetite defeats rational appetite, now rational appetite again gains the upper hand. The victorious one carries the defeated one along with it, as one celestial sphere in its movement carries the other along with it) φύσει δὲ ἀεὶ ἡ ἄνω ἀρχικωτέρα καὶ κινεῖ. (Although of the two spheres the lower often moves the higher, as has just been remarked, this dominion of the lower over the higher is nevertheless contrary to nature; by nature the higher is always the more ruling and the moving one). ὥστε τρεῖς φορὰς ἤδη κινεῖσθαι. It is these words which, although what precedes was not entirely free of difficulties either, have caused the interpreters the greatest perplexity. Two of the movements are, to be sure, easy to specify, but what, people asked themselves, is the third movement supposed to be? The interpretations diverge widely here; our own we have already indicated, and if one attends to the context and the purpose of the whole passage, we do not doubt that it will be given preference over the earlier ones. — The question from which Aristotle started and at which his whole investigation, which closes with the end of this chapter, aims is that of the principle of local movement, in particular of the movement of man. Local movement in man is really composed of three movements. For, first, walking is a continued falling; second, besides bodily weight there acts the influence of the sensitive soul and its appetite; finally, since sensitive appetite is in turn subject to the influence of the intellective part, spiritual appetite, the will, becomes, third, a co-determining factor. Thus, when man acts rationally, we have to distinguish, as it were, three spheres superordinated and subordinated to one another, each following one of which has a more complicated movement, since a new moving factor is added in it: rational soul, sensitive soul, and body. Cf. De Coel. II, 12. p. 293, a, 6. On other interpretations the whole comparison remains highly defective and obscure. Those of the older commentators Trendelenburg has already refuted, on the passage; but with his own we should not like to agree, apart from other reasons, because it is impossible to assume that Aristotle is speaking here of a struggle of sensory desires with one another. There is not even the trace of an indication of this;

[p. 112] But in what way this is possible, since sensory appetite after all follows the forms received in the senses, the consideration of the spiritual powers, to which we shall pass at once, will give us information; for it will show us that the spiritual part has a power by which it is able freely to move and transform the presentations of the imagination<sup>111)</sup>. Before we speak of this active principle, however, we must, as we did with the sensitive part, first treat of the form-receiving spiritual faculty.

for that spiritual appetite could not be called ὄρεξις is incorrect, as, besides many other passages, the two preceding chapters clearly show. Thus he says, 10. §. 6. p. 433, b, 5., ἐπεὶ δ᾽ ὀρέξεις γίνονται ἐναντίαι ἀλλήλαις, τοῦτο δὲ συμβαίνει ὅταν ὁ λόγος καὶ ἡ ἐπιθυμία ἐναντίαι ὦσι, γίνεται δ᾽ ἐν τοῖς χρόνου αἴσθησιν ἔχουσιν (ὁ μὲν γὰρ νοῦς διὰ τὸ μέλλον ἀνθέλκειν κελεύει, ἡ δ᾽ ἐπιθυμία διὰ τὸ ἤδη· φαίνεται γὰρ τὸ ἤδη ἡδὺ καὶ ἁπλῶς ἡδὺ καὶ ἀγαθὸν ἁπλῶς, διὰ τὸ μὴ ὁρᾶν τὸ μέλλον), κ. τ. λ. Likewise one cannot object against our explanation that the being moved of the body could not be named as a third movement together with a twofold appetite, since it is itself no appetite. For Aristotle plainly does exactly the same in the tenth chapter, when he says (§. 7. p. 433, b, 14.): τὸ δὲ κινοῦν διττόν, τὸ μὲν ἀκίνητον, τὸ δὲ κινοῦν καὶ κινούμενον· ἔστι δὲ τὸ μὲν ἀκίνητον τὸ πρακτὸν ἀγαθόν, τὸ δὲ κινοῦν καὶ κινούμενον τὸ ὀρεκτικόν (κινεῖται γὰρ τὸ ὀρεγόμενον ᾗ ὀρέγεται, καὶ ἡ ὄρεξις κίνησίς τίς ἐστιν ἡ ἐνεργείᾳ). τὸ δὲ κινούμενον τὸ ζῷον. Thus there is nothing that would stand in our way. But our interpretation receives still further support from a parallel passage in the first book of the Politics, which we wish to add as a final proof. Polit. I, 5. p. 1254, a, 34. τὸ ζῷον πρῶτον συνέστηκεν ἐκ ψυχῆς καὶ σώματος, ὧν τὸ μὲν ἄρχον ἐστὶ φύσει τὸ δ᾽ ἀρχόμενον. δεῖ δὲ σκοπεῖν ἐν τοῖς κατὰ φύσιν ἔχουσι μᾶλλον τὸ φύσει, καὶ μὴ ἐν τοῖς διεφθαρμένοις. διὸ καὶ τὸν βέλτιστα διακείμενον καὶ κατὰ σῶμα καὶ κατὰ ψυχὴν ἄνθρωπον θεωρητέον, ἐν ᾧ τοῦτο δῆλον· τῶν γὰρ μοχθηρῶν ἢ μοχθηρῶς ἐχόντων δόξειεν ἂν ἄρχειν πολλάκις τὸ σῶμα τῆς ψυχῆς διὰ τὸ φαύλως καὶ παρὰ φύσιν ἔχειν. ἔστι δ᾽ οὖν, ὥσπερ λέγομεν, πρῶτον ἐν ζῴῳ θεωρῆσαι καὶ δεσποτικὴν ἀρχὴν καὶ πολιτικήν· ἡ μὲν γὰρ ψυχὴ τοῦ σώματος ἄρχει δεσποτικὴν ἀρχήν, ὁ δὲ νοῦς τῆς ὀρέξεως πολιτικὴν καὶ βασιλικήν· ἐν οἷς φανερόν ἐστιν ὅτι *κατὰ φύσιν καὶ συμφέρον τὸ ἄρχεσθαι τῷ σώματι ὑπὸ τῆς ψυχῆς καὶ τῷ παθητικῷ μορίῳ ὑπὸ τοῦ νοῦ* καὶ τοῦ μορίου τοῦ λόγον ἔχοντος, τὸ δ᾽ *ἐξ ἴσου ἢ ἀνάπαλιν βλαβερὸν* πᾶσιν.

111) See below, Part IV. n. 28. — Another objection, which Aristotle raises in De Anim. III, 9. §. 6. p. 432, a, 19. against the view that the locally moving power belongs to the sensitive part, he leaves unsolved; probably because the solution is evident to everyone of itself. He remarks, namely, that the moving power seems not to belong to the sensitive part because it is lacking even in fully developed animal organisms, since whatever has generative power is surely to be called fully developed. — To this one need simply reply that what has generative power does indeed show itself complete as regards vegetative life (τέλος τὸ γεννῆσαι οἷον αὐτό. De Anim. II, 4. §. 15. p. 416, b, 24.), but that the faculty of generation is not the completion of the sensitive part and can therefore be combined with a lack of development of sensitive life.

[scan 144]

### Vierter Theil. — Fourth Part.

### Von der intellectiven Seele. — On the Intellective Soul.

#### a. Von dem Verstande, der alles Intelligibele in Möglichkeit ist. — a. On the Intellect Which Is Potentially Everything Intelligible.

1. The first question that occupies us here is naturally this: is there in man a cognitive faculty which is distinct from the sensory cognitive powers already considered, if not in subject, then at any rate in being<sup>1)</sup>?

We know the procedure that we must adopt for its solution, and have already applied it more than once when it was a matter of deciding on the unity or plurality of cognitive faculties. We must start from the consideration of the acts and objects, and if we find in ourselves an act of cognition in which none of the proper objects of our senses is presented, it follows from this that yet another form-receiving power of the soul besides the senses must belong to us. Plainly this is the case<sup>2)</sup>. We have in ourselves the concept of color, the concept of sound in general, and these presentations cannot possibly be counted among the sense images of sight and of hearing. For sight does indeed know white and black and each of the colors in particular, but it does not know color as such. This is neither white nor black; if it were the one, it would be opposed to the other and could [p. 114] not be predicated of it. Likewise sound in general is neither a nor b, and neither the tone of a flute nor that of a harp, nor any other of those which the ear perceives. But if not even these senses, whose proper object is colors and sounds, can grasp the concepts of color and sound, still less can the remaining sensitive faculties named. Further, let us look at the mathematical concepts, at the concepts of surface and line, of the square and of the circle; plainly they contain no presentation of red or warm or sweet or of any other of the proper objects of sense. Finally, the concepts of number, of substance, and others do not even contain within them the presentation of an extension, which nevertheless forms the necessary foundation of all sensible qualities<sup>3)</sup>. There can therefore be no doubt that, besides the sensory powers discussed, we have yet another cognitive faculty within us, and this we call the *intellect*.

1) Cf. De Anim. III, 4. §. 1. p, 429, a, 11. χωριστὸν κατὰ μέγεθος means the same as ibid. II, 2. §. 7. p. 413, b, 15. χωριστὸν τόπῳ, distinct in subject; see above, Part I, n. 68.

2) Cf. De Anim. II, 5. §. 6. p. 417, b, 22. III, 4. §. 7. f. p. 429, b, 10., a passage that we shall examine more closely in a moment. ibid. 6. §. 7. p. 430, b, 28. 8. §. 2. p. 432, a, 2. and §. 3. a, 12. Metaph. Γ, 5. p. 1010, a, 1. 24. Anal. Post. I, 18. p. 81, b, 6., 31. p. 87. b, 28. 37. II, 13. p. 97, b, 26., 19. p. 100, a, 7. 16. Phys. I, 5. p. 189, a, 5. Metaph. Α, 1. p. 981, b, 10. Β, 4. p. 999, a, 26. Ζ, 10. p. 1035, b, 34., 11. p. 1036, a, 28. Eth. Nicom. VI, 6. p. 1140, b, 31., 10. p. 1142, a, 26., 12. p. 1143, b, 4. VII, 5. p. 1147, b, 4. The μαθηματικά in contrast to the αἰσθητά, e.g. De Sens. et Sens. 6. p. 445, b, 14.

2. But if on the one hand it is established that the intellect differs from the senses, it nevertheless cannot be denied on the other hand that it must be thought of as similar to them in many respects<sup>4)</sup>. Through both faculties our soul distinguishes and knows things<sup>5)</sup>, and both become authoritative and guiding for us in our appetite and action<sup>6)</sup>, for which reason older thinkers too, wholly overlooking the difference between the activity of the intellect and sense perception, identified the one with the other<sup>7)</sup>.

Since, then, thinking is similar to sensation in this way, it must presumably be a being affected by the intelligible<sup>8)</sup>, in that less proper sense of being affected which we established for sensation, and in general the intellect will have to be related to the intelligible as sense is to the sensible<sup>9)</sup>. It is therefore impassive, but capable of receiving the intelligible forms<sup>10)</sup>, just as sense is capable of receiving the sensible forms. [p. 115] It is potentially everything intelligible<sup>11)</sup>, without, as Empedocles had believed, actually being any of the objects<sup>12)</sup>—Empedocles, who had earth known by earth, water by water, air by air, fire by fire, friendship by friendship, and strife by strife<sup>13)</sup>. Rather, we must agree with Anaxagoras when he says that the intellect is unmixed<sup>14)</sup>, for in and of itself it is free of all forms, in order to be able to receive all. If anything were already actually in it by nature, this would become a hindrance to the other objects and, so to speak, block their access<sup>15)</sup>. It would be as if one wished to use a written-on sheet instead of a writing tablet, since the latter, after all, may not in and of itself be burdened with any written characters, but must be free of all marks, so that one can write everything on it, now this, now that<sup>16)</sup>. Thus, then, that faculty of the soul which we call intellect is, before it thinks, actually not a single one of all things, and its nature is none other than this, that it is potentiality<sup>17)</sup>.

3) De Sens. et Sens. 6. p. 445, b, 10.

4) De Anim. III, 3. §. 1. p. 27, a, 19. ibid. 4. §. 2. p. 429, a, 13.

5) De Anim. III, 3. §. 1. p. 427, a, 20. ibid. §. 6. p. 428, a, 4. and 9. §. 1. p. 432, a, 15. and elsewhere.

6) De Anim. III, 3. §. 15. p. 429, a, 4. ibid. 10. §. 1. ff. p. 433, a, 9.

7) De Anim. III, 3. §. 1. p. 427, a, 21.

8) De Anim. III, 4. §. 2. p. 429, a, 13. εἰ δή ἐστι τὸ νοεῖν ὥσπερ τὸ αἰσθάνεσθαι, ἢ πάσχειν τι ἂν εἴη ὑπὸ τοῦ νοητοῦ ἤ τι τοιοῦτον ἕτερον. cf. ibid. §. 9. p. 429, b, 24.

9) Ibid. §. 3. p. 429, a, 16. (δεῖ) ὁμοίως ἔχειν, ὥσπερ τὸ αἰσθητικὸν πρὸς τὰ αἰσθητά, οὕτω τὸν νοῦν πρὸς τὰ νοητά cf. the relevant discussions above, Part III. n. 2.

10) Ibid. §. 3. a, 15. ἀπαθὲς ἄρα δεῖ εἶναι, δεκτικὸν δὲ τοῦ εἴδους. cf. 8. §. 2. p. 431, b, 28. Metaph. Λ, 7. p. 1072, b, 22. cf. Part III. n. 2.

3. But how are we to think of the subject of this faculty? — For that it must have a subject is evident, since it is an *accidental* faculty, as the potentiality of an accidental actuality from the category of being affected<sup>18)</sup>. — Is its subject, then, [p. 116] the ensouled body, as was the case with the senses, or is the intellect perhaps a spiritual faculty whose subject is the soul alone? Aristotle, as we have already remarked earlier by anticipation, declared himself with all decisiveness for this latter view; he holds fast to the spirituality of the intellective soul with the same certainty with which his great teacher had asserted it. To him and his pupils apply the words: “They speak well who say that the soul is the place of the ideas, only they ought to assert this not of the whole soul but of the intellective soul alone, and ought also to say that the ideas are in it not actually but potentially<sup>19)</sup>.” Aristotle here clearly indicates the points of agreement and of difference between his doctrine and the Platonic one. He agrees with Plato in holding the intellective soul to be something spiritual; the intellect is for him a faculty of the soul alone, not a faculty of the ensouled body, and its acts count for him as pure activities of the soul. On the other hand, he differs from Plato, first, in that, whereas the latter granted the same position to the sensitive soul as well, he for his part has come to the conviction that an organ of the ensouled body must be the subject of the sensory powers and their acts; and second, in that, whereas Plato thought of the ideas as present in our soul from birth, as acquired in an earlier life<sup>20)</sup>, he for his part asserts nothing other than that the soul possesses as innate the *faculty* of thinking, the *potentiality* of the ideas. [p. 117] But on what grounds, then, does Aristotle’s conviction of the spirituality of the intellective soul rest—a conviction which he not only teaches in clear words at this place: “it is not right to say that the intellect is mixed with the body,” but also, in other and quite frequent remarks, both here in the books On the Soul and in others of his writings, now hints at, now states clearly and without disguise as his view<sup>21)</sup>? The Platonic [p. 118] proofs he could not well adopt as his own, for these proceed in large part from presuppositions which Aristotle designates [p. 119] as erroneous, as, for example, from the view just mentioned, that the ideas are ours from birth. He must therefore [p. 120] have been brought to the same conviction by other reasons; and in fact, in the fourth chapter of the third book On the Soul, he gives us three proofs, but in words so tightly compressed that the first two at least can easily be misunderstood.

11) De Anim. III, 8. §. 1. f. p. 431, b, 20. ibid. 5. §. 1. p. 430, a, 14. and 4. §. 3. p. 429, a. 18.

12) De Anim. III, 4. §. 3. p. 429, a, 16. δυνάμει τοιοῦτον ἀλλὰ μὴ τοῦτο. cf. ibid. 6. §. 6. p. 430, b, 23., where one is to read: ἐνεῖναι ἐν αὐτῷ. (Bekk. Trendelenb.)

13) De Anim. I, 5. §. 5. ff. p. 409, b, 23. ibid. III, 3. §. 2. p. 427, a. 26. Metaph. Β. p. 1000, b, 6.

14) De Anim. III, 4. §. 3. p. 429, a, 18. ἀνάγκη ἄρα, ἐπεὶ πάντα νοεῖ, ἀμιγῆ εἶναι, ὥσπερ φησὶν Ἀναξαγόρας, ἵνα κρατῇ, τοῦτο δ᾽ ἐστὶν ἵνα γνωρίζῃ. That Anaxagoras declared his νοῦς unmixed for a reason other than the one given here is beyond doubt. Aristotle is himself well aware of his reinterpretation of the ἵνα κρατῇ, as clearly emerges from comparison with Phys. VIII, 5. p. 256, b, 24. Cf. Trendelenburg on the passage.

15) De Anim. ibid. a, 20. παρεμφαινόμενον γὰρ κωλύει τὸ ἀλλότριον καὶ ἀντιφράττει.

16) Ibid. §. 11. p. 429, b, 30. δεῖ δ᾽ οὕτως ὥσπερ ἐν γραμματείῳ ᾧ μηθὲν ὑπάρχει ἐντελεχείᾳ γεγραμμένον. Aristotle speaks of a property belonging to the writing tablet in contrast to other writing material. No writing adheres in it, and therefore more can be written on the smallest tablet than in a thousand books—everything can be written on it, since the one writing alternates with the other.

17) Ibid. §. 3. a, 21. ὥστε μηδ᾽ αὐτοῦ εἶναι φύσιν μηδεμίαν ἀλλ᾽ ἢ ταύτην, ὅτι δυνατόν. cf. §. 11. b, 30. and the passages cited in n. 11.

18) If the receptive intellect were a substantial potentiality, it would be one with substantial matter, which—apart from the fact that it cannot be thought of for a thousand other reasons—is also never found stripped of all forms; against which De Anim. III, 4. §. 12. p. 430, a, 5.

19) De Anim. ibid. §. 4. p. 429, a, 27. καὶ εὖ δὴ οἱ λέγοντες τὴν ψυχὴν εἶναι τόπον (cf. Part I. n. 68.) εἰδῶν, πλὴν ὅτι οὔτε ὅλη ἀλλ᾽ ἡ νοητική, οὔτε ἐντελεχείᾳ ἀλλὰ δυνάμει τὰ εἴδη.

20) Plato did not believe that the ideas are actually known by us from the beginning and without interruption; he assumed only that, in consequence of a knowledge preceding its union with the body, the soul had retained within itself a disposition by virtue of which it recollects the ideas again on occasion. It is therefore evident that the arguments of Aristotle just adduced, which refute the view that our intellect is by its nature an actuality, are not directed against Plato; for even if the soul were in possession of the ideas from birth in the manner in which Plato conceived it, a change of thoughts would not thereby become impossible. The simile of the writing tablet, in which one commonly believes the opposition of Aristotle to Plato to be expressed in the sharpest way, so little contradicts the doctrine of this philosopher that he could have adopted it as his own in entirely the same sense. When he makes use of it in the Theaetetus, he does, to be sure, connect a different sense with it. But Aristotle, as we see, also denies the existence of the ideas in our soul in the manner in which Plato had them actually be in it. The reasons why he does this we shall come to know later. For the present cf. Anal. Post. II, 19.

21) One must indeed be surprised that, where the passages in proof are so numerous, any interpreter of Aristotle could ever have entertained doubt in this respect. But since this has nevertheless happened, we shall cite the more important of them here, and at the same time combine with them some of those which show that, according to Aristotle, the intellective in man is not a purely spiritual substance alien to the other parts of the soul, as others, falling into the opposite error, have maintained.

Already in the first chapter of the first book On the Soul, where Aristotle designates the soul as ἀρχὴ τῶν ζῴων, he says (De Anim. I, 1. §. 1. p. 402, a, 9.) that one must consider: ὅσα συμβέβηκε περὶ αὐτήν· ὧν τὰ μὲν *ἴδια πάθη τῆς ψυχῆς* εἶναι δοκεῖ, τὰ δὲ δι᾽ ἐκείνην καὶ τοῖς ζῴοις ὑπάρχειν. Then with the words: ἔτι δ᾽ εἰ μὴ πολλαὶ ψυχαὶ ἀλλὰ μόρια, πότερον δεῖ ζητεῖν πρότερον τὴν ὅλην ψυχὴν ἢ τὰ μόρια; (§. 5. b, 9.) he indicates still more definitely that the intellective soul differs from the sensitive not as substance from substance, but as part from part. Further, in the course of the same chapter (§. 9. p. 403, a, 3.), he says: ἀπορίαν δ᾽ ἔχει καὶ τὰ πάθη τῆς ψυχῆς, πότερόν ἐστι πάντα κοινὰ καὶ τοῦ ἔχοντος ἢ *ἐστί τι καὶ τῆς ψυχῆς ἴδιον αὐτῆς*· . . . φαίνεται δὲ τῶν πλείστων οὐθὲν ἄνευ σώματος πάσχειν οὐδὲ ποιεῖν, οἷον ὀργίζεσθαι, θαρρεῖν, ἐπιθυμεῖν, ὅλως αἰσθάνεσθαι. *μάλιστα δ᾽ ἔοικεν ἴδιον* (others read ἰδίῳ) *τὸ νοεῖν*. . . . εἰ μὲν οὖν ἐστί τι τῶν τῆς ψυχῆς ἔργων ἢ παθημάτων ἴδιον, ἐνδέχοιτ᾽ ἂν αὐτὴν χωρίζεσθαι· εἰ δὲ μηθέν ἐστιν ἴδιον αὐτῆς οὐκ ἂν εἴη χωριστή. Here too his view of the unity, but partial spirituality, of the soul makes itself known; and likewise the words at the beginning of §. 11. (p. 403, a, 27.): φυσικοῦ τὸ θεωρῆσαι περὶ ψυχῆς ἢ πάσης ἢ τῆς τοιαύτης (namely τῆς αἰσθητικῆς), point to the immateriality of the intellective part. But Aristotle states it unambiguously in the third chapter, when he says (§. 12. p. 407, a, 2.): *οὐ καλῶς τὸ λέγειν τὴν ψυχὴν μέγεθος εἶναι* (cf. II, 12. §. 2. p. 424, a, 26.)· τὴν γὰρ τοῦ παντὸς δῆλον ὅτι τοιαύτην εἶναι βούλεται (namely Plato) οἷόν ποτ᾽ ἐστὶν ὁ καλούμενος νοῦς· οὐ γὰρ οἷόν γ᾽ ἡ αἰσθητική, οὐδ᾽ οἷον ἡ ἐπιθυμητική. Likewise in the fourth chapter (§. 13. p. 408, b, 18. *ὁ δὲ νοῦς ἔοικεν ἐγγίνεσθαι οὐσία τις οὖσα* (pure form, not composed of matter and form), *καὶ οὐ φθείρεσθαι*. μάλιστα γὰρ ἐφθείρετ᾽ ἂν ὑπὸ τῆς ἐν τῷ γήρᾳ ἀμαυρώσεως, νῦν δ᾽ ἴσως ὅπερ ἐπὶ τῶν αἰσθητηρίων συμβαίνει· εἰ γὰρ λάβοι ὁ πρεσβύτης ὄμμα τοιονδί, βλέποι ἂν ὥσπερ καὶ ὁ νέος· ὥστε τὸ γῆρας οὐ τῷ τὴν ψυχήν τι πεπονθέναι, ἀλλ᾽ ἐν ᾧ, καθάπερ ἐν μέθαις καὶ νόσοις. (cf. III, 3. §. 15. p. 429, a, 7.) καὶ τὸ νοεῖν δὴ καὶ τὸ θεωρεῖν μαραίνεται ἄλλου τινὸς ἔσω φθειρομένου, αὐτὸ δὲ ἀπαθές ἐστιν. τὸ δὲ διανοεῖσθαι καὶ φιλεῖν ἢ μισεῖν οὐκ ἔστιν ἐκείνου πάθη, ἀλλὰ τουδὶ τοῦ ἔχοντος ἐκεῖνο, ᾗ ἐκεῖνο ἔχει. διὸ καὶ τούτου φθειρομένου οὔτε μνημονεύει οὔτε φιλεῖ· οὐ γὰρ ἐκείνου ἦν, ἀλλὰ τοῦ κοινοῦ, ὃ ἀπόλωλεν· *ὁ δὲ νοῦς ἴσως θειότερόν τι καὶ ἀπαθές ἐστιν*. Just as Aristotle here gives us unambiguously to understand that he holds the intellective soul to be something spiritual, so shortly before he has shown that in his view it forms *one* soul with the sensitive, since in §. 6. p. 408, a, 16. he regards the plurality

of souls in *one* body plainly as something unacceptable: συμβήσεται οὖν (on the view to be refuted) πολλάς τε ψυχὰς ἔχειν καὶ κατὰ πᾶν τὸ σῶμα. Then, at the end of the chapter, where he wants to prove that the soul cannot be a number of points, we again clearly perceive that according to his doctrine not every soul is dependent on the body for its existence, but some are separable from it. (§. 22. p. 409, a, 28.): ἔτι δὲ *πῶς οἷόν τε χωρίζεσθαι τὰς στιγμὰς καὶ ἀπολύεσθαι τῶν σωμάτων*, εἴ γε μὴ διαιροῦνται αἱ γραμμαὶ εἰς στιγμάς; But it is evident that this can be his opinion only with regard to the rational soul. In the fifth chapter, likewise, there are found both passages that designate the intellect as a part of the soul, e.g. §. 14. p. 410, b, 24. εἰ δέ τις καὶ ταῦτα παραχωρήσειε, καὶ θείη τὸν νοῦν μέρος τι τῆς ψυχῆς, ὁμοίως δὲ καὶ τὸ αἰσθητικόν κ. τ. λ., and also those that point to its spirituality. Thus Aristotle gives to Thales’ saying that all things are full of gods the interpretation that he held all things to be ensouled (§. 17. p. 411, a, 7.), evidently because in his view the soul is something akin to God. (Cf. also §. 12. p. 410, b, 12.); and further below (§. 25. p. 411, b, 14.) he says: ἀπορήσειε δ᾽ ἄν τις καὶ περὶ τῶν μορίων αὐτῆς (namely τῆς ψυχῆς), τίν᾽ ἔχει δύναμιν ἕκαστον ἐν τῷ σώματι. εἰ γὰρ ἡ ὅλη ψυχὴ πᾶν τὸ σῶμα συνέχει, προσήκει καὶ τῶν μορίων ἕκαστον συνέχειν τι τοῦ σώματος. τοῦτο δ᾽ ἔοικεν ἀδυνάτῳ· *ποῖον γὰρ μόριον ἢ πῶς ὁ νοῦς συνέξει, χαλεπὸν καὶ πλάσαι*. Why does he find difficulty especially in the case of the intellect? Evidently because in his opinion it is unmixed with the body and makes use of no organ in its activity. — In the *second book*, right in the first chapter, we meet the following words (§. 12. p. 413, a, 4.): ὅτι μὲν οὖν οὐκ ἔστιν ἡ ψυχὴ χωριστὴ τοῦ σώματος, ἢ μέρη τινὰ αὐτῆς, εἰ μεριστὴ πέφυκεν, οὐκ ἄδηλον· ἐνίων γὰρ ἡ ἐντελέχεια τῶν μερῶν ἐστὶν αὐτῶν. οὐ μὴν ἀλλ᾽ *ἔνιά γε οὐθὲν κωλύει. διὰ τὸ μηθενὸς εἶναι σώματος ἐντελεχείας*. Here he states with all definiteness that the soul, as to a certain part, is not the entelechy of the body, and is thus spiritual. But why does he say ἀλλ᾽ ἔνια and not ἀλλ᾽ ἕν γε οὐθὲν κωλύει, since in his opinion only a single part, namely the intellective, is immaterial? He seems to us to speak in this way because the intellective part comprises a plurality of powers within itself. In the second chapter (§. 2. p. 413, a, 20.) it is said that the νοῦς is something which, like αἴσθησις, κίνησις καὶ στάσις ἡ κατὰ τόπον and κίνησις ἡ κατὰ τροφὴν καὶ φθίσιν τε καὶ αὔξησιν, belongs to mortal beings; but it is indicated that it is a power which they have in common with immortal beings, and this implies that it is spiritual and that therefore the mortal being that shares in it, man, must also be spiritual and immortal as regards this part. (§. 4. p. 413, a, 31.: χωρίζεσθαι δὲ τοῦτο μὲν (namely τὸ θρεπτικόν) τῶν ἄλλων δυνατόν, τὰ δ᾽ ἄλλα τούτου ἀδύνατον *ἐν τοῖς θνητοῖς*.) Further below, then, the διανοητικὴ ψυχή is clearly designated as a part of the soul in the sense we have already established earlier, and it is given to be understood that it is nevertheless separate from the other parts of the soul not merely in concept but also in subject; not, say, as if it dwelt in a special bodily organ, but because it is spiritual, immortal, imperishable, a substance of an entirely different genus and higher nature: ἔστιν ἡ ψυχὴ τῶν εἰρημένων τούτων ἀρχὴ καὶ τούτοις ὥρισται, θρεπτικῷ, αἰσθητικῷ, διανοητικῷ, κινήσει. πότερον δὲ

τούτων ἕκαστόν ἐστι ψυχὴ ἢ μόριον ψυχῆς, καὶ εἰ μόριον (which is laid down as the correct view at the basis of the further questions) πότερον οὕτως ὥστ᾽ εἶναι χωριστὸν λόγῳ μόνον ἢ καὶ τόπῳ, περὶ μὲν τινῶν τούτων οὐ χαλεπὸν ἰδεῖν (namely in the case of the vegetative, sensitive and moving part), ἔνια δὲ ἀπορίαν ἔχει (namely the νοητικὸν μέρος, which is why he continues:) περὶ δὲ τοῦ νοῦ καὶ τῆς θεωρητικῆς δυνάμεως οὐδέν πω φανερόν, ἀλλ᾽ *ἔοικε ψυχῆς γένος ἕτερον εἶναι, καὶ τοῦτο μόνον ἐνδέχεται χωρίζεσθαι, καθάπερ τὸ ἀΐδιον τοῦ φθαρτοῦ*. τὰ δὲ λοιπὰ μόρια τῆς ψυχῆς (so the νοητικὴ ψυχή too is a μόριον) φανερὸν ἐκ τούτων ὅτι οὐκ ἔστι χωριστά, καθάπερ τινές φασιν. In the third chapter, where Aristotle illustrates the unity of the soul by the example of the figures, he repeatedly enumerates the διανοητικόν and the νοῦς in *one* series with the other powers of the soul. Here too he points to the spiritual nature of the latter and indicates that in respect of the intellect man is akin to higher beings (§. 4. p. 414, b, 18.): ἑτέροις δὲ (ὑπάρχει) καὶ τὸ διανοητικόν τε καὶ νοῦς, οἷον ἀνθρώποις καὶ εἴ τι τοιοῦτον ἕτερόν ἐστιν ἢ *καὶ τιμιώτερον*. Then further below (§. 7. p. 415, a, 7.): τελευταῖον δὲ καὶ ἐλάχιστα (τῶν ζῴων ἔχει) λογισμὸν καὶ διάνοιαν· οἷς μὲν γὰρ ὑπάρχει λογισμὸς *τῶν φθαρτῶν*, τούτοις καὶ τὰ λοιπὰ πάντα. κ. τ. λ. And immediately afterwards: περὶ δὲ τοῦ θεωρητικοῦ νοῦ ἕτερος λόγος, that is, it is found also in purely spiritual substances, which then lack the vegetative and sensitive part. At the beginning of the fourth chapter the νοητικόν is once more listed, with the αἰσθητικόν and θρεπτικόν, as a part of the soul (§. 1. p. 415, a, 17.). In the *third* book, however, in the third chapter, Aristotle mentions, as an opinion of older thinkers and poets heterogeneous to his own, that thinking is bodily (§. 2. p. 427, a, 26.): πάντες γὰρ οὗτοι τὸ νοεῖν σωματικὸν ὥσπερ τὸ αἰσθάνεσθαι ὑπολαμβάνουσιν.

According to the doctrine of Aristotle, then, as it can be recognized already from earlier remarks in the books On the Soul, the νοῦς is the capacity of a spiritual subject, which, however, in man is most intimately bound up with the body and is a part of the same soul that, by virtue of other parts, gives being and life to the body as its substantial actuality. The same doctrine of the spirituality of the intellect is contained and grounded in the fourth chapter, which we have to explain in greater detail. Here we wish only to point further to some passages from other writings of Aristotle which likewise bear witness to his view.

For the spirituality of the νοῦς cf. Metaph. Γ, 5. p. 1009, b, 12. (as a parallel to De Anim. III, 3, see above) E, 1. p. 1026, a, 5. (parallel De An. I, 1. §. 11. see above) Λ, 3. p. 1070, a, 26. (parallel. De An. II, 1. see above; cf. also De Anim. III, 5. §. 2. p. 430, a, 22., a passage which we shall discuss later) De Sens. et Sens. 1. p. 436, a, 6. (Were the intellect a faculty of the ensouled body and not of the soul alone, it would necessarily have to be named here.) Phys. VII, 3. p. 248, a, 6. De Anim. III, 6. p. 430, b, 30. De Part. Animal. I, 1. p. 641, a, 22 — b, 10. II, 10. p. 656, a, 7. IV, 10. p. 686, a, 25—29 (parallel De An. I, 4. §. 13. f. see above, toward the end) De Generat. Animal. II, 3. p. 736, b, 27. (A passage which is of exceptional importance and which we shall consider more closely later. For the present let it only be remarked that by the νοῦς that enters the fetus θύραθεν is to be understood, not, as some think, the νοῦς ποιητικός alone, but the whole ψυχὴ νοητική (p. 736, b, 14.).) Eth. Nicom. I, 4. p. 1096, b, 29. (That in which the activity of sense resides is the body,

4. He gives the first proof, after having shown that the intellect, in order to be able to know all things, must by its nature be pure potentiality, in the following words: “Hence it is also not well said when one says that it is mixed with the body; for otherwise it would presumably be something of a certain quality, cold or warm<sup>22)</sup>.”

These words have been explained in the following way. The human intellect, it was said, is, as Aristotle has just remarked, by its nature capable of knowing all things; there is none among all bodily things whose concept it would not be able to grasp. Hence it must by its nature be free of everything bodily, a pure faculty of the soul; for were it mixed with the body, it would already have definite bodily qualities in itself, which would make it incapable of receiving the contrary forms; it would be warm or cold, and in the one case would be hindered from knowing cold, in the other from knowing warmth.

But if this is Aristotle’s line of proof, it is little suited to promote belief in the spirituality of the intellective soul. For here the objective<sup>23)</sup> reception of sensible qualities is evidently confused with the physical or material reception. Admittedly, just as in the sense there can be only *one* sense image at a time, [p. 121] so in the intellect there can be only *one* thought at a time; and if, therefore, the concept of a bodily quality were actually in it by nature, if, for example, warmth were by nature objectively fixed in the intellect, the presentation of cold would thereby become impossible for it<sup>24)</sup>; it must therefore objectively have in itself neither the one nor the other quality. But if it has no bodily quality in itself objectively, and is all things merely in potentiality, must it for that reason also be physically free of all bodily qualities, so that one could conclude from this that it is unmixed with the body? This seems by no means to follow; otherwise the sense of touch, too, could not have a bodily organ as its subject, for it distinguishes the warm and the cold and knows them both. Now, however, this faculty, as we have seen, is nevertheless bodily, and is so little hindered by the proper physical constitution of its organ from knowing contrary qualities, that, on the contrary, it perceives precisely those most which are farthest removed from its own<sup>25)</sup>; so it seems that for the intellect, too, no hindrance can arise from such a condition.

but the subject of thinking is the soul alone.) VI, 13. p. 1144, a, 29. X, 7. p. 1177, a, 15. b, 28. 30. p. 1178, a, 1., 8. p. 1178, b, 26. and 9. p. 1179, a, 26.

For the substantial unity of the ψυχὴ νοητική with the rest of the man, cf., besides those among the passages just named which, like e.g. De Part. Animal. I, 1., teach this too in the clearest way, also Metaph. α, 1. p. 993, b, 10. (τῆς ἡμετέρας ψυχῆς ὁ νοῦς) Λ, 9. p. 1075, a, 7. (νοῦς ἀνθρώπινος) Θ, 8. p. 1050, b, 1. (the force of the proof lies in the fact that the εὐδαιμονία, as the Ethics teaches, consists in rational activity and in particular in thinking). Most striking of all, however, are some passages of the Nicomachean Ethics. IX, 8. p. 1168, b, 28 — fin., especially p. 1168, b, 35. p. 1169, a, 2. and ibid. X, 7. p. 1178, a, 2.

22) De Anim. III, 4. §. 4. p. 429, a, 24. διὸ οὐδὲ μεμίχθαι εὔλογον αὐτὸν τῷ σώματι· ποιός τις γὰρ ἂν γίγνοιτο, ψυχρὸς ἢ θερμός.

23) We have already stated above (Part III, n. 6.) in what sense we make use of this word. We employ it, because it makes greater brevity of expression possible, in the manner customary also among the Scholastics, when someone takes something into himself as object, that is, as something known. Whoever, for example, perceives warmth by sense has it in himself objectively; whoever is warm has it in himself physically or materially.

5. This inconvenience is avoided by another attempt at explanation, although otherwise it comes fairly close to the one just discussed. For if we must grant that one and the same organ can at the same time have cold in itself physically and warmth objectively, in other words, that something cold can perceive a warm object, this by no means says that its own physical quality is without influence on the sensation. If we touch an equally warm object with a warm hand, we do not perceive its warmth; and so even if the organ is in an intermediate state, which is what is most suitable for the sense of warmth, so that we feel the colder as cold and the warmer as warm, since the mean stands to each extreme as the opposite extreme, still a certain warmth always remains imperceptible<sup>26)</sup>. Now since this is not the case with the intellect, which is able at all times to think all degrees of warmth and to judge their relation, Aristotle could arrive at the conclusion that the intellect must be free of all bodily qualities, and thus a faculty of the soul alone, and cannot have an organ as its subject. To this is further added that the organ of the sense of warmth, because, being something bodily, it also physically has in itself a certain warmth or cold that can both increase and decrease, will perceive one and the same object now as warm, now as cold; [p. 122] indeed, that it can happen even during the perception of a certain object that the organ, gradually cooled or more and more warmed, is modified in its sensations of it. It is otherwise with the intellect, whose concepts are fixed and delimited for all time without wavering and without change. And so Aristotle seems in every way justified in saying that the intellect cannot possibly be warm or cold, or have in itself any other of the changeable qualities of the body, that it must therefore be altogether free of what is bodily.

24) Cf. De Anim. III, 6. §. 6. p. 430, b, 23.

25) Cf. De Anim. II, 11. §. 11. p. 424, a, 2.

26) Ibid.

Now, that this explanation deserves preference over the one mentioned before may be granted; for it seems more in harmony with the remaining doctrines of Aristotle. But it, too, is nevertheless subject to several weighty doubts. In the first place, Aristotle, if this had been his thought, would have expressed it in a way that would make one believe that he had wanted to pose a riddle rather than to solve one. But then, too, it is surely not correct that the intellect, like the sense of warmth and the other sentient faculties, has sensible things, as such, for its object. When Aristotle wanted to demonstrate the existence of an inner sense that lets us distinguish the objects of different senses, he began thus: “With what do we perceive that white differs from sweet and each sensible from each? Surely it must be with a sense, for they are sensible objects<sup>27)</sup>.” If, therefore, he has said here in the fourth chapter that the intellect knows all things<sup>28)</sup>, this meant as much as everything *intelligible*, just as he had said immediately before that as the sentient is related to the sensible, so the intellect is related to the intelligible<sup>29)</sup>. So too, then, further below it is said quite unambiguously: “The intellect is in potentiality, in a certain way, the intelligible things<sup>30)</sup>.” And at the beginning of the eighth chapter Aristotle contrasts the sensible with the intelligible, saying: “Things are partly sensible, partly intelligible; now knowledge (under which he here, as the context shows, includes all knowledge of the intellect) is in a certain way the intelligible, while sensation is the sensible,” and so, he concludes, “the soul is in a certain way all things<sup>31)</sup>.”

6. With this, this explanation of our passage too has become impossible, but at the same time the correct mode of explanation has been suggested. [p. 123] All cognitive faculties that have the ensouled body as their subject become actually cognizant through the action of a bodily quality, which is the proper object of the faculty and assimilates the faculty to itself, as in general like produces like<sup>32)</sup>. It stands in proportion to the faculty as the adequate active principle to the passive; it is that which is presented in it first; everything else, as we have discussed earlier<sup>33)</sup>, is presented only through it and with it. If the presentation of sound vanishes from hearing, then hearing also has no presentation of near or far, of quick or slow, etc.; if sight perceives no color, then it also sees neither large nor small, neither angular nor round; in short, if that sensible quality which forms its proper object is not in the sense, then there is nothing sensible in it at all. In the manner of speaking of Aristotle, who likes to say that the faculty of sensation, when it actually perceives, is the thing perceived<sup>34)</sup>, we can also express this thus: if the sense of sight does not come to be of some quality, if it does not become black or white, or receive some other color, then it takes no object into itself at all and knows nothing whatever.

27) De Anim. III, 2. §. 10. p. 426, b, 12. (see above, Part III. n. 33.) cf. De Sens. et Sens. 6. p. 445, b, 15. and the passages cited in n. 2.

28) De Anim. III, 4. §. 3. p. 429, a, 18. πάντα νοεῖ.

29) See above, n. 9.

30) De Anim. III, 4. §. 11. p. 429, b, 30. δυνάμει πώς ἐστι τὰ νοητὰ ὁ νοῦς.

31) De Anim. III, 8. §. 1. p. 431, b, 20. νῦν δὲ περὶ ψυχῆς τὰ λεχθέντα συγκεφαλαιώσαντες, εἴπωμεν πάλιν ὅτι ἡ ψυχὴ τὰ ὄντα πώς ἐστι πάντα. ἢ γὰρ αἰσθητὰ τὰ ὄντα ἢ νοητά, ἔστι δ᾽ ἡ ἐπιστήμη μὲν τὰ ἐπιστητά πως, ἡ δ᾽ αἴσθησις τὰ αἰσθητά.

Now if the intellect, too, were a capacity of the ensouled body, like the sentient faculties, if it differed from them only as *one* sense from other senses, as a higher from lower ones, then it too would have to have a proper sensible object, a quality that affected it and thereby brought it to actuality; and this quality, since it assimilated the intellect to itself, would have to form the basic determination in all its presentations. But this is so little the case that, on the contrary, we cannot observe anything of such a sensible quality in a single one of its presentations. Or what would be the sensible object that acted on it when, for example, it grasps the concept of color? Some color perhaps, white or black? Neither of the two; for white stands in opposition to black, but color to neither of them; and, apart from this, the intellect would otherwise have to have a color in itself with every presentation; now, however, in the concept of tone, in the concept of taste, nothing of a color is to be found, and in other concepts there is lacking any presentation of a sensory [p. 124] quality, as for example in the concepts of line and surface, of square and circle, of number, of substance, of relation, and so in many others besides, as we have already mentioned above. So it is not true, then, that the intellect, in becoming cognizant, always receives a sensible quality; it does not come to be of some quality, warm or cold, and therefore cannot be a faculty of the ensouled body; it is unmixed with matter.

32) Cf. De Anim. II, 5. §. 3. p. 417, a, 17. and our earlier general discussions. Part. I. n. 15.

33) Part III, n. 3.

34) De Anim. III, 8. §. 1. f. p. 431. b, 22. ibid. 2. §. 3. p. 425, b, 22. ἔτι δὲ καὶ τὸ ὁρῶν ἔστιν ὡς κεχρωμάτισται· τὸ γὰρ αἰσθητήριον δεκτικὸν τοῦ αἰσθητοῦ ἄνευ τῆς ὕλης ἕκαστον Cf. De Anim. III, 5. §. 2. p. 430, a, 19., further ibid. II, 5. §. 3. p. 417, a, 20. and §. 7. p. 418, a, 4., 11. §. 11. p. 424, a, 1. III, 4. §. 6. p. 429, b, 5.

So the words of Aristotle now appear to us quite simple and easily intelligible. The intellect, he had said, becomes all that is intelligible and is therefore by nature none of its objects actually. Now he draws a second consequence, which, as we have just developed, follows from the same proposition: “Hence it is also not well said when one says that it is mixed with the body, for otherwise it would come to be of some quality, become warm or cold<sup>35)</sup>.” The intellect takes up no sensory quality; its object is the intelligible; and this becomes manifest especially through the fact that it knows *all* that is intelligible, for because of this it cannot escape even superficial consideration that no sensory quality runs through all its concepts as its proper object.

7. We come to the second proof, which is just as briefly expressed, since Aristotle, continuing in the same sentence, adds only the few words: “or it would also have to have an organ, like the sentient faculty; but as it is, it has none<sup>36)</sup>.”

This proof, too, is difficult to understand; for the wording, to be sure, is clear—Aristotle says that the intellect, if it were mixed with the body like the senses, would also, like them, have to have an organ, which is, however, not the case—but what he meant to have achieved by this is not so easy to say. For this proof seems to be an obvious petitio principii, so crude a blunder as one would hardly have thought possible in so acute a thinker. Whoever assumes that the intellect, like the senses, is in [p. 125] the ensouled body surely also assumes that it, like the senses, has an organ as its subject; and precisely this was what was to be proved, that not an organ but the soul is the subject of ideas. How, then, can Aristotle presuppose this? Perhaps because no special organ can be demonstrated anatomically as the seat of the intellect? Obviously nothing is decided by this, since the organ of the intellect would not have to be different from the subject of the other sense faculties; for, as we have already seen above, according to Aristotle the external senses have the ultimate organ, which is their subject, in common with one another and with the inner sense; indeed, according to him this organ is at the same time supposed to be the central organ of vegetative and sensitive life; why, then, should not the intellect too be able to find its place in it?

35) This explanation is also more appropriate to the wording than the earlier ones. Torstrik remarks on the passage: Ceterum huic loco inest aliquid quod dubito num graece dictum sit: videtur enim 25. et 26. ποιός τις γὰρ ἂν ἐγένετο scribendum esse et κἂν ὄργανόν τι ἦν collatis 27. νῦν δ᾽ οὐθὲν ἐστιν. In fact, on the first two interpretations at least, γένοιτο would have to be read instead of γίγνοιτο; for they take it in the sense of *being* warm or cold, not of *becoming* warm or cold, as we do. On our interpretation, however, the optative too might appear more fitting, at least for the first member of the sentence, and the construction of the first part may then have had an influence on that of the second.

36) De Anim. III, 4. §. 4. p. 429, a, 26. ἢ κἂν ὄργανόν τι εἴη (sc. τῷ νοητικῷ), ὥσπερ τῷ αἰσθητικῷ· νῦν δ᾽ οὐθέν ἐστιν.

Admittedly these remarks seem to annihilate Aristotle’s proof; but on closer consideration it turns out here too, as in other places, that he does not deserve the reproaches that have been made. Of which organs, then, is Aristotle speaking? Is he speaking of the ultimate one, common to all the senses, which is the subject of sensations, or is he speaking of other organs, which convey sense perception to this ultimate organ<sup>37)</sup>. Obviously the latter is the case. We would have to have, he means to say, an organ that conveyed the concepts of the intellect to us as the eye conveys visual images; and of such an organ it is obvious that we do not possess it.

But why this? Why would such an external organ be absolutely necessary for the intellect, since even the inner sense does not need an external organ? The reason is evident: namely, because the intellect in its concepts can grasp not merely, like the inner sense, the sensations of the cognizing being itself, but also, without any admixture of a subjective element, the essence of external things<sup>38)</sup>. We not only raise our eye to the heavens, but our intellect also measures the paths of the moon and the stars and weighs their masses by comparison.

But in order that Aristotle’s thought may become entirely clear, we must attend to the intimate connection of this proof with the preceding one. In it Aristotle had inferred the incorporeality and supersensible nature of the intellect from the fact that it [p. 126] has no sensory quality as its proper object, which appeared quite obviously false because its knowledge, quite apart from the more abstract concepts, extends in the same way over the domain of colors and tones and of all other sensory qualities. Now he makes use of the same middle term, but he grounds it in a different way. If, as for the senses, so also for the intellect, a bodily quality of the object were the active principle, then its concepts, whenever it grasps something external, would have to be conducted to it through mediating organs, analogously to the other sense perceptions; for in this case it would surely not draw them from the presentations of the other senses, especially since each sense knows the proper objects of the other senses only per accidens. Neither sight nor hearing would be determined in any way by the proper object of the intellect, which would give all its concepts their common character. Now, however, there are no such mediating organs of the intellect; for neither can the organ of another sense, e.g. that of touch or of hearing, at the same time convey the concepts of the intellect—otherwise the man born blind would not lack, together with the sensory presentation, the concept of colors as well<sup>39)</sup>—nor can the intellect, which would otherwise be the most obvious suggestion, have a special mediating organ, for, apart from the fact that such an organ cannot be demonstrated, in this case, too, the origin of concepts would be independent of the lower sense presentations. Least of all, however, can it be assumed that the concepts of the intellect, with their alleged sensible quality, streamed into the intellect without any organ, from all sides and, so to speak, through all the pores of the body. It is therefore obvious that no such sensible quality exists at all, and that the intellect is therefore not one sense among other senses, but a supersensible faculty; it is unmixed with the body.

37) Cf. above, Part III. n. 35. — It should be noted that Aristotle designates with the name αἰσθητήριον both the mediating sense organs and the ultimate, properly sentient one, but calls only the former ὄργανα; *they* are, after all, preeminently the serving instruments. This already makes it indubitable that in our passage the mediating sense organs must be meant.

38) De Sens. et Sens. 6. p. 445, b, 16.

8. The third proof (for we may regard it as one) that Aristotle gives for the immateriality of the intellect<sup>40)</sup> is easily understood from his own words. He had, as we recall, designated sense as in a certain way impassible, because the motion of the sentient as such, that is, sensation, is not a real alteration, not a real undergoing; and from the similarity of the intellect to sense he has therefore [p. 127] inferred at the beginning of this chapter that the intellect, too, must be impassible. Now, however, he draws attention to the fact that the impassibility of the senses is not as perfect as that of the intellect. For sense, although, since sensing is not an undergoing, it does not undergo anything as such, is nevertheless altered per accidens while it senses, inasmuch as the organ with which it forms a unity as an accidental form undergoes a transformation through the action of objects that are sensible in a high degree. A very loud sound makes hearing incapable of perceiving a softer tone; a very intense light, a very penetrating smell render us unable, at least for the time immediately following, to see or to smell anything; indeed, the senses can be permanently weakened or destroyed as a result of such a strong sense impression. Precisely the opposite is the case with the intellect; when it has known a highly intelligible object, it knows the less intelligible not less well, but even better. The principles are more intelligible than the propositions derived from them<sup>41)</sup>, and it is precisely as a result of knowing the principles that the secondary truths, too, become knowable to us. Mathematical truths are more intelligible than those of physics<sup>42)</sup>, but who will deny that someone who has made himself acquainted with mathematical propositions will thereby find himself advanced in his physical inquiries as well? In general, through occupation with mathematics his intellect will be sharpened for the knowledge of every truth. — Whence, then, this peculiarity of the intellect, wholly opposed to the senses? — Of that of which the affirmation is the cause of the affirmation, says Aristotle in his Analytics, the negation is the cause of the negation<sup>43)</sup>; if, then, the reason why a very intense object weakens—be it for the time immediately following or even for all time—and often entirely destroys the faculty of sensation lies in the fact that this faculty, as the form of an organ, is mixed with the bodily, then the reason why none of all this is the case with the intellect, and why indeed rather the opposite occurs, must be sought in the lack of an organ, in the immateriality of the subject; and so once again we arrive at the assumption that the intellective soul is unmixed with the body.

39) Anal. Post. I, 18. p. 81, a, 38.

40) De Anim. III, 4. §. 5. p. 429, a, 29. ὅτι δ᾽ οὐχ ὁμοία ἡ ἀπάθεια τοῦ αἰσθητικοῦ καὶ τοῦ νοητικοῦ, φανερὸν ἐπὶ τῶν αἰσθητηρίων καὶ τῆς αἰσθήσεως. ἡ μὲν γὰρ αἴσθησις οὐ δύναται αἰσθάνεσθαι ἐκ τοῦ σφόδρα αἰσθητοῦ, οἷον ψόφου ἐκ τῶν μεγάλων ψόφων, οὐδ᾽ ἐκ ἰσχυρῶν χρωμάτων καὶ ὀσμῶν οὔτε ὁρᾶν οὔτε ὀσμᾶσθαι· ἀλλ᾽ ὁ νοῦς ὅταν τι νοήσῃ σφόδρα νοητόν, οὐχ ἧττον νοεῖ τὰ ὑποδεέστερα ἀλλὰ καὶ μᾶλλον· τὸ μὲν γὰρ αἰσθητικὸν οὐκ ἄνευ σώματος, ὁ δὲ χωριστός.

9. If we compare these three proofs for the spirituality of the intellective part with the three proofs we took from Aristotle in order to demonstrate the opposite nature of the sensitive [p. 128] soul<sup>44)</sup>, we find that two of them, here and there, correspond symmetrically to one another. The proof that we gave there in the third place, and which, because it started from the real ground, was the properly demonstrative proof, corresponds to the first of the proofs given here, and this one too is, for the same reason, the most excellent. Likewise the second proof, of which we made use there, has its exact counterpart in the third proof given here. The first proof given there, on the other hand, which was based on the divisibility of some sensitively ensouled beings into several substances of the same species, could not conversely be used for the spirituality of the intellective soul, since the cause of such divisibility lies not in the nature of the sensitive being in general, but in the particular constitution of certain species of animals; and just as little could Aristotle use the second proof for the spirituality of the intellective part, which started from the lack of an organ, conversely for demonstrating a bodily subject of sensation, since not every faculty to which its cognitions are in some way conveyed through organs must itself be mixed with the body. Our intellect, as we shall yet see, receives its concepts through the mediation of the senses; and so someone could, despite the mediating bodily organs, still regard the ultimate terminus, the proper subject of sensation, as something spiritual, as Plato did.

41) Anal. Post. I, 2. p. 72, a, 27., ibid. II, 19. p. 99, a, 26. and 100, b, 9.

42) Metaph. α, 3. p. 995, a, 14.

43) Anal. Post. I, 13. p. 78, b, 17. εἰ ἡ ἀπόφασις αἰτία τοῦ μὴ ὑπάρχειν, ἡ κατάφασις τοῦ ὑπάρχειν.

10. Through these proofs of the spirituality of the intellective soul, a presupposition on which we have already relied several times in earlier discussions has now proved to be correct. What we have so far developed hypothetically about the immortality of the intellective soul and about the manner of its union with the remaining parts of man now receives its full significance. We refer to what was said there<sup>45)</sup>.

44) See above, Part III. n. 13.

45) See above, Part I. n. 7. cf. also Part IV. n. 21. — That the soul, if it continues to exist after separation from the body, remains something individual is indubitable, for according to Aristotle the universal does not exist outside thought otherwise than in individuals (cf. Anal. Post. I, 11. princ.) It is likewise obvious that it must still be the same individual as before (only, because of the loss of the lower part, it will not be a complete man and not a complete substance at all; of which later), for even when an individual substance changes into another of the same kind, this change is a substantial one (cf. Part I. n. 5.), which according to Aristotle would be impossible without matter. — Here, now, it has been thought that a contradiction in the Aristotelian doctrine has been discovered. Men are living beings of the same species; they therefore also have souls belonging to one species. If, therefore, the intellective souls of Plato and Socrates continue to exist after death, then there are several purely

[p. 129] 11. But now we come back once more to the impassibility of the intellect and of the faculties of sensation. We have just [p. 130] seen that the intellect, since it can in no way be corrupted by its objects, must be called impassible in a still higher sense than the sentient powers. On the other hand, there nevertheless belongs to the intellect an undergoing—admittedly an undergoing in an improper sense—in which the senses have no share; it is [p. 131] that change which it undergoes in acquiring the completed disposition for knowing, and which is a real change, only not a corruption but a perfecting of its natural state<sup>46)</sup>. To the senses this readiness for operating is already given by nature<sup>47)</sup>; no one needs to learn to see color and to hear sound; every sense can perceive as soon as the sensible object is present to it. Not so the intellect, which only when it has acquired habitual knowledge—which happens either through learning or through its own discovery—is able, whenever it wills and without outside help, actually to grasp the thoughts within itself<sup>48)</sup>. If it has known anything at all, then it can also know itself<sup>49)</sup>, for it is then able to make its thinking itself into an object<sup>50)</sup> and so to grasp itself as thinking<sup>51)</sup>.

spiritual substances of the same species. But this, as Aristotle repeatedly asserts, is impossible. (cf. e.g. Metaph. Λ, 8. p. 1074, a, 31.) Therefore, it was concluded, Aristotle, if he assumes an immortality of the soul, falls into contradiction with his own principles.

In order to judge whether this reproach is justified or not, we must make clear to ourselves the reason that determined Aristotle to deny any plurality of immaterial beings of the same species. This reason would indeed be easy to see if the word εἶδος, and likewise the expression τὸ τί ἦν εἶναι etc., which Aristotle uses both to designate the form (μορφή) and the concept of the species, meant the same thing for him in both cases. If species and form were the same, then obviously even with material beings, insofar as they belong to one *species*, the *form* too could be only a single one, and the plurality would be exclusively a plurality of matter. No wonder, then, that with beings that are pure forms a plurality within *one* species would be declared unthinkable. Now, however, the matter stands otherwise. Aristotle distinguishes in the most definite way between εἶδος (and τὸ τί ἦν εἶναι etc.) in the sense of the species and in the sense of the form. Even where the former is something universal, common to many, the latter is for him something individual (cf. Metaph. Λ, 5. p. 1071, a, 27. ibid. Z, 3. p. 1028, b, 33.), and if both are separated from matter by abstraction, it is nevertheless by an abstraction of quite different character (cf. what was determined about this in the First Part, especially n. 3. and n. 12. with the notes.). An εἶδος in the latter sense is, for example, the soul, since it may not be identified with the concept of the species of this plant or of this animal. When Aristotle names the εἶδος as one of the principles of things, he means the form in the sense in which the soul is so called, not the concept of the species. (cf. De Anim. I, 1. §. 5. p. 402, b, 8.)

How, then, we must ask, does Aristotle come to deny any plurality of immaterial beings of the same species? — It is explained thus. Although Aristotle does not identify the form with the species, the form nevertheless counts for him in a certain sense as the *principle* of the species, and matter—although it too is by no means identical with the individual difference—as the *principle* of the ultimate difference, which is no longer a specific difference. (cf. e.g. Metaph. Z. 8. p. 1034, a, 7. Λ, 8. p. 1074, a, 33.) But in what sense they count for him as such has often not been correctly recognized. To make it clear, we must ask what Aristotle understands by species and specific difference. By the specific difference he understands the ultimate essential determinateness of a thing over against other things that the intellect can grasp, that is, the ultimate *intelligible* difference. Now since, according to his doctrine, everything that is spiritual is perfectly intelligible, down to its ultimate essential determinateness (De Anim. III, 4. §. 12. p. 430, a, 3. Metaph. Λ, 9. p. 1074, b, 38.), it is clear that with spiritual things the ultimate intelligible, that is, the specific difference coincides with the ultimate difference simply; therefore with them the ultimate specific difference is at the same time the individual difference, and species and individual are one and the same (cf. Metaph. Λ, 8. p. 1074, a, 36.). But since, according to his doctrine, everything that is material is not perfectly intelligible, not intelligible down to its ultimate essential determinateness, it is just as clear that here the specific, that is, the ultimate intelligible difference is not identical with the absolutely ultimate one

; there still remains a further, merely sensible difference (cf. De Anim. III, 4. §. 7. p. 429, b, 10.); species and individual therefore do not coincide here; there are several individuals of one species (Metaph. Λ, 8. p. 1074, a, 34.). What, then, is the ground of the plurality of individuals in a species? Obviously that which is the reason why the thing is not perfectly intelligible; and this is matter, as the principle of possible being-otherwise, of indeterminacy, of fluctuating change; what is in itself indeterminate (cf. Part I. n. 36.) is the ground of the lack of determinability (cf. Metaph. Γ, 5. p. 1010, a, 1—15.). Matter is therefore the ground of the diversity of individuals within *one* species, and hence, to make use of the Scholastic expression (which, admittedly, is also used in other and several senses), the principle of individuation. What, on the other hand, is the reason why the thing nevertheless actually has a species, a specific difference? Obviously that which is the reason why the thing is intelligible at least to a certain degree (for without being intelligible it could have no intelligible difference); and this is the form, which makes the thing what it is, which gives actuality to what is merely possible (cf. Part I. n. 3.), for everything is intelligible only insofar as it is actual (cf. Metaph. Θ, 9. p. 1051, a, 29. and Part I. n. 36.); and therefore it is, so to speak, the principle of the species. As the principle of the species, Aristotle then often calls it species (εἶδος) itself.

Now that this discussion has made clear to us the relation of form and species in Aristotle, and we have recognized the reason for his doctrine about the possibility or impossibility of a plurality of individuals of the same species, it is no longer difficult to defend him against the reproach that, by assuming the immortality of the intellective part, he becomes untrue to his general principles. For since, according to him, every being differs from every other not merely in matter (if, that is, it has matter) but also in form (cf. Metaph. Λ, 5. p. 1071, a, 27.), one man too differs from another not merely in matter but also in form, and this holds, as regards the latter, both of its part mixed with the body and of its spiritual part. The bodily part of man is, as material, not completely intelligible; his spiritual part, on the other hand, is completely intelligible. Hence the ultimate difference of the bodily part is not an intelligible, that is, not a specific difference, whereas the ultimate difference of the spiritual part is intelligible and therefore a specific difference—not as though it were greater, but because it is the difference of a spiritual being, and thus of one intelligible down to its ultimate determinateness. The bodily part of all men is therefore of *one* species, but the spiritual part is specifically different in each, without our therefore standing any less close to one another in respect of this part. Bodily and spiritually we form *one* human race. If, now, our spiritual part is separated from the bodily part in death, it is clear that its individual difference, which is at the same time a specific difference, remains, and that therefore nothing can be objected from this side against the individual continuance of the human soul after death.

In this pronouncement of our philosopher we perceive a twofold difference from the doctrine of Plato. For, first, Plato held that in our spirit there is present already from birth a knowledge of all the intelligible objects that we ever come to know in later life—a darkened and, so to speak, slumbering knowledge, to be sure, which must first be awakened from its sleep; all learning counted for him as recollection. Second, he held that our knowledge is similar to our sensory memory, and that by virtue of knowledge we could therefore renew in ourselves the consciousness of the spiritual objects formerly beheld, without a new action of those objects. Aristotle rejects both. In his view no knowledge is innate to the intellect, and even acquired knowledge is not a remaining of the thoughts in the intellect comparable to imagination and sensory memory<sup>52)</sup>; rather, it is nothing other than the completed disposition of the receptive intellect, through the action of the active principle proper to it, [p. 132] to become thinking, just as the sense becomes perceiving through the action of its proper object; knowledge, then, is analogous to the readiness, innate in us, for seeing and hearing and for other sense perception. Hence, just as we never see anything without the object acting on the sense of sight, so we never think a thought—even if we had already thought it a hundred times—without the active principle, through whose action the intellect received it when it thought it for the first time, moving it anew.

46) De Anim. II, 5. §. 5. p. 417, b, 9. Cf. what is said in Metaph. Θ, 6. p. 1048, b, 28. of μάθησις in contrast to νοεῖν (34.).

47) De Anim. II, 5. §. 6. p. 417, b, 16.

48) De Anim. II, 5. §. 4. p. 417, a, 27. De Anim. III, 4. §. 6. p. 429. b, 5. ὅταν δ᾽ οὕτως ἕκαστα γένηται ὡς ὁ ἐπιστήμων λέγεται ὁ κατ᾽ ἐνέργειαν, (τοῦτο δὲ συμβαίνει ὅταν δύνηται ἐνεργεῖν δι᾽ αὐτοῦ,) ἔστι μὲν καὶ τότε δυνάμει πως, οὐ μὴν ὁμοίως καὶ πρὶν μαθεῖν ἢ εὑρεῖν.

49) De Anim. III, 4. §. 6. p. 429, b, 9. καὶ αὐτὸς δὲ αὐτὸν τότε δύναται νοεῖν. cf. Metaph. Λ, 9. p. 1074, b, 35.

50) De Anim. III, 4. §. 12. p. 430, a, 2. καὶ αὐτὸς δὲ νοητός ἐστιν ὥσπερ τὰ νοητά.

51) Metaph. Λ, 7. p. 1072, b, 20. αὐτὸν δὲ νοεῖ ὁ νοῦς κατὰ μετάληψιν τοῦ νοητοῦ, νοητὸς γὰρ γίγνεται θιγγάνων καὶ νοῶν.

52) This point has often been insufficiently heeded, even by great experts on Aristotle. Avicenna, whose peculiar doctrine of the treasuries of memory we set out above, evidently followed in Aristotle’s footsteps when he denied such a treasury for spiritual knowledge. See above, Section I. n. 33.

11. These two points, in which Aristotle departs from the Platonic doctrine, are of the highest importance for understanding his theory of knowledge, and for that reason we shall come back to them later as well, and shall both confirm them from new passages in proof and also demonstrate them in their grounds. But they stand in intimate connection with a third point, in which the two great thinkers diverge from each other and of which Aristotle comes to speak immediately.

Socrates had already rightly recognized and brought to bear that what is grasped in the definition and the individual thing that we perceive outside us, and which participates in the definition, by no means fully coincide, since the definition of corporeal things, even in their ultimate difference, is still always common to a plurality of individual things. Aristotle therefore held fast to this truth. According to him, too, species and individual coincide in no corporeal thing, and only in spiritual things is the ultimate difference of the species one and the same with the ultimate difference of the thing<sup>53)</sup>. To this extent, then, he finds himself in complete agreement with Plato, who had so strongly emphasized the difference of the ideas from sensible things. “Magnitude,” he says, “is one thing and the being of magnitude another, and water and the being of water, and so it is with many other things as well; but not,” he adds, “with all; for in some (he means spiritual things) the two are one and the same<sup>54)</sup>.” Now since flesh, for example, is not something immaterial but is composed of matter and form, it follows from what has been said that flesh and the being of flesh must be different from each other [p. 133] and from this it further follows that they are known through different things; for we know, after all, through that which we receive into the cognitive faculty, and that which we receive into it is precisely the known object<sup>55)</sup>, here, then, the flesh and the being of flesh. Evidently, then, the two are known through different things.

53) For by the species he understands that which is intelligible in the thing. Spiritual things are spiritually knowable down to the ultimate individual difference, but material things are not. Hence in the former, but not in the latter, species and individual are one and the same. See above, n. 45.

54) De Anim. III, 4. §. 7. p. 429, b, 10. ἐπεὶ δ᾽ ἄλλο ἐστὶ τὸ μέγεθος καὶ τὸ μεγέθει εἶναι καὶ ὕδωρ καὶ ὕδατι εἶναι· οὕτω δὲ καὶ ἐφ᾽ ἑτέρων πολλῶν, ἀλλ᾽ οὐκ ἐπὶ πάντων· ἐπ᾽ ἐνίων γὰρ ταὐτόν ἐστι. κ. τ. λ. (Which passage Trendelenburg has made intelligible by correcting the punctuation.)

But the question arises in what way that through which the one is known and that through which the other is known differ from each other. Are they different as *one* thing from another thing, or as a thing is different from itself when it is disposed now one way and now another<sup>56)</sup>? — Here is the point at which Plato and Aristotle part in opposite directions. Plato held that we know flesh and the being of flesh by receiving into ourselves two different things, and indeed two things separated from each other in substance; for the idea is, according to him, a thing by itself and subsists separately from the material. If, then, someone perceives one and the same body with touch as this warm thing and with sight as this white thing, one can say of him, if Plato is right, that he grasps one and the same thing with two faculties even sooner than one can of the person who feels something warm and at the same time thinks the being of the warm with the intellect; for this white thing and this warm thing are at least identical in subject, but this warm thing and the being of the warm would be entirely separated from each other.

Now Aristotle, as was said, teaches the exact opposite of this. The white that sight, and the warm that touch, receives into itself are, to be sure, according to him, to be called identical in a certain sense when one and the same body underlies them; they are at least one per accidens, inasmuch as both properties belong to one thing; but this is only an improper identity; essentially they are in any case different, which is also why the definition of each of them is a different one. On the other hand, this warm thing which the sense grasps and the being of the warm which the intellect grasps are not merely one inasmuch as both are in *one* body; nor are they different properties of this body; they are essentially identical, and through the definition in which the being of the warm is laid out, precisely that warm thing which the sense grasps is defined<sup>57)</sup>. Like genus and difference, then, so [p. 134] according to Aristotle species and individual difference, too, form a unity of essence, whereas Plato abolished this unity for the one pair as for the other, since he conceived species and genus as two different spiritual hypostases existing in the world of ideas, but regarded the individual thing as a being of the earthly world different from both. In both respects Aristotle, especially in the Metaphysics<sup>58)</sup>, has pointed out to him the numerous incongruities that follow from his doctrine. Here he touches only on the second point and, from the side of psychology, raises the question how the object through which, when we have received it into ourselves, we know the individual flesh is related to that through which we know the being of flesh. Of the one, now, he says, it is evident that it is something sensible-corporeal; but regarding the object received in the intellect, two things are at first conceivable: either it is something supersensible, incorporeal, or it is indeed the same sensible-corporeal flesh that is in the sense, but the state in which it is found in the one faculty and in the other is a different one<sup>59)</sup>. For which of the two shall we decide? [p. 135] Aristotle declares the second to be the correct one, for a reason which he does not state here, but which he could well pass over in silence, since he gave it in the Analytics<sup>60)</sup> and will also repeat it here directly, at the end of the seventh chapter<sup>61)</sup>. Besides, we also find it in several passages of the Metaphysics<sup>62)</sup>. For it would evidently be a ridiculous assertion that someone who wanted to know something and instead grasped something else in his intellect had thereby arrived at the knowledge he desired. Now, however, the natural scientist, for example, wants to come to know the crystals and the plants and the other bodies that he finds here on earth; if, then, he grasped the concepts of tetrahedra and octahedra, of trees and grasses, that belong to another world, he would evidently in no way attain his end. So, then, says Aristotle, it is not. When the intellect knows the being of flesh, it is not something other and immaterial, but the same object that is in the sense, which is received by it; but in the intellect it is abstract, in the sense concrete with individual matter. Quite aptly, therefore, he compares the relation of what is thought in the intellect to what is perceived by sense with the relation of a line that was bent and then straightened to itself in its former state. It is still now the line that it was, but it is otherwise; it has become simpler; and so the corporeal object that was in the sense is still one and the same in the intellect, but its state is different here and there. Like the line, it has become simpler; the individual difference has been leveled out; and so it comes about that, although something material is in the intellect, it is nevertheless in it as something immaterial<sup>63)</sup>. But as with the concept of flesh, so it is also with every other concept that, like all physical ones, for example, does not abstract entirely from the sensible qualities; and with the mathematical ones, too, it is no different. The individual straight line that is in the sense and the being of the straight that the intellect grasps are essentially identical. Here too, then, one must not believe that the intellect knows something more immaterial than the sense, that it receives into itself something incorporeal or at least something non-sensible; no, the same thing that is in it is also in the sense, but disposed in one way and in another; and so it is to be held generally that only insofar as things outside [p. 136] spiritual knowing are free of matter can that which is in the intellect also be free of it<sup>64)</sup>. We shall return to this when we speak of the dependence of thinking on the phantasms.

55) Cf. the passages cited in n. 34.

56) Aristotle continues in the passage: τὸ σαρκὶ εἶναι καὶ σάρκα ἢ ἄλλῳ ἢ ἄλλως ἔχοντι κρίνει· ἡ γὰρ σὰρξ οὐκ ἄνευ τῆς ὕλης, ἀλλ᾽ ὥσπερ τὸ σιμὸν τόδε ἐν τῷδε.

57) We can indeed say: this warm thing is this white thing (identity in subject); but we cannot say: this warmth is this whiteness, i.e. this white color (identity in essence). On the other hand, we can say both: this warm thing is warm, and: this warmth is warmth. We

can therefore predicate the species essentially of the individual; they are ἓν καθ᾽ αὑτό, whereas this white thing and this warm thing, and everything that is united in a subject in a similar way as they are, can be called only ἓν κατὰ συμβεβηκός. Cf. the discussions in the twelfth chap. of the seventh, and in particular those in the sixth chap. of the eighth book of the Metaphysics, where Aristotle also points to the perplexity of those who conceived the relation of individual and species otherwise. (p. 1045. b, 7.)

58) Metaph. Α and Μ.

59) De Anim. III, 4. §. 7. p. 429, b, 14. Aristotle (for what precedes, see above, n. 56.) continues thus: τῷ μὲν οὖν αἰσθητῷ [as is to be read instead of αἰσθητικῷ] τὸ θερμὸν καὶ ψυχρὸν κρίνει, καὶ ὧν λόγος τις ἡ σάρξ· ἄλλῳ δὲ ἤτοι χωριστῷ, ἢ ὡς ἡ κεκλασμένη ἔχει πρὸς αὐτὴν ὅταν ἐκταθῇ, τὸ σαρκὶ εἶναι κρίνει. As a result of a corruption of the text, this passage has given occasion to many misunderstandings and has contributed not a little to the fact that Aristotle’s doctrine of the νοῦς has almost always been wrongly interpreted. For the traditional text said nothing other than that the intellect, which grasps concepts, is one with the sense—which would stand in the most glaring contradiction to the doctrine developed so far in this chapter and to Aristotle’s other statements. If, on the other hand, one writes αἰσθητῷ instead of the transmitted αἰσθητικῷ, then what Aristotle seemed to say of the faculty of sensation and that of thought holds of the relation between what is grasped in the faculty of sensation and what is grasped in the intellect. The corruption is similar to another in the tenth chapter, where, as is acknowledged, ὀρεκτόν is to be read in one place, although almost all manuscripts have ὀρεκτικόν. In De Memor. et Remin. 1. p. 450, a, 14., too, the previous reading αἰσθητικοῦ seems to need replacing by αἰσθητοῦ, for it forms the contrast to νοουμένου. Our conjecture, then, is certainly not a bold one, and all the less so since the corruption is easily explained by the unusual expression; for everyone says that one perceives through the sensitive faculty, but that one perceives through the sensible which is received in the sense is an entirely peculiar Aristotelian

manner of speaking. In De Anim. III, 6. §. 4. p. 430, b, 16. (ᾧ νοεῖ), too, people have stumbled over it.

60) Anal. Poster. I, 22. p. 83, a, 32. τὰ γὰρ εἴδη χαιρέτω· τερετίσματά τε γάρ ἐστι, καὶ εἰ ἔστιν, οὐδὲν πρὸς τὸν λόγον ἐστίν· αἱ γὰρ ἀποδείξεις περὶ τῶν τοιούτων εἰσίν.

61) De Anim. III, 7. §. 8. p. 431, b, 16.

62) Metaph. Α, 9. p. 991, a, 12. (cf. p. 992, a, 24.) and Μ, 5. p. 1079, b, 15.

63) See above, n. 59.

12. Now, however, before we go further, we wish to pause for a moment in order to remove the misgivings that may perhaps have arisen for some from the determinations given so far concerning the intellect.

The intellect, we said, has by nature not a single one of the things actually in itself, and its operation is an undergoing; we said further that it knows what is corporeal, but that it also knows itself, and that it itself is spiritual, hence free of all corporeal qualities; finally, that although it knows itself, it nevertheless knows itself neither always nor first, but only secondarily.

13. Already the first point seems to violate the general law that the agent and the patient must have something in common, as, for example, the warm, which warms the cold, has the genus in common with it<sup>65)</sup>, for both are tangible qualities. If, now, the intellect has nothing at all in itself, and hence also has nothing in common with any thing, how, we ask, is it then to undergo anything<sup>66)</sup>?

Yet to this difficulty, at least, it is easy for us to reply, since it is no other than the one that can also be raised with regard to the senses. Aristotle, therefore, in order to meet it, already in the fifth chapter<sup>67)</sup> of the second book distinguished two kinds of patient: [p. 137] each, he says, undergoes by virtue of something common, but in one sense and in another. The one kind of undergoing is corruption by the contrary, and here the agent and the patient have the genus in common, as in the example above the warm and the cold are identical in genus; the other kind of undergoing, however, is not a corruption but rather a preserving completion of that which is in potentiality, and here the patient and the agent are similar and akin to each other in the way in which a potentiality is similar to the actuality corresponding to it. Now sensing is such an undergoing, and spiritual knowing is also such a one; and it therefore does not follow that the intellect, because it undergoes, must have something actually in itself; rather it suffices if, as we saw, it is the potentiality of all thoughts<sup>68)</sup>.

64) De Anim. III, 4. §. 8. p. 429, b, 18. Aristotle (for what precedes, see above, n. 59.) continues: πάλιν δ᾽ ἐπὶ τῶν ἐν ἀφαιρέσει ὄντων τὸ εὐθὺ ὡς τὸ σιμόν· μετὰ συνεχοῦς γάρ· τὸ δὲ τί ἦν εἶναι, εἰ ἔστιν ἕτερον τὸ εὐθεῖ εἶναι καὶ τὸ εὐθύ, ἄλλῳ· ἔστω γὰρ δυάς (a view which is not his own; cf.
