Metaph. Ζ, 11. p. 1036, b, 14. and Ν, 3. p. 1090, b, 22.). ἑτέρῳ ἄρα ἢ ἑτέρως ἔχοντι κρίνει. [καὶ] ὅλως ἄρα ὡς χωριστὰ τὰ πράγματα τῆς ὕλης, οὕτω καὶ τὰ περὶ τὸν νοῦν. cf. De Anim. III, 7. §. 7. f. p. 431, b, 12. The καί, which is missing in several manuscripts, is perhaps better omitted, for only with this sentence does the answer begin to the question which of the two assumptions one is to decide for. But since his view was already recognizable in the way in which Aristotle put them forward, the καί is not absolutely to be rejected.

65) De Generat. et Corrupt. I, 7. p. 323, b, 29. ἀλλ᾽ ἐπεὶ οὐ τὸ τυχὸν πέφυκε πάσχειν καὶ ποιεῖν, ἀλλ᾽ ὅσα ἢ ἐναντία ἐστὶν ἢ ἐναντίωσιν ἔχει, ἀνάγκη καὶ τὸ ποιοῦν καὶ τὸ πάσχον τῷ γένει μὲν ὅμοιον εἶναι καὶ ταὐτό, τῷ δ᾽ εἴδει ἀνόμοιον καὶ ἐναντίον· πέφυκε γὰρ σῶμα μὲν ὑπὸ σώματος, χυμὸς δ᾽ ὑπὸ χυμοῦ, χρῶμα δ᾽ ὑπὸ χρώματος πάσχειν, ὅλως δὲ τὸ ὁμογενὲς ὑπὸ τοῦ ὁμογενοῦς. . . . ὥστ᾽ ἀνάγκη πῶς μὲν εἶναι ταὐτὰ τό τε ποιοῦν καὶ τὸ πάσχον, πῶς δ᾽ ἕτερα καὶ ἀνόμοια ἀλλήλοις.

66) De Anim. III, 4. §. 9. p. 429, b, 22. ἀπορήσειε δ᾽ ἄν τις, εἰ ὁ νοῦς ἁπλοῦν ἐστι καὶ ἀπαθὲς καὶ μηθενὶ μηθὲν ἔχει κοινόν, ὥσπερ φησὶν Ἀναξαγόρας, πῶς νοήσει, εἰ τὸ νοεῖν πάσχειν τί ἐστιν, ᾗ γάρ τι κοινὸν ἀμφοῖν ὑπάρχει, τὸ μὲν ποιεῖν δοκεῖ τὸ δὲ πάσχειν.

67) De Anim. II, 5. §. 5. p. 417, b, 2. οὐκ ἔστι δ᾽ ἁπλοῦν οὐδὲ τὸ πάσχειν, ἀλλὰ τὸ μὲν φθορά τις ὑπὸ τοῦ ἐναντίου, τὸ δὲ σωτηρία μᾶλλον τοῦ δυνάμει ὄντος ὑπὸ τοῦ ἐντελεχείᾳ ὄντος καὶ ὁμοίου οὕτως, ὡς δύναμις ἔχει πρὸς ἐντελέχειαν.

14. This misgiving, then, would be removed; let us now see how matters stand with the others. We have said that the intellect, in knowing, receives the corporeal things into itself; but that it then also receives itself, for when it has known something else, it can also know itself. From this it seems to follow either that the corporeal things, too, have intellect and are thinking, as it is, or, conversely, that it itself has something corporeal; for that which it receives must, after all, as with all other receptive powers, be of one and the same quality in genus, just as, for example, what the sense of sight receives must be colored and what hearing grasps must be sounding<sup>69)</sup>. If, then, it receives itself as thinking, the corporeal things, too, seem to have to have intellect in order to be grasped, as it is, as thinking things; or, if it receives these by virtue of a corporeal quality, it too seems to have a share in the corporeal—both of which are contrary to our earlier determinations<sup>70)</sup>.

How, then, shall we resolve this objection? — The remark [p. 138] that everything that is grasped in the intellect must be intelligible in the same way is correct. But the intelligibility of the received body and that of the intellect can be of *one* genus, although the body, as it is outside the mind, has no property in common with the intellect. For the intelligible is that which is in a spiritual way, and both the intellect and the corporeal objects, when they are thought in the intellect, are in it in a spiritual way. In the case of the intellect this is self-evident, for it is, after all, immaterial by its nature; the corporeal thing, however, is indeed something material and remains so even when it is received in the intellect<sup>71)</sup>, but it is in it in an immaterial way and not as it exists outside it. For outside it, it is individually determined, since a universal without individual difference cannot exist at all; but in the intellect it has lost its individual determinateness—the bent line, to use the earlier simile, has been straightened out—and in this state, originally foreign to it, the corporeal, too, can now be in the intellect.

68) De Anim. III, 4. §. 11. p. 429, b, 29. ἢ τὸ μὲν πάσχειν κατὰ κοινόν τι διήρηται πρότερον, ὅτι δυνάμει πώς ἐστι τὰ νοητὰ ὁ νοῦς, ἀλλ᾽ ἐντελεχείᾳ οὐδέν, πρὶν ἂν νοῇ. This passage is not, as Torstrik thinks, corrupt. In the objection in §. 9. (see above, n. 66.), the πάσχειν κατὰ κοινόν τι had been asserted as a general law to which the undergoing of the νοῦς, too, must be subject. Aristotle grants this, but remarks that the πάσχειν κατὰ κοινόν τι had already been distinguished earlier, namely in the fifth chap. of the second book (see n. 67.), as twofold. The undergoing of the νοῦς, he says, is one in which the patient is not identical in genus with the agent, but only akin to it as the potential is to the corresponding actual. The πάσχειν κατὰ κοινόν τι is therefore to be regarded as a single infinitive used as a noun, and the κοινόν τι is not to be construed with the διήρηται.

69) See above, Part III. no. 3.

70) De Anim. III, 4. §. 10. p. 429, b, 26. ἔτι δ᾽ (ἀπορήσειεν ἄν τις) εἰ νοητὸς καὶ αὐτὸς (ὁ νοῦς). ἢ γὰρ τοῖς ἄλλοις ὁ νοῦς ὑπάρξει, εἰ μὴ κατ᾽ ἄλλο αὐτὸς νοητός, ἓν δέ τι τὸ νοητὸν εἴδει, ἢ μεμιγμένον τι ἕξει, ὃ ποιεῖ νοητὸν αὐτὸν ὥσπερ τἆλλα.

The intellect, on the other hand, is knowable just as it is; its ultimate difference simply is also its ultimate specific, i.e. its ultimate intelligible, difference, and we therefore have not merely a universal concept of our intellect but also an individual spiritual self-consciousness. The “I think” we grasp with the same evidence with which we know that there is thinking at all; our own individual being is just as clear and certain to us as being as such, which is the first principle of metaphysics. The intellect, then, is completely intelligible, and completely so with the highest intelligibility, whereas corporeal things, on the one hand, as already remarked, permit only an indeterminate universal knowledge, and on the other are not equally knowable in all their determinations. We know them the more surely and clearly, and thus have them in us the more intelligibly, the more they have been estranged by abstraction from their natural mode of existence<sup>72)</sup>. Hence mathematics is more intelligible than physics, and metaphysics more intelligible than mathematics; also, the more universal physical concept is more intelligible than the more special one, the genus more than the species, and the higher genus more than the lower; the more incomplete the knowledge of the object<sup>73)</sup>, the more intelligibly [p. 139] is it thought in us—all of which certainly proves in the clearest way that the corporeal is intelligible in the intellect not in consequence of a natural quality, but through a change that estranges it from its own proper state. By its nature, then, it is to be called intelligible only in potentiality; and therefore from the fact that the intellect is immaterial and free of every corporeal quality it follows neither that the corporeal things must have intellect nor that the intellect cannot be intelligible<sup>74)</sup>.

71) As we have shown under no. 11.

72) Cf. Anal. Poster. I, 2. p. 72, a, 3. ἁπλῶς δὲ πρότερα καὶ γνωριμώτερα τὰ πορρώτερον (τῆς αἰσθήσεως). ἔστι δὲ πορρωτάτω μὲν τὰ καθόλου μάλιστα, ἐγγυτάτω δὲ τὰ καθ᾽ ἕκαστα. See further Metaph. α, 3. p. 995, a, 14. and De Anim. III, 7. §. 7. p. 431, b, 15.

73) Knowledge merely according to the genus is more incomplete than specific knowledge. Hence De Anim. II, 3. §. 5. p. 414, b, 25. διὸ γελοῖον ζητεῖν

τὸν κοινὸν λόγον καὶ ἐπὶ τούτων (in the case of souls and figures) καὶ ἐφ᾽ ἑτέρων, ὃς οὐδενὸς ἔσται τῶν ὄντων ἴδιος λόγος, οὐδὲ κατὰ τὸ οἰκεῖον καὶ ἄτομον εἶδος, ἀφέντας τὸν τοιοῦτον.

74) De Anim. III, 4. §. 12. p. 430, a, 2. Here Aristotle gives what we have set out at greater length in the brief words: καὶ αὐτὸς δὲ νοητός ἐστιν ὥσπερ τὰ νοητά (its intelligibility is of the same genus as that of the corporeal beings known by it; see §. 10, b, 28.). ἐπὶ μὲν γὰρ τῶν ἄνευ ὕλης τὸ αὐτό ἐστι τὸ νοοῦν καὶ τὸ νοούμενον (cf. §. 7. b, 13.)· ἡ γὰρ ἐπιστήμη ἡ θεωρητικὴ καὶ τὸ οὕτως ἐπιστητόν (namely ὡς τὰ ἄνευ ὕλης, for these are knowable just as they are, whereas the material finds reception in the mind only in altered form (cf. ibid.), which is why Aristotle will presently designate it as only δυνάμει νοητὸν) τὸ αὐτό ἐστιν· τοῦ δὲ μὴ ἀεὶ νοεῖν τὸ αἴτιον ἐπισκεπτέον (an intermediate clause which we shall discuss shortly; see no. 15.)· ἐν δὲ τοῖς ἔχουσιν ὕλην δυνάμει ἕκαστόν ἐστι τῶν νοητῶν. ὥστ᾽ ἐκείνοις μὲν οὐχ ὑπάρξει νοῦς (ἄνευ γὰρ ὕλης δύναμις ὁ νοῦς τῶν τοιούτων), ἐκείνῳ δὲ τὸ νοητὸν ὑπάρξει.

The same doctrine of the different intelligibility of material and immaterial objects, and of the perfect intelligibility of our νοῦς, is contained in the ninth chapter of the twelfth book of the Metaphysics (p. 1074, b, 38.), only with the difference that, whereas in our passage Aristotle derived the perfect knowability of the νοῦς from the general proposition that everything immaterial is completely intelligible, there, conversely, he wants to establish this general proposition inductively, starting from the intelligibility of our spiritual knowing, both poietic and theoretical. Since everything spiritual that presents itself to our νοῦς as an object of knowledge (but this, at least so long as it is united with the body, is only itself (see below, n. 109.) in its various acts, for having become knowing it becomes knowable to itself; cf. Metaph. Λ, 7. p. 1072, b, 20.)—since, then, I say, everything spiritual, as far as our experience reaches, whether it be something contemplatively knowing or something acting, is completely intelligible, we can see the ground of perfect knowability in nothing other than freedom from matter. It follows, therefore, that everything immaterial is perfectly intelligible, that with it there is no difference between the object and the thought of the object.

We let the words of Aristotle himself follow. Against a point of his doctrine of God he had asserted the proposition: οὐδὲ γὰρ ταὐτὸ τὸ εἶναι νοήσει καὶ νοουμένῳ. This remark, which stated in such generality is untrue, he then restricts by saying: ἢ ἐπ᾽ ἐνίων ἡ ἐπιστήμη τὸ πρᾶγμα (in the case of some objects of knowledge (cf. De Anim. III, 4. §. 7. p. 429, b, 12.) the knowledge is the object, i.e. between the thought and that of which it is the thought there exists no difference; knowledge and the object of knowledge correspond to each other perfectly)· ἐπὶ μὲν τῶν ποιητικῶν ἄνευ ὕλης (i.e. in the case of the

[p. 140] 15. But if the intellect is intelligible, and indeed, as we said, intelligible according to its natural mode of existence, a new collision seems to arise between our assertions; for we have said that it knows itself neither always nor first, but only secondarily, and the former, at least, experience teaches everyone so clearly that only those who did not shrink from flouting all experience have dared to deny it. But how can this fact be explained? Why does the intellect not know always, if it is intelligible, and hence something intelligible is always present to it? — To this question, which Aristotle raises at the end of the fourth chapter<sup>75)</sup>, he has added no answer. But the difficulty is no other than the one that he raised with regard to the sense faculties at the beginning of the fifth chapter of the preceding book and settled with all the precision one could wish. For, as we have seen earlier, sensation is not a pure faculty of the soul; rather, its subject is a bodily organ. This organ participates in sensible qualities of all genera; it has a certain warmth or coldness, softness or hardness, a certain color, a certain taste, and the like. Why, then, Aristotle asks himself in the passage mentioned, why do we have no sensation without an external object<sup>76)</sup>? The answer he gives had become unintelligible through a corruption of the text, which Torstrik has happily removed. Put into clear words, however, it is the following: the faculty of sensation is by its nature not actual sensation, but is the mere potentiality of it. Now since no potentiality attains to actuality without an active principle—as, for example, something combustible is not, without something that ignites it, [p. 141] actually ignited—it is clear that from the faculty of sensation, too, no actual sensing can come about unless something sensible acts on the sensing organ. But the sensing organ cannot itself exercise this action through its own sensible qualities; for something that is warm does not, after all, warm itself, but, when it is warmed, is warmed by something else that is warmer; and the same holds with regard to all other sensible qualities. Evidently, then, one can in no way wonder that no activity of sense takes place without an external object<sup>77)</sup>. But, perhaps someone says, if the sensitive organ has the sensible qualities in itself, is not that which is sensed nevertheless in that which senses<sup>78)</sup>? — In a certain sense, indeed, it is in it. For we speak of sensing in a twofold sense, since we call both what senses in potentiality and what senses in actuality sensing<sup>79)</sup>; and in quite a similar way a twofold being-sensed is also to be distinguished, that in potentiality and that in actuality. In the case of the sensitive organ, then, because it has sensible qualities in itself by nature, that which is sensed (the sensible) is indeed in that which senses (the sensitive); but sensing and being sensed are both used here in the sense of potentiality, and therefore it is by no means said thereby that the sensing organ senses its own qualities<sup>80)</sup>.

arts, for these, in contrast to the natural causes, which are synonymous with their effects in the most perfect way, are designated as those which are the εἶδος ἄνευ ὕλης of what is to be produced; cf. e.g. Metaph. Ζ, 7. p. 1032, a, 32. b, 11.) ἡ οὐσία καὶ τὸ τί ἦν εἶναι, ἐπὶ δὲ τῶν θεωρητικῶν ὁ λόγος τὸ πρᾶγμα καὶ ἡ νόησις. That is, both the artistically knowing intellect and the theoretical are perfectly intelligible; in the one, ἡ οὐσία καὶ τὸ τί ἦν εἶναι (namely the τί ἦν εἶναι of the work to be produced; for this is the art; cf. Metaph. Ζ, 7. p. 1032, b, 14.), in the other, ὁ λόγος (the concept of the thing known by the theoretical intellect), is both the object and the thought, τὸ πρᾶγμα καὶ ἡ νόησις. From this Aristotle then draws the general conclusion: οὐχ ἑτέρου οὖν ὄντος τοῦ νοουμένου καὶ τοῦ νοῦ, ὅσα μὴ ὕλην ἔχει, τὸ αὐτὸ ἔσται, καὶ ἡ νόησις τῷ νοουμένῳ μία.

75) Τοῦ δὲ μὴ ἀεὶ νοεῖν τὸ αἴτιον ἐπισκεπτέον. See the preceding n.

76) De Anim. II, 5. §. 2. p. 417, a, 2. ἔχει δ᾽ ἀπορίαν διὰ τί καὶ τῶν αἰσθήσεων αὐτῶν οὐ γίνεται αἴσθησις, καὶ διὰ τί ἄνευ τῶν ἔξω οὐ ποιοῦσιν αἴσθησιν, ἐνόντος πυρὸς καὶ γῆς καὶ τῶν ἄλλων στοιχείων, ὧν ἐστιν ἡ αἴσθησις καθ᾽ αὐτὰ ἢ τὰ συμβεβηκότα τούτοις. We pay particular attention to the second part of the aporia, since the solution of the first is self-evident after what we have heard about the nature of the faculty of sensation.

Now this proof we can transfer completely from the sensitive to the intellective domain. For the intellect, too, is by its nature not actual thinking but the mere potentiality of thoughts, just as the faculty of sensation is the mere potentiality of sensations. It is evident, then, that the intellect, like the faculty of sensation, needs something to act upon its subject [p. 142] in order that it may be raised to actual thinking. But its own intelligible qualities can exercise this influence just as little as the sensible qualities of the sensing subject were able to exercise it<sup>80 a)</sup>; for these stand in entirely the same relation to the senses as that in which the properties of the spiritual part stand to the intellect, since, just as the intellect can grasp what is intelligible by its nature, so the sense can grasp the sensible, not in a foreign, abstract way, but in its ultimate individual determinateness, as it is outside the sense.

77) Aristotle continues: δῆλον οὖν ὅτι τὸ αἰσθητικὸν οὐκ ἔστιν ἐνεργείᾳ ἀλλὰ δυνάμει μόνον. διὸ καθάπερ τὸ καυστὸν οὐ καίεται αὐτὸ καθ᾽ αὐτὸ ἄνευ τοῦ καυστικοῦ· ἔκαιε γὰρ ἂν ἑαυτό, καὶ οὐθὲν ἐδεῖτο τοῦ ἐντελεχείᾳ πυρὸς ὄντος.

78) And it is precisely through having the object of sensation in itself that what senses seems to sense.

79) Thus we say that the lizard sees even when its eyes are closed, in contrast to the snail, which does not see, i.e. which lacks the sense of sight.

80) Aristotle adds: ἐπειδὴ δὲ τὸ αἰσθάνεσθαι λέγομεν διχῶς (τό τε γὰρ δυνάμει ἀκοῦον καὶ ὁρῶν ἀκούειν καὶ ὁρᾶν λέγομεν, κἂν τύχῃ καθεῦδον, καὶ τὸ ἤδη ἐνεργοῦν), διχῶς ἂν λέγοιτο καὶ ἡ αἴσθησις, ἡ μὲν ὡς δυνάμει, ἡ δὲ ὡς ἐνεργείᾳ. ὁμοίως δὲ καὶ τὸ αἰσθητόν (so it is to be read, with Torstrik, instead of αἰσθάνεσθαι), τό τε δυνάμει ὂν καὶ τὸ ἐνεργείᾳ. Note the different way in which an αἰσθητόν was spoken of here and a νοητὸν δυνάμει in De Anim. III, 4. §. 12. (see n. 74.). There nothing spiritual belonged to the νοητὸν δυνάμει, whether it was actually known or not. Here, on the other hand, αἰσθητὸν ἐνεργείᾳ is used to designate what is actually sensed, in agreement with De Anim. III, 2. §. 8. p. 426, a, 23.

If here, too, one raises the objection that, after all, already by nature, because the subject of the intellect has intelligible properties, the object of spiritual knowing is in that which knows spiritually, the answer is likewise analogous to the earlier one. One can indeed say that in our case something that is spiritually known (something intelligible) is by nature in something that knows spiritually (something intellective); but being known and knowing must then be taken in the less proper sense of what is in potentiality, and so it is by no means yet stated therein that the subject of the intellect has a consciousness of its spiritual properties. If this were really the case, if, then, self-knowledge were a natural necessity, then the intellect would, after all, be incapable of thinking any other object; the continual actuality of the one would abolish the potentiality of all the others, as has been shown in more detail above.

So, then, the difficulty is not such an extraordinary one, and one can only wonder that many could believe that Aristotle added no solution because he himself was at a loss for one, whereas earlier, when he had met with exactly the same difficulty in the sensitive domain, he knew how to discuss it in so simple a way. He will not have forgotten his proof so quickly; rather, the whole chapter, which from beginning to end moves almost entirely in analogies to the fifth chapter of the second book, shows that he undoubtedly had it in mind. The first difficulty that he raised here, too, was entirely the same as one touched on there, and in solving it he even referred back expressly to a distinction made there<sup>81)</sup>; and now, when raising a question that is an equally clear parallel to another treated there, is that solution supposed not to have been present to his mind at all? Certainly this would be incredible, even if, as is in fact the case, yet another circumstance did not show that Aristotle must have had in mind here, too, the same solution that he gave there. For Aristotle passes on, at the [p. 143] one and the other passage to quite related questions: in the one cited from the second book, to the question of the active principle of sensation, in that he investigates whether this is similar to that which perceives or not<sup>82)</sup>; but in ours, to the question of the active principle of thoughts, when he says: “But since, as in the whole of nature there is for every genus something that is the matter (and this is that which is potentially all those things), but something else that is the cause and the active principle, in that it makes them all actual and is related to the former as the producing art is to its material, these differences must also be found in the soul. And the one intellective power has the stated property [namely, that it is potentially all things], because it becomes all things, but the other, because it effects all things, is like a habitus [an actual positive property], similar to light, etc.<sup>83)</sup>.” In the first passage the natural course of his thoughts had led him to such a question about the active principle; it had arisen out of the solution of the preceding one, which rested on the necessity of a cause that alters the perceiving subject. In our passage too, then, we must presumably suppose a quite similar transition of thought.

80 a) Cf. also Metaph. Θ, 1. p. 1046, b, 28.

81) See above, n. 68. cf. n. 67.

16. In everything that we have discussed so far concerning the spiritual part of the soul, we have followed step by step that order which Aristotle himself observed, so that our exposition can be regarded as a running commentary on the fourth chapter of the third book On the Soul. We have done this for a twofold reason: first, because there is hardly a sentence in it that would not also be of significance for the doctrine of the νοῦς ποιητικός; but then also because we wished thereby to justify in the most complete manner the assertion, already made in the first Section of our treatise<sup>84)</sup>, that in the whole fourth chapter no passage is found which refers *directly* to the νοῦς ποιητικός, although even then we did not leave it without grounds.

Besides this result, the following among the truths we have gained are of particular importance to us as points of support in investigating the doctrine of the νοῦς ποιητικός: First, that the intellect of man is a passive, form-grasping faculty analogous to the senses, and by its nature the mere potentiality of thoughts, and that it therefore, like the senses, needs a principle that brings it to actuality.

Second, that this faculty is not a faculty of the ensouled body but of the soul alone, and that therefore the intellect which receives thoughts, the νοῦς δυνάμει, is spiritual and immortal. [p. 144] This will become important for us particularly in determining the union of the νοῦς δυνάμει and the νοῦς ποιητικός, the latter of which, according to Aristotle, is indisputably something spiritual.

82) De Anim. II, 5. §. 3. p. 417, a, 14. —

83) De Anim. III, 5. princ. See below n. 32. at the beginning. —

84) Section I. n. 13. and n. 100.

Third, that man has only a single spiritually cognitive faculty, since actual cognition is not given to the human mind by nature, while that intellect which is the potentiality of spiritual cognition is only a single one for everything intelligible<sup>85)</sup>. This proposition is of significance to us particularly because it preserves us from the widespread error of taking the νοῦς ποιητικός, too, for a spiritual cognitive faculty of man.

Thus we have arrived, not unequipped, at the place where the success or failure of our attempt must be decided. Nevertheless it will be well, where the difficulties are so great and the numerous failed attempts admonish us to caution, not to take up the fifth chapter at once, but first to consider still other doctrines from which we may promise ourselves new help. This holds in particular of what Aristotle teaches about the relation of the possible intellect to the phantasms.

17. According to Aristotle, our spiritual thinking is dependent on sensory presentations in such a way that it arises by means of them and is always and necessarily accompanied by them.

These propositions rest on observation and experience.

The mere fact that in certain states of the bodily part the intellect is incapable both of receiving new thoughts and of actually renewing within itself the knowledge already acquired proves how much it depends, in the one respect and in the other, on the lower man.

85) De Anim. III, 4. §. 3. p. 429, a, 18. cf. our earlier discussion of this and the following §. (n. 2 ff., esp. n. 6.) — Besides in these words, Aristotle’s conviction of the unity of our spiritually cognitive faculty also expressed itself most clearly in his saying that the intelligible is of *one* genus (ἓν δέ τι τὸ νοητὸν εἴδει, §. 10. p. 429, b, 28.); for had he assumed several intellective cognitive faculties, as he distinguished several sensitive ones, he would also have had to assume a plurality of ἴδια νοητά, corresponding to the plurality of ἴδια αἰσθητά. Finally, in this case the possibility of spiritual self-consciousness could not have caused him any difficulty. For if we had a plurality of intellectively cognitive powers, as we have a plurality of sensitive ones, there would have to be a special intellect for the intellect’s own activities, a νοῦς κοινός, just as there is a special sense of sensation, an αἴσθησις κοινή, so that we should be able to compare the objects of different spiritually cognitive faculties with one another and, in judging, to combine or separate them. (see above Part III. n. 6 ff.) Now, however, according to Aristotle we have only *one* spiritually cognitive power, and that is why we saw him at pains to explain the fact, puzzling at first sight, that, since one and the same intellect grasps itself and the concepts of bodily things, the difference between the spiritual and the bodily known does not abolish the unity of the genus of the intelligible.

[p. 145] Everyone knows, for example, that very small children are incapable of any learning<sup>86)</sup>, even those who in riper years reveal the most splendid intellectual gifts. But evidently the spiritual part, which is the subject of the intellect, cannot itself be subject to the growth and development which man undergoes when from a child he becomes a man; what grows and develops there is only the bodily, and so it was through the imperfect condition of the bodily that the intellect too was paralyzed in its activity.

Another phenomenon, as common as it is significant, is this: that fatigue from bodily exertion, sleep, sickness, drunkenness, which are surely all bodily states<sup>87)</sup>, nonetheless often rob the intellect too of every possibility of thinking; and here again its dependence on the bodily shows itself, and indeed also with regard to the renewal of thoughts already grasped earlier<sup>88)</sup>. It also frequently happens that with age memory fades, not merely for particulars that one takes in with the senses, but also for universal and scientific truths, which are knowable to the intellect alone<sup>89)</sup>.

18. More important still is the experience that where a sense is lacking, a science is also wanting, which one cannot possibly acquire<sup>90)</sup>. The man born blind lacks not merely the sensory images of colors; he also has no concept of color; and likewise he who is deaf from birth lacks not merely the sensory presentation of individual tones, but also the knowledge of tone in general. This experience, I say, is still more important, because it shows not merely in an indefinite way that the bodily part of man somehow takes part in the activity of the intellect, but that specifically sensory knowledge is the necessary precondition for the coming into being of the corresponding spiritual thought.

But not merely in acquiring a spiritual knowledge, but also in every new consideration of a truth already known earlier, sensory presentations are indispensable to us. We [p. 146] never think a universal thought with the intellect without a sensory individual image accompanying it. Just as the mathematician who wants to give the proof of the universal proposition that the sum of the angles of a triangle equals two right angles draws a single triangle in the sand and, looking at it, recognizes the universal truth, so too whoever contemplates something else spiritually always has a corresponding presentation in his sensitive faculty. Aristotle has the merit of having been the first to establish this proposition of experience by subtle self-observation<sup>91)</sup>. From it is explained the fatigue that will always set in at last when we dwell very long on the consideration even of truths already known; from it, further, are explained also those disturbances of thinking in consequence of bodily states of which we have just spoken.

86) Phys. VII, 3. p. 247, b, 18.

87) Sleep, according to Aristotle, is a state of the primary sense organ De Somn. et Vigil. 2. p. 455, a, 25. ibid. 3. p. 458, a, 28.

88) Phys. VII, 3. p. 247, b, 13.

89) De Anim. III, 5. §. 2. p. 430, a, 23. see below n. 32. cf. ibid. I, 4. §. 13 f. p. 408, b, 18.

90) Anal. Poster. I, 18. p. 81, a, 38. De Sens. et Sens. 6. p. 445, b, 16. De Anim. III, 8. §. 3. p. 432. a, 8.

19. But in what way are we to conceive this dependence of the intellect on the phantasms? Aristotle answers that the intellect is related to the phantasms as sense is to external sensible things<sup>92)</sup>. Sense receives its images by turning toward external objects; the intellect receives its ideas by, as it were, looking at the phantasms<sup>93)</sup>; and hence, just as seeing and hearing are no longer possible when the object seen or heard vanishes from the field of vision or from the range of hearing, so thinking too is no longer possible as soon as the corresponding phantasms are no longer present in the senses. Sensation is a kind of being affected by the sensible; so thinking is a kind of being affected by the intelligible<sup>94)</sup>, and this intelligible, by which the intellect is affected, is, as Aristotle says, in the sensory presentations<sup>95)</sup>. The sensitive part, in which the phantasms [p. 147] are, thus acts on the intellect, and by this it becomes thinking<sup>96)</sup>.

91) De Memor. et Remin. 1. p. 449, b, 30. ἐπεὶ δὲ περὶ φαντασίας εἴρηται πρότερον ἐν τοῖς περὶ ψυχῆς καὶ νοεῖν οὐκ ἔστιν ἄνευ φαντάσματος· συμβαίνει γὰρ τὸ αὐτὸ πάθος ἐν τῷ νοεῖν ὅπερ καὶ ἐν τῷ διαγράφειν· ἐκεῖ τε γὰρ οὐθὲν προσχρώμενοι τῷ τὸ ποσὸν ὡρισμένον εἶναι τὸ τριγώνου, ὅμως γράφομεν ὡρισμένον κατὰ τὸ ποσόν· καὶ ὁ νοῶν ὡσαύτως, κἂν μὴ ποσὸν νοῇ, τίθεται πρὸ ὀμμάτων ποσόν, νοεῖ δ᾽ οὐχ ᾗ ποσόν. ἂν δ᾽ ἡ φύσις ᾖ τῶν ποσῶν, ἀόριστον δέ, τίθεται μὲν ποσὸν ὡρισμένον, νοεῖ δ᾽ ᾗ ποσὸν μόνον. διὰ τίνα μὲν οὖν αἰτίαν οὐκ ἐνδέχεται νοεῖν οὐδὲν ἄνευ τοῦ συνεχοῦς, οὐδ᾽ ἄνευ χρόνου τὰ μὴ ἐν χρόνῳ ὄντα, ἄλλος λόγος . . . . . . . ἡ δὲ μνήμη καὶ ἡ τῶν νοητῶν οὐκ ἄνευ φαντάσματός ἐστιν. De Anim. III, 5. §. 2. p. 430, a, 25. ibid. 7. §. 3. p. 431, a, 16. ibid. 8. §. 3. p. 432, a, 8. 13.

92) De Anim. III, 7. §. 3. p. 431, a, 14. cf. ibid. II, 5. §. 6. p. 417, b, 19.

93) De Anim. III, 7. §. 5. p. 431, b, 2. τὰ μὲν οὖν εἴδη τὸ νοητικὸν ἐν τοῖς φαντάσμασι νοεῖ.

94) De Anim. III, 4. §. 2. p. 429, a, 13. see above n. 8.

95) De Anim. III, 8. §. 3. p. 432, a, 4. ἐν τοῖς εἴδεσι τοῖς αἰσθητοῖς τὰ νοητά ἐστι, τά τε ἐν ἀφαιρέσει λεγόμενα, καὶ ὅσα τῶν αἰσθητῶν ἕξεις καὶ πάθη.

In the seventh chapter of the third book On the Soul, Aristotle gives the proof that the intellect grasps its concepts in the phantasms in the manner indicated, starting first from the practical intellect, in which this truth comes to light most clearly<sup>96 a)</sup>. First he brings out the similarity between the intellect and the faculty of sensation. Both originally cognize potentially; for even if, absolutely speaking, actual thinking precedes potential thinking<sup>97)</sup>, as actuality in general precedes potentiality, yet in the intellect of the individual man potential thinking is earlier than actual thinking. Likewise both sensing and spiritual thinking are a being affected, but not a being affected in the proper sense, not an alteration in the proper sense, but a motion of another kind<sup>98)</sup>. Further, if the intellect partly grasps concepts and partly, in predicating and denying, combines or separates concepts, we find in the sensitive part something similar to both. When it perceives something, this is similar to the simple grasping of a concept; but when it desires or flees what it has perceived, this presupposes a combination or separation of sensory presentations<sup>99)</sup>. If, for example, a dog greedily rushes toward the piece of meat that it sees, this is a sign that it has combined a pleasant presentation of taste with that of sight.

Now where the similarities are so numerous, we are led of necessity to the thought that this further similarity also exists between the two faculties: that, as sense grasps the sensible form in the object to which it belongs, so the intellect will cognize the intelligible form in that in which it is contained. If, then, as Plato believed, the ideas existed as spiritual beings separate from sensible things, the intellect would grasp them through the action of these spiritual objects<sup>100)</sup>; if, on the other hand, they exist in the sensible, it will cognize them in the sensible world around us or in its copies, the sensory presentations, [p. 148] in that the thoughts are communicated to it through the action of the sensitive part in which the corresponding phantasms are. That this last is in fact the case comes clearly to light, as has been said, especially in the practical intellect. Just as the perception of something that is presented as pleasant or unpleasant is followed by a striving toward or fleeing from that to which the perceived sensible form belonged, so too intellectual knowledge, when it is a matter of practical truths, is followed by a striving toward or fleeing from that in which the concept of the good was found or found wanting<sup>101)</sup>. But here too we strive toward and flee sensible things. Accordingly it is evident that we have also cognized the concepts of the intellect in these sensible things. But we have not cognized them immediately in the things, as all those phenomena prove in which the dependence of the intellect on the operations of the sensitive part reveals itself. Nothing remains, then, but to assume that we cognize them in their copies, the sensory presentations<sup>102)</sup>; and from this is also resolved the doubt that can be raised against the assumption that the practical intellect draws its concepts from the world of sense, namely, that in this case the intellect, like sense, would be able to grasp and take into account only what is spatially and temporally present, whereas it nevertheless also takes into reckoning the hope and danger of the distant future. For this becomes possible for it precisely by its grasping what it cognizes in the sensory presentations, since imagination shows it also what is spatially and temporally distant<sup>103)</sup>.

96) De Anim. II, 5. §. 6. p. 417, b, 19. Metaph. Λ, 7. p. 1072, a, 30. Anal. Poster. II, 19. p. 100, b, 5. These passages explain and supplement one another.

96 a) He uses it similarly Metaph. Γ, 4. p. 1008, b, 26.

97) A proposition which we shall explain later.

98) De Anim. III, 7. §. 1. p. 431, a, 1. — 7. Aristotle only indicates the points of comparison fleetingly, since, as we have seen, he had already pointed out these similarities earlier.

99) Ibid. §. 2. a, 8. τὸ μὲν οὖν αἰσθάνεσθαι ὅμοιον τῷ φάναι μόνον καὶ νοεῖν· ὅταν δὲ ἡδὺ ἢ λυπηρόν, οἷον καταφᾶσα ἢ ἀποφᾶσα, διώκει ἢ φεύγει. Cf. Part III. n. 12.

100) Cf. De Anim. III, 8. §. 3. p. 432, a, 3.

Thus, then, it is with all intellectual knowledge that [p. 149] relates to action<sup>104)</sup>; and with that which is merely theoretical it will therefore not be otherwise either<sup>105)</sup>; for when the theoretical intellect says that something is true or false, and the practical that something is good or evil, we do not have in the one case and the other different genera of intelligible forms; the difference is only this, that theoretical truth holds absolutely, practical truth with reference to someone. What is theoretically true is true for all, but what is good is not good for all<sup>106)</sup>.

101) De Anim. III, 7. §. 3. p. 431, a, 15. ὅταν δὲ ἀγαθὸν ἢ κακὸν φήσῃ ἢ ἀποφήσῃ (cf. ibid. 6. §. 5. p. 430, b, 21.), φεύγει ἢ διώκει. Cf. n. 99.

102) Hence Aristotle continues: διὸ οὐδέ ποτε νοεῖ ἄνευ φαντάσματος ἡ ψυχή. He will presently repeat it more clearly.

103) Ibid. §. 5. p. 431, b, 2. τὰ μὲν οὖν εἴδη τὸ νοητικὸν ἐν τοῖς φαντάσμασι νοεῖ, καὶ ὡς ἐν ἐκείνοις ὥρισται αὐτῷ τὸ διωκτὸν καὶ φευκτόν, καὶ ἐκτὸς τῆς αἰσθήσεως, ὅταν ἐπὶ τῶν φαντασμάτων ᾖ κινεῖται· οἷον αἰσθανόμενος τὸν φρυκτὸν ὅτι πῦρ, τῇ κοινῇ (sc. αἰσθήσει, in the sense it has in De Anim. III, 1. §. 7. p. 425, a, 27. Torstrik reads κινήσει) γνωρίζει, ὁρῶν κινούμενον, ὅτι πολέμιος. ὁτὲ δὲ τοῖς ἐν τῇ ψυχῇ φαντάσμασιν ἢ νοήμασιν ὥσπερ ὁρῶν λογίζεται καὶ βουλεύεται τὰ μέλλοντα πρὸς τὰ παρόντα· καὶ ὅταν εἴπῃ, ὡς ἐκεῖ τὸ ἡδὺ ἢ λυπηρόν, ἐνταῦθα φεύγει ἢ διώκει· καὶ ὅλως ἐν πράξει. Torstrik explains the example of the φρυκτὸς πολέμιος by Thucyd. II, 94. Schol. φρυκτοί εἰσι λαμπάδες τινὲς ἀπὸ ξύλων γιγνόμενοι, ἅστινας βαστάζοντες ἄνωθεν τῶν τειχῶν ἐσήμαινον τοῖς πλησιοχώροις ἢ τοῖς συμμάχοις, ὅτ᾽ ἄν τινας ἑώρων πολεμίους ἐπιόντας, ὡς δεῖ προφυλάξασθαι. οὐ μόνον δὲ ἐπὶ τῶν πολεμίων τοῦτο ἐποίουν, ἀλλὰ καὶ ἐπὶ φίλων· ὅτ᾽ ἂν ἑώρων βοήθειαν αὐτοῖς ἐρχομένην, ἐσήμαινον πάλιν διὰ τῶν φρυκτῶν ὡς οὐ δεῖ θορυβεῖσθαι. καὶ ὅτ᾽ ἂν μὲν φίλους ἐδήλουν, ἐβάσταζον τοὺς φρυκτοὺς ἠρεμοῦντες (perhaps better, as Torstrik would have it, ἠρεμοῦντας·) ὅτ᾽ ἂν δὲ πολεμίους, ἐκίνουν τοὺς φρυκτούς.

Now someone might perhaps grant all this with regard to physical knowledge, but entertain doubts with respect to mathematical knowledge, whether it too can be grasped in the sensory presentations, since the concepts of mathematics are free of sensible matter<sup>107)</sup>. But it would be without justification that this gave him [p. 150] misgivings. In the sensory presentation of the snub-nosed, the concept of being snub-nosed and the concept of curvature are contained. The mathematical concepts do not exist outside the mind in separation from sensible bodies; rather they are in them, as are the physical ones too, and together with these they enter into our sense presentations. The intellect, then, when it grasps them, does not cognize something that is separated from sensible matter, but only cognizes something that is not separated from it in a separated way<sup>108)</sup>. Only if the intellect were to grasp the concept of a supersensible being, if it were to cognize a spiritual substance, could this knowledge not come to it in the phantasms. But this is the case neither with the mathematical concepts nor with any others, if we except self-knowledge and the more universal concepts abstracted from itself, since, at least so long as it is united with the body, it [p. 151] cannot come into immediate contact with other spiritual beings<sup>109)</sup>. Now since it is knowable even to itself only when it has become actually thinking through the action of the sensitive part<sup>110)</sup>, it is evident that this knowledge too, even if it does not draw it from the phantasms, nevertheless falls to its share by means of the phantasms; and so during this life the Aristotelian proposition holds quite universally: “The soul never cognizes without sensory presentations<sup>111)</sup>.”

104) See the end of the passage cited in the preceding note.

105) Thus Aristotle also says, De Sens. et Sens. I p. 437, a, 2., that from sense perceptions ἥ τε τῶν νοητῶν ἐγγίνεται φρόνησις καὶ ἡ τῶν πρακτῶν, by which he designates, though in unusual expressions, theoretical and practical knowledge.

106) Aristotle continues: καὶ τὸ ἄνευ δὲ πράξεως τὸ ἀληθὲς καὶ τὸ ψεῦδος ἐν τῷ αὐτῷ γένει ἐστὶ τῷ ἀγαθῷ καὶ κακῷ· ἀλλὰ τῷ γε ἁπλῶς διαφέρει καὶ τινί. What is medicine for one can be poison for another, and what is duty for one can, if the other did it, be the greatest wrong. — Less probable is another explanation, according to which the words τῷ γε ἁπλῶς διαφέρει καὶ τινί would mean that, whereas theoretical truth is thought for its own sake, practical truth serves an end; although Aristotle does indeed usually place the difference between theoretical and practical truth in this. So in this book 10. §. 2. p. 433, a, 14. νοῦς δὲ ὁ ἕνεκά του λογιζόμενος καὶ ὁ πρακτικός· διαφέρει δὲ τοῦ θεωρητικοῦ τῷ τέλει. Further Metaph. α, 1. p. 993, b, 20. θεωρητικῆς μὲν γὰρ τέλος ἀλήθεια, πρακτικῆς δ᾽ ἔργον· καὶ γὰρ ἐὰν τὸ πῶς ἔχει σκοπῶσιν, οὐ τὸ ἀΐδιον ἀλλὰ πρός τι καὶ νῦν θεωροῦσιν οἱ πρακτικοί. Cf. ibid. Α, 1. p. 981, b, 21. and 2. p. 982, a, 30, b, 27. In the Nicomachean Ethics, to which he refers in Metaph. Α, 1., he says, VI, 4. p. 1140, a, 10., of τέχνη that it is ἕξις μετὰ λόγου ἀληθοῦς ποιητική, and of φρόνησις (b, 5.) that it is ἕξις ἀληθὴς μετὰ λόγου πρακτική περὶ τὰ *ἀνθρώπῳ* ἀγαθὰ καὶ κακά. In the same place he says, 9. p. 1141, b, 33., εἶδος μὲν οὖν τι ἂν εἴη γνώσεως τὸ *αὐτῷ* εἰδέναι κ. τ. λ. These latter passages speak for our interpretation, which in substance does not differ much from the other.

107) Cf. Metaph. Z, 10. p. 1036, a, 9. — The mathematical concepts are free of sensible matter, says Aristotle, but not of intelligible matter; that is, the mathematical concepts do contain something that belongs only to bodies, since magnitude or figure is, after all, a κοινὸν αἰσθητόν, but they contain nothing that is sensible in the most proper sense; they abstract entirely from every ἴδιον αἰσθητόν. In the passage cited from the Metaphysics he says (a, 11.): νοητὴ δὲ (ὕλη ἐστὶν) ἡ ἐν τοῖς αἰσθητοῖς ὑπάρχουσα μὴ ᾗ αἰσθητά, which words confirm the correctness of the reading κατὰ συμβεβηκός De Anim. III, 1. §. 5. p. 425, a, 15. (see above Part III. n. 55.). How Aristotle thought this complete abstraction from every ἴδιον αἰσθητόν possible he indicates at De Anim. III, 1. §. 8. p. 425,

b, 4. We have several senses, and through each we cognize the κοινὰ αἰσθητά together with a different ἴδιον αἰσθητόν. Through the sense of sight we perceive magnitude with color but without the tangible quality; through the sense of touch we perceive it with the tangible quality but without color; and this makes it possible for the intellect to grasp the concept of magnitude free and pure of both: ζητήσειε δ᾽ ἄν τις τίνος ἕνεκα πλείους ἔχομεν αἰσθήσεις, ἀλλ᾽ οὐ μίαν μόνην. ἢ ὅπως ἧττον λανθάνῃ, τὰ ἀκολουθοῦντα καὶ κοινά; οἷον κίνησις καὶ μέγεθος καὶ ἀριθμός· εἰ γὰρ ἦν ἡ ὄψις μόνη, καὶ αὐτὴ λευκοῦ (λευκοῦ stands, as the foremost color, for all colors, just as Aristotle also usually calls the faculty of the sensory affections ὀρεκτικόν and designates its object as the ἡδύ, whereas it is at the same time φευκτικὸν τοῦ λυπηροῦ. cf. De Anim. III, 7. §. 2. p. 431, a, 9. 13.), ἐλάνθανεν ἂν μᾶλλον καὶ ἐδόκει ταὐτὸ εἶναι πάντα (namely χρώματα καὶ μεγέθη) διὰ τὸ ἀκολουθεῖν ἀλλήλοις ἅμα χρῶμα καὶ μέγεθος. (It would be as it now is with the concepts of figure and of magnitude. We cannot think any concept of a magnitude that would abstract entirely from the concept of figure, nor any concept of a figure that would abstract entirely from that of magnitude, because we never perceive a magnitude without figure, or the reverse. That is also why it easily happens that someone who wants to state the specific differences of surfaces divides them into triangle, quadrangle, and the like, whereas these are after all differences of figures, for which reason he ought rather to have divided them into three-foot, four-foot, and the like.) νῦν δ᾽ ἐπεὶ καὶ ἐν ἑτέρῳ αἰσθητῷ τὰ κοινὰ ὑπάρχει, δῆλον ποιεῖ ὅτι ἄλλο τι ἕκαστον αὐτῶν. We see from this that, according to Aristotle, we would presumably also be unable to abstract the concept of substance entirely from that of extension, if our intellect, in grasping itself as thinking, did not grasp an unextended substance. Now, however, it is possible for it, just as it abstracts the concept of magnitude from that of color and of tangible quality, also to make that of substance completely free both of that of extension and of that of thinking. Cf. Metaph. Z, 11. p. 1036, b, 2.

108) De Anim. III, 7. §. 7. p. 431, b, 12. cf. above 4. §. 7. f. p. 429, b, 10. Metaph. Ε, 1. p. 1026, a, 8. and the critique of the Platonic doctrine in the first and in the last two books.

109) De Anim. III, 7. §. 8. p. 431, b, 16. ὅλως δὲ ὁ νοῦς ἐστιν ὁ κατ᾽ ἐνέργειαν τὰ πράγματα νοῶν. ἆρα δ᾽ ἐνδέχεται τῶν κεχωρισμένων τι νοεῖν ὄντα αὐτὸν μὴ κεχωρισμένον μεγέθους, ἢ οὔ, σκεπτέον ὕστερον. The intellect cognizes the things. If, then, all the concepts which the intellect grasps so long as it is united with the body refer to the sensibly corporeal, it will also draw them all from the sensibly corporeal, that is, from the phantasms. And this is in fact the case (self-knowledge excepted); for ideas in Plato’s sense do not exist; the being of flesh is, as we have said, not a substance different from sensible flesh (see above n. 11.); but the purely spiritual substances, which really exist, our intellect, so long as it is united with the body, cognizes in no other way than by drawing the universal concept of a spiritual being from self-knowledge (cf. n. 107.) and then, cognizing in the sensible the effects of a thinking spirit that is not itself, inferring back to the existence of such a spirit. This last is evident, since otherwise (that is, if we grasped the pure spirits immediately, as we do sensible things and ourselves) no one, except a skeptic who doubts even the existence of bodily things, could doubt the existence of God, which after all is not the case. (Cf. De Anim. III, 8. §. 3. p. 432, a, 3—4. ibid. II, 1. §. 3. p. 412, a, 11—12. Metaph. α, 1. p. 993, b, 7—11. Γ, 3. p. 1005, a, 31. Ε, 1. p. 1026, a, 27. Κ, 7. p. 1064, b, 9. Ζ, 2. p. 1028, b, 18. Λ, 1. p. 1069, a, 31. and the way in which Aristotle himself, in the Physics and in the twelfth book of the Metaphysics, proves the existence of God and of other spiritual beings. Hence he also says, Metaph. Θ, 10. p. 1051, b, 32., concerning the knowledge of the pure spirits: τὸ τί ἐστι (Cod. Ab τὸ ἔστι) ζητεῖται περὶ αὐτῶν, εἰ τοιαῦτά ἐστιν ἢ μή.) — The discussions to which Aristotle refers we are unable, like the earlier interpreters, to identify with certainty. Probably they relate to investigations which Aristotle intended to weave into his Metaphysics and which, whether he carried them out or not, have not come into our possession. Let us be content with the fact that there can be no doubt about his opinion. (cf. De Memor. et Remin. 1. p. 450, a, 4. 7. n. 91.) In our passage too it makes itself clearly known through the circumstances and the way in which he asks. For he had, after all, set himself the task of proving the proposition οὐδέποτε νοεῖ ἄνευ φαντάσματος ἡ ψυχή, and he mentions the knowledge of the spiritual substances here only as a last objection that might be raised against it, an objection that would really force him to retract his assertion if that knowledge were possible for us otherwise than in relation to the sensible.

110) See above n. 11. and n. 49. and 51.

111) After the development of the train of thought in the seventh chapter of the

[p. 152] 20. But against this doctrine of Aristotle’s concerning the dependence of our spiritual cognition on the phantasms—a doctrine which, as we have seen, rests on subtle psychological observation and on acute arguments, and which, considered from the teleological standpoint as well, since according to it the senses offer the intellect such great help, gives, concerning the union of the spiritual and the bodily [p. 153] part, a satisfying explanation<sup>112)</sup>—two weighty misgivings arise.

third book as we have given it here, that chapter will, we hope, no longer appear as a mere disconnected heap of various pronouncements, which is what it has passed for with some. We have passed over only §. 3. med. p. 431, a, 17 — §. 4. incl. p. 431, b, 2. This portion falls into two parcels, of which the one (a, 17—20.) was cited by us when we treated of the unity of the sentient subject (see above Part III. n. 35.). Aristotle has just said that the intellect grasps its concepts in the phantasms. But this doctrine, according to the common opinion, which lets the eye see and the ear hear, is opposed by the unity of the intellect. For if the intellect is only *one*, it can also be present only in *one* part of the body, not in several separate ones; and if, therefore, the sense of sight and the sense of hearing and their presentations are found in two separate parts, the intellect, not being present in the one or the other, seems unable to receive any action from it. Now since the intellect grasps both the concepts of colors and those of tones, it seems to draw both in some other way, and not from the corresponding sensory presentations. — It is this objection, then, that Aristotle seeks to meet by at once recalling the unity of the sensitive part. Just as the air, he says, gives a certain quality to the pupil, and the pupil to something else, namely to the organ that properly senses, so too the organ that is first affected by the sound waves (note what we said in Part III. n. 35. about the meaning of ἀκοή) passes the effect on, and indeed to the same organ to which the eye conveyed the presentations of color; for the last terminus, the proper subject of sensation, is a single one for all sensible qualities and differs only in being, that is, the *one* sentient organ has a plurality of sentient faculties. Besides the external senses, the sense of sensation, the αἴσθησις κοινή, is also found in the same subject, and this subject is thereby enabled to perceive also the difference between the objects of different senses. In short, everything that we cognize sensitively is concentrated in *one* organ, and therefore the doctrine that the intellect cognizes its concepts in the sensory presentations is secured from this side against every attack. To this Aristotle attaches (and this is the second part of the passage we passed over (§. 4. a, 20 — b, 2.) by way of episode a renewed discussion of the question how it is possible for the inner sense to distinguish the sweet from the white. We have already discussed this part earlier (Part III. n. 11. and n. 49.) and have also made the necessary remarks concerning the text. If in this part we repeatedly agreed with Torstrik, we cannot do so with regard to the remaining numerous changes that he proposes for the seventh chapter. He assumes, as in other places too, a greater corruption of the text than, thank God, actually exists.

First, according to this doctrine every inference, every defining, in general all free, methodically progressing thinking, appears as something incomprehensible. If the intellect can think only that for which the imagination happens to offer it a presentation, then, bound to it in every movement, it seems to become a plaything of the phantasms; it seems to be borne out, in the worst sense, that according to Aristotle the human mind is only a tabula rasa on which external things write at will. Surely this is a doctrine that contradicts clear consciousness as much as the dignity of the thinking part, since on this view even the sensitive part would be freer in the reception of its objects, inasmuch as the animal can, by its motive power, turn away from one object and toward another, flee the one and bring the other near to its senses.

To this difficulty is joined a second, which seems to demonstrate a no less inadmissible element in the Aristotelian theory. If the intellect received the intelligible forms through the action of the sensitive part in which the phantasms are, then, as in other cases the bodily acts on the bodily, in this case the bodily would act on the spiritual; and since all acting follows upon a striving, this would compel us to assume in the bodily either an unconscious drive or a conscious desire for such an action on the spiritual. But the latter is evidently impossible; for just as little as the sensory part can present anything spiritual, just as little can it desire anything spiritual; and the former too seems unthinkable, for the unconscious natural drives follow bodily qualities, as, for example, the drive toward heat follows heat; but if thoughts, like sensations, were to arise through the action of a sensible quality on the thinking subject, then, as we have already remarked above, this quality would be the necessary fundamental determination of all our concepts<sup>113)</sup>. Just as we see nothing without seeing a color, hear nothing without hearing a sound, and in general perceive nothing by sense without perceiving some sensible quality, so we would also think nothing without our thought containing a certain bodily quality. Now since this is not the case, the bodily seems in general to have no form and no striving (neither a [p. 154] conscious nor an unconscious one) within itself from which a being affected of the spiritual could be explained, and therefore that influence on the genesis of concepts which Aristotle ascribes to the sensitive part with its phantasms seems unable to belong to it either. This, then, is a second and certainly no less far-reaching misgiving; for whereas the first objection reproached the Aristotelian theory with making progressive thinking incomprehensible, this second one seeks to show that under such conditions the genesis of not even a single thought can be explained.

112) Cf. De Anim. III, 12. §. 4. p. 434, b, 3. ibid. I, 3. §. 19. p. 407, b, 3. De Sens. et Sens. 1. p. 437, a, 1.

113) See above n. 6.

21. In each of these two difficulties our attention is drawn to a spiritual power of the soul that has so far remained undiscussed by us; for in the first lies a pointer to that power by which the intellective part consciously intervenes in the sphere of the sensitive, but in the second a pointer to the νοῦς ποιητικός, which is the proper active principle of our thoughts.

Before entering upon the discussion of the latter, we wish first to turn our attention to the consciously moving power of the intellective soul. But since the movement that proceeds from the intellective part stands in the same relation to the will as that which proceeds from the sensitive part stands to the sensory appetitive faculty—since, just as sensory desire becomes the principle of the movement of the body, so actual willing becomes the principle of the movement of the sensitive part—we must speak of the will first.

#### b. Von der geistig begehrenden Kraft. — b. On the Spiritually Appetitive Power.

22. Aristotle assumed, besides the sensory, a higher, spiritual appetitive faculty. He ascribed freedom to it, and indeed freedom not merely in the sense of that unconstrained inclination toward or turning away from the presented object which belongs to the animals too<sup>114)</sup>, but a freedom that excludes all necessity, even inner necessity<sup>115)</sup>. He granted this freedom to it, [p. 155] but not for all its acts, only for the acts of choosing<sup>116)</sup>. Choice and its freedom are made possible, first, by the fact that every concept grasped gives at the same time the knowledge of the thing and of its opposite<sup>117)</sup>, and secondly by the fact that the thoughts of the intellect are universal, so that a comparison of different things which participate in common in the concept of the good can take place from this point of view. The universal concept serves us as the unit with which we measure both<sup>118)</sup>. The first of the two conditions is found in a similar way also in the sensory presentations of animals<sup>119)</sup>; the second, [p. 156] on the other hand, is peculiar to the spiritual cognitive faculty; in it, therefore, we have to recognize the proper ground of the freedom of choice. For it will frequently happen that, when we compare one of the things to be chosen with another, each of them appears in a certain respect as the more desirable. Whichever way, therefore, our choice may decide, in every case we shall choose in a certain respect the better, in a certain respect the worse; and thus *after* the choice, however we may have chosen, our appetite is not wholly satisfied, while *before* the choice, correspondingly, it is not perfectly drawn to the one or the other object, so that the movement of our will does not take place with necessity<sup>120)</sup>.

114) After Aristotle has said, Eth. Nicom. III, 1. p. 1109, b, 35.: δοκεῖ δὲ ἀκούσια εἶναι τὰ βίᾳ ἢ δι᾽ ἄγνοιαν γινόμενα. βίαιον δὲ οὗ ἡ ἀρχὴ ἔξωθεν, τοιαύτη οὖσα ἐν ᾗ μηδὲν συμβάλλεται ὁ πράττων ἢ ὁ πάσχων, .. he declares (3. p. 1111, a, 24.) οὐ καλῶς λέγεται ἀκούσια εἶναι τὰ διὰ θυμὸν ἢ δι᾽ ἐπιθυμίαν. πρῶτον μὲν γὰρ οὐδὲν ἔτι τῶν ἄλλων ζῴων ἑκουσίως πράξει, οὐδ᾽ οἱ παῖδες. But he by no means wishes on that account to grant them freedom in the proper sense (4. p. 1111, b, 6.): ἡ προαίρεσις δὴ ἑκούσιον μὲν φαίνεται, οὐ ταὐτὸν δέ, ἀλλ᾽ ἐπὶ πλέον τὸ ἑκούσιον· τοῦ μὲν γὰρ ἑκουσίου καὶ παῖδες καὶ τἆλλα ζῷα κοινωνεῖ, προαιρέσεως δ᾽ οὐ, καὶ τὰ ἐξαίφνης ἑκούσια μὲν λέγομεν κατὰ προαίρεσιν δ᾽ οὔ.

115) To man, therefore, his actions are to be imputed: Eth. Nicom. III, 7. p. 1113, b, 6. ἐφ᾽ ἡμῖν δὲ καὶ ἡ ἀρετὴ ὁμοίως δὲ καὶ ἡ κακία. ἐν οἷς γὰρ ἐφ᾽ ἡμῖν τὸ

πράττειν καὶ τὸ μὴ πράττειν, καὶ ἐν οἷς τὸ μὴ καὶ τὸ ναί· ὥστ᾽ εἰ τὸ πράττειν καλὸν ὂν ἐφ᾽ ἡμῖν ἐστι, καὶ τὸ μὴ πράττειν ἐφ᾽ ἡμῖν ἔσται αἰσχρὸν ὄν, καὶ εἰ τὸ μὴ πράττειν καλὸν ὂν ἐφ᾽ ἡμῖν, καὶ τὸ πράττειν αἰσχρὸν ὂν ἐφ᾽ ἡμῖν. εἰ δ᾽ ἐφ᾽ ἡμῖν τὰ καλὰ πράττειν καὶ τὰ αἰσχρά, ὁμοίως δὲ καὶ τὸ μὴ πράττειν, τοῦτο δ᾽ ἦν τὸ ἀγαθοῖς καὶ κακοῖς εἶναι, ἐφ᾽ ἡμῖν ἄρα τὸ ἐπιεικέσι καὶ φαύλοις εἶναι. ibid. 8. p. 1114, b, 31. τῶν μὲν γὰρ πράξεων ἀπ᾽ ἀρχῆς μέχρι τοῦ τέλους κύριοί ἐσμεν, εἰδότες τὰ καθ᾽ ἕκαστα, τῶν ἕξεων δὲ τῆς ἀρχῆς, καθ᾽ ἕκαστα δὲ ἡ πρόσθεσις οὐ γνώριμος, ὥσπερ ἐπὶ τῶν ἀρρωστιῶν· ἀλλ᾽ ὅτι ἐφ᾽ ἡμῖν ἦν οὕτως ἢ μὴ οὕτω χρήσασθαι, διὰ τοῦτο ἑκούσιοι. Virtue and vice deserve praise and blame, reward and punishment, etc. Eth. Nicom. II, 4. p. 1106, a, 1. ibid. III, 7. p. 1113, b, 21. — 1114, a, 31.

116) The προαίρεσις is distinguished from the βούλησις, which is likewise a spiritual appetite, chiefly by the fact that the latter is directed to the end, the former to the means. Thus in particular the ultimate end, the εὐδαιμονία (for this is always desired for its own sake, never as a means to something else, Eth. Nicom. I, 5. p. 1097, b, 1.), is no object of choice, and hence there can also be no man who would desire to be wretched (Eth. Nicom. III, 7. p. 1113, b, 16. ibid. p. 1114, b, 18.). Eth. Nicom. III, 4. p. 1111, b, 26. ἔτι δ᾽ ἡ μὲν βούλησις τοῦ τέλους ἐστὶ μᾶλλον ἡ δὲ προαίρεσις τῶν πρὸς τὸ τέλος, οἷον ὑγιαίνειν βουλόμεθα, προαιρούμεθα δὲ δι᾽ ὧν ὑγιανοῦμεν, καὶ εὐδαιμονεῖν βουλόμεθα μὲν καὶ φαμέν, προαιρούμεθα δὲ λέγειν οὐχ ἁρμόζει· ὅλως γὰρ ἔοικεν ἡ προαίρεσις περὶ τὰ ἐφ᾽ ἡμῖν εἶναι.

117) Phys. VIII, 1. p. 251, a, 28. Eth. Nicom. V, 1. p. 1129, a, 13. Metaph. Θ, 2. p. 1046, b, 1. — 9. ibid. 5. p. 1048, a, 5. — 15. Hence it is also said, Eth. Nicom. III, 4. p. 1112, a, 15.: ἡ γὰρ προαίρεσις μετὰ λόγου καὶ διανοίας. ὑποσημαίνειν δ᾽ ἔοικε καὶ τοὔνομα ὡς ὂν πρὸ ἑτέρων αἱρετόν.

118) De Anim. III, 11. §. 2. p. 434, a, 5. ἡ μὲν οὖν αἰσθητικὴ φαντασία, ὥσπερ εἴρηται, καὶ ἐν τοῖς ἄλλοις ζώοις ὑπάρχει ἡ δὲ βουλευτικὴ ἐν τοῖς λογιστικοῖς· πότερον γὰρ πράξει τόδε ἢ τόδε, λογισμοῦ ἤδη ἐστὶν ἔργον· καὶ ἀνάγκη ἑνὶ μετρεῖν· τὸ μεῖζον γὰρ διώκει. ὥστε δύναται ἓν ἐκ πλειόνων φαντασμάτων ποιεῖν. V, 10. p. 1135, b, 8. VI, 2. p. 1139, a, 23.

III, 7. §. 2. p. 431, a, 9. οἷον καταφᾶσα ἢ ἀποφᾶσα, also §. 3. a, 16. Hence in animals, too, the ὄρεξις must be added to the received form if motion is to follow. De Anim. III, 10. §. 1 ff. p. 433, a, 9., with which compare Metaph. Θ, 5. p. 1048, a, 5. and above, Part III. n. 19.

23. In these propositions we have summarized Aristotle’s doctrine of the spiritual appetitive faculty according to its main features. For us here the first point is of especial importance, namely that Aristotle, besides the faculty of the sensory affects, also assumed a spiritually appetitive power, a will; for the account that we have given of the Aristotelian doctrine of the parts of the soul receives confirmation thereby, and if we have grasped it correctly, our understanding of the νοῦς ποιητικός will be made easier to a high degree.

The passages in proof that are at our disposal here are numerous and can be divided into several classes.

The first place belongs, naturally, to those in which an appetite and appetitive faculty distinct from the sensory one and belonging to the rational part is expressly ascribed to man. [p. 157] Aristotle often calls this higher appetitive faculty itself λόγος or νοῦς<sup>121)</sup>.

120) We do not find this last remark stated in Aristotle. We believed we had to add it by way of explanation, because otherwise it is not to be seen how, starting from his grounds, one is to arrive at knowledge of the possibility of freedom of the will. If the goods to be chosen were good in only *one* respect, so that the higher good included everything desirable that the lower contains, then, weighed against one another, they would behave like heavier and lighter bodies, and with the same necessity as in the bodily balance, in the balance of the will too the greater weight would tip the scale. Hints of the thought of a partial superiority in each of the objects to be chosen are found De Anim. III, 10. §. 6. p. 433, b, 5. ἐπεὶ δ᾽ ὀρέξεις γίνονται ἐναντίαι ἀλλήλαις, τοῦτο δὲ συμβαίνει ὅταν ὁ λόγος καὶ ἡ ἐπιθυμία ἐναντίαι ὦσι, γίνεται δ᾽ ἐν τοῖς χρόνου αἴσθησιν ἔχουσιν· (ὁ μὲν γὰρ νοῦς διὰ τὸ *μέλλον* ἀνθέλκειν κελεύει, ἡ δ᾽ ἐπιθυμία διὰ τὸ *ἤδη*· φαίνεται γὰρ τὸ ἤδη ἡδὺ καὶ ἁπλῶς ἡδὺ καὶ ἀγαθὸν ἁπλῶς, διὰ τὸ μὴ ὁρᾶν τὸ μέλλον·) κ. τ. λ. and still more Eth. Nicom. VII, 5. p. 1147, a, 31. ὅταν οὖν ἡ *μὲν καθόλου* ἐνῇ κωλύουσα γεύεσθαι, ἡ δὲ (sc. καθόλου), ὅτι πᾶν τὸ γλυκὺ ἡδύ, τουτὶ δὲ γλυκύ κ. τ. λ. The one is more desirable, for it affords pleasure; the other is more desirable, for it is noble and in accordance with duty. I recognize both; I am drawn to both in a certain way, but to neither perfectly.

In the second place we draw attention to those statements of Aristotle in which he ascribes a pleasure (ἡδονή) to the spiritual part<sup>122)</sup>. For a spiritual pleasure without a spiritual appetitive faculty is unthinkable, since in general nothing that is capable of feeling pleasure can be without an appetitive faculty<sup>123)</sup>; indeed, according to Aristotle, appetite and pleasure are acts of one and the same faculty<sup>124)</sup>. The appetitive faculty rejoices in the possession of the good desired.

Related to these passages are others in which Aristotle speaks of those who live to please what is the most excellent in them, namely their rational part, and do not slavishly serve the lower, sensory part<sup>125)</sup>. These passages, too, prove [p. 158] our assertion; for if something is done to please someone, he must also take pleasure in something. Moreover, Aristotle says in the passages concerned that what is done under the guidance of the rational part is done most of all of one’s own will (ἑκουσίως), because the rational part is the one which chiefly constitutes the man.

121) See De Anim. III, 9. §. 3. p. 432, b, 3. — 7. At ibid. 11. §. 3. p. 434, a, 12. (on which cf. Part III. n. 110.) the rational and the sensory parts of the soul are compared to two celestial spheres, and their appetite to the motions of these. As here, Aristotle also speaks, De Anim. III, 10. §. 6. p. 433, b, 5., of a conflict which, he says, often arises between the higher and the lower appetite, and in this passage he calls the lower ἐπιθυμία, the higher λόγος. At Polit. I, 5. p. 1254, a, 34., a passage which we have likewise adduced as a parallel in Part III. n. 110., the spiritual appetite is designated as νοῦς. Besides, cf. in the Politics III, 4. p. 1277, a, 6. and esp. VII, 15. p. 1334, b, 18. Also at Eth. Nicom. IX, 8. p. 1169, a, 17. (πᾶς γὰρ νοῦς αἱρεῖται τὸ βέλτιστον ἑαυτῷ) he calls the appetite of the higher part νοῦς. At Eth. Nicom. III, 5. p. 1113, a, 5. he says: παύεται γὰρ ἕκαστος ζητῶν πῶς πράξει, ὅταν εἰς αὐτὸν ἀγάγῃ τὴν ἀρχὴν καὶ αὐτοῦ εἰς τὸ ἡγούμενον· τοῦτο γὰρ τὸ προαιρούμενον. But by the ἡγούμενον, again, the νοῦς is to be understood. (cf. Zeller II, 2. p. 460. n. 5.) Cf. further ibid. VI, 2. p. 1139, b, 4. At ibid. I, 1. p. 1094, a, 1. he ascribes an ἐφίεσθαι to what plainly belongs to the spiritual part. Similar statements recur frequently, e.g. ibid. 2. p. 1095, a, 14. and 4. p. 1097, a, 5. etc. Finally, a passage in the ninth book is noteworthy, where Aristotle says that the perfect man desires the same thing with his whole soul, plainly in contrast to those who with the one part of the soul have a longing for one thing, with the other for something else and opposed. Eth. Nicom. IX, 4. p. 1166, a, 12.

122) Metaph. Λ, 7. p. 1072, b, 24. Eth. Nicom. X, 4. p. 1174. b, 20. ibid. b, 34. Cf. further the first and sixth chap. of the Nicomachean Ethics and III, 13. p. 1117, b, 28. VI, 13. p. 1144, a, 3. X, 2. p. 1173, b, 16. and 7. p. 1177, b, 19.

123) This is why Aristotle infers from the presence of sensory pleasure and pain a sensory appetitive faculty, De Anim. II, 2. §. 8. p. 413, b, 23. and III, 11. §. 1. p. 434, a, 2.

124) De Anim. III, 7. §. 2. p. 431, a, 10. Cf. Eth. Nicom. X, 5. p. 1176, a, 11., where the ἡδύ is put together with the φιλητόν, and the λυπηρόν with the μισητόν. So it is also said, ibid. III, 13. p. 1117. b, 29.: ἑκάτερος γὰρ τούτων χαίρει οὗ φιλητικός ἐστιν. At Eth. Nicom. X, 5. p. 1175, b, 34. it is therefore denied that pleasure is a thinking or a perceiving.

125) Eth. Nicom. IX, 4. p. 1166, a, 16. ibid. 8. p. 1168, b, 28. — p. 1169, a, 1.

Finally, we draw a fourth proof from the fact that Aristotle, although he by no means places wickedness (κακία), as Plato does, in a mere error of cognition<sup>126)</sup>, nevertheless regards it as a corruption of the intellective and not of the sensory part<sup>127)</sup>. For him it is precisely a corruption of the spiritual appetitive faculty.

It would certainly be superfluous if we wished to seek out still further passages in proof, since after such clear and manifold statements no one, presumably, will still be in doubt about Aristotle’s doctrine on this point. He certainly assumed a special spiritual appetitive faculty, as indeed he had to assume one, if what we have said about the relation of the intellective to the sensitive part, as well as what we have said about the nature of the appetitive faculties in general, is really his doctrine.

24. For we saw above that not only the form-grasping but also the appetitive powers of the soul are passive faculties. Hence, just as the cognitive powers differ according to the difference of their objects, insofar as these move to cognition, and just as we were therefore compelled to assume a separate cognitive faculty for each peculiar object, so the appetitive faculties too will have to differ according to the difference of their objects, insofar as these move to appetite. Now, however, as Aristotle says, we are moved to appetite by objects not insofar as they are outside us, but insofar as they are presented to us in the thoughts of the intellect or in the phantasms<sup>128)</sup>. Hence, as the intelligible form and the phantasm differ, so too the appetitive faculty moved by the one and that moved by the other will have to differ.

The necessity of assuming such a twofold appetitive faculty becomes still more evident from the fact that, as was shown earlier, the intellect is distinct in subject from the faculties that grasp the sensory forms; the subject of the [p. 159] sense is bodily, but that of the intellect spiritual. Now since the intelligible forms are not in the sentient part, its affects cannot be excited by them either; and if, therefore, as we undoubtedly really do<sup>129)</sup>, we also desire such things as the intellect cognizes, we must also have a second, spiritual appetitive faculty, joined with the intellect.

126) Metaph. Δ, 29. p. 1025, a, 6. Eth. Nicom. III, 4. p. 1112, a, 8. ibid. 7. p. 1113, b, 24.

127) Eth. Nicom. VII, 7. p. 1150, a, 1. ibid. II, 4. p. 1105, b, 31.

128) De Anim. III, 10. §. 6. p. 433, b, 11. Metaph. Λ, 7. p. 1072, a, 27. Eth. Nicom. III, 6. p. 1113, a, 24.

#### c. Von der bewussten Einwirkung des geistigen Theiles auf den sinnlichen. — c. On the Conscious Action of the Spiritual Part upon the Sensory Part.

25. As the faculty of conscious movement of the body attaches itself to sensitive appetite, so to spiritual appetite there attaches itself in the closest manner a faculty of the intellective part by which it consciously acts upon the lower man.

That Aristotle ascribed such a power to the intellective soul is evident from numerous statements. To convince ourselves of it we need only cast a glance at those passages where he speaks of a local motion in which, he says, man is guided by his reason<sup>130)</sup>, or at those where he speaks of the struggle of rational and sensory appetite<sup>131)</sup> and grants to reason and to the rational part the natural dominion over the sensory part, or at those where he says that the intellect moves<sup>132)</sup>, or where he designates the arts and poietic sciences as ἀρχαὶ μεταβλητικαὶ ἐν ἄλλῳ ἢ ἄλλο<sup>133)</sup>, or at those where he divides the irrational part into one which in no [p. 160] way has a share in reason, and one which in a certain sense partakes of it, inasmuch as it obeys it<sup>134)</sup>.

129) What view Aristotle held on this point is shown most clearly by, e.g., De Anim. III, 7. §. 6. p. 431, b, 10. Likewise Eth. Nicom. VI, 2. p. 1139, a, 22. At De Anim. II, 5. §. 4. p. 417, a, 27. Aristotle says that one who knows can think what he knows as soon as he *wills*. We will, therefore, to cognize spiritually. cf. Metaph. Α, 1. princ. Eth. Nicom. I, 5. p. 1097, b, 2. ibid. X, 10. p. 1180, b. 20. The ethical virtues, too, appear beautiful to us, and we desire them; but none of them can be cognized otherwise than with the intellect, for they lie, after all, in the mean determined by reason (Eth. Nicom. II, 6. princ.), and some of them show especially clearly that they are not knowable by sense, as, e.g., truthfulness, which on account of its object quite plainly does not admit of a sensory presentation. (Eth. Nicom. II, 7. p. 1108, a, 19. Metaph. Δ, 29. p. 1025, a, 6.) Finally, we know that according to Aristotle the sensitive part grasps nothing universal; yet he says, Rhet. II, 4. p. 1382, a, 4.: καὶ ἡ μὲν ὀργὴ ἀεὶ περὶ τὰ καθ᾽ ἕκαστα, οἷον Καλλίᾳ ἢ Σωκράτει, τὸ δὲ μῖσος καὶ πρὸς τὰ γένη· τὸν γὰρ κλέπτην μισεῖ καὶ τὸν συκοφάντην ἅπας.

130) De Anim. III, 9 ff. also ibid. I, 3. §. 10. p. 406, b, 24.

131) See above, n. 121., the passages named first.

132) E.g. Metaph. Λ, 6. p. 1071, b, 36.

133) Metaph. Θ, 2. p. 1046, b, 2. cf. Ζ, 7. p. 1032, b, 9.

26. This faculty is distinct both from the intellect and from the will, since it is active, whereas those two are passive. Nonetheless, the capacity of the spiritual part to act upon the bodily is in a certain sense to be called one with the will; for since practical willing itself is the principle of this acting, the spiritual part, precisely insofar as it can desire, also has the disposition for this activity<sup>135)</sup>. In general, there exists between the will and the spiritual power that acts consciously exactly the same relation as between the faculty of the sensory affects and that of the conscious movement of the body. We therefore refer to the discussion given there<sup>136)</sup>.

27. By its action upon the bodily, the spiritual part modifies the vital activities of the latter. Yet this does not hold of all of them; rather, the vegetative functions, as Aristotle says in the first book of the Ethics<sup>137)</sup>, are withdrawn from its dominion. Of the sensitive ones, however, it influences the phantasms as well as the affects and the motive activities. Its influence on the imagination becomes visible especially in the phenomena of memory, for only through it does recollection become possible, since one who recollects proceeds from one thing to another in the manner of one who reasons syllogistically. Hence, too, many animals do share in memory, but to man alone it belongs to recollect<sup>138)</sup>. Its influence on sensory appetite shows itself in the suppression and the excitation of the affects, over which, as we already said above<sup>139)</sup>, the spiritual part, according to Aristotle, loses dominion only through a corruption of nature, so that even the reverse relation sets in and it itself becomes the slave of the passions. Its influence on movement, finally, reveals itself in every rational doing and acting<sup>140)</sup>.

134) As at Eth. Nicom. I, 13. p. 1102, b, 28.

135) De Anim. III, 10. §. 3. p. 433, a, 22. ibid. §. 5. a, 30. §. 6. b, 10. §. 7. b, 17. §. 8. b, 27. Metaph. Θ. 5. p. 1048, a, 5.

136) Part III. n. 19.

137) Eth. Nicom. I, 13. p. 1102, b, 29. τὸ μὲν γὰρ φυτικὸν οὐδαμῶς κοινωνεῖ λόγου.

138) De Memor. et Remin. 2. p. 453, a, 7. τοῦ μὲν μνημονεύειν καὶ τῶν ἄλλων ζώων μετέχει πολλά, τοῦ δ᾽ ἀναμιμνήσκεσθαι οὐδὲν ὡς εἰπεῖν τῶν γνωριζομένων ζώων, πλὴν ἄνθρωπος. αἴτιον δ᾽ ὅτι τὸ ἀναμιμνήσκεσθαί ἐστιν οἷον συλλογισμός τις· ὅτι γὰρ πρότερον εἶδεν ἢ ἤκουσεν ἤ τι τοιοῦτον ἔπαθε, συλλογίζεται ὁ ἀναμιμνησκόμενος, καὶ ἔστιν οἷον ζήτησίς τις. τοῦτο δ᾽ οἷς καὶ τὸ βουλευτικὸν ὑπάρχει, φύσει μόνοις συμβέβηκεν· καὶ γὰρ τὸ βουλεύεσθαι συλλογισμός τίς ἐστιν.

139) Part III. n. 20. —

140) See n. 130. and 133.

[p. 161] 28. But although the intellective part makes its influence felt in all kinds of sensitive activity, it nevertheless does not act upon all of them immediately. Just as the external sensory objects do indeed excite in us not merely sensations but also affects, and have local movements as their consequence, but nevertheless immediately call forth only sensations, since something is desired only once it has been perceived, and movement is directed toward what is desired, so too the intellective part, when it exercises an influence upon the sensitive activities, always acts first upon the phantasms; and through the changes it produces in the imagination the desires are then modified too, and as these become different and different again, the movements that proceed from them also change.

In the case of some movements the mediation through the imagination is evident at first glance, as, e.g., in the movement of speaking, where a presentation of sounds, or in the activities of art, where an artistic image of the imagination is plainly indispensable. But in all other strivings and movements, too, the same must be the case, because of the natural relation in which the form-grasping, appetitive and motive faculties stand to one another. Without a corresponding sensory appetite the principle for the movement would be lacking, and without the presentation of something sensorily desirable such a principle would be wanting for the appetite. Thus every sensory desire and passion regulated by reason, such as those of the courageous, the temperate and the continent man, and every movement guided by reason, points back to the imagination as that which has immediately undergone the action of the spiritual part. Hence, Aristotle says in the tenth chapter of the third book On the Soul, insofar as a living being is able to desire, it is capable of moving itself locally; but it cannot desire without imagination, and imagination is either rational, i.e., formed under the action of reason, or sensitive, i.e., such as is a mere after-effect of sensation, and in the latter, but not in the former, the animals besides man have a share<sup>141)</sup>. It is thus only through the reshaping of the images of the imagination that reason exercises its influence determining the desires and the movements. If, further, Aristotle says in the eleventh chapter of the same [p. 162] book that what moves proximately is not the universal but the particular judgment<sup>142)</sup>, then in these words, too, it is clearly expressed that the spiritual part guides movement by means of the sensory presentation; for it is the business of the senses to cognize the particular, while the intellect, as was already said earlier, cognizes only the universal.

141) De Anim. III, 10. §. 8. p. 433, b, 27. ὅλως μὲν οὖν, ὥσπερ εἴρηται, ᾗ ὀρεκτικὸν τὸ ζῷον, ταύτῃ αὐτοῦ κινητικόν· ὀρεκτικὸν δὲ οὐκ ἄνευ φαντασίας φαντασία δὲ πᾶσα ἢ λογιστικὴ ἢ αἰσθητική· ταύτης μὲν οὖν καὶ τὰ ἄλλα ζῷα μετέχει. cf. De Mot. Animal. 8. p. 702, a, 17., a book which, as in no other point, so in this one in particular, does not depart from the doctrine of Aristotle as it is contained in his undoubtedly genuine writings.

29. Thus we see, then, that that power of the spirit which acts consciously upon the bodily always produces in the first place a reshaping of the sensory presentations. But of what extraordinary importance it is, is easily recognized. No artistic activity, no rational conduct, no intercourse of minds would be possible without it, and by this alone, of course, the intellectual development of each individual would also be hindered. But directly, too, because of the dependence in which our thinking stands on the phantasms, it becomes for our cognitive activity one of the most important factors, without which the most ordinary phenomena of our thinking cannot be explained. It is this side that is of chief interest to us here; for it is a matter both of rebutting the reproaches that are unjustly raised against the Aristotelian theory of knowledge and of determining exactly the influence of all the individual factors that come into account in the formation of our thoughts, so that the operation of the νοῦς ποιητικός may stand out clearly for us.

We said above that we are unable to cognize any concept with the intellect unless at the same time a corresponding individual presentation exists in our senses. As the eye no longer sees when the external object has been taken away, so the spiritual eye too, the intellect, no longer sees when the phantasms of the sensitive part have vanished. From this a twofold difficulty arose<sup>143)</sup>, and the one consisted in this, that all ordered thinking, every systematic arrangement of a train of thoughts, every methodical investigation of a question, every proof, even the slightest, and likewise, on the other side, the deliberate holding fast to and contemplative absorption in one and the same thought, threatens to become an impossibility; the thoughts of the intellect, too, seem to be irresistibly swept along by the change of the phantasms. This difficulty is resolved by the demonstration of a conscious action of the spiritual [p. 163] part upon the presentations of the imagination. However much the intellective part may cognize only under the action of sensory images, it nevertheless remains the ruling part. Far from becoming a will-less plaything of the phantasms, it is rather these that must obey its commands and let themselves be reshaped and ordered in accordance with its will, and they become the means that serve its end.

142) De Anim. III, 11. §. 4. p. 434, a, 16. ἐπεὶ δ᾽ ἡ μὲν καθόλου ὑπόληψις καὶ λόγος, ἡ δὲ τοῦ καθ᾽ ἕκαστα, (ἡ μὲν γὰρ λέγει ὅτι δεῖ τὸν τοιοῦτον τὸ τοιόνδε πράττειν, ἡ δὲ ὅτι τόδε τὸ νῦν τοιόνδε, κἀγὼ δὲ τοιόσδε,) ἤδη αὕτη κινεῖ ἡ δόξα, οὐχ ἡ καθόλου. ἢ ἄμφω, ἀλλ᾽ ἡ μὲν ἠρεμοῦσα μᾶλλον, ἡ δ᾽ οὐ.

143) See above n. 20.

#### d. Vom νοῦς ποιητικός. — d. On the νοῦς ποιητικός.

30. But yet another objection has been raised against the Aristotelian theory of knowledge<sup>144)</sup>, and this one is not refuted, as the first was, by the dominion of our will over the phantasms. The sensitive part, it was said, being something bodily, has no principle within itself by which it would be capable of acting upon something spiritual; but if it cannot act upon the spiritual, then neither can the intellect receive the concepts through its influence, however much these may be contained in the sensory presentations. With this the whole doctrine of Aristotle on the origin of our thoughts would be destroyed at the root.

How shall we answer this objection?
