We would begin anew that senseless chatter which had already been carried on all too long by those who believed they beheld the principle of the world in water, or in air, or in the dust of the atoms, until Anaxagoras spoke, as it were, the first sober word in saying that an intellect was the principle that had formed everything<sup>234)</sup>. For we would now indeed say that an intellect is the principle, but an intellect that would be as good as no intellect, since it would think nothing of that whose order it is supposed to explain<sup>234 a)</sup>. This thought, then, of an ignorance on the part of the first principle, Aristotle rejects as something ridiculous and irrational.

222) Metaph. Λ, 4. p. 1070, b, 30. ἐπεὶ δὲ τὸ κινοῦν ἐν μὲν τοῖς φυσικοῖς ἀνθρώπῳ (as Zeller, probably rightly, recommends reading instead of ἀνθρώποις) ἄνθρωπος, ἐν δὲ τοῖς ἀπὸ διανοίας τὸ εἶδος ἢ τὸ ἐναντίον, τρόπον τινὰ τρία αἴτια ἂν εἴη, ὡδὶ δὲ τέτταρα. ὑγίεια γάρ πως ἡ ἰατρική, καὶ οἰκίας εἶδος ἡ οἰκοδομική καὶ ἄνθρωπος ἄνθρωπον γεννᾷ· ἔτι παρὰ ταῦτα ὡς τὸ πρῶτον πάντων κινοῦν πάντα. For those who believed that Aristotle denies that God knows anything outside himself, this passage was naturally, in its last words, an insoluble riddle. Bonitz wanted to read τὸ ὡς πρῶτον instead of ὡς τὸ πρῶτον. But the text, as will emerge from our explanation, is in no way corrupt.

230) Eth. Nicom. X, 9. p. 1179, a, 22. ὁ δὲ κατὰ νοῦν ἐνεργῶν καὶ τοῦτον θεραπεύων καὶ διακείμενος ἄριστα καὶ θεοφιλέστατος ἔοικεν εἶναι· εἰ γάρ τις ἐπιμέλεια τῶν ἀνθρωπίνων ὑπὸ θεῶν γίνεται, ὥσπερ δοκεῖ, καὶ εἴη ἂν εὔλογον χαίρειν τε αὐτοὺς τῷ ἀρίστῳ καὶ τῷ συγγενεστάτῳ (τοῦτο δ᾽ ἂν εἴη ὁ νοῦς) καὶ τοὺς ἀγαπῶντας μάλιστα τοῦτο καὶ τιμῶντας ἀντευποιεῖν ὡς τῶν φίλων αὐτοῖς ἐπιμελουμένους καὶ ὀρθῶς τε καὶ καλῶς πράττοντας. κ. τ. λ. Hence he argues in De Divinat. p. Somn. 1. p. 462, b, 20. and 2. p. 463, b, 15. against the assumption that God sends dreams, since otherwise they would fall to the lot of the best and most rational. On God’s providence cf. also Oecon. 3. p. 1343, b, 26. and De Coel. II, 9. p. 291, a, 24. One should note also passages such as Top. IV, 5. p. 126, a, 34, which quite obviously presupposes a knowledge in the deity of what is to be effected.

But it appears to him just as degrading for the deity that it should have an object which would be lower than itself. Even for us it is better not to think some things than to think them<sup>235)</sup>, not as though not every piece of knowledge, even the slightest, were valuable and contained something divine<sup>236)</sup>, but rather only because, for us who think through concepts and therefore never have more than *one* thought in us at the same time<sup>237)</sup>, it hinders, if it is something lowly, our thinking of what is higher<sup>238)</sup>, and so in a certain measure draws us down, since that which thinks is in a certain way one with what is thought. What sort of happiness would that be if we, supposing we thought as changelessly as the deity<sup>239)</sup>, remained stuck with our thought on a stone, or on a plant, or on an animal? Our highest happiness we find in the moments when we raise ourselves to the thought of the deity<sup>240)</sup>. But we [p. 193] cannot dwell on it long<sup>240 a)</sup>, nor do we grasp him so perfectly<sup>241)</sup> that we comprehend in him, the cause of everything, all effects; and so our thinking rises and sinks on the ladder of being. All this is not possible for the deity, for it is unchangeable. If, then, something other than itself were its object, it would be forever drawn down and fettered in lowliness. But as it is, it has itself for its object<sup>242)</sup>—not in the way in which we have something for our object, we who are passively moved by the object; no, without becoming, without motion of any kind whatever<sup>243)</sup>, it rests<sup>244)</sup> in its own knowledge, being a pure knowing<sup>245)</sup>, and by virtue of this perfect manner of knowing it comprehends itself, the principle of all being, so perfectly that it sees all being, every effect, in the cause<sup>246)</sup>.

231) Metaph. Λ, 10. p. 1075, a, 13.

246) A third parallel passage, which, so as not to hold up the progress of the treatise here too long, we discuss in this note, is offered us by the tenth chapter of the same book. Here Aristotle gives a concise but apt critique of the earlier philosophical views concerning the good and the order of the universe. He comes (p. 1075, b, 8.) to Anaxagoras. The latter “assumed that the good is a principle in the manner of that which moves, for the intellect moves. But it moves for the sake of an end, and thus something else seems to be the principle. Yet the matter stands as we have set it forth, since the medical art is in a certain way health.” (Ἀναξαγόρας δὲ ὡς κινοῦν τὸ ἀγαθὸν ἀρχήν, ὁ γὰρ νοῦς κινεῖ· ἀλλὰ κινεῖ ἕνεκά τινος, ὥστε ἕτερον. πλὴν ὡς ἡμεῖς λέγομεν· ἡ γὰρ ἰατρική ἐστί πως ἡ ὑγίεια.) Aristotle here reproaches Anaxagoras with this: that although he rightly assumed that the intellect is the first moving principle, he nevertheless in no way explained how this is possible, since a great difficulty after all stands in the way of his assumption. For every intellect acts for the sake of an end (cf. Metaph. α, 2. p. 994, b, 15.), and the end seems not to lie in the intellect itself, as, e.g., good battle order is outside the general and health outside the physician. Hence the intellect seems to be active in dependence on another principle, and the doctrine of Anaxagoras seems to come to nothing. Toward the solution of this difficulty Anaxagoras himself did nothing at all. We, on the other hand, says Aristotle, are able to remove the objection, for we have indeed established that the art of healing is in a certain way health. So, then, the divine thinking too is in a certain way the order of the world, for the sake of which each single one of things exists (cf. the beginning of the chap.), and therefore we have to seek the ultimate principle of that order in it alone. (Let it be remarked in passing that the ἔστι γὰρ διττὸν (as must be read instead of τινί, see Schwegler and Bonitz)

[p. 194] Here we have touched upon the most sublime doctrine to which the mind of Aristotle was able to soar, but which would also have shown him to all centuries as the greatest of thinkers, had not later times, instead of bringing the scattered doctrines into unity, treated what was separate as separate and (since taken by itself it is in fact ambiguous) for that very reason misunderstood it. When one then came upon the contradictory passages, one either rejected them outright, or mutilated them, or declared them accommodations to the notions of common opinion, or contradictions which, although a child can grasp them with its hands, were not noticeable to the intellect of Aristotle. One will never do justice to this philosopher so long as one persists in such prejudices. Machiavelli’s maxim, divide et impera, may have its validity in politics; in the investigation of a philosophical system, and especially of one so perfect as that of the great Stagirite, the opposite one is in any case in place. It must be the passages taken together that help to explain to us what was unintelligible taken by itself. Indeed, if one wanted to rule in the history of philosophy in *that* sense—that is, to do as one pleases—that one makes anything out of any philosopher, just as it happens to suit one, then such a division of the inquiry would certainly be the most advisable means. But in our time at least there is probably no longer any inquirer of significance who would aim at this, and the manner of constructing history a priori according to a ready-made pattern is a [p. 195] standpoint that is to be called generally superseded. One *wants* historical truth, but one nevertheless often fails to attain it, because one fancies oneself all too far above antiquity, and therefore—since unfortunately even in our time one does not quite know how to guard against contradictions—when one has to examine an ancient philosopher, a Plato and Aristotle, one expects from the outset to find nothing other than a heap of contradictions and childish follies.

τὸ οὗ ἕνεκα Metaph. Λ, 7. p. 1072, b, 2. refers not to the distinction of the οὗ ἕνεκα οὗ and the οὗ ἕνεκα ᾧ, but to that of the οὗ ἕνεκα in the agent and in what is to be effected, of which Metaph. Λ, 10. princ. speaks.)

We see that the thought here is at bottom none other than the one we found in Metaph. Λ, 4. fin., and the two passages serve to explain each other. There Aristotle said that the art of healing is in a certain way health, and that the first moving principle is in a certain way, but not quite in the same way (παρὰ ταῦτα), everything. Here he disregards this finer distinction. Because in the intellect of the physician, as well as in the first moving intellect, that which is to be brought about is contained as something thought, he merely recalls, for the solution of the difficulty, the example of the art of healing, of which we have seen that it is in a certain way health.

The second reproach that Aristotle then makes against Anaxagoras (ἄτοπον δὲ καὶ τὸ ἐναντίον μὴ ποιῆσαι τῷ ἀγαθῷ καὶ τῷ νῷ.) likewise seems, to add this briefly, to be only a reproach of incompleteness with regard to the grounding and the removal of the objections. At least the comparison with De Anim. I, 2. §. 22. p. 405, b, 19., cf. Phys. VIII, 5. p. 256, b, 24., would seem to speak for this. Hence too the perplexity of the interpreters, who cannot comprehend why Aristotle censures in Anaxagoras what he himself maintains. See Bonitz on the passage.

We seem to have strayed somewhat from our path, but this was no useless digression; for the all-ignorance of the Aristotelian God, which cannot be reconciled with our explanation, has become for many something like a standing dogma, ever since the authority of some significant inquirers, rightly held in high esteem, declared itself for it<sup>247)</sup>. It therefore seemed all the more necessary to support our contrary view with reasons, as we may by no means claim the same weight for our words. Of anyone who is willing to consider more closely and without prejudice the passages to which we have here only pointed in passing, we believe we may be sure that he, like ourselves, will recognize not an all-ignorance but an omniscience of God as corresponding to the Aristotelian doctrine.

But before we proceed further in the explanation of our chapter, we must still discuss another doctrine of Aristotle, which, if we do not gain a clear notion of it, will necessarily cast its shadows over our interpretation of this passage too. We mean the doctrine of the origin of the spiritual part of our soul. Whence does it come to us? Does it have a coming into being, or has it none? But if it has a coming into being, when and where and how and through whom did it come to be<sup>248)</sup>? All these questions we shall briefly answer for ourselves in the sense of Aristotle.

Had the spiritual part of man had being and life before the body, its past would surely have left some traces in it. But we find in it no such demonstrable trace. Plato believed he had found it when, noticing the difference of our spiritual concepts from sensible objects, he held it to be impossible that from the corporeal [p. 196] the spiritual, and from the individual the universal, should derive—which then led him further to his theory of recollection and thereby to the preexistence of the soul. But this doctrine in no way explained what it sought to explain<sup>249)</sup>; it itself everywhere offended against the most ordinary facts of experience<sup>250)</sup>, and showed itself to be a grandiose error, just as it had also grown up on the soil of an erroneous assumption. No knowledge, says Aristotle, is innate in us; indeed, not even the habitus of the principles is ours from the beginning; everything we must first acquire through the mediation of sense and experience<sup>251)</sup>. This alone, then, certainly makes it improbable in a high degree that the human spirit preexisted the body.

247) Yet the opposite view too has its representatives. We refer here only to Brandis, who is certainly inferior to no one in knowledge of the Aristotelian doctrine.

248) Aristotle raises these questions timidly, and, conscious of their difficulty, he asks that no more than the possible be demanded. De Generat. Animal. II, 3. p. 736, b, 5. διὸ καὶ περὶ νοῦ, πότε καὶ πῶς μεταλαμβάνει καὶ πόθεν τὰ μετέχοντα ταύτης τῆς ἀρχῆς, ἔχει τ᾽ ἀπορίαν πλείστην, καὶ δεῖ προθυμεῖσθαι κατὰ δύναμιν λαβεῖν καὶ καθ᾽ ὅσον ἐνδέχεται.

But with this another and still far weightier reason is joined. The spiritual part of man, as we have seen, forms with its bodily part one single substance. The intellective and the vegetative-sensitive soul are not two souls; no, they are one single form, which in one part animates the body, but in another part is free from it and spiritual<sup>252)</sup>. As the spiritual and the bodily are here most intimately linked, so we also see the spiritual and the bodily activities entangled with one another in a wonderful way; they are mutually dependent on one another and, the ones serving, the others ruling, they receive help and furtherance from each other on both sides<sup>253)</sup>. Not only does the gullet serve the stomach; imagination also serves the intellect<sup>254)</sup>; not only does the eye light the foot on its ways; the spirit also lights all the bodily powers, and leads them to food and clothing, and holds them back from what brings danger and ruin. Now as a man, when a foot or another limb is torn from him, is no longer a complete substance, so he is naturally still much less a complete substance when the whole bodily part has fallen prey to death. The spiritual part does indeed continue to exist, but those err very greatly who, like Plato, believe that separation from the body is for it a furtherance and, as it were, a liberation from an oppressive prison<sup>255)</sup>; for the soul must now, after all, forgo all the numerous [p. 197] services which the powers of the body have rendered it<sup>256)</sup>.

249) See above n. 60. ff.

250) Anal. Poster. I, 18. p. 81, a, 38. Metaph. A, 9. p. 992, b, 33. p. 993, a, 7.

251) Anal. Poster. II, 19. p. 99, b, 26.

252) See Part I. n. 7.

253) Polit. I, 4. p. 1254, b, 6. see above Part III. n. 110. cf. De Anim. III, 12. §. 4. p. 434, b, 3.

254) Their services are of such great importance that, considering this relation, one might almost come to doubt the possibility of a continued existence of the intellective soul after death. cf. De Anim. I, 1. §. 9. p. 403, a, 8.

255) De Anim. I, 3. §. 19. p. 407, b, 2.

As, therefore, it is not in the order of nature that men are born mutilated and that only afterwards foot and hand complete the crippled body into the perfect shape of a man, so too it cannot be in the order of nature that man, existing first according to his spiritual part, then, so to speak, completes himself out of this fragment into the perfect spiritual-bodily being. Rather, as the hand that has been cut off does not heal on again<sup>257)</sup>, so too the soul, once it exists separately, will not be united with the body again; there is no resurrection of the dead according to the natural course of things<sup>258)</sup>; but a separate existence of the spiritual part before the body and a union with it only following thereupon would obviously have about it all the unnaturalness that the resurrection of the dead would have about it. Thus the same holds of the human soul as holds of all forms of purely corporeal substances: it exists neither wholly nor in part before its body, even though after the death of the latter a part of it, which, because it was not the form of the body, did not end in its dissolution, now by itself alone, as something purely spiritual<sup>259)</sup>, continues to exist<sup>260)</sup>.

The body of man has come into being; the soul did not exist before the body; therefore the soul too has come into being<sup>261)</sup>. But *how* has it come into being? — Has it perhaps developed out of matter? Has the father, in begetting, formed a spiritual-bodily fetus? — But that would surely be a double absurdity, for the immaterial would then have matter, and the bodily, of which we have just denied that it can by its own power produce the concepts in the spirit, would now be so powerful in the seed of the father that it could form the spiritual substance itself. However the spiritual part of the soul may come into being, out of matter [p. 198] and through the vegetative power it does not come into being<sup>262)</sup>. But if it does not come into being out of a matter, then it evidently comes into being out of nothing<sup>263)</sup>, for there is no other substrate of coming to be and passing away than matter itself. Does it then perhaps—since, just as it cannot be formed out of corporeal matter, so too it cannot be formed by the vegetative power—come into being, as a further consequence, also without any efficient cause at all? Surely that is impossible; the law of synonymy would here be transgressed in so extravagant a way that something actual would come into being without any actuality going before. But what else would this be than to want, with those ancient theologians, to explain the being of things out of Night? Aristotle demands something actual, and that it act<sup>264)</sup>. Moreover, he declares to us expressly that, as the union of form and matter in general, so also that of soul and body has its ground in the efficient principle<sup>265)</sup>.

256) De Anim. I, 3. §. 23. p. 407, b, 25.

257) Cf. Metaph. Δ, 27. p. 1024, a, 27.

258) De Anim. I, 3. §. 6. p. 406, b, 3.

259) De Anim. III, 5. §. 2. p. 430, a, 22. A passage which we shall presently consider more closely.

260) Metaph. Λ, 3. p. 1070, a, 21. τὰ μὲν οὖν κινοῦντα αἴτια ὡς προγεγενημένα ὄντα, τὰ δ᾽ ὡς ὁ λόγος ἅμα. ὅτε γὰρ ὑγιαίνει ὁ ἄνθρωπος, τότε καὶ ἡ ὑγίειά ἐστιν, καὶ τὸ σχῆμα τῆς χαλκῆς σφαίρας ἅμα καὶ ἡ χαλκῆ σφαῖρα. εἰ δὲ καὶ ὕστερόν τι ὑπομένει, σκεπτέον· ἐπ᾽ ἐνίων γὰρ οὐθὲν κωλύει, οἷον εἰ ἡ ψυχὴ τοιοῦτον, μὴ πᾶσα ἀλλ᾽ ὁ νοῦς· πᾶσαν γὰρ ἀδύνατον ἴσως. Cf. De Anim. II, 1. §. 12. p. 413, a, 6.

261) Cf. also Eth. Nicom. VIII, 14. p. 1162, a, 6., where it is said of parents and the gods that they are for us αἴτιοι τοῦ εἶναι. This could not be said if the spiritual part, which most of all constitutes our self (Eth. Nicom. IX, 8. p. 1168, b, 35.), had not come into being.

The coming into being of the human soul and its union with the body thus has an efficient principle; but in this case that principle will not be able to be a unitary one; for we say, and surely not without reason, that one man begets another<sup>266)</sup>, while on the other hand it has turned out that the generative power of man is not capable of producing the spiritual part of another man, but that for this, rather, a power is required that can effect something out of nothing, i.e., without the prior existence of a matter. Now that this can only be that being which contains the fullness of all being, that principle on which, as Aristotle says, heaven and earth depend<sup>267)</sup>, and from which, as he gives us to understand still more clearly in other passages, the superhuman pure spirits and the heavenly spheres<sup>268)</sup> too, though not in time<sup>269)</sup>, have proceeded<sup>270)</sup>—that, I say, must [p. 199] surely be evident to everyone of itself. No other being is capable of this, certainly, but this being is capable of it; for, as Aristotle, applauding Agathon, assures us in a passage of the Nicomachean Ethics, it has in its omnipotence no other limit than that it cannot make what has happened not to have happened<sup>271)</sup>. From the deity, then, the intellective part of man must enter into the fetus<sup>272)</sup>, and through this the latter’s development into an actual human body will at the same time reach its completion. For since the human soul cannot be without the intellective part, while the human body is what it is only through the human soul, it is only in the same moment in which the spiritual part was united by the deity with the body into one substance that the human body has become a human body and an actual new man has come into being<sup>273)</sup>.

262) De Generat. Animal. II, 3. p. 736, b, 15—28.

263) Phys. VIII, 6. p. 258, b, 18.

264) Metaph. Λ, 6. p. 1071, b, 29. ibid. b, 12.

265) Metaph. Λ, 10. p. 1075, b, 34. ἔτι τίνι οἱ ἀριθμοὶ ἓν ἢ ἡ ψυχὴ καὶ τὸ σῶμα καὶ ὅλως τὸ εἶδος καὶ τὸ πρᾶγμα, οὐθὲν λέγει οὐθείς· οὐδ᾽ ἐνδέχεται εἰπεῖν, ἐὰν μὴ ὡς ἡμεῖς εἴπῃ, ὡς τὸ κινοῦν ποιεῖ. cf. ibid. H, 6. p. 1045, a, 31. — fin.

266) Metaph. Λ, 4. p. 1070, b, 34.

267) Metaph. Λ, 7. p. 1072, b, 13. ἐκ τοιαύτης ἄρα ἀρχῆς ἤρτηται ὁ οὐρανὸς καὶ ἡ φύσις.

268) Which according to Aristotle likewise have no substantial matter. cf. e.g. Metaph. Λ, 2. p. 1069, b, 24. and elsewhere.

269) Aristotle teaches, both in the Metaphysics and in the physical writings, with all definiteness the eternity of motion, and with it, as a matter of course, at the same time the beginningless existence of the moved spheres and of the moving spirits. Yet one would err if one believed that according to Aristotle they, because eternal, had proceeded from God by a kind of emanation; rather

So, then, by an immediate act of God the spiritual part is effected out of nothing, and at the same time the bodily part is given its determinateness [p. 200] as a human body. What, after this, still remains for the activity of the begetting father? It cannot reach further than to give the impulse to the development that gradually leads to that disposition of matter which is suited for the reception of the human soul.

he brings them forth through an acting in which the agent undergoes no change of any kind; for he is absolutely simple and unchangeable and without any potentiality (Metaph. Λ, 7. p. 1070, a, 25.), and touches, so to speak, his effects without being touched by them (De Generat. et Corrupt. I, 6. p. 323, a, 31.); he acts with conscious freedom (Top. IV, 5. p. 126, a, 34.) Aristotle also says expressly that local motion (φορά) is the first motion and prior to any generation whatever, i.e., to any development of a substance out of another substance (γένεσις). So, e.g., Phys. VIII, 7. p. 260, b, 24. f.

270) See the Appendix.

271) Eth. Nicom. VI. 2. p. 1139, b, 8.

272) De Generat. Animal. II, 3. p. 736, b, 27. λείπεται δὲ τὸν νοῦν μόνον θύραθεν ἐπεισιέναι καὶ θεῖον εἶναι μόνον· οὐθὲν γὰρ αὐτοῦ τῇ ἐνεργείᾳ κοινωνεῖ σωματικὴ ἐνέργεια. (cf. *Trendelenburg*, De Anim. Comm. p. 175. and 496. Eth. Nicom. VIII, 14. p. 1162, a, 4—7. and Polit. I, 12. p. 1259, b, 12. are also to be compared here.) For the coming into being of animals too, says Aristotle, a nobler and in a certain measure more divine principle is needed than for that of lifeless beings, and the nature which he calls πνεῦμα (cf. De Mot. Animal. 10.) is analogous to the element of the stars (b, 29. 37.). He does not mean by this that a heavenly element must here be mixed into the earthly substances, for the heavens are, after all, incorruptible in his view, whereas this is dissolved (p. 737, a, 11.); rather, he means only this: that the germ of life forms under the influence of the sun (cf. De Generat. et Corrupt. II, 10. p. 336, b, 17. and elsewhere) or of animal heat, but not through the action of a heat of a lower kind, and the like (p. 737, a, 1.). What he here calls θειότερον, but nevertheless does not equate with the θεῖον proper, might perhaps not be far removed from what he calls δαιμόνιον in De Divinat. p. 463, b, 14. Although corporeal, it can nevertheless be found only in living beings, and can come about only through the influences of organic or of other higher, e.g. heavenly, bodies. The former is evident with all clarity from the content of the passage.

273) Hence Theophrastus, in Themistius De Anim. 91., says that our νοῦς is οὐχ ὡς ἐπίθετον ἀλλ᾽ ἐν τῇ πρώτῃ γενέσει συμπεριλαμβανόμενον.

There is nothing striking in this if one compares it with the way in which the irrational animals and the plants produce one another; for these too, after all, do not in the very moment of generation give existence to a new living<sup>274)</sup> being of the same kind. Plant and animal are organisms, but the fetus is at first not yet an organism<sup>275)</sup>, and the fetus of the animal, even when it already allows a plurality of members to be distinguished and participates in vegetative functions of life, is not yet an animal; for it still lacks the distinguishing mark of the animal, namely the organ and power of sensation; and if the fetus of the horse already has sensation, it is even then still no horse, so long, namely, as it lacks the specific difference of the horse. Only in the moment in which the development arrives at the point where the fetus is disposed similarly to the bodies of other horses does it actually become of like kind with horses; it becomes a horse<sup>276)</sup> and is now animated by a soul of this animal species<sup>277)</sup>.

So it is, then, with the coming into being of man as well. The human fetus is at first lifeless and then leads first a plant life, then an animal life, and finally a human life. For the proper end of each species consists in what is peculiar to it<sup>278)</sup>, and what is prior according to nature and end is later according to time and coming into being<sup>279)</sup>; hence each species receives last [p. 201] those powers and those particular properties which distinguish it from all others<sup>280)</sup>; and so man too receives last the specifically human powers, namely the intellective ones<sup>281)</sup>, in whose activity [p. 202] his proper end is attained<sup>282)</sup>. Last of all, then, after the fetus has already come to share in vegetative and sensitive life, it attains the disposition at which, through the accession of the spiritual part, the production of a unitary, spiritual-bodily substance becomes possible. In this moment the human fetus, similarly to the animal fetus, only in a different way, namely with that particular cooperation of the deity, becomes of like kind with men, and the soul that animates it, but is not wholly sunk in it, is now a human soul.

274) Cf. Metaph. M, 2. p. 1077, a, 20.

275) De Generat. Animal. II, 1. p. 732, a, 27.

276) De Generat. Animal. II, 3. p. 736, a, 35. ὅτι μὲν οὖν τὴν θρεπτικὴν ἔχουσι ψυχήν (τὰ σπέρματα καὶ τὰ κυήματα τῶν ζῴων), φανερόν (δι᾽ ὅτι δὲ ταύτην πρῶτον ἀναγκαῖόν ἐστι λαβεῖν, ἐκ τῶν περὶ ψυχῆς διωρισμένων ἐν ἄλλοις φανερόν!)· προϊόντα δὲ καὶ τὴν αἰσθητικήν, καθ᾽ ἣν ζῷον. οὐ γὰρ ἅμα γίνεται ζῷον καὶ ἄνθρωπος οὐδὲ ζῷον καὶ ἵππος, ὁμοίως δὲ καὶ ἐπὶ τῶν ἄλλων ζῴων. That the vegetative soul, too, is not there from the beginning is said immediately afterward (b, 8.): τὴν μὲν οὖν θρεπτικὴν ψυχὴν τὰ σπέρματα καὶ τὰ κυήματα τὰ χωριστὰ (as, e.g., the eggs of birds) δῆλον ὅτι δυνάμει μὲν ἔχοντα θετέον, ἐνεργείᾳ δ᾽ οὐκ ἔχοντα, πρὶν ἢ καθάπερ τὰ χωριζόμενα τῶν κυημάτων ἕλκει τὴν τροφὴν καὶ ποιεῖ τὸ τῆς τοιαύτης ψυχῆς ἔργον. (cf., on the gradations of the animals that bear live young, that lay eggs, etc., ibid. II, 1. p. 733, a, 32.)

277) De Anim. II, 2. §. 15. p. 414, a, 25.

278) Cf. Eth. Nicom. I, 13. p. 1102, a, 13., together with b, 2.

279) Metaph. M, 2. p. 1077, a, 19. 26. ibid. 8. p. 1084, b, 10. De Generat. Animal. II, 6. p. 742, a, 20.

280) De Generat. Animal. II, 3. p. 736, b, 3. ὕστερον γὰρ γίνεται τὸ τέλος, τὸ δ᾽ ἴδιόν ἐστι τὸ ἑκάστου τῆς γενέσεως τέλος.

281) De Generat. Animal. II, 3. p. 736, b, 12. πρῶτον μὲν γὰρ ἅπαντ᾽ ἔοικε ζῆν τὰ τοιαῦτα φυτοῦ βίον, ἑπομένως δὲ δῆλον, ὅτι καὶ περὶ τῆς αἰσθητικῆς λεκτέον ψυχῆς καὶ περὶ τῆς νοητικῆς. πάσας γὰρ ἀναγκαῖον δυνάμει πρότερον ἔχειν ἢ ἐνεργείᾳ. It is evident from this passage in the clearest way that Aristotle is so far from thinking of the spiritual part of the soul as enclosed in the seed that he rather has it come in last, after the vegetative and sensitive powers are already present. The same is proved by the passages in the second and third chapters of the second book On the Soul, where Aristotle, as we have seen, speaks of the necessity that wherever the higher parts of the soul are found, the lower are found too; and to these he expressly refers here. (p. 736, a, 37.). That the intellective power, although in the pure spirits it exists without the vegetative powers, presupposes them in mortal beings, he declares in clear words (De Anim. II, 2. §. 4. p. 413, a, 31., together with §. 9. b, 24. Cf. esp. also 3. §. 6. p. 414, b, 28.), so that one cannot say that he meant to speak only of the sensitive part. A further proof that according to Aristotle the νοῦς is not yet in the seed is given by a later passage of our chapter (p. 737, a, 16.), where, quite generally, without distinguishing between vegetative, sensitive and intellective soul, he states as the result of his discussion: περὶ μὲν οὖν ψυχῆς, πῶς ἔχει τὰ κυήματα καὶ ἡ γονὴ καὶ πῶς οὐκ ἔχει, διώρισται· δυνάμει μὲν γὰρ ἔχει, ἐνεργείᾳ δ᾽ οὐκ ἔχει.

With this, however, a passage that almost immediately precedes the one just cited stands in the starkest contrast; for in it Aristotle seems to teach nothing other than that the seed (for the predicate incorporeal obviously cannot be referred to τῆς γονῆς σῶμα) is in part free from body—namely in those living beings that have within them the divine, the so-called νοῦς—but in part not free from body. This seed, it says, dissolves and passes away, being of a moist and watery nature (p. 737, a, 7.). τὸ δὲ τῆς γονῆς σῶμα, ἐν ᾧ συναπέρχεται τὸ σπέρμα τὸ τῆς ψυχικῆς ἀρχῆς, τὸ μὲν χωριστὸν ὂν σώματος, ὅσοις ἐμπεριλαμβάνεται τὸ θεῖον (τοιοῦτος δ᾽ ἐστὶν ὁ καλούμενος νοῦς), τὸ δ᾽ ἀχώριστον, τοῦτο τὸ σπέρμα τῆς γονῆς διαλύεται καὶ πνευματοῦται, φύσιν ἔχον ὑγρὰν καὶ ὑδατώδη. But this passage is quite certainly corrupt. For, apart from its contradiction with the doctrine of Aristotle stated so often, and even in this chapter, it is obviously absurd; for it says in clear words that the seed of some living beings, namely of men, is immaterial, but that of the rest material. If it were to have a reasonably acceptable sense, it would rather have to say: the seed of man is partly immaterial, partly material (because of the νοῦς already substantially united with it), but that of the other living beings something wholly material. As the words now read, the seed of man would be pure spirit. But if, in order to recognize the absurdity of the passage still more fully, we assume for a moment that this really was Aristotle’s opinion, although the contrary is plain as day, it would further follow that according to Aristotle something spiritual could be moist and watery and could dissolve and pass away, for Aristotle immediately attributes all these properties to the seed. — The passage is therefore corrupt. But it

So much, in brief, on the Aristotelian doctrine of the coming into being of the human soul and of its spiritual part. Let us now see whether it really affords us, for his theory of knowledge, that light which we hoped to receive from it.

We weighed above the misgiving which, with a doctrine such as that of Aristotle—that our thoughts arise through the joint working [p. 203] of two factors, imagination and the active intellect, irresistibly forces itself upon us. In neither of the two, we said, is there any other likeness to what is to be produced than such as also exists, in chance coming-to-be, between the agent and what it effects. We then came to the insight that, unless there were a higher principle of our thoughts which already actually contained them in itself, and by which the active intellect and the imagination had been coordinated in a manner corresponding to this effect, the conditions required for resolving this difficulty would in fact be lacking in the Aristotelian doctrine. We have also seen that this higher principle must be the efficient cause not merely of the union of the active intellect with the imagination, but in general of the union of the spiritual part of man with the bodily part, since the active intellect is, after all, no more separable from the intellective soul than the sensitive power is from its bodily subject. Accordingly, everything came down to the question: is there, according to Aristotle, a being which actually contains all our thoughts in itself, and has this being brought our intellective soul into that union with the body in which it actually finds itself?

is not corrupted in such a way that the words of Aristotle would have been altered; rather, to restore the correct text, one has nothing else to do than to remove the note on τὸ μὲν χωριστὸν ὂν σώματος made by an unfortunate commentator and then taken up into the text, namely: ὅσοις ἐμπεριλαμβάνεται τὸ θεῖον, τοιοῦτος δ᾽ ἐστὶν ὁ καλούμενος νοῦς—and everything is in order. The error of that commentator consisted in his thinking that χωριστὸς ὂν σώματος must here too, as in the books On the Soul, designate something spiritual; and since there had been talk just before of a θεῖον, namely of the νοῦς, he referred it to this. But the σῶμα of which there is talk here is not the body of the fetus but the body of the female parent, and χωριστὸν σώματος therefore means the same thing that χωριστόν by itself meant at p. 736, b, 9. (see n. 276.), namely the egg’s having been discharged from the mother’s body. Aristotle means to say that the egg (for this is τὸ τῆς γονῆς σῶμα), in which the seed detached from the begetter has been received, is in some living beings separated from the body of the female parent, in others not separated. In this egg, now, one looks in vain for the seed, although it has not escaped from it again either; rather, through dissolution and transformation, it has become so mixed and one with the whole material that it no longer forms any particular part. The passage is therefore, with the elimination of that misunderstanding remark, to be read thus: τὸ δὲ τῆς γονῆς σῶμα, ἐν ᾧ συναπέρχεται τὸ σπέρμα τὸ τῆς ψυχικῆς ἀρχῆς, τὸ μὲν χωριστὸν ὂν σώματος, τὸ δ᾽ ἀχώριστον· ... τοῦτο τὸ σπέρμα τῆς γονῆς διαλύεται καὶ πνευματοῦται, φύσιν ἔχον ὑγρὰν καὶ ὑδατώδη. διόπερ (Aristotle continues) οὐ δεῖ ζητεῖν ἀεὶ θύραζε αὐτὸ ἐξιέναι, οὐδὲ μόριον οὐθὲν εἶναι τῆς συστάσης μορφῆς, ὥσπερ οὐδὲ τὸν ὀπὸν τὸν τὸ γάλα συνιστάντα· καὶ γὰρ οὗτος μεταβάλλει καὶ μόριον οὐθέν ἐστι τῶν συνισταμένων ὄγκων. (By the dots after ἀχώριστον we meant to indicate the anacoluthon which, as is frequently the case elsewhere in the Aristotelian writings too, is evidently found in this passage; for the subject of the sentence becomes τὸ σπέρμα τῆς γονῆς, and this is identical not with τὸ τῆς γονῆς σῶμα but with τὸ τῆς ψυχικῆς ἀρχῆς σπέρμα.)

282) Eth. Nicom. X, 7. p. 1178, a, 6. ibid. I, 6. p. 1097, b, 22., the whole chapter.

Now if this question could, in its first part, be answered first of all with Yes, since that intellect which, according to Aristotle, is the thinking of its thinking, beholds eternally in this one and eternal object at the same time the whole multiplicity of things, so that in it human thoughts too are prefigured, we now see that the second part of the question is also certainly to be answered in the affirmative, since the Deity has united the intellective part of our soul with the bodily man.

Indeed, Aristotle ascribes to his God not merely the *union* of the two; he also, as we have seen, has him give both of them their *existence*, teaching that the spiritual part, at that moment at which the fetus in its natural development reaches the final disposition for the reception of a human soul, is produced immaterially by the Deity in such a way that it becomes a part of the same substance of which the bodily man forms another constituent. According to him, then, God appears not merely like the builder of a mill, who joins spoke to spoke and puts together all its wheelwork and conducts the driving water to it; rather, he is orderer and creator at once. Were he only the former, our thinking would indeed no longer appear as the work of a lucky chance, but neither would it appear as a work of nature, but only as an artificial product. Man would not take up his spiritual food from the external world of sense as the plant draws its nourishment from the soil; rather, [p. 204] his intellect would resemble the canvas and would receive the intelligible image from the active intellect and the phantasm as *it* receives the painting from the color and the brush, which the ordering hand of the painter makes work together in accordance with his idea. For nature brings forth the plant and all the roots and fibers of the plant; it does not merely use and arrange—no, it also generates its instruments; art alone we see confining itself to bringing substances which it finds at hand near to one another and into that position in which they become its means for the work of art<sup>283)</sup>.

Thus, then, by looking up to the creative spirit, that great objection is in fact removed in the most perfect manner, and thinking now appears as what it is, as something natural in man, indeed as that by which his natural destiny is most of all attained. We can therefore, now that the small but, as we have seen, so exceedingly important addition has compelled us to dwell on it for a long time, proceed with the explanation of our chapter without having to fear the reproach that we have given the passage an interpretation which does not fit into the context.

χωρισθεὶς δ᾽ ἐστὶ μόνον τοῦθ᾽ ὅπερ ἐστί, καὶ τοῦτο μόνον ἀθάνατον καὶ ἀΐδιον. οὐ μνημονεύομεν δέ, ὅτι τοῦτο μὲν ἀπαθές, ὁ δὲ παθητικὸς νοῦς φθαρτός, καὶ ἄνευ τούτου οὐθὲν νοεῖ. “But when the intellect has been separated from the body, it is only that which it is [by itself alone]; and only this part [of the soul] is immortal and imperishable. But that we lose memory comes from this, that *it* is indeed impassible, while the passible faculty of thought is corruptible, and it can think nothing without the latter.”

Here there is much that claims our attention. Above all, the question arises what is to be thought of as the subject of the first sentence. Knowledge (ἐπιστήμη), of which there was talk immediately before, cannot be it, for a grammatical reason. Does Aristotle, then, perhaps turn back to the νοῦς ποιητικός, of which he had spoken before? This too is not conceivable, for several reasons. First, we have seen that the active [p. 205] intellect is a power of the soul, hence an accidens, which cannot subsist without its subject. Next, the fourth chapter has taught us that the receptive intellect, too, is spiritual and therefore immortal. Finally, we have found that the Aristotelian νοῦς ποιητικός is not a thinking faculty but a faculty that makes thinking; here, however, as the concluding words show, something that thinks is spoken of, and it would therefore still be more possible that the receptive intellect alone was being spoken of than that the active intellect alone was. But this no one has yet maintained. Rather, the intellect must be thought of as subject in that sense of the word in which it signifies the intellective part of the soul, and to this, to the μόριον νοητικόν of the soul, we must accordingly also refer the τοῦτο and everything that follows. That Aristotle very frequently attached this sense to the word νοῦς (for this, without doubt, is to be supplied as the subject) is proved by passages such as De Anim. II, 2. §. 9. p. 413, b, 24. ibid. I, 4. §. 13. p. 408, b, 18., along with many others, which anyone willing to go through the passages cited in n. 21. as evidence for the spirituality of one of the parts of our soul will find with little effort. But that he must have attached this sense to it here in particular as well follows, apart from the reasons just given, from a comparison with those passages which evidently speak of the intellective soul in parallel with ours, whether with the whole of it or with a part of it. Thus Aristotle says, e.g., in the first chapter of the first book On the Soul, that most affections of the soul are common to the soul and the body, such as anger, courage, desire, and in general all the sensitive ones, and that thinking still has the most in its favor for being held to be something proper to the soul alone. Then he continues: “Now if anything of what the *soul* does or undergoes is proper to it alone, then *it* might well be able to be separated from the body<sup>284)</sup>.” Evidently these words aim at nothing other than what is also expressed at the beginning of our passage, but with all definiteness. For in the meantime it has been established by the investigations of this and the preceding chapter that there is indeed both an active and a passive power of our soul in which our body has no share, and so Aristotle could now draw with certainty the conclusion that the soul must really be immortal. But it is not the whole soul, but only that part which is the subject of our spiritual powers, that he means to declare immortal here too. Therefore he says that after the separation the intellect is only that which it is (μόνον τοῦθ᾽ ὅπερ ἐστί), evidently in contrast [p. 206] to what it had been before, when it was not a soul by itself but a part of the intellective-sensitive soul, and a part of the spiritual-bodily man. A very clear parallel to the first of the sentences now before us for consideration is also offered by the third chapter of the twelfth book of the Metaphysics, and here too the νοῦς that outlasts death appears as a part of the soul<sup>285)</sup>. Let it suffice to have pointed this out.

283) That God is the cause of what he effects in a still far more perfect way than the natural begetter is evident. Likewise the law of synonymy, too, is preserved in his working in the most perfect way, more perfectly even than in natural production; for no likeness can be as perfect as that which exists between the work thought by God and the work produced by him. If, therefore, Aristotle often groups together what is produced by God and what is produced by nature, he nevertheless, where he speaks more precisely, as in the passage cited from the fourth chap. of the twelfth book of the Metaphysics and considered above (see n. 222.), distinguishes the two quite well from each other.

284) De Anim. I, 1. §. 10. p. 403, a, 10. εἰ μὲν οὖν ἐστί τι τῶν τῆς ψυχῆς ἔργων ἢ παθημάτων ἴδιον, ἐνδέχοιτ᾽ ἂν αὐτὴν χωρίζεσθαι.

The second thing that draws our attention is the word χωρισθείς, which, had we not already earlier refuted on other grounds those who take the νοῦς ποιητικός to be a separate substance, e.g., the intellect of the Deity, would amply offer us the means of doing so. For had that χωριστός which was earlier attributed to the νοῦς ποιητικός designated a substantial separation from the bodily man, how could Aristotle now continue: “But after it has been separated”? for it is χωρισθείς, not κεχωρισμένος or χωριστός, that we read in this passage. For us, on the other hand, it is easy to recognize the two in their harmony. The intellect was χωριστός, insofar as it was spiritual, even during its union with the body, and hence there is no contradiction when Aristotle, of the intellect which he has just called χωριστός, says immediately afterward that it is separated—namely, when death destroys the bodily man.

This finds further support in what follows, since Aristotle says that the intellect, when it has been separated, is only that which it is. *We* could easily explain these words; but for one who takes what is separated here to be the divine intellect, they have no sense at all. For what is it supposed to mean, that after the separation the divine intellect is that which it is? Had it perhaps become something other through its union with man—it, which is free from all change<sup>286)</sup>? Had it become a part of the human substance, and is it now pure God again? Who would wish to answer any one of these questions in the affirmative, so as to assert something that would be absolutely unthinkable and contrary to the whole theology of Aristotle? No; to one who wishes to regard the spiritual in man as a separate substance, almost every sentence of this chapter holds up the proofs of his error.

In the third place we must ask what the expression νοῦς παθητικός, and, before that, what the οὐ μνημονεύομεν is meant to signify. The older commentators for the most part referred the latter to the life to be expected after death; Trendelenburg, on the other hand, thinks he must refer it to the present life. We agree with him<sup>287)</sup>, [p. 207] even if we cannot follow his explanation in everything. The sentence seems, namely, to be nothing other than a parallel to a passage in the fourth chapter of the first book. In both places Aristotle has, in what precedes, declared the spirituality and the immortality of the intellective part; and since against this that objection especially suggests itself which points to the decline of memory so usual in old age, even with respect to the objects of knowledge, as to a phenomenon which is said to show that the mind too grows weak and ages along with the body, he hastens to refute it, and does so in both places in quite the same way, and indeed in a way that corresponds perfectly to what we have heard about the important services which the sensory part is supposed to render us in spiritual thinking. Thus he says in the fourth chapter of the first book<sup>288)</sup>: “Spiritual thinking and contemplating fade because something else within us perishes, for it itself is impassible. Sensory<sup>288a)</sup> thinking (διανοεῖσθαι), on the other hand, and passionate desiring and fleeing are not states of the soul alone, but of the ensouled body as such. From this, then, it is explained why man, as the body dies away, loses memory and no longer has the former vehemence [p. 208] of desire; for these had belonged not to the soul, but to what consists of soul and body, which has fallen prey to perishing; but the intellect is perhaps something more divine, and imperishable.” The intellect of which Aristotle speaks here is evidently the receptive intellect, for what is at issue is, after all, the knowledge acquired by it and the possibility of its loss. Hence, if one looks at this passage, one will hardly run the risk of identifying the activity of the receptive intellect with what is called διανοεῖσθαι. It is otherwise in our passage, if one does not take into account what was said earlier. For here the expression νοῦς stands to designate the same faculty of thought whose act had been called διανοεῖσθαι in the other passage; and the added attribute παθητικός is indeed, in and of itself, certainly sufficient to show clearly that Aristotle is now speaking of something quite other than all that which he had earlier in this chapter called νοῦς (for he had, after all, said in the fourth chapter that the *receptive* intellect is ἀπαθής, had inferred from this in the fifth chapter that the *active* intellect too is ἀπαθής, and had then declared the intellect in the sense of the *intellective part* itself to be imperishable); but the passage of the fourth chapter does already lie somewhat further back, and so it could happen that interpreters who did not think back to it were misled precisely by the expression παθητικός into opposing this νοῦς to that νοῦς which had been designated as the ποιητικόν of our spiritual thoughts. In this way, then, several of the most acute exegetes were led so far from the right path that they declared the receptive intellect itself to be something sensory and corruptible; and naturally, for one in whom this prejudice was once fixed, it was no longer possible to find his way in the Aristotelian doctrine. Aristotle’s whole theory of knowledge, which appeared to us so lucid and simple, was now a tangle of inextricable confusion. Such great harm has this small word caused.

285) Metaph. Λ, 3. p. 1070, a, 24. see above n. 260.

286) Metaph. Λ, 9. p. 1074, b, 26.

287) If, with those older exegetes, one wished to refer the οὐ μνημονεύομεν to the state

after death, one would consistently have to do the same with regard to the οὐθὲν νοεῖ. But just as it would already sound strange in and of itself that after the death of the body we, though living on, should no longer think, so this would in particular be scarcely compatible with Aristotle’s assumption that there is no resurrection. What purpose would the soul still serve in such eternal inactivity? If activity (Metaph. Θ, 8. p. 1050, a, 9.), and in particular thinking, is its end, it would evidently be without purpose. — But what and how the soul cognizes after death, Aristotle has nowhere discussed more closely; only, in the question, De Anim. III, 7. fin. (see n. 109.), whether our intellect is able to cognize a purely spiritual substance while it is not itself separated from the corporeal, there might lie a hint that, once the separation has taken place, such a cognition can in any case fall to its share. But a definite pronouncement, as has been said, we seek in vain. He did not wish to speak in myths, nor to conceal the lack of knowledge with bold assertions. He contents himself with having opened to us, through the proof of immortality, the hope of another life, which in any case will be a life of spiritual activity. Cf. also De Anim. I, 1. §. 9. f. p. 403, a, 5.

288) De Anim. I, 4. §. 14. p. 408, b, 24. καὶ τὸ νοεῖν δὴ καὶ τὸ θεωρεῖν μαραίνεται ἄλλου τινὸς ἔσω φθειρομένου, αὐτὸ δὲ ἀπαθές ἐστιν. τὸ δὲ διανοεῖσθαι καὶ φιλεῖν ἢ μισεῖν οὐκ ἔστιν ἐκείνου πάθη, ἀλλὰ τουδὶ τοῦ ἔχοντος ἐκεῖνο, ᾗ ἐκεῖνο ἔχει. διὸ καὶ τούτου φθειρομένου οὔτε μνημονεύει οὔτε φιλεῖ· οὐ γὰρ ἐκείνου ἦν ἀλλὰ τοῦ κοινοῦ, ὃ ἀπόλωλεν· ὁ δὲ νοῦς ἴσως θειότερόν τι καὶ ἀπαθές ἐστιν.

288a) That by διανοεῖσθαι is meant not a mediating thinking of the universal, but the sensory thinking which is found in the imagination, is shown especially clearly by the μνημονεύει in the following sentence, which refers to it. Cf. below n. 291.

What, then, according to our contention, is the νοῦς παθητικός? It is the imagination, which, as a sensory faculty, has no share, as the fourth chapter teaches<sup>289)</sup>, in the ἀπάθεια of the receptive intellect; which is why the first book of the Politics, too, opposes the sensory part, as παθητικὸν μόριον, to the intellective<sup>290)</sup>. That it is really the imagination that is meant is shown clearly above all by the book On Memory and Recollection; for here μνημονεύειν is ascribed to the imagination<sup>291)</sup>, and it is taught that even the intelligible [p. 209] we could not remember without a phantasm<sup>292)</sup>. But that the imagination, although it belongs to the sensitive part, is called νοῦς has nothing about it that would be surprising. In the Nicomachean Ethics Aristotle on one occasion calls sensation (αἴσθησις) itself νοῦς<sup>293)</sup>. The imagination, however, he often counts as part of νοεῖν, as, e.g., in the third chapter of the third book On the Soul<sup>294)</sup>, and calls it νοῦς and a kind of νόησις, as, e.g., in the tenth chapter of the same book. “But it seems,” he says in the relevant place, “that one of these two is what moves, either appetite or thinking (νοῦς), if, that is, one includes the imagination under this name as a kind of thinking (ὡς νόησίν τινα)<sup>295)</sup>.”

289) De Anim. III, 4. §. 5. p. 429, a, 29.

290) Polit. I, 5. p. 1254, b, 8.

291) De Memor. et Remin. 1. p. 450, a, 22. τίνος μὲν οὖν τῶν τῆς ψυχῆς ἐστὶν ἡ μνήμη, φανερόν, ὅτι οὗπερ καὶ ἡ φαντασία· καὶ ἔστι μνημονευτὰ καθ᾽ αὑτὰ μὲν ὅσα ἐστὶ φανταστά, κατὰ συμβεβηκὸς δὲ ὅσα μὴ ἄνευ φαντασίας.

Now that this has become clear to us, however, the passage and its purpose are easily understood. Aristotle is not concerned to prove that we lose memory even for spiritual cognitions, as those thought who assumed that he was speaking here of a life after death (and a number of other interpreters, too, have imputed this intention to him); his aim is rather this: to remove the objection which could be raised against the immortality and imperishability of the intellective part, just asserted, from the universally known fact that memory often suffers, and particularly with the declining strength of the body<sup>296)</sup>. This, then, he does by pointing to the dependence of thinking on sensory memory and on the imagination; and this dependence is no other doctrine than the one we have already heard him repeat so often, because it is in fact a cornerstone of his theory of knowledge. The eighth chapter, in which the presentation of this theory finds its conclusion, ends with the same thought, and almost with the same words, as this fifth one. Aristotle treats there of the difference between imagination and spiritual cognition; and after this difference has emerged first for judging thought, which joins subject and predicate, he continues with a question. “The *first* thoughts, however<sup>297)</sup>,” he asks, “will not at least [p. 210] these differ in nothing from the phantasms? — But no, the other thoughts too are not themselves phantasms, yet they are not without phantasms<sup>298)</sup>.” Why not without phantasms? We already know the reason from earlier considerations: because, namely, it is the sensitive part through whose influence the thoughts of our intellect become actual.

294) De Anim. III, 3. §. 5. p. 427, b, 27. περὶ δὲ τοῦ νοεῖν, ἐπεὶ ἕτερον τοῦ αἰσθάνεσθαι, τούτου δὲ τὸ μὲν φαντασία δοκεῖ εἶναι τὸ δὲ ὑπόληψις, περὶ φαντασίας διορίσαντας οὕτω περὶ θατέρου λεκτέον.

296) This interpretation is also supported by the words of Theophrastus which we read in Themistius at the often-cited passage. For, after having said that the intellect is incorruptible (ἄφθαρτος), he raises the objection: διὰ τί λήθη καὶ ἀπάτη καὶ ψεῦδος;

297) He means the simple truths, which are not a combination of subject

Thus the fifth chapter, which has named to us the active intellect as the moving principle of the receptive intellect, concludes, without fearing a contradiction, with that seemingly opposed doctrine of the influence of the phantasms on our thinking. The two doctrines are in truth simply not contradictory to each other; rather, rightly understood, they complement each other in such a way that the active intellect without phantasms, like a bow without an arrow, and the phantasms without the active intellect, like an arrow without the propelling force of the bow, could not possibly reach the target and would thus be incapable of producing thoughts.

Let us now, in conclusion of this lengthy discussion, survey the whole chapter once more, in order to let its train of thought come clearly before our eyes. It falls into two parts. The first<sup>299)</sup> develops the doctrine of the active intellect: first the necessity of assuming an active principle for our thoughts is demonstrated<sup>300)</sup>, and then it is determined what this principle is, its properties, such as spirituality, actuality, simplicity, etc., being established one after the other<sup>301)</sup>. If we consider them in their totality, they let us, as we have seen, really recognize the active intellect as that power, acting unconsciously on the phantasms, the need for which in the Aristotelian theory of knowledge had already become clear to us earlier. The second part<sup>302)</sup> contains two additions which are very valuable and which, each in its own way, serve to let the doctrine of the active intellect appear in a brighter light and to furnish the means for its defense. The first<sup>303)</sup> shows its harmony with the metaphysics; by pointing us to the eternal thinking of the Creator, it removes the reproach that according to this theory our thoughts would arise in a way that [p. 211] resembles chance rather than natural coming-to-be. The second<sup>304)</sup> comes to terms with experience; it meets the reproach that the doctrine of the spirituality and immortality of our intellective soul and of its active and receptive intellect runs counter to the observation of a mental power that ages. If the receptive principle of thoughts is impassible, how does it lose the habitus of knowledge? But if this habitus persists unimpaired, how does it come about that the principle no longer renews thoughts within itself with the same ease as before, since the active principle, after all, is also supposed to persist unweakened? — The answer follows from the mediating position of the phantasms. — Thus everything is intelligible and in harmony both with itself and with the requirements of the system.

and predicate, and for which the difference that separates the composite judgment from the phantasm cannot be asserted, the τί ἐστι κατὰ τὸ τί ἦν εἶναι. Cf. De Anim. III, 6. §. 7. p. 430, b, 28.

33. It has been said that Aristotle does not remain faithful elsewhere to his doctrine of the active intellect as he gives it in the books On the Soul, and in particular Renan has asserted that the last chapter of the Posterior Analytics stands in evident and complete opposition to the theory of knowledge developed here<sup>305)</sup>. He was right from the standpoint of his interpretation; but this interpretation was, as we have seen, simply a clouding of the true sense. Far from standing in any contradiction whatever with the theory of the active intellect, that doctrine which conceives all thinking only through the mediation of the senses<sup>306)</sup>, and which designates thinking as an undergoing and the intellect as the mere possibility of receiving thoughts<sup>307)</sup>, rather itself bears witness for that theory and becomes a means to its understanding. All these propositions, and still other, more specific determinations as well, such as, e.g., that habitual spiritual knowledge too must first be acquired<sup>308)</sup>, we found expressed, as here, in the books On the Soul as well, clearly and with all definiteness.

It is true that sometimes an expression sounded contradictory, as, e.g., when it was said that our thoughts arise through the working of an intellect which is an originally given habitus (ἕξις) and higher (τιμιώτερον) than the receptive one<sup>309)</sup>, whereas in the Analytics it is taught that the highest axioms, from which, as principles, demonstrations are derived, arise not from [p. 212] a higher (τιμιωτέρα) habitus (ἕξις), not from an originally existing spiritual knowledge, but from something lower, from the knowledge of the senses<sup>310)</sup>. But for one who knows that that intellect is not something that thinks, and that its working is directed in the first place to the sensitive part—all of which we have, as we hope, proven with the utmost clarity—, the apparent dissonance resolves itself into the pure harmony of most intimately related doctrines.

304) a, 22—25.

305) See Section I. n. 19.

306) Anal. Poster. II, 19. p. 99, b, 32. ibid. p. 100, b, 5. ἡ αἴσθησις τὸ καθόλου ἐμποιεῖ.

307) Ibid. p. 100, a, 13. ἡ δὲ ψυχὴ ὑπάρχει τοιαύτη οὖσα οἵα δύνασθαι πάσχειν τοῦτο.

308) Ibid. p. 99, b, 26. p. 100, a, 10. cf. De Anim. III, 4. §. 6. p. 429, b, 5. ibid. II, 5. §. 4 ff. p. 417, a, 21.

309) De Anim. III, 5. §. 1. p. 430, a, 15. 17. §. 2, a, 18. see the beginning of n. 32.

There is only one point which might need a more detailed discussion. The last chapter of the Analytics, namely, evidently lays great weight on memory as a preliminary stage and precondition of spiritual cognition<sup>311)</sup>, and so the question rightly arises whether this agrees with the account in the books On the Soul, which, after all, both in general neglect memory almost entirely, in a striking manner<sup>312)</sup>, and in particular seem not to call on its help in the origination of our thoughts. Nonetheless we must answer this question too in the affirmative. Above all, namely, it emerges quite clearly from the doctrine in the books On the Soul how important the services of the imagination (and memory, after all, belongs to it) become for us in the exercise of the intellect. For since according to this doctrine every concept must be accompanied by a corresponding phantasm, every free movement of thoughts would become impossible if sense could not gather a store of sensitive presentations. Now how this movement is possible under the given conditions we have seen: it is to be traced back not to the efficacy of the spiritual part in the sensitive that proceeds from the νοῦς ποιητικός, but to that which proceeds from willing. But the νοῦς ποιητικός too requires a certain disposition of the sensitive part for its working. Even the presentations of the drunk and the insane can no longer serve as mediators of thoughts, and so much the less, then, would this be possible if the sentient organ of man were as dull to sensory impressions as is that of those lowest species of animals which lack all imagination and all memory. Thus memory is a necessary preliminary stage of spiritual cognition.

310) Anal. Poster. II, 19. p. 99, b, 30. φανερὸν τοίνυν ὅτι οὔτ᾽ ἔχειν οἷόν τε οὔτ᾽ ἀγνοοῦσι καὶ μηδεμίαν ἔχουσιν ἕξιν ἐγγίνεσθαι. ἀνάγκη ἄρα ἔχειν μέν τινα δύναμιν, μὴ τοιαύτην δ᾽ ἔχειν ἣ ἔσται τούτων τιμιωτέρα κατ᾽ ἀκρίβειαν. φαίνεται δὲ τοῦτό γε πᾶσιν ὑπάρχον τοῖς ζῴοις. ἔχει γὰρ δύναμιν σύμφυτον κριτικήν, ἣν καλοῦσιν αἴσθησιν. ibid. p. 100, a, 10.

311) Anal. Poster. II, 19. p. 99, b, 36 f.

312) For in the books On the Soul Aristotle treats of memory only in general, and insofar as it is included under the imagination; the more specific investigations are given in the treatise De Memoria et Reminiscentia.

[p. 213] But, it will perhaps be objected, Aristotle seems in the Analytics to regard memory as a precondition of spiritual cognition in yet a quite different way; he seems to let a species concept develop out of several sensory presentations, just as a genus concept develops out of several species concepts<sup>313)</sup>. This, however, is a doctrine foreign to the books On the Soul. A phantasm is indeed necessary according to them too, as an instrument of the active intellect, but a plurality of phantasms seems not to have been required<sup>314)</sup>.

To this we reply that the last chapter of the Posterior Analytics does not contain this doctrine either, since Aristotle treats here not of the origination of concepts, but of the origination of other immediate truths which are the presupposition of demonstration, namely of that of the general propositions of experience. Let us hear with what example the related passage in the first chapter of the first book of the Metaphysics illustrates his doctrine. “Art arises,” it says here<sup>315)</sup>, “when out of many observations of experience a single universal judgment about what is alike is formed. For the cognition that a certain remedy has helped Callias in a certain illness, and likewise Socrates and many others individually, is a matter of experience; but the cognition that it helps all who are of this definite like constitution and suffer from this definite illness, e.g., from fever, is a matter of art.” We see that what is at issue here is neither the origination of the concept of fever nor that of any other concept, but that, these concepts being presupposed, a universal judgment connecting two concepts is to be established<sup>316)</sup>. For in the proposition: some fever patient was healed in such and such a way, the concept of the fever patient is, after all, contained just as well as in the proposition which, of all fever patients, [p. 214] asserts the same, and which arises by induction from the repeated cognition of the first<sup>317)</sup>.

313) Anal. Poster. II, 19. p. 100, a, 15. στάντος γὰρ τῶν ἀδιαφόρων ἑνὸς πρῶτον μὲν ἐν τῇ ψυχῇ καθόλου (καὶ γὰρ αἰσθάνεται μὲν τὸ καθ᾽ ἕκαστον, ἡ δ᾽ αἴσθησις τοῦ καθόλου ἐστίν, οἷον ἀνθρώπου, ἀλλ᾽ οὐ Καλλίου ἀνθρώπου) πάλιν ἐν τούτοις ἵσταται, ἕως ἂν τὰ ἀμερῆ στῇ καὶ τὰ καθόλου, οἷον τοιονδὶ ζῷον, ἕως ζῷον· καὶ ἐν τούτῳ ὡσαύτως. δῆλον δὴ ὅτι ἡμῖν τὰ πρῶτα ἐπαγωγῇ γνωρίζειν ἀναγκαῖον· καὶ γὰρ καὶ αἴσθησις οὕτω τὸ καθόλου ἐμποιεῖ. Cf. also what precedes (a, 3.)

314) At least not in all cases, but only with those abstracted from sensible matter. see above n. 107.

315) Metaph. Α, 1. p. 981, a, 5. γίνεται δὲ τέχνη, ὅταν ἐκ πολλῶν τῆς ἐμπειρίας ἐννοημάτων μία καθόλου γένηται περὶ τῶν ὁμοίων ὑπόληψις. τὸ μὲν γὰρ ἔχειν ὑπόληψιν ὅτι Καλλίᾳ κάμνοντι τηνδὶ τὴν νόσον τοδὶ συνήνεγκε καὶ Σωκράτει καὶ καθ᾽ ἕκαστον οὕτω πολλοῖς, ἐμπειρίας ἐστίν· τὸ δ᾽ ὅτι πᾶσι τοῖς τοιοῖσδε κατ᾽ εἶδος ἓν ἀφορισθεῖσι, κάμνουσι τηνδὶ τὴν νόσον, συνήνεγκεν, οἷον τοῖς φλεγματώδεσιν ἢ χολώδεσιν ἢ πυρέττουσι καύσῳ, τέχνης.

316) Cf. also Anal. Poster. I, 31. p. 88, a, 2.

Now the last chapter of the Analytics teaches the same thing; for although this passage would, in and of itself, also admit another sense, it nevertheless receives its sure explanation through comparison with the analogous passage of the Metaphysics.

Only, Aristotle here carries the same thought further; he shows that, just as from many like perceptions of the senses a general proposition for the whole species is derived, so too from many cognitions which extend to whole species a new, higher cognition grows, namely a judgment that is extended to the whole genus. From the genus one then arrives in the same way at the higher genus, until one reaches that concept which is the highest to which the predicate belongs with universality, and in which we have at the same time to recognize the ground of that property itself. Suppose, e.g., that someone finds by induction the law that all living organic bodies are mortal. By what route has he arrived at it? First he recognized, from the death of many individual men, that all living bodies of a certain species—e.g., that all men—are mortal. Then, since he saw the same phenomenon recur universally in many and various species of animals, he rose to the higher genus concept, namely to that of animal: all animals, he said, are mortal. Finally, after he had made the same experiences and established the same particular truths in the various species of plants as well, and in the plant kingdom in general, he arrived at the universal law of the mortality of all living organisms, and in this concept he has now grasped the ground of mortality for all individual mortal beings. It is to this ascent, then, from individual perception to the concept, and from the lower concept to the higher, but not to the origination of the one from the other, that the passage of the Analytics is to be referred. That is why Aristotle had to demand more than *one* sense perception here, whereas according to his doctrine a single phantasm would suffice for the origination of a species concept; and precisely for that reason he also called here not merely on the help of the imagination in general, but specifically on that of memory, whereas for the origination of a concept the phantasm as such suffices. For without cognition of the temporal separateness of [p. 215] earlier perceptions the inductive summation of them would become impossible. Concepts, however, do arise by means of sense perceptions, but not by means of induction in this narrower meaning. And not only concepts, but also many other fundamental truths, Aristotle could not have arise from experience in this way. Plainly no one needs experience in order to know the mathematical axiom that the whole is greater than the part; rather, this follows from the concept of the whole and of the part itself<sup>318)</sup>, and therefore, like that concept, it presupposes sensory knowledge of the individual, but not induction proper<sup>319)</sup>.

317) The indefinite proposition, too: some fever patient was healed in such and such a way, is not a judgment of sense but of the intellect; and it is to these particular judgments of the intellect that De Anim. II, 5. §. 6. (p. 417, b, 26.) is to be referred, when it speaks, in seeming contradiction to all other statements of Aristotle, of a spiritual cognition of the sensible individual.

So much for explaining the agreement of the Posterior Analytics with the third book On the Soul<sup>320)</sup>.

34. From Aristotle’s theory of knowledge in general and from the whole of his psychological views we have demonstrated the need to assume a spiritual power of the nature of the active intellect; we have then, by a careful explanation of the fifth chapter of the third book On the Soul, [p. 216] actually recognized the νοῦς ποιητικός as this faculty demanded for the completion and explanation of the Aristotelian theory, and shown other interpretations to be erroneous; and now, last of all, we have further shown by an example from the logical writings that they contain nothing which would not stand in the most beautiful harmony with Aristotle’s doctrine in the books On the Soul as we understand it. But this one example can stand for all, since it is precisely in reconciling this chapter with the third book On the Soul that the interpreters have found the most difficulty. And so we have, then, a threefold proof of the correctness of our interpretation; for the fact too that it alone is able to resolve the apparent contradictions between one and another of our philosopher’s doctrines may well have for us the significance it has for the physicist when he is able by his hypothesis to explain all the phenomena of nature, even if they previously seemed puzzling and contradictory. But we can also adduce external testimonies for the correctness of our interpretation, and indeed we have a guarantor whose authority no one will set aside, namely the famous pupil of Aristotle whom he himself appointed as his successor in the Lyceum.

318) Cf. Eth. Nicom. VI, 9. p. 1142, a, 16. καὶ τοῦτ᾽ ἄν τις σκέψαιτο, διὰ τί δὴ μαθηματικὸς μὲν παῖς γένοιτ᾽ ἄν, σοφὸς δ᾽ ἢ φυσικὸς οὔ. ἢ ὅτι τὰ μὲν δι᾽ ἀφαιρέσεώς ἐστιν, τῶν δ᾽ αἱ ἀρχαὶ ἐξ ἐμπειρίας· καὶ τὰ μὲν οὐ πιστεύουσιν οἱ νέοι ἀλλὰ λέγουσιν, τῶν δὲ τὸ τί ἐστιν οὐκ ἄδηλον.

319) In Eth. Nicom. I, 7. p. 1098, b. 3. Aristotle distinguishes: τῶν ἀρχῶν δ᾽ αἱ μὲν ἐπαγωγῇ θεωροῦνται αἱ δ᾽ αἰσθήσει κ. τ. λ. — In a wider sense, however, he often calls every arising from sensory knowledge of the individual induction. Cf. e.g. Anal. Poster. II, 18. p. 81, a, 10. Eth. Nicom. VI, 3. p. 1139, b, 26—31.

320) That the principle which, in induction, freely moves the phantasms is not the νοῦς ποιητικός but actual willing, our earlier discussions have shown. In Rénan (Averr. et l’Averroisme, p. 96.) we find the following remark: “Dans une thèse ingénieuse présentée à la Faculté des Lettres (*Dénis*, Rationalisme d’Aristote) on a combattu l’interprétation d’Ibn-Roschd et soutenu que l’intellect actif n’est pour Aristote qu’une faculté de l’âme. L’intellect passif n’est alors que la faculté de recevoir les φαντάσματα; l’intellect actif n’est que l’induction s’exerçant sur les φαντάσματα et en tirant les idées générales.” We did not include this interpretation of the νοῦς ποιητικός among the attempts at explanation cited above (Section I.), since it was unfortunately not possible for us to make use of the work itself, while this quotation does not suffice to give a clear and certain presentation of its content in all its parts. If the author, as appears to be the case, identifies the νοῦς δυνάμει with the faculty of phantasms, and thus takes it for a sensory power, but lets the νοῦς ποιητικός grasp the universal thoughts, then both are erroneous and have already been refuted by us earlier. However that may be (for perhaps the report is not given accurately on these points), the author at any rate seems to have erred in either identifying the conscious and the unconscious influence of the intellective part on the sensitive, or overlooking one of them.

By a happy dispensation a fragment from the fifth book of Theophrastus’s Physics has been preserved for us in Themistius, and however much we must lament the loss of the whole work, it cannot be denied that precisely one of its most important leaves is thereby in our hands; for precisely in this passage Theophrastus speaks of the active and the receptive intellect and of the relation of the two to each other. Brandis<sup>321)</sup> and Torstrik<sup>322)</sup> have already made use of it in order to refute those opinions which separate the νοῦς ποιητικός from the essence of man, and Themistius himself adduced it chiefly with this intention. But many other, more precise determinations can still be clearly discerned from it, which, as we shall see, admit of no interpretation other than ours, but are in the most perfect agreement with it point for point<sup>323)</sup>.

321) Gesch. d. Entwickel. d. griech. Philos. I, p. 572. cf. Arist. Lehrgeb. p. 288.

322) *Aristot.*, De Anim. p. 184. Torstrik has, besides, the intention of proving from Theophrastus’s words that according to Aristotle the human intellect does not always cognize, and in this too he has indisputably succeeded; but what he further infers, namely that in De Anim. III, 5. §. 2. p. 430, a, 22. οὐχ must be deleted, is not correct.

323) Unfortunately the text has not been preserved quite pure. Usener, Brandis and Torstrik have already emended it, but sometimes also altered it where the traditional reading was the right one. Part of the fragment we find in

[p. 217] 1) We have maintained that the receptive intellect, of which the fourth and fifth chapters of the third book On the Soul say that it becomes all things, may not be identified with the passive intellect of which Aristotle speaks at the end of this chapter. The one, we said, belongs to the spiritual part of man, while the other is plainly common to soul and body and for that very reason corruptible.

Quite the same thing Aristotle taught according to Theophrastus. For right at the beginning the latter raises the difficulty how it is possible after all that the νοῦς δυνάμει (the receptive intellect), although it does not stem from matter but comes from outside, and is like something added on, nevertheless belongs to the nature of man<sup>324)</sup>. Later, too, he calls it incorporeal<sup>325)</sup> and says that the senses are not free of the body, but the intellect is free of it<sup>326)</sup>. To that difficulty, however, he answers that the νοῦς δυνάμει is not added to the finished bodily man, but is included in his coming into being<sup>327)</sup>. This last remark too, as we shall recall, is in the best harmony with what we have discussed concerning the origin of the spiritual part of the soul. The passive intellect, on the other hand, Theophrastus, as Themistius says, called something common to soul and body and bodily<sup>328)</sup>; and indeed the words of Aristotle at the end of the fifth chapter were also too clear for him to have been able to speak otherwise.

2) We have further said that the receptive intellect is by its nature the mere potentiality of thoughts, and that its operation is a being affected in that sense in which a being affected was also ascribed to the sensing [p. 218] faculties; finally, that in this way it grasps *everything* intelligible, from which it followed that it must be our sole spiritual cognitive faculty.

an earlier chapter with some deviations, and Torstrik believed he had to correct the second quotation according to the first. We cannot agree with him; rather, the first, which is plainly less accurate in many points, seems to us to be quoted from memory, which is not conceivable in the case of the second, longer quotation. From this the small differences that present themselves line by line are easily explained.

324) *Themist.*, De Anim. fol. 91 r<sup>o</sup> περὶ μὲν οὖν τοῦ δυνάμει (νοῦ) τάδε φησὶν (Θεόφραστος)· “ὁ δὲ νοῦς πῶς ποτὲ ἔξωθεν ὢν καὶ ὥσπερ ἐπίθετος ὅμως συμφυής;” (which is not, as Torstrik would have it, to be changed into σύμφυτος, for it is meant to signify nothing other than that the νοῦς belongs to *one* being with the bodily man, which would not be possible if it came to his finished nature from outside. The objection is directed against De Generat. Animal. II, 3, p. 736, b, 27. ἔξωθεν means here the same as what Aristotle there designates by θύραθεν.

325) Ibid. ἀσωμάτῳ δὲ ὑπὸ σώματος τί τὸ πάθος;

326) Ibid. καὶ προϊὼν φησι (Θεόφραστος), τὰς μὲν αἰσθήσεις οὐκ ἄνευ σώματος, τὸν δὲ νοῦν χωριστόν.

327) Ibid. ἀλλὰ τὸ ‘ἔξωθεν’ ἄρα οὐχ ὡς ἐπίθετον ἀλλ᾽ ὡς ἐν τῇ πρώτη γενέσει συμπεριλαμβάνον (read συμπεριλαμβανόμενον) θετέον.

328) Ibid. ὃν (namely τὸν παθητικὸν νοῦν καὶ φθαρτόν) καὶ κοινὸν ὀνομάζουσι (᾿Αριστοτ. κ. Θεόφρ.) καὶ ἀχώριστον τοῦ σώματος.

All these determinations, which Aristotle himself gave in clear words, the fragment of Theophrastus also repeats with all the clarity one could wish<sup>329)</sup>.

3) We have further said that the receptive intellect, because by its nature it is thinking merely potentially, requires, in order to become actually thinking, an influence on the spiritual part. This influence, we said, it must receive in the first instance from the bodily part, but its proper principle could only be something spiritual and must therefore be sought in the intellect itself. Moreover, it is also actively involved in the formation of its thoughts insofar as it freely and in accordance with its purposes orders and reshapes the phantasms in which it beholds its concepts.

Now Theophrastus, after saying that the operation of the receptive intellect cannot be conceived without a being affected of the spiritual part, likewise discusses, and indeed immediately, the question of its active principle, and in doing so considers only a twofold possibility, namely that the being affected of the human spirit proceeds either from itself or from the bodily part. We see, then, how far from the great pupil of Aristotle lay the thought of deriving our thinking immediately from the Deity itself. He raises, however, a twofold question. First he asks how something spiritual can after all be affected and changed by something corporeal<sup>330)</sup>, and here it plainly shows through that Aristotle really taught such a being affected. Then he asks in which part of man the active principle of thinking lies, whether namely it is in the body or in the spiritual part itself, and he cites three statements of Aristotle, of which the first seems to speak for the first assumption, and the two others for the second. The first he takes from the fourth chapter of the third book On the Soul, where Aristotle says that thinking is a being affected by the intelligible<sup>331)</sup>. The second he takes from [p. 219] the fifth chapter of the third book, where he says of the active intellect that it makes all things, namely all thoughts, actual<sup>332)</sup>. The third statement, finally, he takes from the fifth chapter of the second book, where Aristotle says that it is in the power of one who thinks to think something when he wishes, but not in the power of one who perceives by sense to perceive something when he wishes, since for this the presence of the sensible object is necessary<sup>333)</sup>.

329) Ibid. τίς ἡ φύσις αὐτοῦ (namely τοῦ δυνάμει νοῦ); τὸ μὲν γὰρ ‘μηδὲν εἶναι ἐνεργείᾳ, δυνάμει δὲ πάντα’ (a statement of Aristotle) καλῶς, ὥσπερ καὶ ἡ αἴσθησις. Immediately afterwards Theophrastus says that this νοῦς is ὡς ὑποκειμένην τινὰ δύναμιν, καθάπερ καὶ ἐπὶ τῶν ὑλικῶν. And again: πῶς δέ ποτε γίνεται τὰ νοητά; καὶ τί τὸ πάσχειν αὐτόν; δεῖ γάρ, εἴπερ εἰς ἐνέργειαν ἥξει, ὥσπερ ἡ αἴσθησις. Finally: ὁ νοῦς ὕλης ἔχει φύσιν μηδὲν ὤν, ἅπαντα δὲ δυνατός.

330) After the words just cited he continues: ἀσωμάτῳ δὲ ὑπὸ σώματος τί τὸ πάθος; ἢ ποία μεταβολή;
