331) De Anim. III, 4. §. 2. p. 429. a, 13. εἰ δή ἐστι τὸ νοεῖν ὥσπερ τὸ αἰσθάνεσθαι, ἢ πάσχειν τι ἂν εἴη ὑπὸ τοῦ νοητοῦ ἤ τι τοιοῦτον. ibid. §. 9. p. 429, b, 24. τὸ νοεῖν πάσχειν τί ἐστιν.

Let us hear Theophrastus himself: “In which of the two,” he asks, “is the active principle of thinking found; is it found in the body or in the spiritual part itself? Each has something in its favor. For the fact that Aristotle calls it ‘being affected’ might prove that the principle is in the former, since nothing that behaves passively is affected by itself; but the fact that he says ‘it is the principle of all things’ and ‘thinking rests with it and is not for it as perceiving is for the senses’ [namely, precisely determined by something else and entirely withdrawn from its discretion], ‘this might prove that the active principle is in it itself<sup>334)</sup>.’”

332) De Anim. III, 5. §. 1. p. 430, a, 14. ὁ δὲ (νοῦς), τῷ πάντα ποιεῖν, ὡς ἕξις τις (sc. ἐστίν). Immediately afterwards (a, 19.) Aristotle also uses the expression ἀρχή.

333) De Anim. II, 5. §. 6. p. 417, b, 24 διὸ νοῆσαι μὲν ἐπ᾽ αὐτῷ, ὁπόταν βούληται, αἰσθάνεσθαι δ᾽ οὐκ ἐπ᾽ αὐτῷ· ἀναγκαῖον γὰρ ὑπάρχειν τὸ αἰσθητόν.

334) Theophrastus continues after the words last cited (n. 330.): καὶ πότερον ἐπ᾽ ἐκείνου ἡ ἀρχὴ ἢ ἀπ᾽ (read ἐπ᾽) αὐτοῦ; τὸ μὲν γὰρ ‘πάσχειν’ ἀπ᾽ (read ἐπ᾽) ἐκείνου (namely τοῦ σώματος) δόξειεν (read δείξειεν) ἂν (sc. εἶναι τὴν ἀρχήν)· οὐδὲν γὰρ ἀφ᾽ ἑαυτοῦ τῶν ἐν πάθει· τὸ δὲ ‘ἀρχὴν πάντων εἶναι’ καὶ ‘ἐπ᾽ αὐτῷ τὸ νοεῖν καὶ μὴ ὥσπερ ταῖς αἰσθήσεσιν’ ἀπ᾽ (read ἐπ᾽) αὐτοῦ (i.e. δείξειεν ἂν ἐπ᾽ αὐτοῦ εἶναι τὴν ἀρχήν). This passage, in which the text is plainly not quite pure, was formerly emended in a different way. Scholars noticed the inequality between the first ἐπ᾽ and the following threefold ἀπ᾽, and since they held it more probable that one letter was wrong than that three were, they changed ἐπ᾽ into ἀπ᾽. But if one considers that the ἀφ᾽ ἑαυτοῦ which occurs in the middle of the sentence was bound to lead someone who did not recognize the sense to the opinion that ἀπ᾽ must formerly have been read in place of ἐπ᾽ too, one will perhaps find the opposite still more probable, quite apart from the fact that the sense of the passage, which we have explained above, makes ἐπί desirable, even if it does not absolutely demand it. ἀρχή means, as in De Anim. III, 5. p. 430, a, 19., the active principle of thinking, and with regard to it Theophrastus asks whether it is on the side of the body or in the spirit itself (ἐπὶ is used here as ἐν is in De Gener. et Corr. I, 9. p. 324, a, 26. ἐν ᾧ τε γὰρ ἡ ἀρχὴ τῆς κινήσεως, δοκεῖ τοῦτο κινεῖν. cf. De Generat. Anim. I, 6. p. 442, a, 33.), but not whether it derives from the one or the other. (If one decided for ἀφ᾽ οὗ, one would therefore have to think of it as used in the way that ὅθεν is used in the expression ὅθεν ἡ ἀρχὴ τῆς κινήσεως.) The second time, where ἀπ᾽ now stands for ἐπ᾽, it was especially tempting to think of the preposition as dependent on πάσχειν, whereas in fact this word, like τὸ ‘ἔξωθεν’ earlier (see n. 327. cf. also the way in which Theophrastus quotes, n. 329.), stands by itself as an allusion to a well-known statement of Aristotle. This, then, seems to have given the occasion

[p. 220] Theophrastus, then, here draws attention to the apparent contradictions of the Aristotelian theory of knowledge. Thinking, says Aristotle, is a being affected of the spiritual part, and from this it follows that the spiritual part, in order to think, must receive an influence from something else. But what should this other be, if not the bodily element of man? And so the active principle of thinking seems to have to belong to the body. On the other hand, Aristotle says that the active principle of thoughts is the principle of *all things*, and this cannot possibly be reconciled with the view that some property of the body, as warmth or color is for touch or sight, is the corresponding active principle for the intellect; and so we are once again driven to seek it in the spiritual part. Added to this is the fact that if the spiritual part had no influence at all on the formation of its own thoughts, it would, with respect to its thinking, necessarily fall into the same dependence on the sensitive part in which the latter finds itself, with respect to its perceptions, vis-à-vis the sensible objects. Every free movement of thinking would become impossible. Now Aristotle, however, also recognizes such free movement, and from this it can be seen that he has the spiritual part participate in thinking not merely passively but also actively; but this seems to mean nothing other than that the active principle of thinking is likewise in the spiritual part.

The difficulties which Theophrastus here brings out are not unknown to us; we ourselves have used them in almost the same way in order to make clear the individual factors which, according to Aristotle, work together in the origination of our thoughts. First we pointed to the dependence of thinking on the sensitive part and its phantasms; then we showed that from the sensitive part alone neither a thought at all, nor in particular the free movement of our thinking, can be explained. For this there was needed the [p. 221] assumption of a twofold influence of the intellective part on the sensitive: first the influence of the unconsciously acting intellect, and then the dominion of the will. And it is plainly at the separation of the share which the sensitive soul, the active intellect and the command of the will have in the origination of our thoughts that the difficulty raised by Theophrastus aims, and through this separation it finds its solution. Immediately, to be sure, the spiritual part, when it receives thoughts, must be affected by something else; but this does not say that it does not itself cooperate at all in the origination of its thoughts, since it can indeed be involved mediately as a cause. Indeed, nothing will for that reason stand in the way of its exerting an influence even in several ways and also containing within itself the proper active principle of its thinking, as is the case according to Aristotelian doctrine.

for the change, just as further below, in consequence of a similar misunderstanding, παθητικὸν ὑπ᾽ αὐτοῦ was read instead of παθητικὸν ἐπ᾽ αὐτοῦ, until Torstrik (loc. cit. p. 188.) recognized the right reading. Why we read δείξειεν instead of δόξειεν is self-evident from this. In place of ἀρχή, which had been substituted for ἀρχήν, we had to restore the earlier reading, for here too a statement of Aristotle is alluded to. We say “alluded to”; for it is no more a proper and literal quotation of the whole passage than were ἔξωθεν and πάσχειν and μηδὲν εἶναι κ. τ. λ. before (see n. 329.) and ἐπ᾽ αὐτῷ κ. τ. λ. later. Presupposing a knowledge of the Aristotelian statements, Theophrastus contents himself with pointing to them.

It may further be remarked in passing that further below, where Torstrik wants to read ὅτι δή φαμεν instead of ὃ δή φαμεν, we hold the original text to be the correct one. That ὃ accords entirely with the way in which Theophrastus alludes to Aristotle’s statements in the passages just discussed.

We now pass over some intervening remarks of Themistius which contain only single expressions and short sentences of Theophrastus, if not of Aristotle (for they are also all found almost word for word in the latter), which confirm the spirituality of the receptive intellect and state the difference between its being affected and being moved in the proper sense. After them Themistius again gives us a longer quotation, in which Theophrastus deals with the Aristotelian doctrine of the active intellect (ποιητικὸς νοῦς). After what we have just heard, it cannot be otherwise than that this passage too speaks entirely for our assumption.

4. We have said that the active intellect according to Aristotle is not a substance, but is as an accident in a subject, and indeed in the same spiritual part of our soul of which the receptive intellect too is an accident. For that the latter is something accidental is unmistakable, since if it, which is mere potentiality, were something substantial, it would have to be identical with corporeal matter. In this, then, the two agreed; both turned out for us to be accidents of the intellective soul and as such were present in it from the beginning. On the other hand, we said, the active intellect differs from the receptive in that it is just as much a pure accidental actuality as the latter is a pure accidental potentiality, and in that it does not think, but by its action produces the thoughts.

Now that Aristotle, according to the way in which Theophrastus too understood his doctrine, must have regarded the active intellect as an accident, is evident from the question what, then, is the subject for the active intellect and, as such, connected with it. The following words: “for the intellect is something in a certain way mixed of the active and that which is in potentiality<sup>334a)</sup>,” [p. 222] seem to us to have been added by way of explanation by Themistius, who further below also makes the one stand to the other as matter to form<sup>335)</sup>. This is an obvious error<sup>336)</sup>. If, therefore, the words are to have a correct sense, “mixed” would here signify nothing other than their accidental union in the same subject<sup>337)</sup>, and this improper use of the expression would be indicated by the “in a certain way.” However, it is probable to us, as we said, that Themistius, caught up in the same erroneous opinion which later comes clearly to light, inserted them; and Theophrastus himself, if we compare the earlier quotation of the words that now follow (for we find them quoted in two places), seems to us to have continued, after the question “which, then, are these two natures (namely the active and the receptive intellect), and which, again, is the subject of the active intellect and that which is connected with it?”, thus: “for its power is like a habitus” (an accidental actuality), so that Theophrastus inferred the necessity of the question about its subject from the very words of Aristotle in which we found a proof that the active intellect is an accidental form. Then, after having already repeated all the expressions, such as αἴτιον, ποιητικόν, ποιοῦν, ἀρχή, by which Aristotle had designated this faculty as an active one, he clearly calls the active intellect the mover (ὁ κινῶν), and the following difficulty shows that he thought of it as the moving principle of the receptive intellect and as originally united with it in the soul. “If, now,” he says, “the mover is united by nature (with us or with the receptive intellect that is to be moved), one would think it must move at once and without ceasing; but if it comes to this only later, the question arises with what (with what subject) and how it comes into being. Now surely no coming into being (γένεσις) seems able to belong to it, if indeed it is also subject to no corruption. Since, then, it is in us, why does it not move? or whence come forgetting and deception and error? — Or do these perhaps have their ground in the mixture<sup>338)</sup>?” namely [p. 223] in the commingling of the sensitive part with matter; for this, if we are willing to believe Themistius, who ends his quotation here, would be the sense of the last words, a sense which the end of the fifth chapter of the third book On the Soul also makes probable. That, as we were able to see above from Theophrastus’s words, the spiritual part receives that influence by which it becomes thinking in the first instance from the bodily part also agrees with this.

334a) See the whole passage, n. 338.

335) He says that the active and the receptive intellect are in a certain way *one* nature, and gives as the reason: ἓν γὰρ τὸ ἐξ ὕλης καὶ εἴδους.

336) For Aristotle calls each of the two ἀμιγής in this sense. De Anim. III, 4. §. 3. p. 429, a, 18. ibid. 5. §. 1. p. 430, a. 18.

337) It would be somewhat as if one said that the sensitive part is mixed of sensing and appetitive powers. The expression μεμῖχθαι τῷ σώματι Aristotle often uses to indicate that a power is found in the bodily, not in the spiritual, part of man.

338) In f. 91. Themistius says: διαπορεῖ δὲ (Θεόφραστος) τίνες οὖν αὗται αἱ δύο φύσεις (namely the one which Aristotle designates as ὕλη and δύναμις, and the one which he designates as αἴτιον and ποιητικόν of thinking), καὶ τί πάλιν τὸ ὑποκείμενον ἢ συνηρτημένον

From this, however, the complete doctrine of the active intellect, as we developed it earlier, is now put together. For that it is spiritual Theophrastus repeats in the unambiguous [p. 224] words of Aristotle; but that it belongs to our soul, that it is moreover an accident of the soul, and indeed by nature an accidental actuality of it, that it is a moving faculty, that by its action it produces the thoughts in the receptive intellect, that with its action it is turned in the first instance toward the sensitive part, that it acts unconsciously and therefore necessarily as soon as the sensitive part is capable of receiving its action, that it can least of all be held to be itself something thinking (for otherwise, since it is pure actuality, we would have to have an actual thinking from the beginning, and the last aporia would lose its basis), that the name intellect must therefore have been given to it for another reason, whether because it belongs to the intellective part or because it makes thinking, i.e. because by its action it produces the thoughts — all these determinations could be recognized from Theophrastus’s questions and objections and indicated solutions with certainty, or at any rate with the highest probability; and so we have the satisfaction that the testimony of an immediate pupil recommends our interpretation of the Aristotelian doctrine as the correct one.

τῷ ποιητικῷ· μικτὸν γάρ πως ὁ νοῦς ἔκ τε τοῦ ποιητικοῦ καὶ τοῦ δυνάμει. εἰ μὲν οὖν σύμφυτος ὁ κινῶν, καὶ εὐθὺς ἐχρῆν καὶ ἀεὶ (sc. κινεῖν)· εἰ δὲ ὕστερον μετὰ τίνος (with what subject; just before he had called it τὸ συνηρτημένον τῷ ποιητικῷ) καὶ πῶς ἡ γένεσις; ἔοικεν οὖν καὶ ἀγέννητος (for which one may not, as Torstrik would have it, put ἀγένητος, for only coming into being out of a matter is denied), εἴπερ καὶ ἄφθαρτος. ἐνυπάρχων δ᾽ οὖν, διὰ τί οὐκ ἀεί; (read οὐ κινεῖ; for otherwise, at least according to the grammatical rule, ἐνυπάρχει would have to be supplied, which would give an incorrect sense, since the active intellect is in truth always in the soul. The corruption was all the more easily possible since the words were not written separately, and ἀεὶ immediately preceded) ἢ διὰ τί λήθη καὶ ἀπάτη καὶ ψεῦδος; ἢ διὰ τὴν μῖξιν; — In f. 89., on the other hand, we find the same passage quoted in the following way: εἰ μὲν γὰρ ὡς ἕξις, φησίν (Θεόφραστος), ἡ δύναμις ἐκείνῳ, εἰ μὲν σύμφυτος, ἀεὶ καὶ εὐθὺς ἐχρῆν (What?)· εἰ δ᾽ ὕστερον, μετὰ τίνος καὶ πῶς ἡ γένεσις; ἔοικε δ᾽ οὖν ὡς ἀγέννητος, εἴπερ ἄφθαρτος. ἐνυπάρχων δ᾽ οὖν, διὰ τί οὐκ ἀεὶ (What?); ἢ διὰ τί λήθη καὶ ἀπάτη; ἢ διὰ τὴν μῖξιν; the first quotation is more correct in everything in which the two differ from one another, and has in common with the second only the error (if, that is, it is to be regarded as such) of οὐκ ἀεί for οὐ κινεῖ.

Theophrastus, then, seems to us to have written thus: τίνες οὖν αὗται αἱ δύο φύσεις; καὶ τί πάλιν τὸ ὑποκείμενον ἢ συνηρτημένον τῷ ποιητικῷ; ἔστι γὰρ ὡς ἕξις ἡ δύναμις ἐκείνου. εἰ μὲν οὖν σύμφυτος ὁ κινῶν, καὶ εὐθὺς ἐχρῆν καὶ ἀεὶ (sc. κινεῖν)· εἰ δὲ ὕστερον, μετὰ τίνος καὶ πῶς ἡ γένεσις; ἔοικεν οὖν καὶ ἀγέννητος, εἴπερ καὶ ἄφθαρτος. ἐνυπάρχων δ᾽ οὖν, διὰ τί οὐ κινεῖ; ἢ διὰ τί λήθη καὶ ἀπάτη καὶ ψεῦδος; ἢ διὰ τὴν μῖξιν; It is to be remarked, however, that ὑποκείμενον can have a double sense: on the one hand it can mean, as we have just taken it, the subject of an accident, but on the other hand also the object of an active or passive power (as e.g. De Anim. III, 2. p. 10. p. 426, b, 8.). It can be taken in the latter sense too in our passage; Theophrastus’s question would in this case be directed at that which *immediately* receives the influence of the active intellect, and from this it would then be still more evident that this influence is mediated to the receptive intellect by something else. For that the activity of the active intellect ultimately leads to the latter could no longer be in any question, and moreover what immediately precedes shows that the receptive intellect is not meant by the ὑποκείμενον. — If, according to this explanation of the expression, nothing can be inferred from it for the accidentality of the νοῦς ποιητικός and for its union with the νοῦς δυνάμει in the spiritual part of our soul, the same doctrine nevertheless follows clearly enough from the subsequent ‘σύμφυτος ὁ κινῶν’ and ‘ἀγέννητος εἴπερ καὶ ἄφθαρτος. κ. τ. λ.’

35. Yet our joy at this would be not a little disturbed if what is affirmed by Theophrastus were denied by Eudemus, the faithful pupil of Aristotle. Ravaisson appealed to the testimony of his Ethics when he sought to show that the Deity is the active intellect in Aristotle’s sense, and Eudemus does in fact seem, in a passage of the seventh book, to call God in clear words the active principle of our thinking. Let us hear his own words: “What,” he asks, “is the first principle of motion in the soul? Plainly, as in the universe, so in the soul too it must surely be the Deity. For the divine in us” (namely our thinking) “moves everything else. But the first moving principle for thought cannot again be a thought; rather, it must be something higher. What, now, could one name that is still higher than knowledge, if it is not the Deity? for virtue is exercised in dependence on thinking<sup>339)</sup>.” — Thus Eudemus. But what shall we reply if this passage is held up to us as an objection against the correctness of our explanation? Shall we perhaps say, with some recent critics, that Eudemus here departs from the doctrine of his master [p. 225] ? But how, then, would he speak with such certainty, and as of something that follows as a simple consequence from his philosophical principles, since these are, after all, common to him and Aristotle more than to anyone else? He himself at least seems not to be conscious of any departure from the master.

339) Eth. Eudem. VII, 14. p. 1248, a, 24. τὸ δὲ ζητούμενον τοῦτ᾽ ἐστί, τίς ἡ τῆς κινήσεως ἀρχὴ ἐν τῇ ψυχῇ. δῆλον δή, ὥσπερ ἐν τῷ ὅλῳ θεός, καὶ πᾶν ἐκείνῳ. (read καὶ ἂν ἐν ἐκείνῃ. corr. Fritzsche) κινεῖ γάρ πως πάντα τὸ ἐν ἡμῖν θεῖον. λόγου δ᾽ ἀρχὴ οὐ λόγος ἀλλά τι κρεῖττον. τί οὖν ἂν κρεῖττον καὶ ἐπιστήμης εἴποι (read εἴη?) πλὴν θεός; ἡ γὰρ ἀρετὴ τοῦ νοῦ ὄργανον.

But in fact he does not here depart in the very least from the Aristotelian doctrine<sup>340)</sup>. Aristotle too calls not the receptive or the active intellect, but actual cognition, the divine in us<sup>341)</sup>; and he too, as we have seen, believes that he can fully comprehend the origination of knowledge in no other way than by pointing to that thinking which partakes of all our knowledge and (not through concepts, but in a higher way) “does not now think it and now not think it<sup>342)</sup>.” Had our soul not proceeded from the power of this eternal intellect, the law of synonymy would, in the origination of its thoughts, despite the phantasms and despite the active intellect, be preserved only in that imperfect manner which is peculiar to chance coming-to-be. Eudemus thus goes back to that efficient cause which explains our thinking perfectly and as something that comes into being naturally; of this, however, Theophrastus did not speak; he spoke rather of the proximate principle, which is a God-given power of our soul; and so we see that the statement of his friend and fellow pupil, far from weakening the testimony which he has given us, is in complete harmony with it and supplements it in a welcome way precisely on that point about which he himself was silent.

36. With this demonstration of the concurring testimony of Eudemus and Theophrastus we may now close our inquiry. For what further proof could be demanded of us, after we have shown that the principles of the Aristotelian system, and in particular those of his psychology and theory of knowledge, demand an active intellect in our sense, and that the [p. 226] determinations of the fifth chapter of the third book On the Soul really contain the corresponding doctrine; further, that seemingly contradictory statements of Aristotle are reconciled in our explanation, and that not only does he himself everywhere remain in agreement with this doctrine, but his pupils too, the one on this point, the other on that, confirm it?

340) By this we do not mean to say that the investigations of the fourteenth chap. in the seventh book of the Eudemian Ethics do not contain much that is peculiar to Eudemus, of which it is very doubtful whether it would have met with Aristotle’s approval. Only here, where he raises the metaphysical-psychological question τίς ἐστιν ἡ τῆς κινήσεως ἀρχὴ ἐν τῇ ψυχῇ; and answers it entirely in the spirit of the law of synonymy and with consciousness of that law, does one recognize from the way in which he speaks that he is conscious of his agreement with the principles and views prevailing in the school.

341) Metaph. Λ, 7. p. 1072, b, 23. ibid. 9. princ. That Aristotle also assigned to virtue the position relative to thinking which Eudemus gives it is shown by Metaph. Λ, 7. p. 1072, a, 30. ἀρχὴ γὰρ ἡ νόησις.

342) De Anim. III, 5. §. 2. p. 430, a, 22.

Only this one thing we may perhaps add as something in its favor, namely that the great oppositions among the later interpretations become most readily explicable through the very result of our inquiry. For according to it, it is on the one hand perfectly true that Aristotle, in the fifth chapter of the third book On the Soul, speaks of an active principle of our thoughts which is something thinking and the Deity itself, while on the other hand it has turned out that the so-called νοῦς ποιητικός, the active intellect, does not think but only makes thinking, and is a power of our own soul. Thus our explanation stands in a certain way close to all earlier interpretations, and, because, as the proverb says, the truth lies in the middle, it succeeds, despite the strictest unity, in reconciling within itself all views, even the most heterogeneous, as far as this is possible.

37. But if we ask which of the earlier attempts at explanation has come nearest to the truth, it is undeniable that we must award this honor to St. Thomas Aquinas<sup>343)</sup>. Indeed, I do not know whether I ought not to say that he grasped the whole doctrine of Aristotle correctly. For when he says that nothing corporeal can make an impression on something spiritual<sup>344)</sup>, yet at the same time has the intellectus possibilis attain to thinking through the influence of the phantasms illuminated by the intellectus agens, we must not believe that he committed the folly of having the active intellect produce a spiritual accident in a bodily subject; rather, he surely regarded the impulse given by the intellectus agens as something supra-corporeal only in *the* sense that it did not proceed, and could not proceed, from the nature of the sensitive part itself, or from that of any other body.

Still, we find in him some statements which might indeed betray a certain lack of clarity about the nature of the active intellect. To these belongs in particular a passage in which, in order to prove that the active intellect is a power of our soul, he appeals to experience. Self-observation, [p. 227] he says, makes known that it is we who abstract the concepts from individual things<sup>345)</sup>. Here he evidently took the activity of the active intellect for a self-conscious operation, whereas, as we have seen, it takes place with consciousness and freedom as little as do the vegetative functions of life. We saw, to be sure, that according to Aristotle the intellective part also acts with conscious freedom upon the sensitive part and thereby preserves its mastery over its own thinking; but here the acting principle was willing, and this entirely different kind of operation Thomas seems to have confused with the first<sup>346)</sup>.

343) See the outlines of his interpretation, Section I. n. 9.

344) Nihil autem corporeum imprimere potest in rem incorpoream Summ. Theol. 1<sup>a</sup>, q. 84. a. 6. corp.

On the whole, a certain obscurity remains over Aristotle’s doctrine of the active intellect as Thomas presents it. At least the grounds for its assumption do not come forward as clearly as in Aristotle himself, and this stems especially from the fact that he does not conceive the relation of the spiritual and the bodily part of man to each other in quite the same way as Aristotle had determined it. Thus he has the spiritual part present in the whole body, and has the sensitive and vegetative part of the soul persist together with the intellective after death, just as he also calls the whole soul [p. 228] and not merely, as Aristotle does, its lower parts, the form of the body. But according to the principles of Aristotelian physics and metaphysics, which Thomas did on the whole retain, it is impossible that something should be immortal insofar as it is form of the body, or be form of the body insofar as it is immortal, and a spiritual form of the body would be a contradictio in adjecto. Thomas did not depart from Aristotle here with conscious intent, for the Aristotelian doctrine corresponds to his other views just as well as his own does, indeed better<sup>346a)</sup>, and we therefore believe that with a changed understanding of Aristotle he would certainly also have changed his own doctrine on this point.

345) Summ. Theol. 1a, q. 79. a. 4. corp. Oportet dicere, quod in ipsa (anima) sit aliqua virtus . . ., per quam possit phantasmata illustrare. Et hoc experimento cognoscimus, dum percipimus nos abstrahere formas universales a conditionibus particularibus, quod est facere actu intelligibilia. We find the same view also in earlier writings. Cont. Gent. II. c. 76. Adhuc, si intellectus agens est quaedam substantia separata, oportet, quod ejus actio sit continua et non intercisa, vel saltem oportet dicere, quod non continetur et intercidatur ad nostrum arbitrium. Actio autem ejus est facere phantasmata intelligibilia actu. Aut igitur hoc semper faciet, aut non semper. Si non semper, non tamen hoc faciet ad arbitrium nostrum. Sed tunc intelligimus actu, quando phantasmata fiunt intelligibilia actu; igitur oportet, quod vel semper intelligamus, vel quod non sit in potestate nostra actu intelligere. Quaest. un. De Anim. a. 5. corp. Sicut enim operatio intellectus possibilis est recipere intelligibilia, ita propria operatio intellectus agentis est abstrahere ea: sic enim ea facit intelligibilia actu. Utramque autem harum operationum experimur in nobis ipsis. Nam et nos intelligibilia recipimus et abstrahimus ea.

346) The misunderstanding does not go so far that he identifies the intellectus agens and the will, or the intellectus agens and a certain activity of the will, with each other. Like Aristotle, he too has the will move the sensitive part (see e.g. Summ. Theol. 1a, q. 80. a. 2.), and, like Aristotle, he too has a thinking precede every willing (see e.g. ibid. 1a, q. 82. a. 4. ad 3um), and an activity of the active intellect precede every thinking. Only in this does he seem to us to have erred, that he nevertheless, in some way—I know not which—reckoned the activity of the intellectus agens to the domain of conscious operation. In order to speak as he did in the passages just cited (n. 345.), he necessarily had to trace back to the intellectus agens something that is an effect of willing.

In the explanation of the second part of the fifth chapter of the third book On the Soul<sup>347)</sup> Thomas was less fortunate than in that of the first; yet in substance his exposition contains nothing that would be contrary to the Aristotelian doctrine. Indeed, even if the beautiful and profound passage where Aristotle, in order to make our thinking entirely explicable, points over to the thinking of the Deity is lost to him through a false interpretation, the doctrine itself has nevertheless not escaped him, and that very passage in the Ethics of Eudemus (which he naturally takes for a work of Aristotle himself) has, so to speak, led him through a little back gate into the dwelling whose proper door had remained closed to him. “The principle of our thinking and knowing,” he says in his theological Summa, “is a knowing principle that is exalted above our intellect, and this is God, as Aristotle too teaches in the seventh book of his Ethics<sup>348)</sup>.” But another passage shows that he conceived the relation of this principle to the active intellect in entirely the same way as Aristotle<sup>349)</sup>.

Thus we have here a phenomenon that recurs with astonishing frequency in this interpreter, namely that, although he does not quite find his way with the words, he enters into the spirit of Aristotle—which would not be comprehensible without the intimate spiritual kinship of the two men. For this reason, too, one gladly forgives the small imperfections, and marvels rather at an acuteness which, although, compared with us, he was stripped of all aids and not even master of the Greek language, knew how to make up for all this in such a way that he successfully penetrated into this as well as into others of the most obscure doctrines of Aristotle. And if one further considers [p. 229] how he knew how to work up the gold won from the Aristotelian mine, and how, entirely in the Aristotelian spirit and with equal mastery, he raised the edifice of his theological doctrine, then one no longer knows with what expression of admiration one ought to do him justice. Indeed, people were not thinking of Thomas when they designated the son of the Macedonian as the greatest pupil of Aristotle; for surely he, the prince of Scholasticism and the king of all theologians, deserves this name more than anyone else.

346a) His doctrine, if one traces it back to its historical origin, leads in the point in question ultimately to the Neoplatonists.

347) Cf. besides his commentary on the three books On the Soul also the interpretation of this chapter Cont. Gent. II. c. 78.

348) Summ. Theol. 1a, q. 82. a. 4 ad 3um.

349) Summ. Theol. 1a. q. 79. a. 4. corp.

We are at the end. Let us ask ourselves: what is the philosophical problem that underlies the Aristotelian doctrine of the νοῦς ποιητικός? For what psychological question did Aristotle seek the solution when he introduced it in his books On the Soul? It was a question that is of the highest importance even now, and that at no time has let the drive of inquiry rest, namely the question of the active principle of our thinking.

In what opposite directions have minds not diverged here! How many have gone astray about themselves and ranked themselves among material beings! How many who turned away with indignation from such a thought were driven by the difficulty of the problem to the most extravagant assumptions, so that they denied either all existence of material substances, or at least every influence of the corporeal on the origination of our thoughts. If the first opinion sprang from a lack of inner observation, the second contradicted experience in a far more striking manner still, and the sound sense of mankind could never be satisfied with this hypothesis. What artificial theories have people not devised in order to replace the genuine causal nexus between the sensible and the spiritual; and how simple and luminous, by contrast, appears the assumption of our philosopher, whom the deep insight he gained into human thinking and into the entire mode of operation and order of nature knows how to preserve from all such oddities. He recognizes the great difference between sensory and spiritual cognition, but it does not occur to him to deny the connection of the two. He recognizes that the active principle, which must stand in proportion to our intellect as sound does to our hearing, cannot be a corporeal quality; but he nevertheless lets [p. 230] the effect that leads us to thinking come, in the first instance, from the sensible. A spiritual actuality, he teaches, must be the principle of thinking; but that it should be an alien spirit does not enter his mind, let alone that he should straightway lose himself from what is near and nearest in the farthest distance, and stray from our own spiritual power all the way to the spirit of the Deity. He does acknowledge, to be sure, that only with regard to the omniscience of the creative spirit could the problem find an ultimate solution that satisfies the metaphysical laws in every way; but he nonetheless holds fast to this, that the nature of man must itself bear within it the powers required for the activity most natural<sup>350)</sup> to him. In every respect he brings the sublimity and dignity of the human intellect over against the body to bear; he neither gives up the freedom of thinking, nor does he concede to the lower part, in the reciprocal intercourse between spirit and body, the priority of action; but that independence of objects, and the inner fullness and perfect self-sufficiency of the divine thinking, he has not granted to it. Its activity counts for him not as divine, but only as the most divine thing in us.

Thus we see Aristotle, with admirable art, avoid all the dangerous reefs on which many a thinker before him and after him has come to grief.

But our admiration grows when we attend to the relation of his theory of knowledge to his other psychological doctrines and to his entire worldview. There exists a beautiful harmony between the relations in which, according to him, spirit and body in man on the one hand, and thinking and sensing on the other, stand to each other. The difference as well as the intimate union of the two he upholds in equal measure in the one case and in the other; and just as, according to him, the spiritual part of man does indeed have its preparation in the bodily and enters the fetus with the highest development of matter, but is nevertheless not mixed with the body, nor stems from the power of the corporeal begetter, but from that of the creative spirit: so, according to Aristotle, intellective cognition too does indeed presuppose a preparation in the sensitive part and shows itself not wholly independent of the action of the sensitive objects; but nonetheless it is itself no activity of a bodily organ, and its proper active principle is a spiritual power of the soul.

Further, when we compare the group of the bodily and the group of the spiritual powers with each other, we perceive a perfect [p. 231] analogy between the one domain and the other. In the one as in the other we find unconsciously acting powers; in the one as in the other a capacity for the reception of foreign forms, and likewise a faculty of conscious desire, from which, once it has become actual, an outward operation proceeds. Only, the spiritual part is simple and unitary in each of these three respects, whereas the bodily part, particularly as regards the receptive and unconsciously acting powers, is split into a plurality of faculties, so that herein the higher nobility of the intellective soul makes itself known.

350) Most natural in the sense that the end of the whole human being is attained most fully in it, not in the sense that De Anim. II, 4. §. 2. p. 415, a, 26. connects with this word.

But it is not merely the same unitary spirit that pervades the whole Aristotelian doctrine of the soul; no, its dominion continues beyond its borders too and orders all the parts of the system. And so it has come about that, in order to understand that doctrine, we have repeatedly had to descend even to the first and foundational propositions of his metaphysics. Aristotle was too good a logician to have attempted to deduce everything from *one* principle; he demands a broad foundation of experience. But does this impair the unity of his system? Where is there more unity: there, where one wishes to proceed from *one* principle but in one’s deduction permits oneself all possible sophisms and inconsistencies, so that what one rejected with a loud voice and in broad daylight one seeks to bring back to oneself quietly and in the secrecy of night; or there, where one does not indeed begin without a plurality of presuppositions, but lays layer upon layer securely, and knows how to preserve, from foundation to gable, in all parts the coherence and the uniformity of style?

In Aristotle we first find intimated the profound thought that man is a world in miniature<sup>351)</sup>. Many after him have spoken the word, but according to no view, probably, does it have greater truth than precisely according to his. Let us raise our eyes from the world in miniature, as he has depicted it for us in his psychology, and look upon the great painting in which he shows us the whole of creation. Indeed, we see here, only in grander and more sublime forms, the same features recur.

In man he teaches us to recognize, despite the diversity of the parts, a unity of nature; and even if the one part appeared far more sublime than the other, the highest perfection nevertheless lay in the whole. But the world too, despite all the manifoldness and all the difference of rank among creatures, counts for him as [p. 232] something one, as a single, many-membered whole. If it is not the unity of essence that binds the parts, their union is nevertheless also a natural unity, and therefore here too the whole is what is most perfect, and in the whole of the world lies the end of the existence and the activity of all individual substances. Plants and animals and fishes and birds, rational and irrational beings, are ordered *in* it and *to* it<sup>352)</sup>, just as all the vegetative and sensitive and intellective powers of man are ordered to him as a unity.

351) De Anim. III, 11. §. 3. p. 434, a, 13. see Part III. n. 110. In Phys. VIII, 2. p. 252, b, 26. the living being in general is called μικρὸς κόσμος.

Spirit and body were, according to Aristotle, the two domains into which the nature of man divides. In the great order of the world, too, he shows us a world of bodies and of spirits, and, as in the smaller, so also in this greater whole, he lets a certain reciprocal action exist between the sensible and the supersensible. In the microcosm the spirit, at least according to nature, rules the body; it is the goal and the ordering principle of its movements; on the other side, however, thoughts are mediated to the spirit itself through the influence of the bodily, so that it comes to perfection only through the body, just as it in turn helps the body to greater happiness. Thus we see here both parts, the one serving, the other ruling, furthered by each other. In the macrocosm, although here the spiritual is exalted far more above the corporeal than in the narrow domain of man, where both were embraced as parts by the unity of the same nature, we nevertheless find a certain similarity to this relation. The spirits move<sup>353)</sup> the heavenly spheres as particular final causes and efficient principles, while conversely they experience an influence from the sensible world at least insofar as, on account of the ordering of both parts toward the whole, they necessarily themselves gain along with it in a certain way through the gain of the corporeal. Another kind of perfection cannot fall to their share through the corporeal, because they are already perfected.

To this is further added that, just as in the microcosm the spiritual part does not exert an influence immediately upon each of the members, but in the first instance touches only the noblest of all with its moving power, so also in the macrocosm the pure spirits do not enter into contact immediately with every bodily substance, but only with the noblest of all, with the stars and their moving spheres. But just as, by means of the sensitive central organ in which it is present, the spirit of man extends its dominion over all the subordinate members as well: so too the moving [p. 233] power of the spirits, in that it makes the heavenly spheres revolve, reaches down with its influence to the lowest elements, and intervenes, ruling and ordering, in the realm of earthly beings as well.

352) Cf. Metaph. Λ, 10. p. 1075, a, 11—25.

353) Cf. what we have remarked on this in the Appendix.

If in the microcosm there exists a reciprocal action between spirit and body, the first impulse, without which no reaction and no further reciprocal intercourse between the two parts would ensue, nevertheless proceeds necessarily, as we saw, from the side of the spiritual part. And this too corresponds to the relation in the macrocosm. For since, as far at least as we know, Aristotle taught no other influence of the corporeal upon the spiritual except insofar as the spirits, as part of the world-whole, gain along with it through its perfection, it is evident that, since the order of the lower world comes about under the action of the moved stars<sup>354)</sup>, the reciprocal action, as it may be called, takes its beginning from the spiritual side.

Finally, just as in the microcosm, when we wished to explain the co-ordination of the spiritual and bodily powers and the attainment of the goal, which in man lies chiefly in thinking, we had to look beyond man himself to the creating and ordering Deity, without whose preceding eternal knowledge human knowing would have appeared as a work of chance: so in the macrocosm too we see ourselves directed to a supramundane being as to the creative ground of all order, who eternally has its exemplar within himself and to this end forms and binds everything individual. Here too the attainment of the ultimate goal would otherwise appear as a work of chance, which is so unthinkable to Aristotle that he says that whoever assumes such a thing is like an empty, thoughtless head that merely babbles at random<sup>355)</sup>.

354) De Coel. II, 9. p. 291, a, 25.

355) Metaph. A, 4. p. 984, b, 17.

[scan 265]

### Beilage. — Appendix.

### Von dem Wirken, insbesondere dem schöpferischen Wirken des Aristotelischen Gottes. — On the Activity, in Particular the Creative Activity, of the Aristotelian God.

We said on p. 198. that according to the doctrine of Aristotle the pure spirits and the heavenly spheres are creatures of God.

#### I.

This emerges above all from those passages which call the Deity—if one uses this word in its most proper sense, in which it designates only one single substance—the absolutely first principle and the principle of all that is. Thus, e.g., Metaph. K, 2. p. 1060, a, 27. ἔτι δ᾽ εἴπερ ἔστι τις οὐσία καὶ ἀρχὴ τοιαύτη τὴν φύσιν οἵαν νῦν ζητοῦμεν, *καὶ αὕτη μία πάντων καὶ ἡ αὐτὴ τῶν ἀϊδίων τε καὶ φθαρτῶν*, ἀπορίαν ἔχει διὰ τί ποτε τῆς αὐτῆς ἀρχῆς οὔσης τὰ μέν ἐστιν ἀΐδια τῶν ὑπὸ τὴν ἀρχήν, τὰ δ᾽ οὐκ ἀΐδια· τοῦτο γὰρ ἄτοπον. κ. τ. λ. Ibid. 7. p. 1064, a, 35. καὶ εἴπερ ἔστι τις τοιαύτη (namely χωριστὴ καὶ ἀκίνητος) φύσις ἐν τοῖς οὖσιν, ἐνταῦθ᾽ ἂν εἴη που καὶ *τὸ θεῖον*, *καὶ αὕτη ἂν εἴη πρώτη καὶ κυριωτάτη ἀρχή*. (Note that such a principle is αἰτία τοῦ ὄντος ᾗ ὄν, Metaph. Γ, 1. p. 1003, a, 26. — fin.) In Metaph. Λ, 8. p. 1073, a, 23. the Deity is called ἡ ἀρχὴ καὶ τὸ πρῶτον τῶν ὄντων. Cf. ibid. 10. p. 1075, b, 22. 24. and p. 1076, a, 3. (where the ἀρχαὶ πολλαί of Speusippus are censured and the unity of the ultimate cause for all that is is taught), and finally ibid. 5. p. 1071, a, 36., where the πρῶτον ἐντελεχείᾳ is declared to be a cause common to all the genera of being.

Thus, according to Aristotle, the Deity is the first principle of all that is. But to what is there belong, according to him, the substances more than anything else (Metaph. Λ, 1. princ.), and these are of a threefold kind: corruptible bodies; incorruptible bodies, namely the heavenly spheres and their stars; and spirits (Ibid. p. 1069, a, 30.). All of these, therefore—as is moreover expressly stated in several of the passages named, or is to be recognized most clearly from the context—are included, and owe their existence to the Deity.

[p. 235] The same is expressed where Aristotle says that the being and life of all things, both that which stands fast unchangeably in secure form (ἀκριβέστερον) and that which is weak and wavers insecurely (ἀμαυρῶς), i.e., that of corruptible beings, depends on God (De Coel. I, 9. p. 279, a, 28.), as well as that everything, insofar as it participates in being, participates in the divine (e.g. De Anim. II, 4. §. 2. p. 415, a, 26., with which Metaph. α, 1. p. 993, b, 24. is to be compared.)

#### II.

But some have objected that Aristotle, when he says that the Deity is the principle of all that is, means not that it is its first efficient principle, but only that it is its final cause—as indeed he designates it, e.g., in De Coel. II, 12. p. 292, b, 5. and Metaph. Λ, 7. p. 1072, b, 2., as the οὗ ἕνεκα. Acting, they say, does not belong to the Aristotelian God at all.

But this is an error, and in order to refute it we shall cite some passages where Aristotle expressly and unambiguously designates God as efficient principle.

1. We recall before all else the critique of Anaxagoras (see above Sect. II. Pt. IV. n. 246.). He censures him not because he conceived the νοῦς as moving principle, but because he did not show how the end could be identical with this moving principle itself. He likewise cites him approvingly in Phys. VIII, 5. p. 256, b, 24., and again with the express remark that he taught the νοῦς as moving principle, without raising the least objection to it. Indeed, he even approves the proof of the impassibility and pure simplicity of the νοῦς that rests on the presupposition that it is the mover; from which it clearly follows that Aristotle, in the relevant part of the Physics and in the related part of the Metaphysics, speaks of God as first mover in the same sense. To this is added the high praise that he bestows on him in the first book of the Metaphysics. Metaph. A, 3. p. 984, b, 15. νοῦν δή τις εἰπὼν εἶναι, καθάπερ ἐν τοῖς ζῴοις (cf. Phys. II, 4. p. 196, a, 28.), καὶ ἐν τῇ φύσει τὸν αἴτιον τοῦ κόσμου (*Schaubach*, Anax. fragm. 8. πάντα διεκόσμησε νόος.) καὶ τῆς τάξεως πάσης οἷον νήφων ἐφάνη παρ᾽ εἰκῇ λέγοντας τοὺς πρότερον. Here too the words themselves, what immediately follows (p. 984, b, 22.), and the retrospect in the seventh chapter (p. 988, a, 33.) show how well aware Aristotle is that Anaxagoras regarded the divine νοῦς as efficient principle. (Cf. also Metaph. Λ, 6. p. 1073, a, 5., to which add Θ, 8.)

Perhaps someone will now object that this is not yet a conclusive proof of Aristotle’s view; for he does not declare expressly whether he praises Anaxagoras not merely because he made the [p. 236] νοῦς a principle, but also because he made it the moving principle.

But is it not evident that the two are inseparable? Would he have praised him, say, if he had made the Deity the form or the matter of bodies, on the ground that he had after all made it at least some principle? Surely praise here would be as great an absurdity as that for which he praised! But it would likewise be absurd if one wished to praise someone because he declared what is the efficient cause to be the end, or also the reverse, when this is not actually the case at the same time, on the ground that he had after all at least called it a cause in some way. If someone, to the question: who made the toy? were to answer: the child! would it not be nonsense to say: you have almost hit it, for it was made *for* the child, even if by the craftsman. Or: For whom was the stag shot? For the hunter. Excellent, my friend, you have almost guessed it, for the hunter shot it, even though it is destined for the royal table. Or: Did you get up the mountain through rest or through motion? Through rest. But his purpose had been to rest on the summit. No, if Aristotle, as some believe, had held God to be the mere final cause and had thought it incompatible with his dignity to be at the same time also the efficient principle, he would not have been such a fool as to bestow praise on Anaxagoras for his absolutely false, thoroughly erroneous view.

2. But we have still other, and numerous, passages in which Aristotle in clear words designates God not as end but as efficient principle.

To these belongs Top. IV, 5. p. 126, a, 34. δύναται μὲν γὰρ καὶ ὁ θεὸς καὶ ὁ σπουδαῖος τὰ φαῦλα *δρᾶν*, ἀλλ᾽ οὐκ εἰσὶ τοιοῦτοι· πάντες γὰρ οἱ φαῦλοι κατὰ προαίρεσιν λέγονται. ἔτι *πᾶσα δύναμις τῶν αἱρετῶν*· καὶ γὰρ αἱ τῶν φαύλων δυνάμεις αἱρεταί, *διὸ καὶ τὸν θεὸν* καὶ τὸν σπουδαῖον *ἔχειν φαμὲν αὐτάς*· δυνατοὺς γὰρ εἶναι τὰ φαῦλα *πράσσειν*· ὥστ᾽ οὐδενὸς ἂν εἴη ψεκτοῦ γένος ἡ δύναμις. εἰ δὲ μή, συμβήσεται τῶν ψεκτῶν τι αἱρετὸν εἶναι ἔσται γάρ τις δύναμις ψεκτή. In the consequence of the principles developed here lies the omnipotence of God, which Aristotle, as we shall hear, also actually taught.

Further Phys. II, 6. fin, (with which De Part. Animal. I, 1. p. 641, b, 10—26. is to be compared) τὸν δὲ τρόπον τῆς αἰτίας *ἐν τοῖς ὅθεν ἡ ἀρχὴ τῆς κινήσεως* ἑκάτερον (namely τὸ αὐτόματον καὶ ἡ τύχη)· ἢ γὰρ τῶν φύσει τι ἢ τῶν ἀπὸ διανοίας αἴτιον ἀεί ἐστιν· . . . ἐπεὶ δ᾽ ἐστὶ τὸ αὐτόματον καὶ ἡ τύχη αἴτια ὧν ἂν ἢ νοῦς γένοιτο αἴτιος ἢ φύσις, ὅταν κατὰ συμβεβηκὸς αἴτιόν τι γένηται τούτων αὐτῶν· οὐδὲν δὲ κατὰ συμβεβηκός ἐστι πρότερον τῶν καθ᾽ αὑτό· δῆλον ὅτι οὐδὲ τὸ κατὰ συμβεβηκὸς αἴτιον πρότερον τοῦ καθ᾽ ἑαυτό. ὕστερον ἄρα [p. 237] τὸ αὐτόματον καὶ ἡ τύχη καὶ νοῦ καὶ φύσεως· ὥστ᾽ εἰ ὅτι μάλιστα τοῦ οὐρανοῦ αἴτιον τὸ αὐτόματον, *ἀνάγκη πρότερον νοῦν καὶ φύσιν αἰτίαν εἶναι καὶ ἄλλων πολλῶν καὶ τοῦδε παντός*. It is clearly taught here that a νοῦς is the efficient principle of the world-whole. In the same way in which causality κατὰ συμβεβηκός belongs to the αὐτόματον and the τύχη, it is said to belong to the νοῦς καθ᾽ αὐτόν; but this way is that of the ὅθεν ἡ ἀρχὴ τῆς κινήσεως.

Further De Coel. I, 4. p. 271, a, 33. ὁ θεὸς καὶ ἡ φύσις οὐδὲν μάτην *ποιοῦσιν*.

Further De Generat. et Corrupt. I, 6. p. 323, a, 31. ὥστε εἴ τι κινεῖ ἀκίνητον ὄν, ἐκεῖνο μὲν ἂν ἅπτοιτο τοῦ κινητοῦ, ἐκείνου δὲ οὐδέν. With regard to this passage, comparison with Phys. VIII, 10. p. 266, b; 31. 32. and Phys. VII, 2. p. 243, a, 3. makes impossible any doubt as to whether it might not be speaking of the end rather than of the efficient principle.

Further the remarkable passage De Generat. et Corrupt. II, 10. p. 336, b, 27. ἐπεὶ γὰρ ἐν ἅπασιν ἀεὶ τοῦ βελτίονος ὀρέγεσθαί φαμεν τὴν φύσιν, βέλτιον δὲ τὸ εἶναι ἢ τὸ μὴ εἶναι (τὸ δ᾽ εἶναι ποσαχῶς λέγομεν, ἐν ἄλλοις εἴρηται), τοῦτο δ᾽ ἀδύνατον ἐν ἅπασιν ὑπάρχειν διὰ τὸ πόρρω τῆς ἀρχῆς ἀφίστασθαι, τῷ λειπομένῳ τρόπῳ *συνεπλήρωσε* τὸ ὅλον ὁ θεός, ἐνδελεχῆ (for so, with Codd. F and H, one must read instead of ἐντελεχῆ, cf. ibid. b, 25.) *ποιήσας* τὴν γένεσιν· οὕτω γὰρ ἂν μάλιστα συνείροιτο τὸ εἶναι διὰ τὸ ἐγγύτατα εἶναι τῆς οὐσίας τὸ γίνεσθαι ἀεὶ καὶ τὴν γένεσιν. Here we come to know God as efficient principle not merely for the corporeal world, but for all things (30. ἐν ἅπασιν), of which some (namely the material ones) stand only more remote from him, the others (namely the immaterial and therefore incorruptible ones) nearer; and indeed, for the things whose principle he is, he is principle not merely of their co-ordination and motion, but also of their constitution and of their substance, for he is principle of being in all the categories (29.). He has the best everywhere in view (27.), and therefore gives to that which can share in his imperishability eternal and immortal being; but for that which, as material substance, is incapable of this, he makes up for it as far as possible, by putting everlasting becoming in the place of everlasting being, because becoming comes nearest to being (33.) and in a certain way replaces it (31.), since in the place of the one being that perishes another of the same kind newly develops. (Cf. De Coel. I, 9. p. 279, a, 28. and De Anim. II, 4. §. 2. p. 415, a, 16.).

Further Metaph. Γ, 8. fin. ἔστι γάρ τι ὁ ἀεὶ κινεῖ τὰ κινούμενα, καὶ τὸ πρῶτον κινοῦν ἀκίνητον αὐτό. But should anyone think that it is the final cause and not the efficient principle that is spoken of here, we refer

Further to Metaph. Θ, 8. p. 1050, b, 4. καὶ ὥσπερ εἴπομεν, τοῦ [p. 238] χρόνου ἀεὶ προλαμβάνει ἐνέργεια ἑτέρα πρὸ ἑτέρας ἕως τῆς τοῦ ἀεὶ κινοῦντος πρώτως. That it is the efficient principle that is spoken of is proved by the preceding investigation. We single out only one proposition, on which the whole proof rests: ἀεὶ γὰρ ἐκ τοῦ δυνάμει ὄντος γίγνεται τὸ ἐνεργείᾳ ὂν *ὑπὸ ἐνεργείᾳ ὄντος*.

Further ibid. Λ, 4. fin. (see above p. 189. f.)

Further ibid. Λ, 6. p. 1071, b, 12., where the Deity is designated as a κινητικόν and ποιητικόν, and indeed as one engaged in activity: ἀλλὰ μὴν εἰ ἔσται *κινητικὸν ἢ ποιητικόν*, *μὴ ἐνεργοῦν* δέ τι, οὐκ ἔστι κίνησις ἐνδέχεται γὰρ τὸ δύναμιν ἔχον μὴ ἐνεργεῖν. οὐθὲν ἄρα ὄφελος οὐδ᾽ ἐὰν οὐσίας ποιήσωμεν ἀϊδίους, ὥσπερ οἱ τὰ εἴδη, *εἰ μή τις δυναμένη ἐνέσται ἀρχὴ μεταβάλλειν*. οὐ τοίνυν οὐδ᾽ αὐτὴ ἱκανή, οὐδ᾽ ἄλλη οὐσία παρὰ τὰ εἴδη· εἰ γὰρ μὴ *ἐνεργήσει*, οὐκ ἔσται κίνησις.

Further ibid. Λ, 7. p. 1072, b, 30., where Aristotle, in defending against the Pythagoreans and Speusippus the proposition that the most perfect is the first, compares the Deity, as cause of the world, with the man who begets. He therefore conceives it not merely as end, but also as efficient principle of all things.

The same is pointed to, in the tenth chap. of the same book, by the explanation of the first principle through the analogy of the physician (p. 1075, b, 9. see above p. 193. n. 246.), as well as by the beautiful similes of the royal sole ruler and of the general who orders his army. The general is not merely end; of everything of which he is the end he is also the efficient principle. Now God, like the general, is to be the end of the whole universe, of all earthly and heavenly things; therefore he is also acknowledged as efficient principle of all beings. Something similar follows from the image of the king. For what sort of king would that be who had no power to act? As far as he is king, so far his power reaches; and God is called king of all things, and king in unlimited fashion, εἷς κοίρανός ἔστω. (Metaph. Λ, 10. p. 1075, a, 14. ibid. fin.)

Further Eth. Nicom. I, 10. p. 1099, b, 12. θεῶν *δώρημα*.

Further ibid. VI, 2. p. 1139, b, 6. τὸ γεγονὸς οὐκ ἐνδέχεται μὴ γενέσθαι· διὸ ὀρθῶς Ἀγάθων· *μόνου* γὰρ αὐτοῦ καὶ θεὸς στερίσκεται, ἀγένητα ποιεῖν, ἅσσ᾽ ἂν ᾖ πεπραγμένα. In these words Agathon clearly teaches, besides the unalterable necessity of what has happened, at the same time the omnipotence of God, from whom, he says, only the contradictory is withheld. Descartes might perhaps not agree with him, but just as little would anyone who in any way doubted the efficient power of the Deity. Yet Aristotle designates the saying as true and makes it his own.

Further ibid. VIII, 14. p. 1162, a, 6., where it is said of the gods: *εὖ πεποιήκασι* τὰ μέγιστα; and that they are, like the parents, *αἴτιοι τοῦ εἶναι*. (Cf. Polit. I, 12. p. 1259, b, 12. De Coel. I, 9. p. 279, a, 28. Hence, too, the worship of the Deity is the [p. 239] highest and most important of all in the state. Polit. VII, 8. p. 1328, b. 12.)

Further Eth. Nicom. X, 9. p. 1179, a, 24. *ἐπιμέλεια* τῶν ἀνθρωπίνων ὑπὸ θεῶν.

Further ibid. 10. p. 1179, b, 22. τὸ μὲν οὖν τῆς φύσεως δῆλον ὡς οὐκ ἐφ᾽ ἡμῖν ὑπάρχει, ἀλλὰ *διά τινος θείας αἰτίας* τοῖς ἀληθῶς εὐτυχέσιν ὑπάρχει. That it is the efficient principle that is spoken of is proved by the contrast with λόγος καὶ διδαχή (cf. De Anim. II, 5. §. 5. p. 417, b, 9.)

Further Polit. VII, 3. p. 1325, b, 28. God, it is said, has οἰκείας *πράξεις*.

Further Oecon. 3. p. 1343, b, 26. οὕτω *προῳκονόμηται* ὑπὸ τοῦ θείου ἑκατέρου ἡ φύσις, τοῦ τε ἀνδρὸς καὶ τῆς γυναικός, πρὸς τὴν κοινωνίαν. διείληπται γὰρ τῷ μὴ ἐπὶ ταὐτὰ πάντα χρήσιμον ἔχειν τὴν δύναμιν, ἀλλ᾽ ἔνια μὲν ἐπὶ τἀναντία, εἰς ταὐτὸ δὲ συντείνοντα· τὸ μὲν γὰρ ἰσχυρότερον, τὸ δ᾽ ἀσθενέστερον *ἐποίησεν*, ἵνα κ. τ. λ.

Finally, in Rhet. II, 23. p. 1398, a, 15. we also find the expression “work of God”: τὸ δαιμόνιον οὐδέν ἐστιν, ἀλλ᾽ ἢ θεὸς ἢ θεοῦ ἔργον.

These numerous passages, which could, however, no doubt be increased by a considerable number, show sufficiently that Aristotle by no means meant to deny the acting and the active power of the deity. From some of them one can even see that he held its power to be unlimited, and its efficacy to be such as does not need an already existing matter. Everything, even immaterial being, he makes proceed from it.

But apart from all these pronouncements of Aristotle, we can, if we take into account only those in which God is designated either indeterminately as the first principle of all things or expressly as final cause, prove the correctness of our assertion in the most complete manner. In order to do this, we need only consider somewhat more closely the doctrine of the end as we find it in Aristotle.

1. First of all we ask: Did Aristotle teach that the end by itself alone produces anything, or does the end, according to him, cause nothing without an efficient principle? — Without doubt the latter was his doctrine. He requires in every case something that can act, and that it act (Metaph. Λ, 6. p. 1071, b, 29. ibid. b, 12.). And indeed the contrary is quite unthinkable, and it would be an idle dispute about words if someone wished to deny it and to assert that the end in some case produces something by itself alone. For he would necessarily have to think of this end as something already existing, since out of nothing nothing comes to be (cf. Metaph. Λ, 6. p. 1071, b, 26.). If he were now to say that this existing thing [p. 240] produces something by itself alone, he would evidently have ascribed to it all that we ascribe to the efficient principle, and his contention that this being is to be called only end and nothing else would consist only in the word.

Of whatever, then, there is an end, of that there is, according to Aristotle, necessarily also an efficient principle; and hence, if all things besides God have an end, all things besides him have an efficient principle. But what has an efficient principle must, according to Aristotle (Metaph. α, 2. p. 994, a, 5. Θ, 8. p. 1050, b, 4. and elsewhere), have a first efficient principle, i.e. one which itself has no efficient principle. Now since this is God alone, God is, according to him, the principle of things also in the sense of the efficient principle, ἡ ἀρχὴ καὶ τὸ πρῶτον τῶν ὄντων.

Or is matter perhaps the efficient principle, in that it moves itself toward the deity as its end? — Some seem to want to interpret Aristotle in this way. But nevertheless there is nothing that would contradict his doctrine more. He rejects it expressly (e.g. Metaph. Λ, 6. p. 1071, b, 29. and ibid. A, 3. p. 984, a, 21. cf. also ibid. K, 2. p. 1060, a, 19—22.), he reproaches Empedocles on that account (ibid. Λ, 10. p. 1075, b, 3.), and if he did neither, we should nevertheless be unable to assent to such an opinion, since it is contrary to the fundamental determinations of his system. For to him matter is, after all, a mere potentiality, whereas the principle of acting is always an actuality (see e.g. Phys. VIII, 5. p. 357, b, 9.); and how should matter have the faculty of knowing God? But God moves, as Aristotle says, as something known (νοητὸν Metaph. Λ, 7. p. 1072, a, 26.).

But even supposing that, according to Aristotle, matter were at the same time the efficient principle and moved itself toward its end, this would evidently be the case only with material things. But according to his view there are in the world also immaterial substances, such as the stars, the spheres and the spirits that move them; and these too are not ἐξ ἀνάγκης in the way that God is (Metaph. Λ, 7. p. 1072, b, 10.); they are for the sake of an end, and indeed their end too lies in the perfection of the whole to which they belong, and in the one supramundane spirit, which is end in a higher degree than the whole of the world (Metaph. Λ, 10. princ.) and itself has no further end. But if the immaterial substances, so far as they belong to the world, have a final cause, then according to the Aristotelian doctrine of the causes of things they necessarily also have an efficient principle. That they have no beginning in time changes nothing here; just as little as the eternity of motion makes the mover, and the infinite series of secondary causes [p. 241] the first cause, dispensable (Metaph. α, 2. p. 994, a, 15.), just as little does the everlasting existence of an immaterial substance, if it is for the sake of an end, make the efficient principle appear dispensable for it. That this principle is the deity needs no further proof, and it is therefore not merely the end for the sake of which the immaterial and the material beings are, but at the same time the creative power from which the former, and then surely also the latter, proceed.

Let us look back once more on our proof. According to Aristotle, all being, even that of the immaterial substances, has an end, if we except the deity alone. But of whatever there is an end, of that there is, according to him, also an efficient principle, and of whatever there is an efficient principle, of that there is a first efficient principle, i.e. one which itself has no efficient principle and thus also no final cause. Now since such a principle is the deity alone, the final cause of all things, itself ordered to no end, it is evident that in Aristotle’s view it must at the same time be the first and universal efficient principle.

2. But although we consider this proof perfectly conclusive, we will nevertheless not begrudge the trouble of considering the matter once more from another side, and we hope in this way to make it so evident that the Aristotelian God, so far as he is end, is also efficient cause, that hardly anyone, however decidedly he may have held the opposite opinion, will be able to close his mind any longer to this conviction. For with regard to the preceding discussion someone might perhaps think that *we* had indeed drawn this doctrine as a consequence from Aristotelian premises, but that he himself had failed to do just this and, by a shortsightedness that is admittedly hard to comprehend, had stopped short at the principles. With regard to the proof now to be conducted, however, even this evasion is quite impossible.

When Aristotle says that God is the end of something, the question arises in what sense he understands this. As is well known, there is according to him (De Anim. II, 4. §. 2. p. 415, b, 2. cf. Phys. II, 2. p. 194, a, 35.) a twofold “wherefore” (οὗ ἕνεκα), as he likes to designate the final cause: first, the οὗ ἕνεκα οὗ, that which is desired, and second, the οὗ ἕνεκα ᾧ, that for which something is desired. Is the Aristotelian God, we must therefore ask, an οὗ ἕνεκα οὗ? He certainly is this, as all interpreters too acknowledge, for he is designated as that which is desired (ὀρεκτόν) (Metaph. Λ, 7. p. 1072, a, 26.). But a new question arises: How can something that already is be thought of as οὗ ἕνεκα οὗ? for this seems, after all, rather to be something future [p. 242] (ἐσόμενον De Part. Animal. I, 1. p. 640, a, 3.), which is the terminus of the acting, as, e.g., health is the οὗ ἕνεκα οὗ in the healing activity of the physician. An already existing, absolutely necessary substance, independent of all acting, such as the deity, thus seems unable to be the οὗ ἕνεκα οὗ. (Cf., besides the passage from Metaph. Λ. to be cited presently, esp. ibid. K, 1. p. 1059, b, 35.) This is to be answered with a new distinction. The οὗ ἕνεκα οὗ, too, is twofold, “of which the one is and the other is not.” (Metaph. Λ, 7. p. 1072, b, 1. ὅτι δ᾽ ἔστι τὸ οὗ ἕνεκα ἐν τοῖς ἀκινήτοις ἡ διαίρεσις δηλοῖ. ἔστι γὰρ διττὸν (as is to be read, with Schwegler, with whom Bonitz and others agree, instead of τινί) τὸ οὗ ἕνεκα, ὧν τὸ μὲν ἔστι, τὸ δ᾽ οὐκ ἔστι.) The οὗ ἕνεκα of which Aristotle here says that it is not is that which is to be effected as such; the οὗ ἕνεκα, on the other hand, of which he says that it is, is that which is to be effected insofar as, by virtue of the law of synonymy, it pre-exists according to likeness in that which acts, as, e.g., the order of the army in the intellect of the general. This already is when the general orders the army, whereas the order, insofar as it is in the army, is not yet. Both can be designated as οὗ ἕνεκα, the former even more than the latter, because the latter is for the sake of the former and not conversely (Metaph. Λ, 10. princ.). In this way, then, the deity too, although already existing, is an οὗ ἕνεκα and ὀρεκτόν. It is thus an οὗ ἕνεκα insofar as this is found in that which acts, and hence it is evident that in it itself the efficient cause is at the same time present. (Cf., for further confirmation, what is said in De Anim. III, 10., from which the passages given on p. 109. n. 103. are taken, about the ὀρεκτόν and κινοῦν ἀκίνητον, of which Metaph. Λ, 7. p. 1072, a 26. clearly reminds us.)<sup>1)</sup>. If this were not the case, there would have to be yet a third οὗ ἕνεκα οὗ, which would be neither in that which acts nor in that which is effected. But Aristotle knows of no such thing.

1) It must not be ascribed to an oversight that we do not appeal here to De Generat. Animal. II, 6. p. 742, a, 22., where Aristotle seems to distinguish a twofold οὗ ἕνεκα, of which the one is the ὅθεν ἡ κίνησις and is explained by τὸ γεννητικόν. For the reading adopted by Bekker is wrong here. One must, with Codd. P. and S., read τούτου ἕνεκα instead of οὗ ἕνεκα. Of this, Aristotle says, the one is the ὅθεν ἡ κίνησις, the other the ᾧ χρῆται τὸ οὗ ἕνεκα; and as an example of the one he names the γεννητικόν, of the other the ὀργανικόν of that which is generated and which is the οὗ ἕνεκα of both. This emerges clearly from what follows, esp. a, 28.: τριῶν δ᾽ ὄντων, *ἑνὸς* μὲν τοῦ τέλους, ὃ λέγομεν εἶναι *οὗ ἕνεκα*, *δευτέρου* δὲ *τῶν τούτου ἕνεκα* τῆς *ἀρχῆς* τῆς κινητικῆς καὶ γεννητικῆς . . ., *τρίτου* δὲ τοῦ χρησίμου καὶ ᾧ *χρῆται* τὸ τέλος, κ. τ. λ. To draw in this passage for the explanation of the Aristotelian distinctions of the οὗ ἕνεκα could therefore only have made understanding more difficult.

[p. 243] And what, indeed, should he have understood by it? Evidently it would be nothing other than a mere model (παράδειγμα). But Aristotle does not accept a mere model as οὗ ἕνεκα. Indeed, if someone were to say that the deity is the cause of the world in the manner of a model, this would be, according to what Aristotle aptly remarks in the first book of the Metaphysics, empty talk and a mere poetic metaphor, under which the lack of a clear concept seeks to hide itself. For what use can a model be if there is not, outside it, an efficient cause which in its creating looks to the model? If there is no such cause, the model by itself alone is useless; but if there is one, then the model itself, and so in our case the deity, becomes dispensable. For why should the acting of the cause of the world necessarily be the imitation of a model? Let us hear how Aristotle himself expresses himself on this. In Metaph. A, 9. p. 991, a, 20. he says: τὸ δὲ λέγειν παραδείγματα αὐτὰ (namely τὰ εἴδη) εἶναι καὶ μετέχειν αὐτῶν τἆλλα κενολογεῖν ἐστὶ καὶ μεταφορὰς λέγειν ποιητικάς. τί γάρ ἐστι τὸ ἐργαζόμενον πρὸς τὰς ἰδέας ἀποβλέπον; ἐνδέχεται γὰρ καὶ εἶναι καὶ γίγνεσθαι ὅμοιον ὁτιοῦν καὶ μὴ εἰκαζόμενον πρὸς ἐκεῖνο, ὥστε καὶ ὄντος Σωκράτους καὶ μὴ ὄντος γένοιτ᾽ ἂν οἷοσπερ Σωκράτης· ὁμοίως δὲ δῆλον ὅτι κἂν εἰ ἦν ὁ Σωκράτης ἀΐδιος. . . . . ἐν δὲ τῷ Φαίδωνι οὕτως λέγεται, ὡς καὶ τοῦ εἶναι καὶ τοῦ γίγνεσθαι αἴτια τὰ εἴδη ἐστίν. καίτοι τῶν εἰδῶν ὄντων ὅμως οὐ γίγνεται τὰ μετέχοντα, ἂν μὴ ᾖ τὸ κινῆσον, κ. τ. λ. So Aristotle. But we ask: Can we ascribe to this philosopher a view which he himself so zealously combats and so strikingly refutes? Certainly we should not be entitled to do this, even if he did not, where he speaks of the deity in the twelfth book, remark once more expressly that a being such as the Ideas does not suffice, that rather a principle is required to which efficient power and acting belong. Metaph. Λ, 6. p. 1071, b, 14. οὐθὲν ἄρα ὄφελος οὐδ᾽ ἐὰν οὐσίας ποιήσωμεν ἀϊδίους, *ὥσπερ οἱ τὰ εἴδη*, εἰ μή τις δυναμένη ἐνέσται ἀρχὴ μεταβάλλειν. οὐ τοίνυν οὐδ᾽ αὕτη ἱκανή, οὐδ᾽ ἄλλη οὐσία παρὰ τὰ εἴδη. εἰ γὰρ μὴ ἐνεργήσει, οὐκ ἔσται κίνησις.

With this we consider our task accomplished. For after what has been said, there is no doubt that Aristotle, when he held the deity to be the end of all that is, also regarded it as the efficient cause of all things.

If we now apply this result specifically to the question from which our investigation started, it is evident that we have rightly asserted of the Aristotelian God that he is the efficient principle of all other immaterial substances: of the spirits, of the spheres and of their stars. But the efficient principle of something immaterial is, as a matter of course, a creative principle. Thus, according to Aristotle, God is the creator of the heavens and of the powers [p. 244] of the heavens, and this is as certain as it is certain that, according to him, these are not, like the deity, absolutely necessary, but are for the sake of an end. We have already (under I. and II.) cited numerous passages in which this was clearly stated. But because our investigation finds its proper conclusion in this, we will add one more passage here too in proof of it.

We take it from the eighth chapter of the twelfth book of the Metaphysics, where Aristotle infers the number of the pure spirits from the number of the spheres. How does he arrive at this inference, which at first glance is quite incomprehensible? The ground that is decisive for him here is a teleological one. The part is for the sake of the whole, and is therefore good and perfect only insofar as it is ordered to the whole in its nature and its activity. This holds so without exception that even a substance so perfect in and for itself as that of a pure spirit would, if it did not stand in relation to the other parts of the world and make its perfection effective through an influence on the lower beings—thus, if it did not, joined with the others into a unified order, cooperate with them toward the perfection of the universe—be in a certain way imperfect in spite of its perfection; and such an imperfection is indeed admissible up to a certain degree in the individuals of lower species, because several are of *one* species (De Coel. I, 9. p. 278, a, 15.) (for nature has the species especially in view), but in those highest, purely spiritual beings, each of which is a species of its own, it would be quite intolerable. (Metaph. Λ, 10. p. 1075, a, 19. see also Θ, 9. p. 1051, a, 19.) Thus Aristotle says that it is not credible that, besides the spirits which, as particular final causes, move the heavenly spheres, there should be yet another impassible substance, existing in and for itself (οὐσία ἀπαθὴς καὶ καθ᾽ αὑτήν), i.e. a purely spiritual substance; for it would not have attained the whole perfection belonging to it, its full end; but this, he says, is unthinkable. Metaph. Λ, 8. p. 1074, a, 14. τὸ μὲν οὖν πλῆθος τῶν σφαιρῶν ἔστω τοσοῦτον· ὥστε καὶ τὰς οὐσίας καὶ τὰς ἀρχὰς τὰς ἀκινήτους καὶ τὰς αἰσθητὰς τοσαύτας εὔλογον ὑπολαβεῖν· τὸ γὰρ ἀναγκαῖον ἀφείσθω τοῖς ἰσχυροτέροις λέγειν. εἰ δὲ μηδεμίαν οἷόν τ᾽ εἶναι φορὰν μὴ συντείνουσαν πρὸς ἄστρου φοράν (cf. ibid. 10. p. 1075, a, 16. 19. 24. For it is through the star that the sphere acts upon the lower world, as, e.g., the solar sphere does through the sun (De Generat. et Corrupt. p. 236, b, 17.). Hence a sphere that contributed in no way to the motion of a star would remain without influence on the lower world), ἔτι δὲ πᾶσαν φύσιν καὶ πᾶσαν οὐσίαν ἀπαθῆ καὶ καθ᾽ αὐτὴν τοῦ ἀρίστου τετυχηκυῖαν τέλους (The above explanation shows that this passage is not corrupt, as Bonitz thought when he proposed to put τέλος [p. 245] in place of τέλους. The proof would become ineffective, since it is rooted solely in the thought that the world cannot be episodic and disjointed like a bad tragedy (Metaph. M, 3. p. 1090, b, 19.), and that therefore even the most sublime creature, if it were not ordered in a corresponding manner to the still more sublime good, to the whole of the world, and thus did not also cooperate in unity with the lower beings and make its ordering influence effective in the corporeal world, would not perfectly correspond to its end and so would not be perfectly good.) εἶναι δεῖ νομίζειν, οὐδεμία ἂν εἴη παρὰ ταύτας ἑτέρα φύσις, ἀλλὰ τοῦτον ἀνάγκη τὸν ἀριθμὸν εἶναι τῶν οὐσιῶν. εἴτε γὰρ εἰσιν ἕτεραι, κινοῖεν ἂν ὡς τέλος οὖσαι φορᾶς.

So from this passage, too, we see anew that, according to Aristotle, the deity that truly and properly deserves the name is the only supramundane being (Metaph. Λ, 10. fin. K, 2. p. 1060, a, 28. Phys. VIII, 6. p. 259, a, 12.), while the other spiritual substances, and the heavenly spheres and the stars, although they too are sometimes called divine, are comprised in the world as parts. Like the other parts, they too have their end in the whole of the world, and still more in the supramundane deity; and with this, as we have shown, it is at the same time stated that they also owe their existence to an efficient principle, and that this efficient and creative principle is the same deity in which we recognize their ultimate end.

We have endeavored to keep our argument as brief as possible, since these discussions do not belong to the proper subject of our treatise, although on the other hand they could not be avoided either, lest what essentially belongs to it should lose credibility. We must therefore also indicate, at least in all brevity, how the difficulties that stand in the way of our view—which, if we are not mistaken, has been completely proved—and that have given occasion to erroneous interpretations, can be removed.

They are chiefly the following;

1. Aristotle teaches that God moves as something desired (ὀρεκτόν, ἐρώμενον Metaph. Λ, 7. p. 1072, a, 26. b, 3.), not that he moves as desire (ὄρεξις); and he had to teach so, since otherwise, according to his principles, God would not be the first unmoved mover (De Anim. III, 10. §. 6. p. 433, b, 11. §. 7. b, 14. 17. cf. K, 1. p. 1059, a, 37.). He thus teaches, rather, that the desire is in things, inasmuch as things strive toward God as their end. Thus the first efficient principle is in things, and God is only the end; for [p. 246] it is desire, not what is desired, that is the moving principle in us (see above p. 108. ff. p. 159. ff.)

To this the following is now to be replied. It is correct that, according to Aristotle, God moves as something desired, because he is the ultimate end of the world. But from this it does not follow that he cannot at the same time be the efficient principle, nor, just as little, that no willing can belong to him—provided only that it does not belong to him in such a way that, as is the case with us, he *has* it *in himself*, but in such a way that he *is* it, just as he does not have his thinking in himself, but is it. We can even show that Aristotle ascribed to God such an analogue of our desire. For he ascribed to him<sup>2)</sup> a pleasure (ἡδονή), which in us, even when it is a joy in one’s own thinking, is not the thinking itself (Eth. Nicom. X, 5. p. 1175, b, 34.), but an accompanying affection of the appetitive faculty (see above p. 355. and n. 124.). It was precisely on this that we earlier grounded a proof of an appetitive power of our spiritual part. Where there is pleasure (ἡδονή), Aristotle says, there is desire (ὄρεξις see above loc. cit.); but in God, he says, there is pleasure (ἡδονή. Eth. Nicom. VII, 15. p. 1154, b, 26.). — But is it, then, in him too, as in us, distinct from thinking? No; for God is perfectly simple and nothing other than his thinking (Metaph. Λ, 9. p. 1074, b, 34.). Thus in him desire and thinking, ὄρεξις and νόησις, are one in the most perfect manner, and hence he can indeed, without moving himself, be at the same time the end and the efficient principle of things.

But, it will perhaps be objected, God rejoices, as Aristotle says, with a unitary joy in his own thinking, ὁ θεὸς ἀεὶ μίαν καὶ ἁπλῆν χαίρει ἡδονήν (Eth. Nicom. loc. cit.), and from this it follows that he himself is the sole object of his desire, blessed in the eternal and necessary possession of what is desired; but such a desire, directed at no ἀγαθὸν πρακτόν, by no means explains an efficacy (De Anim. III, 10. §. 4. p. 433, a, 29.). — The answer to this lies near at hand. Just as God, in knowing himself, knows the whole of creation, and knows it through his own knowing, so [p. 247] he also, in desiring himself, desires for his own sake the universe and the whole order of things; and so this blessed desire of himself is at the same time the omnipotent principle from which the universe has proceeded, ordered and according to plan, as health proceeds from the art and the will of the physician (Metaph. Λ, 10. p. 1075, b, 9. ibid. Θ, 7. p. 1049, a, 5.).

2) The same follows immediately from passages such as Top. IV, 5. p. 126, a, 34. (see above p. 600.) Eth. Nicom. X, 9. p. 1179, a, 24. ibid. VII, 1. p. 1145, a, 20. 26. For the ἀρετή that is spoken of here is a moral one. Further, it follows from the passages in which the omnipotence of God is asserted (see the passage Nicom. VI, 2., which was cited on p. 601.); for what acts without desire does not have the power for opposites (Metaph. Θ, 5. p. 1048. a, 5. ibid. 7. p. 1049, a, 5.). — Almost all these passages show not merely that God has a willing, but at the same time that it is directed also at things other than himself. The passage from Eth. Nicom. VII., however, points to the difference of the divine willing from ours, of which we shall speak presently.

But, it is said, Aristotle does say that nature desires the order of the world (Metaph. Λ, 10. princ.), and hence not the deity. — The conclusion is evidently false. For if the sons and daughters and the whole staff of servants of the house strive after that order of the whole for the sake of which the father of the house has given each one his commissions (ibid. a, 19.), does it follow from this that he himself cannot also desire it? — That would indeed be a ridiculous inference; on the contrary, he will desire it even more than they, and will love most the one who strives after it most zealously. So too God. He lays down in each being its nature, as it were as a communication of his law and his command (ibid. a, 22.), and loves most those that most comply with them, as, e.g., among men, those who live for the spirit (Eth. Nicom. X, 9. p. 1179, a, 28. De Divin. 2. princ. and p. 464, a, 21. ibid. 1. p. 462, b, 20.), because this life is in accordance with their nature, and thus with his command.

We see that the first difficulty is of no weight whatever. A contradiction with what we have shown does not exist. Aristotle would rather have been unfaithful to his principles as set forth by us if he had spoken otherwise.

2. The same will turn out to hold with regard to a second objection, however dangerous it may sound at first. For Aristotle says in the second book On the Heavens (De Coel.
