II, 12. p. 292, a, 22.) that that being which surpasses all others in perfection is without action; and in the seventh book of the Politics (3. p. 1325, b, 29.) he says that the deity and the world have no intercourse with anything outside. So, too, in the Nicomachean Ethics (X, 8. p. 1178, b, 7.) he infers that the theoretical life is the most perfect from the fact that it is the one most like God; for the life of God, he says, is neither productive nor practical, but pure contemplation. What, then—do these passages not teach that God has no acting? —

No, they do not teach it, and in each of them the context itself shows clearly enough that Aristotle meant to say something quite different. For Aristotle distinguishes a threefold kind of activity through which man becomes partaker of a certain happiness: first, the *productive* life, which creates external works, be it from need, advantage or pleasure; [p. 248] second, the *practical* life, which has its goal and finds its happiness in virtuous, lawful action; finally, the *theoretical* life, which is blessed in the contemplation of truth, in particular of the highest truth, namely God.

Now it is evident that God, even if he creates the most glorious works, is happy not through this producing but through himself; for he needs no work and is enriched in nothing by it (De Coel. I, 9. p. 279, a, 35.). It would therefore be ridiculous to say that his happiness is that of the productive life. Just as little can one say that he finds his happiness in the exercise of ethical virtues: through justice in buying and selling, through courage in dangers, through liberal disposal of his possessions, e.g. magnanimous gifts of money to poor fellow gods, through self-conquest and resistance to evil desires, and the like. Whoever wished to attribute such virtues to God would dishonor him instead of honoring him; for they relate partly to social life (cf. Eth. Nicom. VIII, 9. p. 1158, b, 35. p. 1159, a, 5. Polit. I, 2. 1253, a, 27.), partly to human weakness and frailty. There thus remains only the theoretical life as that in which God can partake of his happiness. And indeed we have seen that he is pure knowing. If, then, he is happy through himself, not through anything else, his blessedness is a theoretical one, and his life is the knowing of knowing. This is what the passage of the Nicomachean Ethics teaches. Cf. Metaph. Λ, 7. p. 1072, b, 14—30. ibid. 9. p. 1074, b, 34. We see that Aristotle does not remotely think of denying to the deity the power of acting; he only denies that any need or imperfection drives it to act, and that anything other than itself could constitute or increase its happiness.

That the passage from the second book On the Heavens likewise means to say nothing other than that God is without needs and blessed in himself is shown by the reason that Aristotle adds immediately afterward. The being that comprises all perfection in itself, he says, needs no action (De Coel. II, 12. p. 292, b, 5.). He thus denies in God that acting which aims at acquisition, or which seeks, through intercourse or in any way whatever, an increase of one’s own happiness.

The passage of the Politics, finally, shows still more clearly that Aristotle means to deny to the deity only that acting which would be the consequence of its own insufficiency, an insufficiency that would compel it to remedy its own lack through mutual intercourse and exchange of goods and service and counter-service, so that the deity would then fall into partial dependence on alien ends, whereas it is, after all, the sole end of all that is. The deity can only give (Eth. Nicom. I, [p. 249] 10. p. 1099, b, 3.), not receive; it is a mover that remains wholly unmoved, and therefore, according to Aristotle’s beautiful phrase, touches without itself being touched (De Generat. et Corrupt. I, 6. p. 323, a, 31.).

3. These two objections were directed against the acting of God in general. But two others are urged in particular against the claim that Aristotle also traced the being of the immaterial substances back to the efficient power of God.

For, first, in the first book of the Physics (4. p. 187, a, 34.) and in many other places he commends and defends the axiom of the earlier natural philosophers that out of nothing nothing comes to be, and, where he speaks of the principles of coming to be, he requires a matter for it; and accordingly he also defines, in the twelfth chapter of the fifth book of the Metaphysics (p. 1020, a, 4.), active power as the faculty of producing a *change in something other* (ἀρχὴ μεταβλητικὴ ἐν ἄλλῳ ἢ ἄλλο).

But these passages prove nothing. When Aristotle says that thinking is a being affected (De Anim. III, 4. §. 2. p. 429, a, 14. ibid. §. 9 b, 25. §. 11. b, 29.), he means *our* thinking, and perhaps all thinking of created spirits (for the ninth chapter of the twelfth book of the Metaphysics speaks only of *one* divine νοῦς); but divine thinking, as this passage proves and as all admit, is not included. Likewise nothing guarantees to us that, where he speaks of acting, he always includes the acting of divine omnipotence as well; rather, the contrary has every probability in its favor (cf. the position, set apart from all other efficient principles, which he assigns to the deity in Metaph. Λ, 4. fin.); indeed, the expression μεταβάλλειν, strictly taken, does not even fit all the acting of creatures (cf. De Anim. II, 5. §. 4. p. 417, a, 32. and the preceding and the following §. §.).

Likewise, when Aristotle designates matter as a necessary ground of coming to be, he has in view only that coming to be which is the passing away of something else; the reasons that he gives in Metaph. Λ, 2. (p. 1069, b, 3.) relate only to this kind of becoming. (Cf. also Metaph. H, 5. p. 1044, b, 28.) And when he says in the first book of the Physics, as he also repeats elsewhere, that out of nothing nothing comes to be, he means thereby only to acknowledge a limit of the natural powers, not to decide whether this is impossible absolutely, and thus also for the deity. This emerges clearly from his saying (Phys. I, 4. p. 187, a, 34.): τὸ μὲν ἐκ μὴ ὄντων γίνεσθαι ἀδύνατον (περὶ γὰρ ταύτης ὁμογνωμονοῦσι τῆς δόξης ἅπαντες *οἱ περὶ φύσεως*). Cf. Phys. I, 7, p. 189, b, 30. 31., where one likewise sees that he always has in view only [p. 250] what is universally valid in physics. And likewise he says in Phys. VIII, 6. (p. 258, b, 12. 16—22.) that in these books he does not wish to decide anything on the question whether there is a substantial becoming and ceasing that is not a coming to be and passing away (γένεσις and φθορά). We see, then, that this objection rests on an entirely uncertain foundation.

4. Let us hear whether another and final objection will succeed in bringing forward anything more tenable. Aristotle says that φορά is prior to γένεσις (Phys. VIII, 7. p. 260, b, 24. cf. p. 261, a, 7. Metaph. Λ, 7. p. 1072, b, 8.). But γένεσις is the becoming of substance, while φορά is local motion, which already presupposes a substance. Hence there is a substance (and indeed a substance that is not immovable) which is not produced by another.

But this objection can, so to speak, be solved by any child, if it looks at the passage cited immediately before. Aristotle does not call all substantial becoming γένεσις, but only that which is at the same time the passing away of something else. Hence also the title Περὶ γενέσεως καὶ φθορᾶς. In the passage cited from the Metaphysics the thought emerges still more unambiguously; for Aristotle says there φορὰ γὰρ ἡ πρώτη τῶν *μεταβολῶν*. So we see how the last of these four objections too—in which we have summed up everything that, to our knowledge, has been objected against the assumption of a creative omnipotence on the part of Aristotle—dissolves into nothing, and that what could be positively proved from numerous passages could also easily be defended against all attacks.

The Aristotelian doctrine of the soul, which makes God appear as the creator of the human spirit, thus does not on this account stand in any contrast to his doctrine of God in general; rather, it only shows in one example what is shown in many others besides, and what the physical writings and the Metaphysics and the Ethics state several times in the most general manner: the deity is not merely the mover of the corporeal world; it is the creative principle of all things, ἡ ἀρχὴ καὶ τὸ πρῶτον τῶν ὄντων (Metaph. Λ, 8.). Therefore Agathon is right when he says that only what is self-contradictory is withheld from God’s omnipotence (Eth. Nicom. VI, 2. p. 1139, b, 8.), and the word of Homer<sup>3)</sup> which calls the king of the universe the father of gods and men is truth (Polit. I, 12. p. 1259, b, 10.).

3) Even if Aristotle here praises the old poet’s word (πατὴρ ἀνδρῶν τε θεῶν τε) in the first instance for another reason, Eth. Nicom. VIII, 14. p. 1162, a, 4—7., where the deity, like the parents, appears as the cause of the being of man (αἴτιος τοῦ εἶναι), nevertheless shows that he would have had no hesitation in making it his own in an even more significant sense as well.
